# Document Structure

This document is available in both PDF (for printing) and Markdown (for AI assistants) formats. We value the real-world, human insights you bring, and welcome your use of AI to help you to provide them. We suggest that reviewers leveraging AI assistants monitor for text truncation or generalizations. This profile’s structure intentionally departs from typical guidance, so some pre-processors may omit table rows or appendices unless explicitly directed to parse the text in its entirety. 

This document begins with guidance for reviewers, followed by an Introduction covering the profile's audience, scope, approach to AI trustworthiness, and how to use this document. The core content is a table of 13 distinct Practices, subdivided into Tasks and Implementations, structured around operational workflows and extensively cross-referenced. Notes for reviewers are prefixed by a "\>" and highlighted in blue; treat these as meta-commentary rather than guidance. After the table come the Acknowledgements and Glossary, five appendices, and a conceptual changelog. Appendices A, C and D are placeholders; Appendix B maps real-world incidents to Practices, and Appendix E holds additional information for reviewers. The document ends with the marker END OF DISCUSSION DRAFT.

# Notes to the Reviewer

Thank you for taking the time to review this early-stage discussion draft\! To maximize your opportunity to shape this resource from the ground up, we are intentionally prioritizing early availability for community review over editorial refinement. Please expect rough text, incomplete sections, partial/incomplete references, uneven emphasis, and layout inconsistencies as we trial options and rapidly integrate ongoing feedback. To make the best use of your time, please focus on high-level content, structural utility, and terminology. Future iterations will address consistency, copyediting, and formatting.

We are incredibly grateful for the input this community has already shared. If a past suggestion isn't reflected in this draft, please rest assured it isn't forgotten; our team is navigating a substantial processing backlog alongside complex cross-sector perspectives and scope constraints. To help you avoid duplicating effort, incomplete fields are marked "Candidates \- suggestions welcome" or "None yet \- suggestions welcome", and [Appendix E: Additional Information for the Reviewer](#bookmark=id.nlv4g5r6cdno) lists roadmap items not yet in the text. If there is a missing point you feel strongly about that isn't reflected in either this draft or that list, we welcome a brief summary that highlights the core issue and its operational importance, so we can ensure it stays front and center.

## Tips for Providing Feedback

To help our team give every piece of input the deep attention it deserves and avoid losing important concepts in the noise, we would be incredibly grateful if you could keep these guidelines in mind:

**Direct and Actionable:** If a specific practice, task, or implementation needs refinement, please include its index number and your suggestion for concrete alternative text or structural changes. Where a point applies to the document as a whole, to Table 2, or to an appendix, please provide surrounding context in lieu of the index number.

**Summarize Big Ideas:** For complex or high-level recommendations, please provide a clear, actionable, one- or two-sentence summary of your core point. Feel free to indicate if you are open to a brief follow-up conversation with our team to explore your suggestions further. 

**Sector and Governance Implications:** We value your domain expertise on how AI impacts the operational, safety, and governance realities of your specific sector. Where this document introduces an AI concept, the input we find most useful is how it will land with your colleagues: which terms may be read differently in your sector, and how we might introduce them better.

**Ground AI in Real-World Experience:** We also use AI to assist with this work and are particularly looking to you for the insights that it cannot provide, rather than for general explanation of AI concepts. If you use an AI assistant to help with your feedback, please ensure that your real-world insights are accurately and concisely captured, so that the output prioritizes actionable advice and references that end users can readily implement. Please do also add a short disclosure and, if you like, a brief description of how AI helped you. 

**Help Us with References:** Let us know if the references we have pointed to are in the right place, if there are better ones, and which specific sections offer the most transferable utility to an AI deployment. Please prioritize sources that are actionable and add a brief note on the operational context and where it applies. 

**Build on our To-Do Items:** Please review the "Ideas and References Already in Progress" section in [Appendix E: Additional Information for the Reviewer](#bookmark=id.nlv4g5r6cdno) and see if we’re already on the right track, and let us know of any additional insights that might help. Similarly, we welcome suggestions for placeholders currently marked “TBD.”

## Key Areas for Feedback

We have inserted reviewer notes throughout the tables and at the end of the document. We are also particularly seeking feedback on the following general points. 

**Scope & Sector Alignment:** We prioritize shared challenges that offer transferable, cross-sector insights. What universally critical elements are missing, and which current examples should be adjusted to resonate more widely? Which sections are better suited for future sector-specific companion resources? How can this central document best serve as a foundational reference for guidance that you or your sector’s organizations may be developing?

**Operational Feasibility & Safety:** Are these tasks realistically achievable across legacy operational technology (OT) and resource-constrained environments? For example, in high-speed automated systems, when are manual "human-in-the-loop" workflows practical versus when do operational realities and resource constraints require automated, deterministic safety boundaries or fail-safe logic? Is the general language clear and functional when applied to your sector's specific safety realities?

**Strategic Governance & Executive Translation (Open Design Area):** We seek inputs, including use cases, operational scenarios, and references to regulations and other governance artifacts, to help executive leadership map high-level governance, organizational missions, and compliance obligations to the practices in this document. We also welcome suggestions for how to best facilitate this connection in a way that is appropriate for this cross-sector profile, as well as factors to consider for possible future sector-specific guidance. 

**Document Design Criteria:** Do the current entries successfully hit the relevant Document Design Criteria that appear in [Appendix E: Additional Information for the Reviewer](#bookmark=id.nlv4g5r6cdno)? Are there additional design criteria we should adopt to ensure this profile remains practical and actionable?

**Glossary Priorities:** Which terms are most likely to cause confusion when bridging the gap between AI development and real-world critical infrastructure governance? What specific definitions or semantic conflicts should we explicitly clarify to prevent communication breakdowns between sectors or along the supply chain?

## Suggested Ways to Review this Document

This document is not meant to be read cover to cover. We suggest reading this front matter, then choose your own adventure: start wherever your interest or responsibility lies and follow the cross-references or hand the Markdown to your AI assistant and ask it for the parts and paths that matter to you. We welcome any level of feedback you can provide. Here are some suggested paths, depending on the time you have available. 

**Narrative Scan:** Focus on the Introduction and the high-level Practice/Task headings. Evaluate whether our overall approach and risk families align with your sector's realities.

**Priority Check:** Review the core practices in the main table. If your organization could only focus on a limited subset of these, which are your immediate "must-haves"? Which ones are missing? 

**Technical Deep Dive:** Examine the specific Implementation rows and framework mappings relevant to your technical, operational, procurement, or policy role. Help us refine the operational, contractual, and technical "bridge language" to ensure these controls work cleanly in real-world environments and organizational workflows. (Note: Some practices are more complete than others, and some fields are empty or minimal. We warmly welcome your ideas for filling these out.)

1. # Introduction

The Trustworthy Artificial Intelligence (AI) in Critical Infrastructure and High-Consequence Applications Profile (TACIP) assists organizations across the 16 Critical Infrastructure (CI) sectors (shown in Table 1\) and high-consequence applications, their associated supply chains, and broader ecosystem. It provides actionable guidance for adopting trustworthy AI systems, including AI-enabled agents and tools, to meet growing demands for enhanced capacity, reliability, efficiency, and safety within real-world operational environments. While framed around critical infrastructure, the risk management practices within this profile apply broadly to any high-consequence or safety-critical application where AI failures pose significant risk. 

For the purpose of this document, AI is defined as “a machine-based system that can, for a given set of human-defined objectives, make predictions, recommendations or decisions influencing real or virtual environments.” This definition, adapted from 15 U.S.C. (United States Code) § 9401, includes reasoning, planning, optimization, and statistical systems, deep learning neural networks, large language models (LLMs), and hybrid systems that combine features of multiple forms of AI. 

This document is not intended to be exhaustive and should not be treated as a rigid compliance checklist, but rather as a collection of common risk management considerations with cross-sector applicability. It establishes that operational trust requires both governance and technical evidence. This profile populates the NIST AI RMF from the control and task level downwards to examples of actionable implementations, and upward towards governance to a shared practice layer. This guidance is intentionally not 'governance-complete' and stops where sector-agnostic guidance ends. Sector-specific organizations are encouraged to adapt and extend this profile and make it their own, complete with sector-specific prioritizations, contextualizations, use cases, operational scenarios, hazards, and case studies. 

AI systems are software-based and should be subject to the same risk management controls, including those for testing, evaluation, validation, and verification (TEVV) and cybersecurity. However, AI systems are developed and operated in ways that differ from more traditional software systems. As such, many of the assumptions that underpin existing governance and risk management controls need to be adjusted, extended, and re-interpreted to achieve their original governance and risk management intent. Furthermore, AI systems can introduce new risks, and heighten risks previously considered lower priority, and thus require new governance and risk management approaches. 

The NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF) establishes a foundation for AI risk management by contextualizing AI's impacts within widely accepted risk management principles. Based on the AI RMF, the TACIP helps CI organizations address their unique risks and requirements for AI trustworthiness in Information Technology (IT), Operational Technology (OT), and Industrial Control Systems (ICS) environments. 

The TACIP assists organizations in defining how trustworthy AI relates to operational considerations and the complex balance between factors such as safety, accuracy, optimization, and resource availability. For instance, when prioritizing safety, a hypothetical 75% accurate system with guaranteed safe failures may be preferable to another hypothetical 95% accurate system that has unknown failures. The profile helps organizations locate and contextualize relevant, actionable guidance and resources to understand and manage AI risks within the enterprise's overall risk management. By improving transparency into the trade-offs between leveraging the benefits of different AI systems and allocating resources to manage AI risk, this profile can help enhance the efficiency and competitiveness of critical infrastructure organizations and the vendors of AI systems that serve them. 

| Chemical | Commercial Facilities | Communications | Critical Manufacturing |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| Dams | Defense Industrial Base | Emergency Services | Energy |
| Financial Services | Food and Agriculture | Transportation Systems | Information Technology |
| Healthcare and Public Health | Nuclear Reactors, Materials, and Waste | Government Services and Facilities | Water and Wastewater Systems |

Table 1: The 16 Critical Infrastructure Sectors defined in National Security Memorandum 22 (NSM-22). 

This profile assists CI organizations, including operators, managers, owners, suppliers, and government agencies in effectively navigating the balance between the benefits and risks of AI, throughout the AI lifecycle. The introduction of AI into CI creates a unique risk landscape in which anomalies and unforeseen interactions can cascade, leading to potentially significant consequences for large populations. CI systems are required to have high levels of availability, safety, transparency, security, and reliability, in addition to accuracy and efficiency. They may be expected to have long operational lifespans, operate in changing conditions without needing intervention, and integrate with legacy systems. This profile highlights practices and resources that assist CI organizations in understanding how the convergence of different forms of AI into CI systems affects their ability to meet these requirements and controls for managing the associated risks. 

The AI RMF uses the term trustworthiness to refer to a set of abstract characteristics, such as being safe, secure, or fair, that an AI system may need to have, or that may be desirable, in a given context of use. This profile translates these abstract characteristics into actionable risk management controls for the selection, development, deployment, operation, maintenance, and decommissioning of AI systems in CI. 

1. ## Audience

This profile is written for audiences and organizations across a wide range of AI and CI experience and expertise, AI maturity, and available resources for risk management. 

The guidance in this document deliberately spans a spectrum, from low-cost, high impact initial precautions through to more advanced and comprehensive measures. Organizations with limited expertise and resources will find actionable guidance on basic measures that they can take immediately and that can provide measurable improvements in their risk management. Organizations with greater expertise and resources will find more advanced guidance to further improve their management of AI risk. See Section 1.4 for ways to use this document. 

The primary audience for this profile is CI system acquirers (including procurement, contract, and legal officials), operators, managers, and owners, who wish to adopt and risk-manage AI systems and communicate their requirements and challenges to vendors of CI and AI systems. As such, it is organized according to suggested practices, drawn from use cases that directly speak to governance challenges in CI applications and environments.

\> **To the Reviewer:** We welcome suggestions on how to best set expectations for what the reader needs to already know to understand the different parts of this document. The Practices, in particular, need to be properly understood by non-AI-focused executive leadership, who will have different understandings of basic terms (e.g., “AI,” “bias,” or “model”). We have found enough variation that whatever definition we use, there will be some readers who will (unknowingly) have a different understanding that can cause problematic misinterpretation. We do/will define these in the glossary/crosswalk (where we also solicit suggestions\!) but, of course, a reader who thinks they know what a term means may not think to look there. We’d love suggestions on how we might address this issue while keeping this document manageable and within scope. 

Practices are written for strategic or executive-level managers, Tasks for technical managers, and Implementations for personnel who carry out the task directly. This layout enables strategic leaders to read top-down when delegating work, or technical teams, including engineering staff, policy writers, and procurement leads, to read bottom-up to locate guidance for specific AI use cases, lifecycle stages, or identified risks. Organizations superimpose their own top-level enterprise risk governance onto these concrete tasks, dynamically establishing which controls must be implemented immediately versus which can be phased in across the asset lifecycle and allocate limited resources within their unique risk profile.   

Secondary audiences include developers looking to integrate AI capabilities into CI environments. By breaking down tasks and implementations and providing references, this profile assists them in translating CI requirements into technically meaningful controls.  Throughout this profile, "organizations" and "stakeholders" refer collectively to acquirers, operators, managers, owners, developers, and vendors (including those who may not be exclusively serving CI), unless a specific role is called out. 

For systems engineers, enterprise architects, and compliance officers seeking to map these operational practices into formal system lifecycle and AI management frameworks (such as ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288, NIST SP 800-160, or ISO/IEC 42001), crosswalks are provided in [Appendix D: Crosswalks](#bookmark=id.aw7vvy5odm75). 

\> **To the Reviewer:** The crosswalks are still a work-in-progress. We’re open to suggestions for other high-priority crosswalks that we should call out here\! 

2. ## Scope

This profile applies to all AI systems used in CI environments and high-consequence applications, spanning the full range from general-purpose productivity or analytics tools adopted without CI-specific vetting to AI systems deliberately developed or adapted for CI and mission-critical tasks. This includes cases where the AI system's vendor no longer exists or may be unaware that their product has entered a CI environment, shifting the responsibility for CI-specific adaptation and risk management downstream in the AI system supply chain. 

General-purpose AI assistants used to generate configuration files, automation code, or operational procedures, or to summarize OT-actionable documentation and communications, fall within this scope even when they have no direct connection to an OT or ICS control loop. Because these tools are frequently procured and classified under standard IT or SaaS contracts, the resulting path from assistant output, to engineer review, to deployed configuration or action can be overlooked by hazard review and risk classification processes that were designed around direct OT connections.

This profile also encompasses stand-alone AI tools, agentic systems capable of autonomous action, and 'embedded' AI features integrated into larger software or hardware systems, where the presence, and nature of the AI system may be hidden from the operator, whether based on machine learning, other AI approaches such as symbolic logic and reasoning, or hybrid approaches.

Traditional IT, OT, and ICS systems are generally designed to produce consistent outcomes through a documented, engineering process. While the environment and hardware are understood to have a random component, control programs are generally deterministic (produce the same outputs for the same inputs). Indeed, much of OT and ICS revolves around making systems overall deterministic and compensating for randomness caused by the environment, wear and tear, and so on. In contrast, AI system outcomes can vary in unpredictable ways, due to both the inherent non-deterministic (random) behavior of many AI systems, as well as the ability to respond to a much wider variety of signals in a more complex manner, particularly as AI systems are deployed in contexts that are different to those in which they are trained. 

Existing assurance regimes, including system authorization and the continuous monitoring that many of them already incorporate, can remain the foundation for managing AI systems. AI introduces additional factors that can invalidate the assessment. An AI system's behavior can shift after it is authorized, such as through model updates, drift, changes in its inputs, and seemingly unrelated changes to the operating environment, without any change the organization itself made, and outside the bounds of traditional change management. This profile therefore builds on those regimes, with particular attention to what should trigger reassessment and to monitoring what an AI system does, not only how it is configured.

Introducing AI systems into critical environments alters the total risk profile, meaning that organizations should ensure performance or efficiency gains do not introduce unmitigated brittleness or run the risk of degrading essential operational skillsets over long system lifecycles. While holistic AI trust extends well beyond cybersecurity, operational resilience and cyber defense remain deeply intertwined. To avoid duplicating dedicated cybersecurity guidance, this profile defines high-level operator expectations across shared domains, such as identity, supply chain, logging, and incident response. Control-level specifics are deferred to established and upcoming cybersecurity guidance, AI-specific and otherwise. Rather than specifying control-level exploit mitigations, the practices emphasize bounding downstream physical and operational consequences.

In doing so, this profile applies the principle behind zero trust architecture, that trust is not granted implicitly on the basis of location or prior authorization but verified at each boundary, to AI risk more generally. An AI system's outputs, actions and inputs warrant verification where they cross into other systems, physical processes and human decisions, whether a departure from expected behavior arises from compromise, malfunction or a change in operating conditions.

In machine-speed environments, the practices in this document can provide vital layers of resilience against operational disruption, regardless of whether the underlying cause is adversarial or otherwise. Examples of such AI systems include, but are not limited to: 

* Chatbots, general-purpose AI systems, and generative AI systems based on Large Language Models (LLMs) for internal, inter-organizational, and public use. 

* Automated decision support and governance systems used for tasks such as summarizing regulatory compliance requirements and automating routine administrative approvals. 

* AI-enabled decision support and advisory tools that generate recommendations, prioritizations, triage rankings, or root-cause hypotheses from structured operational, sensor, or tabular data.

* Embodied intelligent systems such as robots, edge devices with data processing and autonomous decision making capabilities, and other cyberphysical systems. 

* Strategic simulations and digital twins that use AI-driven models for long-term resource forecasting, optimization, "what-if" scenario planning, and predicting the cascading impacts.

* Dynamic resource and logistics management tools that optimize the real-time distribution, routing, and load distribution of essential resources.

* Intelligent surveillance and monitoring systems that use a wide variety of different information sources to detect unauthorized access, environmental hazards, faults, and anomalous behavior.

* Operator advisory and situational awareness systems designed to prioritize notifications and alarms, and provide human operators with the context needed for high-impact interventions.

* High-consequence enterprise applications, such as automated claims clearinghouses, credit and liquidity risk algorithms, prior-authorization workflows, and automated compliance reporting tools.

* Safety-critical decision support tools, including clinical diagnostic algorithms, emergency service dispatch triage, disaster relief logistics, and public safety threat prioritization systems.

* Critical digital substrates and software pipelines, including AI-assisted code generation, infrastructure-as-code (IaC) synthesizers, automated CI/CD deployment pipelines, and configuration management tools.

* AI agents and agentic systems, machine vision and perception systems, AI systems that include machine learned models, including but not limited to neural networks, and AI systems that include, but are not limited to, symbolic reasoning, planning, logic programming, and mathematical modeling. 

This profile aligns with, contextualizes, references, interprets, adapts, and facilitates the operationalization of guidance documents at the intersection of AI, IT, OT, ICS, software development, operations, cybersecurity, and critical infrastructure. The profile provides guidance for applying the four AI RMF Core functions of Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage, that is relevant to all critical infrastructure sectors and high-consequence applications. Examples include: 

* Unifying definitions for key terms and concepts at the intersection of AI, critical infrastructure, and related domains to facilitate efficient, effective cross-sector co-operation and interoperability. 

* Guiding requirements analysis to tailor the risk management of AI systems to the performance and reliability expectations and operational realities of critical infrastructure, including legacy systems, physically distributed assets, and resourcing. 

* Addressing stringent requirements in the critical infrastructure sector, including the need for deterministic behavior, explainability, graceful degradation, and fail-safe operation. 

* Emphasizing the heightened need for adversarial robustness in all lifecycle stages of AI in critical infrastructure. 

* Supporting the critical infrastructure needs for rigorous testing, evaluation, validation, and verification (TEVV) of systems, including those that include AI. 

* Illuminating critical infrastructure-specific capabilities and trade-offs with competing and complementary AI techniques, technologies, and approaches. 

* Promoting visibility and collaboration across the supply chain of AI to address the unique needs, challenges, and risks of AI in critical infrastructure. 

* Highlighting practical, actionable, and measurable steps that can be taken by organizations at any level of AI expertise and risk management maturity. 

While this profile natively supports risk management across all lifecycle stages, practices are structured around operational workflows (such as procurement, incident response, and decommissioning) to maximize utility for infrastructure operators, rather than development phases.

This document is not intended to be exhaustive or duplicative. Rather, it focuses on highlighting a variety of practices with high impact and/or general applicability across CI sectors. Where possible, it serves as a translator, crosswalk, and index to existing relevant guidance, frameworks, and other resources. These practices have been chosen as starting points for CI organizations to make informed benefit-risk determinations for integrating AI into CI operations. 

The practices guide organizations toward tasks and example implementations that can be tailored to their unique needs, risk profiles, expertise, and available resources. They are subdivided into tasks and implementations that highlight a wide spectrum of options, offering accessible entry points that can result in impactful risk reduction for resource-constrained environments alongside more advanced strategies. Accordingly, organizations may apply this profile at different levels of rigor, recognizing that what constitutes an accessible or "foundational" step for one entity may require significant operational balancing for another. The overarching goal is to ensure this profile provides practical utility for everyone, allowing stakeholders to choose from a flexible menu of tasks that best match their unique operational realities, asset lifecycles, and risk tolerances. 

3. ## Trustworthiness

This profile defines trust as “a belief that an entity meets certain expectations and therefore, can be relied upon.”[^1] It is vital that this trust, across the wide variety of CI organizations, is appropriately calibrated to the underlying trustworthiness of the system. Both over- and under-trust can result in adverse impacts and opportunity costs. Trustworthiness is defined according to several characteristics that are considered together. For example, a system might be highly effective and secure, but may be fragile and sensitive to changes in its environment. While adversarial compromise of such a system may be unlikely, it still cannot be trusted to function as intended if the environment changes. 

To preserve consistency with the foundational NIST AI RMF, this profile builds on and recontextualizes its trustworthiness characteristics. While these terms may differ from standard process safety or industrial control lexicons, retaining this shared baseline provides a practical, common framework for cross-sector communication. This profile deliberately re-contextualizes, re-prioritizes, and re-groups these baseline characteristics into an operational set specifically aligned to the needs of CI and safety-critical applications, as shown in [Table 2](#bookmark=id.j664aovubcbh). The concept of trustworthiness in this context is further expanded upon in [Appendix A: Trustworthiness Characteristics](#bookmark=id.v0ka56ywsdml). By highlighting these specific characteristics and terms, this profile helps organizations to evaluate the suitability of these systems relative to other AI and non-AI systems, including the systems they may already be using. 

This profile facilitates improved communication of trustworthiness requirements and capabilities between actors in the supply chain of AI systems. For example, the Reliable, Robust and Resilient characteristics correspond to the terms of a typical service-level agreement (SLA): the performance it commits to and how consistently it is delivered, the conditions under which that commitment holds, and the degraded service and restoration expected when it cannot be met. For AI systems, performance should include correctness, which is hard to measure, not least because a system can deliver 99.999% availability while giving convincing but wrong answers.

| Characteristic | Illustrative Considerations for Critical Infrastructure |
| ----- | ----- |
| ***Does it do the correct thing?***  |  |
| **Valid**  *(the right system for this use, established for this deployment)* | An AI system valid in one deployment (a particular system, place, and time) can be wrong in another: a different plant, patient population, sensor mix, or organizational convention. Validity can change over time, through seasonal variation and changes in equipment, terminology, or regulation. Defining validity fixes what counts as expected, a scope that is specified rather than discovered and that determines whether a given departure is a question of reliability, robustness, or resilience. |
| **Accurate**  *(how well outputs agree with a known or accepted reference)* | Meaningful AI system accuracy is rarely captured by one number: a missed safety trip and a spurious shutdown are not equivalent, and neither are a summary that drops a safety caveat and one that adds an irrelevant detail. Complexity makes the reference hard to choose; a system tuned to whichever was easiest to obtain can improve on the measure while getting worse at the task. |
| **Bias Managed**  *(systematic error identified and deliberately handled)* | Every system has errors that affect performance, each with a direction and a cause; these should be removed, compensated for, or bounded. They enter AI systems via datasets, encoded background and domain knowledge, assumptions needed for learning but seldom stated, and how humans use outputs. Errors that fall unevenly on those affected are covered in Fair.  |
| ***Does it keep doing the correct thing, particularly as conditions change?***  |  |
| **Reliable**  *(performs as required over time, in expected conditions and use)* | A mean time between failures or SLA figure for AI systems may be misleading when the scope of relevant performance data and failures is poorly defined, because wrong outputs may be hard to detect. Updates that can invalidate prior statistics may not look like updates: a vendor swapping a model, or the system keying on a dimension that varies without being operationally relevant. |
| **Robust**  *(keeps performing and limits harm when conditions or use go beyond expectations)* | Conditions and use vary: weather, load, degraded sensors, unfamiliar formats, or a summarization tool used to draft a procedure. Every variable can sit within its stated limits while the combination is one nobody expected. For AI systems, the space is often too large to characterize unless testing is designed and interpreted based on how the AI system generalizes. |
| **Resilient**  *(degrades gracefully when it must and maintains the ability to resume operation)* | For many AI systems, behavior is characterized by observation rather than first principles. The boundaries of expected conditions and use, and where the fallback is needed, may not be obvious. Resilience has to be considered and maintained deliberately, and fallback plans made with appropriate consideration of the way the AI system degrades.  |
| ***Does it avoid unintentional or deliberate harm?***  |  |
| **Safe**  *(avoid harm to people, property or environment, in defined conditions)* | Every system is blind to hazards its design and development left out, and AI systems often cover enough situations that the gaps are hard to find, especially if they include learning without human direction. Harm does not require it to act either: a harmful recommendation carried out by a person also reaches the process, so risk management should follow the influence of AI system outputs.  |
| **Secure**  *(withstands attack)* | Built for generality, AI systems often take high-dimensional inputs such as images, sensor records, documents and free text, which cannot be meaningfully sanitized. An adversary who shapes them changes what the system does without breaching anything, and probing may reveal capability nobody intended to deploy, or boundaries the operator never knew were there. |
| ***Does it allow its behavior to be observed, understood, and accounted for?*** |  |
| **Transparent**  *(the information exists and can be obtained)* | Critical infrastructure may have formal root-cause analysis obligations. An AI system may act on a document, a retrieval, a model version, internal state, or external agentic interactions that no log captures, and what mattered is known only afterward. Capture has to be designed, not inherited. |
| **Explainable & Interpretable**  *(what the output means, and how it was reached)* | AI systems can consider more variables, in more complex ways than a person can understand given available time, attention, training, and cognitive capacity. Explanations and interpretability can help people understand its behavior and act appropriately, be it in the moment or in an investigation. |
| **Accountable** *(responsibility is named and can be enforced)* | The scale and variety of inputs that influence an AI system throughout its lifecycle can impair the ability to meaningfully assign responsibility for the system's behavior, even where that behavior can be explained. Responsibility therefore has to be allocated in advance, not deduced after the event. |
| ***Does it meet its obligations to those who depend on it?*** |  |
| **Privacy-Enhanced**  *(sensitive data stays contained)* | AI systems can identify individuals, or previously private information about them, from records that revealed nothing before. In critical infrastructure the same applies to operations, where telemetry can expose staffing or production. What reaches a model or a vendor's log cannot be recalled. |
| **Fair**  *(service does not degrade for some of those it affects)* | AI-mediated allocation, detection, prioritization or recommendation can perform unevenly across equipment, conditions, locations, populations, or customers, potentially violating service obligations that an operator may have. This profile helps identify and measure that variation; what level is acceptable is a determination for the operator in its regulatory and operational context. |

Table 2: Trustworthy AI Characteristics as re-contextualized for the priorities of Critical Infrastructure, along with examples of illustrative considerations. [Appendix A: Trustworthiness Characteristics](#bookmark=id.v0ka56ywsdml) provides definitions of the trustworthiness characteristics in full along with distinctions and tensions between them. 

4. ## Using this Profile

This document is not meant to be read from start to finish. It is structured so that readers can start wherever their interest or responsibility lies and follow the cross-references from there. Each Practice, Task and Implementation carries an index number, and the cross-references between items use those numbers. The labelled fields in each cell state what an item covers, what it does not, and where the rest is addressed, so that a reader who arrives at any item can find its neighbors.

Readers may find the following paths useful as starting points. Executive leadership may begin with [Appendix B: Historic Incident-to-Practice Mapping](#bookmark=id.s3moontz7wd), which connects a selection of real-world incidents to one or more Practices, then read the corresponding Practice descriptions to establish governance ownership and delegate Task-level implementation. Technical managers and practitioners may begin from a specific AI system, lifecycle stage or risk, using the Search Keywords to locate the relevant Practice or Task, then working down to its Implementations and following the cross-references to related items. Those concerned with a particular trustworthiness characteristic may begin with [Table 2](#bookmark=id.j664aovubcbh) and search the table for the items that contribute to it, work against it, or balance it against another characteristic. Those mapping this profile to existing frameworks may begin with the AI RMF subcategory fields and [Appendix D: Crosswalks](#bookmark=id.aw7vvy5odm75). Organizations with limited resources may begin with the AI systems whose outputs most directly reach physical processes, safety functions, or essential services, since the effort that several Tasks call for scales with consequence (for example, Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b), Task [2.2: Establish requirements for redundancy in AI-Driven functions](#bookmark=kix.rbl0o5b5wwkj) and Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)).

We anticipate that many readers will load the Markdown version of this document into an AI assistant, and use it to understand how the guidance applies to their situation and to help them act on it. The index numbers, cross-references and labelled fields described above are intended to support this use as well. Because this profile is deliberately sector-agnostic, how useful an assistant's answers are depends largely on the organizational context it is given alongside it: the sector, the AI system and what it is connected to, the regulatory and contractual obligations that apply, existing policies and risk management processes, and the resources available. How that context is provided will vary. It may be described in the conversation, supplied as documents where organizational policy permits, or drawn out by asking the assistant to question the user about their situation before making recommendations. Some of this context may be sensitive, so consider what the assistant is permitted to receive and where that information will go. Suggested ways to work with the document through an assistant include: 

* Describing your AI system, sector and role, and asking which Practices and Tasks apply.  
* Asking what a given Task would involve for your organization, and which References to consult.  
* Asking which items address a specific risk.  
* Describing an incident, whether your own or one reported elsewhere, and asking which items might have prevented or limited it, as input to a lessons-learned or after-action review.  
* Asking which trustworthiness characteristics may be in tension for your use case, and where this profile discusses them.  
* Where the assistant can search, asking it to find current or related references using the descriptions this profile gives for each citation.

An assistant can make this document easier to use, but it can also misrepresent it. Some tools truncate long documents or summarize tables without saying so, so ask the assistant to work from the full text, and check that it has seen all of the Practices and appendices. An assistant may also present a Practice or Task as a requirement, or fill gaps with guidance that is not in this document. Ask it to cite the index numbers it is drawing on, and check important points against the text itself. This profile is guidance to inform professional judgment. Neither this document nor an assistant's reading of it is a substitute for that judgment, or for the regulatory and contractual obligations that apply to your organization. The assistant you use may itself be an AI system within the scope of this profile, whose risks should be managed accordingly, particularly if its outputs will inform configurations, procedures or operational decisions.

\> To the Reviewer: We welcome suggestions and reports of other ways of using this document that you have found useful, and in particular, prompts that you have found work well. We are also in the process of developing additional resources, such as accompanying metadata, that can further help AI assistants navigate this document.  

2. # The Trustworthy AI in Critical Infrastructure Profile

The Trustworthy AI in Critical Infrastructure Profile is organized to help critical infrastructure stakeholders translate trustworthy AI objectives into actionable risk management activities. It does so through a structured set of practices, tasks, and illustrative implementations grounded in critical infrastructure use cases, case studies, and operational scenarios expanded upon in [Appendix B: Historic Incident-to-Practice Mapping](#bookmark=id.s3moontz7wd) and [Appendix C: Use Cases and Operational Scenarios](#bookmark=id.lq3btar7o53g), and informed by operational realities and existing risk management resources.

Each practice presents an application-relevant description of the risks that it addresses along with examples of tasks, implementations, and references that facilitate the translation of these high level practices into actionable controls for a given CI environment. These are designed to be generally relatable across infrastructure sectors and include context to help organizations navigate the inherent trade-offs between different risk management considerations. 

* **Practice:** A high-level, governance-focused description of a risk management objective aimed at a management and governance audience, specifically CI Asset Owners, C-Suite Executives, and Strategic Managers. Each practice includes a unique identifier and a clear incentive for adoption, emphasizing organizational mission and resilience rather than purely technical goals. Practices are designed to be conceptually distinct and ready for delegation to appropriate internal departments. 

* **Tasks:** Discrete, assignable steps necessary to achieve the goals of a practice, tailored for a technical management audience, specifically Technical Managers, Compliance Officers, and Operations Leads. These tasks identify specific actions that can be executed independently and provide a clear bridge between governance policy and operational implementation. Tasks often draw from across the AI RMF Functions, Categories, and Subcategories to emphasize a holistic approach to managing risk.

* **Implementations:** Illustrative examples of tools, processes, or methods used to fulfill a task, serving as a guide for Engineering Personnel, Field Operators, Systems Integrators, AI Vendors, and Governance/Compliance Personnel. These suggestions provide a flexible spectrum of options designed to offer immediate value to organizations of all resource levels, while also outlining more advanced strategies.

Each cell in the table also carries up to four labelled fields. These serve the three audiences above differently, and a reader may use only some of them.

* **Search Keywords:** Terms that should lead a reader to this item, including words other sectors use for the same thing, and suggestions for terms that the reader might search for in other resources for more information. Search and navigation aids rather than a taxonomy, and not exhaustive.

* **Trustworthiness:** Up to three headings; a heading appears only where there is something to put under it. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions** lists characteristics from [Table 2](#bookmark=id.j664aovubcbh) that this item may contribute to, and the limits of those contributions. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider** lists characteristics that this item may work against as a side effect, and points to where that is addressed. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance** lists characteristics that may pull against each other within this item, including a characteristic against itself, together with the conditions that bear on the choice between them.

* **Related AI RMF subcategories:** The subcategories of the NIST AI RMF (1.0) that this item relates to. Listing one means this item can contribute toward that outcome. It does not mean that following this item satisfies the subcategory: AI RMF subcategories are organization-wide outcomes, and any single item here addresses part of one at most. The lists are not exhaustive. 

* **References:** Pointers to material that may help in implementing this item. Each citation in a cell names the part that applies and why it is relevant there. These descriptions are also intended to help a reader, or an AI assistant, locate the current version or successor of a reference, or related material, when the cited document has been revised or superseded. Each description opens with a label denoting the kind of document: **Regulation**, **Standard**, **Guidance**, **Research**, **Commentary**, **Incident Report**, or **Working Document**, followed by information about the publisher and the reference itself. **Working Document** marks a source that presents itself as unfinished, such as a draft or a concept paper; it is cited because the work is relevant and under way, not because it is settled. The labels describe the document, not its worth: a seminal research paper may be as relevant as a standard, and the description says why each source is cited. References are neither requirements nor endorsements, and are not exhaustive.

\>To the Reviewer: 

\>The references in Practices 1 to 6 and 13 have been rebuilt on the scheme described above. Practices 7 to 12 have not yet had that pass. We would welcome comment on the scheme as much as on the individual references.

\>Where a field is incomplete, a marker says why. “Candidates \- suggestions welcome” means we have some and would welcome more. “None yet \- suggestions welcome” means we intend content here and have none. 

\>The trustworthiness and AI RMF subcategory fields are being rebuilt on the descriptions above, and this draft shows that work partway. Practices 1 to 6 and 13 carry both fields in the new form while Practices 7 to 12 are in progress. A cell whose field still reads Trustworthiness Characteristics is awaiting the three headings described above rather than carrying a different field. We would welcome comment on Practices 1 to 6 and 13 as examples of the approach, as much as on any individual entry.

The 13 core practices are summarized below. Many of the practices detailed herein are not novel inventions, but rather time-tested principles drawn from classic systems security engineering (such as NIST SP 800-160), process safety, and operational resilience. As the ease of fielding AI capabilities outpaces traditional engineering workflows, this profile highlights these foundational disciplines, adapting established practices to AI systems.

[**Practice 1: Establish requirements for success**](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k)

Define performance, safety, and security baselines to help ensure that AI systems, if adopted, deliver measurable operational value aligned with organizational mission goals. 

[**Practice 2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations**](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41)

Establish multi-layered safeguards to maintain essential service delivery and functional safety when AI performance deviates or degrades.  

[**Practice 3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior**](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p)

Set deterministic boundary logic, risk thresholds, and independent safety wrappers for high-risk autonomous AI actions.  

[**Practice 4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness**](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf)

Maintain "break-glass" intervention procedures, out-of-band communication channels, and real-time situational awareness displays so human operators can safely override AI actions.  

[**Practice 5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools**](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck)

Implement distinct non-human identities (NHIs), cryptographically verifiable authentication, and least-privilege permissions to bound an AI agent's operational scope and blast radius.  

[**Practice 6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management**](#bookmark=kix.gk6f3b3rnwyn)

Apply pre-acquisition procurement oversight and supply-chain transparency (using AIBOMs and hazard reachability disclosures) to evaluate external vendor dependencies.  

[**Practice 7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance**](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m)

Maintain post-acquisition operational governance over internal AI deployments, dataset provenance, prompt versioning, and safety wrapper configurations.  

[**Practice 8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures**](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb) 

Formulate forensic response protocols, triage pathways, and root-cause analysis capabilities tailored to probabilistic and non-deterministic AI system failures.  

[**Practice 9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training**](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) 

Deliver role-specific training to combat automation complacency, build accurate mental models of AI limitations, and preserve critical manual control skills.  

[**Practice 10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities**](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) 

Capture decision-level runtime telemetry, prompt history, and guardrail interactions in immutable, tamper-evident logs for forensic auditability.  

[**Practice 11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning**](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) 

Pre-allocate backup operational resources and document "manual mode" fallback playbooks to ensure essential service delivery during extended AI system outages.  

[**Practice 12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle**](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul)

Subject machine-authored code, configurations, and procedures to multi-layered deterministic testing and subject matter expert reviews prior to operational execution and across their lifecycle. 

[**Practice 13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority**](#bookmark=id.ieoypls7vre2)

Withdraw an AI system's authority completely at end of life, preserve the knowledge and evidence that would otherwise be destroyed with it, and record what happens to everything that depended on it. 

**\> To the Reviewer:**   
\> The following text describes the intended complete state of the document. Many of its aspects are currently incomplete (e.g., not all tasks have complete mappings and the glossary is still a work-in-progress). 

The Table of Practices, Tasks, and Implementations that follows is space-constrained and not intended to be exhaustive, nor is it intended to be a checklist. Rather, it is intended to inform active professional discretion, illustrating specific examples of controls that are generally achievable and impactful in managing risk, and how to contextualize them in a critical infrastructure setting. Organizations should consider related additional risk management controls that may apply to their situation. Much more information on each practice can be found in the references and associated guidance and commentary. Each task includes mappings to AI RMF sub-categories. Organizations may cross-reference these with other AI risk management resources, including other AI RMF profiles, for additional guidance. 

The order of the practices, tasks, and notional implementation examples in the table does not imply any ordering, be it importance, sequence, or otherwise. Prioritization is specific to the implementing sector or organization, and should be based on factors that include their regulatory and other obligations, operating environment, localized risk tolerances, legacy dependencies, and available resources. [Appendix B: Historic Incident-to-Practice Mapping](#bookmark=id.s3moontz7wd) may help to inform this determination.

Many of the terms used in this table are defined in [Glossary and Terminology Crosswalk](#bookmark=id.htdfpisgfl1c), particularly where they have different definitions across sectors and/or between the AI and cybersecurity communities. In many cases, terms are context-specific and may have slightly different meanings between sectors. 

Unless this profile defines a term, this profile uses it in its general sense. Where a sector, regulation or standard gives a term a precise meaning, such as validation, verification, incident or safety function, this profile does not invoke that meaning, and following this profile does not by itself establish conformance with a requirement stated in those terms. 

**\> To the Reviewer:**   
\> We welcome suggestions for terms that should be in the glossary\! We particularly welcome suggestions that might be confused between sectors, or that have specific compliance/regulatory meanings that we should clarify. 

Organizations adopting this profile should ensure that terms are appropriately defined in the context of their own environments and use cases and crosswalked to similar terms and concepts used by organizations and supply chain entities with whom they interact. 

# Table of Practices, Tasks, and Implementations

**\>To the Reviewer:** 

\>We welcome suggestions for additional practices, tasks, implementations, and references from within and beyond your sector. This profile prioritizes content and examples that generalize and offer broad, transferable value across all 16 critical infrastructure sectors, even if they may be focused on one sector or application. Highly specialized or unique content is still welcome and valuable for potential future sector-specific companion resources, developed by, or in partnership with, sector organizations. 

\>This profile addresses AI risk from an operational and consequence-containment perspective, intentionally deconflicting with and referencing cybersecurity-focused control-level guidance. Security-adjacent content still appears where it best illustrates an AI-specific failure mode, or where a control area sits at the boundary between operational and cybersecurity risk. Our intent is to address the operational and CI-specific context and to point to cybersecurity-specific resources that will cover it in depth. We welcome suggestions on how to bridge this profile with such resources. 

\> We welcome suggested additions to “Search Keywords” that would help readers scanning for concepts relevant to their sector or role find their way to the right Practice and Task. 

\> References currently appear at all three levels: some at the Practice level, intended for a governance/executive audience already familiar with high-level policy, and covering multiple Tasks and Implementations at once rather than repeating them; others at the Task and Implementation levels, aimed at a more technical audience implementing that specific control. Adding operational context to these references is ongoing work. We welcome thoughts on how to make these references and associated contextualizations more useful for these specific audiences. 

| Practice | Task | Implementation |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| **1: Establish requirements for success** Define performance, safety, and security benchmarks across the entire end-to-end system, including hardware, control logic, data sources, human operators, as well as AI components. These establish the baseline for existing and proposed systems to ensure AI-driven systems align with the organization’s mission and operational resilience goals while achieving desired improvements.  This helps ensure that where AI is adopted, it delivers measurable operational value rather than purely technological optimization. These requirements provide a verifiable framework for system acceptance and ongoing oversight, supporting clear accountability and audit-readiness throughout the asset lifecycle. Because these requirements are defined independently of any particular solution, a valid outcome of this Practice is the determination that AI is not the appropriate means of meeting them here,  whether because a non-AI approach serves them better, or because a local constraint prevents an otherwise suitable AI system from meeting them in this specific deployment. This practice focuses on helping to ensure that the adoption of AI provides measurable improvements. It is related to Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41), which helps ensure that any trade-offs still result in acceptable overall levels of robustness, resilience, and quality of service.  \> **To the Reviewer:**  \> Specific, standardized risk management scoring metrics are considered too domain- and sector-specific for this document \- instead, we encourage sector-specific organizations to develop such metrics. Having said that, what additional resources would you like to see, be it in this document or possible companion resources, that might facilitate organizations developing such metrics?   **Search Keywords:**   operational baseline, success criteria, system acceptance, factory acceptance test (FAT), site acceptance test (SAT), operating envelope, cyber-physical consequence tiering, TEVV scope, performance benchmark, key performance indicator (KPI), legacy process baselining, edge-case stress testing, domain-specific terminology, business case for AI, non-AI alternative evaluation, audit-readiness, requirements matrix  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Valid:** Contributes the statement of what success means for this deployment, written before a solution is chosen, so that whether an AI system is the right means here is decided on evidence rather than assumed. It does not hold that judgment in place once performance later deviates from those requirements. For that, see Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41). **Accurate:** Contributes the reference that performance is judged against, set across the end-to-end system rather than the AI component alone, with minimum and preferred levels stated in advance. It does not decide how much of a given kind of error the organization is willing to carry. For that, see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d). **Accountable:** Contributes acceptance criteria fixed in advance, together with evidence that they were met, so that a decision to proceed rests on a documented bar and can be reviewed against it later. It does not capture what the system did once in service. For that, see Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl). **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Secure:** The requirements this Practice produces describe where the system's limits sit, which conditions it is expected to meet, and what exceeding them would cost. Handling and distribution of that documentation is a matter for the organization's existing information classification practice. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Valid versus Robust:** Where a deployment's conditions are stable and well understood, drawing the requirements tightly makes both the bar and the evidence clear. Where conditions vary widely, a tightly drawn scope makes acceptance easier while leaving behavior outside that scope uncharacterized, and the organization carries whatever falls there. One resolution is to require behavior beyond the stated scope to be characterized separately before acceptance. Others include widening the scope and funding the evidence it then needs, or declining the deployment where neither is affordable.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 1.4** (on defining AI business value):This Practice requires that AI adoption be justified by measurable operational value rather than purely technological optimization, and that the value be stated in terms that the organization can check later; it does not determine whether the trade-offs made to obtain that value leave acceptable service levels. For that, see Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41). **GOVERN 1.2** (on defining trustworthy AI policies):This Practice turns trustworthiness characteristics into benchmarks that a specific system can be accepted or rejected against; it does not set the standing policies that bound AI behavior once the system is running. For those, see Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p). **MEASURE 1.1** (on selecting and prioritizing measurements):This Practice establishes performance, safety and security benchmarks across the end-to-end system, and treats what cannot be measured directly as requiring additional verification rather than assumption; it does not enumerate the risks those measurements should be prioritized against. For that, see Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93). **MANAGE 1.1** (on authorizing the system):This Practice supplies the acceptance criteria and audit-ready evidence on which a proceed decision rests; it does not identify who holds the authority to make that decision. For that, see Implementation [3.9.1: Establish Accountable Ownership](#bookmark=id.w3n0hyju2d4t).  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [Principles for the Secure Integration of Artificial Intelligence in Operational Technology](https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/joint-guidance-principles-for-the-secure-integration-of-artificial-intelligence-in-operational-technology-508c.pdf), December 2025, joint guidance from nine national cybersecurity agencies across seven countries, led by the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. Section 2.1, Consider the OT Business Case for AI Use, is the closest external counterpart to this Practice: it treats whether to adopt AI as a question to be answered rather than assumed. Sections 4.1 and 4.2 cover monitoring and oversight mechanisms, and safety and failsafe mechanisms. **Guidance** [Roles and Responsibilities Framework for Artificial Intelligence in Critical Infrastructure](https://www.dhs.gov/sites/default/files/2024-11/24_1114_dhs_ai-roles-and-responsibilities-framework-508.pdf), from the United States Department of Homeland Security. Part III, Critical Infrastructure Owners and Operators, sets out what that role is expected to carry, which is the perspective from which the requirements in this Practice are written. **Standard** [ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288:2023, Systems and software engineering: system life cycle processes](https://www.iso.org/standard/81702.html), published jointly by ISO, IEC and IEEE. Clause 6.4.2, the stakeholder needs and requirements definition process, is the general systems-engineering form of what this Practice asks for, and is useful where an organization already runs a lifecycle process that AI requirements should join rather than sit beside. **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-218A, Secure Software Development Practices for Generative AI and Dual-Use Foundation Models](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218A.pdf), from NIST. Tasks PO.1.1 and PO.1.2 cover identifying and documenting security requirements for AI software development, which is the security portion of the requirements this Practice establishes.   | **1.1: Identify current requirements** Catalog the functional, safety, and performance requirements for the operational processes the AI system is intended to support or augment.  This task helps ensure that AI-specific objectives are grounded in established mission-critical benchmarks and provides the authoritative framework for system acceptance and ongoing oversight.  It also helps ensure that visibility is maintained into the expected benefits to be gained relative to the trade-offs elsewhere, and highlights when those benefits might no longer be present.  Note that establishing operational success criteria requires evaluating the initial Risk Surface according to Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93) to ensure optimization gains do not introduce unmitigated exposure elsewhere. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Valid:** Contributes the specification of expected conditions and use: mission scope, operating envelopes, environmental parameters and functional dependencies across sensors, networks, control software, actuators and operators, recorded as a structured requirements matrix that remains auditable (Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm)). It does not test the delivered system against that specification. For that, see Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b). **Safe:** Contributes consequence tiering: requirements that reach physical machinery, environmental safety or human life are classified as such, which is what raises verification rigor and calls for deterministic boundary constraints (Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm)). It does not define those constraints or the states entered when one is exceeded. For those, see Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla) and Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p). **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Fair:** Requirements are elicited through internal functions such as engineering, operations, safety, compliance and legal (Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm)), so obligations toward the people a service reaches enter through the roles that already hold them. Because this Task is the authoritative list that later testing works from, a service obligation absent here is absent from acceptance. Task [1.4: Establish the AI System’s Domain Knowledge and Linguistic Alignment Requirements](#bookmark=kix.ddne831a11g5) carries part of this for systems that work in natural language, by making comprehension of community language variation a success criterion. \>TBD \- Whether evenness of service across the populations an operator serves belongs in the requirements matrix for AI systems that allocate, prioritize or triage rather than communicate. This entry is incomplete until that is settled. Suggestions welcome.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 1.1** (on defining operating context):This Task documents the operational processes, deployment settings, operating envelopes and environmental conditions that the AI system will meet, drawn from safety manuals, SOPs and SLAs that already exist; it does not derive the adverse and adversarial conditions that it catalogs. For those, see Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93). **MAP 1.6** (on defining and communicating user requirements):This Task elicits requirements from engineering, operations, safety, compliance and legal subject matter experts and records them as a structured requirements matrix that remains auditable later; it does not test the delivered system against them. For that, see Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b). **MAP 2.1** (on defining AI system task and methodology):This Task defines the operational tasks and mission scope that the AI system and the wider system are expected to perform, together with their dependencies on sensors, networks, control software, actuators and human operators; it does not select the model class or the method by which those tasks are implemented. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST AI 600-1, Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.AI.600-1), from NIST. Organizes suggested actions against the AI RMF subcategories for generative AI specifically, including dual-use foundation models, so an organization identifying requirements for a generative deployment can work from a set already organized by AI RMF subcategory rather than assembling one. | **1.1.1: Operational baseline review** Perform a baseline review of existing operational documentation, such as safety manuals, standard operating procedures (SOPs), and service level agreements (SLAs), for the targeted process. Distill these into a "Requirements Summary" that explicitly lists measurable success criteria (e.g., latency or throughput targets) along with minimum and ideal thresholds for performance. This implementation differs from Task [1.2: Create quantitative baselines for existing systems in both normal and challenging conditions](#bookmark=kix.yl9k2ljvy4q2) by focusing on establishing a baseline from which improvements can be measured.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping** Conduct cross-functional requirements analysis with SMEs, including those from engineering, operations, safety, compliance, and legal departments, to translate high-level mission goals into a structured requirements matrix for the AI system, and the broader operational system within which it operates.  Requirements should explicitly define: Operational Task and Mission Scope, including the tasks that the system (AI and overall) is expected to perform, functional dependencies across sensors, network backbones, control software, physical actuators, and human operators in the oversight loop.   Defined Operating Envelopes such as quantified input/output ranges (e.g., deterministic setpoint limits, clinical dosage thresholds, transaction execution ceilings), latency ceilings, and physical tolerance boundaries. Environmental Operating Conditions, including physical, digital, and operational boundary parameters (e.g., sensor noise thresholds, data schema variations, network latency ranges, equipment wear tolerances).  Adverse and Adversarial Conditions, including enumerating expected environmental stresses (e.g., weather events, process upsets, sensor degradation) and attack surfaces as mapped in Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93) (e.g., prompt injection, data poisoning, compromised telemetry) and corresponding explicit behavioral requirements, such as those outlined in Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p).  Cyber-Physical Consequence Tiering such as explicit classification of requirements that directly impact physical machinery, environmental safety, or human life (see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d)), triggering elevated verification rigor (see Implementation [12.1.1: Artifact Risk Classification](#bookmark=id.jzylj1u7qf60)) and deterministic boundary constraints (see Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla)).  Other non-functional requirements, such as specific auditability standards and data retention periods (see Task [10.1: Define decision-level logging and metadata retention policies](#bookmark=kix.7y5rv6pa7wxa)). **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.1.3: Establish triggers for re-validating requirements on change of operating context** Define the events that invalidate the Requirements Summary produced in Implementation [1.1.1: Operational baseline review](#bookmark=kix.rvq272p41vl0), and require re-validation when those events occur rather than only on a fixed review cycle.  For assets that relocate, or whose governing rule set is contract-dependent, the applicable standards, regulations, and customer-specific terms can change without any change to the AI system itself. Examples include a mobile or relocatable asset entering a different regulatory jurisdiction, where a different set of national, sectoral, or customer-specific requirements becomes applicable, a change in contract status such as going off-contract or being stacked, and the layering of customer-specific procedural requirements on top of a regional baseline.  Where the applicable rules are encoded in the AI system in its prompts, retrieval corpus, rule base, or guardrails treat the change as a required system update under Task [7.5: Conduct Impact Assessments and Risk Management for AI System and Model Updates](#bookmark=kix.up940xmlpl4i). Where the rules are not encoded, but the AI system relies on standard operating procedures that reflect them, the trigger should raise an explicit action to review and, where necessary, rewrite those procedures before operation continues. Without such triggers, an AI system may continue to perform correctly against a rule set that no longer applies to the asset, producing outputs that are internally consistent and externally non-compliant. |
|  | **1.2: Create quantitative baselines for existing systems in both normal and challenging conditions** Establish quantitative performance baselines for current legacy or manual processes to provide a comparative metric for evaluating AI system effectiveness.  These baselines capture performance under both normal and stressed operating conditions, enabling technical managers to identify specific risks and measure improvements associated with the adoption of AI. This task differs from Implementation [1.1.1: Operational baseline review](#bookmark=kix.rvq272p41vl0) by focusing on minimum performance under challenging conditions to ensure that performance gains do not result in increased brittleness.  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Valid:** Contributes the measured "before" that a claimed improvement is checked against (Implementation [1.2.1: Establish manual or legacy process performance and safety baselines](#bookmark=kix.6r10jvokh7y9)), which is what lets a conclusion that an AI system is not the right means here rest on evidence. It does not cover benefits for which the legacy process offers no counterpart, such as a capability that did not previously exist. **Robust:** Contributes the comparison point beyond expected conditions: the manual or legacy process measured under stress and edge cases (Implementation [1.2.2: Develop high-fidelity operational baselines including edge-case performance under stress](#bookmark=kix.ybapqigop99h)), so brittleness introduced by an AI system appears as a like-for-like difference rather than as a surprise in service. It does not characterize the AI system's own behavior in those conditions. For that, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accurate versus Robust:** Where only normal operation is baselined, a candidate that improves the average compares well. Where the stressed baseline is measured as well, a candidate that is sharper on average and weaker at the edges shows the trade, and the two comparisons can point to different decisions. One resolution is to set an acceptance bar on the stressed baseline as well as the normal one, so that an average gain does not by itself carry the decision. Others include accepting the trade with the brittleness recorded as a known limitation, or keeping the legacy path available for the conditions in which it performs better, with the resources for that allocated under Task [11.1: Identify and prioritize AI-dependent essential functions](#bookmark=kix.v616use07xyf). **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 3.1** (on understanding benefits):This Task measures how the manual or legacy process actually performs before anything is deployed, so a claimed benefit can be checked against a documented "before" rather than an estimate; it does not examine benefits for which the legacy process offers no counterpart, such as a capability that did not previously exist. **MAP 3.2** (on understanding costs):This Task baselines performance under stressed and edge-case conditions as well as normal ones, so that brittleness introduced by the AI system shows up as a like-for-like comparison rather than as a surprise in service; it does not relate the resulting costs to a level of risk the organization is willing to carry.
