For that, see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d). **MANAGE 2.1** (on allocating resources):This Task documents how the viable non-AI alternative performs, including under stress, so that keeping that alternative becomes a costed choice rather than an assumption; it does not allocate the resources needed to keep it available. For that, see Task [11.1: Identify and prioritize AI-dependent essential functions](#bookmark=kix.v616use07xyf).  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome | **1.2.1: Establish manual or legacy process performance and safety baselines** Before deploying an AI system, measure and document the current performance and safety metrics of the manual or legacy process it will support or replace, giving the organization a concrete "before" picture to compare against once the AI system is live. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.2.2: Develop high-fidelity operational baselines including edge-case performance under stress** Extend the baseline in Implementation [1.2.1: Establish manual or legacy process performance and safety baselines](#bookmark=kix.6r10jvokh7y9) to include performance under stressed or edge-case conditions, not just normal operation, so that any brittleness introduced by the AI system can be identified by comparing like-for-like stress performance rather than only normal-condition performance. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle** Develop and execute a systematic testing, evaluation, validation, and verification (TEVV) program that assesses AI performance against established requirements, as defined in Task [1.1: Identify current requirements](#bookmark=kix.uqvdk8m0gq1) and, in particular, Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm), at each stage of the asset lifecycle, and against previous baselines (if any).  This task serves as the anchor for TEVV considerations throughout this profile. It supports the generation of verifiable, audit-ready evidence, with re-evaluation as environments change handled under Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i).  Specialized applications of this framework appear throughout this profile: Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5) extends TEVV to anomaly and edge-case behavior, Implementation [4.1.3: Regularly practice and evaluate intervention processes](#bookmark=kix.h13vn9nyfxoi) applies TEVV to override and intervention procedures, Implementation [7.5.2: Perform automated “champion-challenger” and drift assessment](#bookmark=kix.a635i9rnvd1s) applies TEVV to champion-challenger drift assessment, and Task [11.4: Establish system integrity re-validation and re-commissioning protocols](#bookmark=kix.ux0xlmdtpo4p) applies TEVV to system re-commissioning.  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Valid:** Contributes the evidence that a delivered system meets the requirements and boundary envelopes set in Task [1.1: Identify current requirements](#bookmark=kix.uqvdk8m0gq1), with scope, environment and pass/fail criteria fixed per lifecycle stage before testing begins (Implementation [1.3.1: Define TEVV scope and requirements per AI system](#bookmark=kix.54aziklwcuxz)), and with the conditions beyond which that evidence does not hold recorded alongside it. It does not detect the loss of validity that appears only after the operating environment moves. For that, see Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i). **Safe:** Contributes the verification that a system exceeding its defined boundaries derates or transitions predictably, and the rule that a system whose evidence does not support autonomy is held in a more restrictive oversight mode, with the oversight capacity that mode needs confirmed as available rather than assumed (Implementation [1.3.1: Define TEVV scope and requirements per AI system](#bookmark=kix.54aziklwcuxz)). It does not specify the fallback states themselves. For those, see Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p). **Transparent:** Contributes the acceptance record: test plans, dataset provenance documentation, model cards and empirical results held as one package for regulatory audit, lifecycle review and post-incident evaluation (Implementation [1.3.4: Generate and maintain audit-ready TEVV evidence](#bookmark=kix.y0osxmcw67n4)). It does not capture what the system did in service. For that, see Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Valid versus Safe:** Where an application can sustain a planned outage, such as a report summarization tool or an off-peak batch process, testing during an authorized window yields evidence about the system that will actually run (Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj)). Where service delivery or a physical control loop cannot tolerate disruption, that evidence has to come instead from staging mirrors, hardware-in-the-loop rigs, simulators or digital twins, whose behavior may differ from the live system in ways the test will not show. One resolution is to record the difference between the test environment and the live system as a stated limit on the evidence, and to bound what is claimed from it. Others include raising fidelity for the specific behaviors that carry the most consequence, or confirming a narrow set of them live during scheduled downtime. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 2.3** (on applying scientific methodologies):This Task fixes TEVV scope, test environment and pass/fail criteria per lifecycle stage before testing begins, and keeps destructive and adversarial testing off live production paths, except during scheduled downtime as discussed in Implementation [1.3.1: Define TEVV scope and requirements per AI system](#bookmark=kix.54aziklwcuxz) and Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj); it does not address the data availability, representativeness and suitability that MAP 2.3 also names. For those, see Task [7.2: Validate data provenance for model inputs](#bookmark=kix.1ax7q7mnm5yj). **MEASURE 2.5** (on measuring validity and reliability):This Task demonstrates validity and reliability against the requirements and boundary envelopes set in Task [1.1: Identify current requirements](#bookmark=kix.uqvdk8m0gq1), and records the conditions beyond which that evidence does not hold; it does not detect the loss of validity that appears only after the operating environment moves. For that, see Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i). **MEASURE 2.6** (on measuring safety):This Task verifies that a system exceeding its defined boundaries derates or transitions predictably, and constrains it to a more restrictive oversight mode where the evidence does not support autonomy; it does not specify what the fallback states themselves should be. For that, see Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p).  **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome The following references illustrate how different CI sectors have formalized TEVV-equivalent processes. Readers should look for analogous frameworks within their own sector's regulatory or standards environment. **Regulation** [DoD Manual 5000.101, "Operational Test and Evaluation and Live Fire Test and Evaluation of Artificial Intelligence-Enabled and Autonomous Systems," December 9, 2024](https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/5000101p.PDF?ver=FfOR56lIK5S1LDFfSlYwYQ%3d%3d), a policy issuance of the United States Department of Defense, binding on DoD components. Section 4 sets out the process for operational and live fire test and evaluation of AI-enabled and autonomous systems. **Regulation** [DoD Directive 3000.09, "Autonomy in Weapon Systems," January 25, 2023](https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/300009p.pdf), from the same department. Section 3 covers verification and validation and testing and evaluation of autonomous and semi-autonomous systems.  | **1.3.1: Define TEVV scope and requirements per AI system** For AI systems deployed in safety-critical CI operational roles, define TEVV scope requirements that map to the categories defined in Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm) and are proportionate to consequence severity of errors identified during risk assessments within Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) and explicitly account for CI-specific failure modes, including model drift, adversarial manipulation, and degraded-mode operation.  Test suites should be proportionate to the system's cyber-physical consequence tier and evaluate performance across:   Nominal & Boundary Envelope Testing: Validating system execution against the defined input/output ranges, setpoint limits, and latency ceilings established under Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm).   Adverse & Adversarial Stress Testing: Assessing system stability and containment logic against the specific environmental stresses and attack vectors cataloged under Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93) and Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm).   Degraded-Mode & Fallback Verification: Verifying that when operating parameters exceed Implementation [1.1.2: Structured requirements mapping](#bookmark=kix.w4fyoig3jqdm) boundaries, the system predictably initiates derated operations or transitions to an independent safe fallback state (Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p)).  Establish pass/fail criteria for each lifecycle stage including pre-deployment, post-incident, and periodic drift or degradation checks. Test and Evaluation (T\&E) planning documentation, of the kind already used for many traditional CI systems to specify what will be tested, under what conditions, and against what success criteria before testing begins, provides a useful model for defining these requirements up front, rather than determining them ad hoc during testing.  Where an AI system cannot be shown to satisfy reliability requirements for autonomous operation, constrain it to a more restrictive oversight mode, appropriate to the consequence severity of the application, until TEVV evidence supports a higher level of autonomy (see Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud) for the available modes).  Confirm that the oversight capacity required by that mode is actually available, rather than assuming it: high algorithmic error rates can quickly exceed operator triage capacity, and that capacity is shared with every other system the same operators watch. Where it is not available, limit use of the AI system to what the available oversight capacity can support, as discussed in Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y). To prevent accidental operational outages caused by evaluation overhead, procurement and engineering policies should explicitly avoid high-load active adversarial testing, automated input fuzzing, or dense red-teaming (testing a system by deliberately seeking to compromise it as an attacker would) vectors on live, production model paths.  All destructive profiling or exploratory boundary testing should be restricted to isolated staging environments, offline hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) test rigs, high-fidelity digital twins (detailed virtual replicas of physical equipment or processes that are kept up to date with real data), or during scheduled downtime, with sufficient time and resources to recover if necessary. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Section 6.3.2, Security Continuous Monitoring, covers the monitoring side of lifecycle evaluation in an OT setting, and Appendix C.3.4, Accidental Events, covers the non-adversarial failure modes that TEVV scope has to reach as well as attacks. **Guidance** [Principles for the Secure Integration of Artificial Intelligence in Operational Technology](https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/joint-guidance-principles-for-the-secure-integration-of-artificial-intelligence-in-operational-technology-508c.pdf), December 2025, joint guidance from nine national cybersecurity agencies across seven countries, led by CISA. Section 1.2 covers the secure AI system development lifecycle and Section 3.3 covers AI testing and evaluation. **Standard** ISA/IEC 62443-4-1, Security for industrial automation and control systems, Part 4-1: Secure product development lifecycle requirements, from the International Society of Automation and the International Electrotechnical Commission. Clause 9, Practice 5: Security Verification and Validation Testing, is the industrial control equivalent of the verification requirements set here. **Guidance** [NIST AI 100-2e2025, Adversarial Machine Learning: A Taxonomy and Terminology of Attacks and Mitigations](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.100-2e2025.pdf), from NIST. Provides the baseline taxonomy of adversarial machine learning vulnerabilities, including evasion and data poisoning, which can be used to structure lifecycle test coverage. **Regulation** [DoD Manual 5000.101, Section 4.1](https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/5000101p.PDF?ver=FfOR56lIK5S1LDFfSlYwYQ%3d%3d), a policy issuance of the United States Department of Defense. Covers the process for planning operational test and evaluation of AI-enabled systems.  **\>To the Reviewer:**  \>Implementation references are intended to help those implementing these tasks find further reading, be it to help them implement this task, or to better understand where this task fits in with other things that they may already be familiar with, including other policies and regulations. We would like to provide just enough context to help the reader determine what is useful (particularly if we’re referencing long documents) but we also need to keep this short as most of these implementations will have references. We are trying a few different ways of doing this and while we do this, the references will look different in different places in this document. Let us know what you think about this balance and which ones you prefer\!   |
|  |  |  |
|  |  | **1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments** TEVV activities across the system lifecycle should be conducted within a validated environment tailored to the operational nature of the underlying infrastructure:   For critical applications where service delivery, safety constraints, or physical control loops cannot tolerate production path disruption or performance overhead, adhere to the production path testing exclusions defined in Implementation [1.3.1: Define TEVV scope and requirements per AI system](#bookmark=kix.54aziklwcuxz). Testing, including active verification, automated input fuzzing, and adversarial red-teaming should be restricted to non-production systems such as staging mirrors, hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) test rigs, simulators, or high-fidelity digital twins. For critical applications that possess regular, planned downtime or can sustain temporary outages without safety impacts (e.g., administrative or transactional batch processors, data summarization tools, or off-peak fraud queues), it may be safe to test directly on the active production system strictly during authorized maintenance or scheduled outage windows. Where feasible, this may be preferable to testing on simulators or staging systems, whose behavior may unintentionally differ from the live system.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.3.3: Evaluate AI system behavior against requirements and operational baselines** Assess AI system performance against TEVV requirements defined in Implementation [1.3.1: Define TEVV scope and requirements per AI system](#bookmark=kix.54aziklwcuxz), and the operational baseline reviewed in Implementation [1.1.1: Operational baseline review](#bookmark=kix.rvq272p41vl0). DoD 5000.101 Section 4.3 Test Execution provides examples of high level guidance to understand operational test and evaluation considerations. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.3.4: Generate and maintain audit-ready TEVV evidence** TEVV activities should produce structured, verifiable evidence suitable for regulatory audit, lifecycle review, and post-incident evaluation. To establish operational trust, evidence packages should pair governance evidence (test plans, dataset provenance documentation, model cards) with technical evidence (empirical evaluation results, simulation logs, boundary test receipts). Evidence artifacts should include test plans, dataset provenance documentation, model cards, and evaluation results. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **1.4: Establish the AI System’s Domain Knowledge and Linguistic Alignment Requirements** For AI systems that interact in natural language (including chatbots, summarization tools, schematic/diagram parsers, and user interfaces that generate unique textual or graphical human output), identify and document domain-specific knowledge, industry terminology, operational ontologies, graphical conventions, and community context that the AI system needs to understand in order to perform its intended function accurately and safely.  Especially for multimodal and language models trained on large, general datasets, success criteria should include a verified understanding of sector-specific jargon, graphical symbols, operational nuances, and community variations to language, particularly where colloquial variations exist or where domain-specific conventions can be expected to diverge from those of the broader community.  For high-consequence applications, including otherwise benign AI systems, such as chatbots, or parsers of scanned technical diagrams that provide outputs that are used for high-consequence decisions, verification of understanding should be commensurate with the consequence. This may exclude post-hoc statistical explanations that are not guaranteed to reflect the AI system’s understanding (see Implementation [4.6.2: Derive confidence and rationale for learned components from their decision processes](#bookmark=id.kdty3el67ume) and Implementation [8.1.2: Identify AI systems that may not satisfy root cause analysis requirements](#bookmark=kix.o0fgigw7uop7)) and/or necessitate additional verification of the AI system’s outputs. This helps ensure that the system's outputs, whether in optimization, predictive maintenance, digital twins, report summarization, or AI-generated code, procedures, and configurations, are aligned with the precise technical, conceptual, safety, and execution logic requirements of the industry and the community it serves. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Valid:** Contributes the sector knowledge requirement: the terminology, operational ontologies, graphical conventions and community context an AI system needs to hold for this industry, written as success criteria, verified in proportion to consequence, and with post-hoc statistical explanation ruled out as evidence of understanding. It does not test a delivered system against those criteria. For that, see Task [2.4: Perform Linguistic and Domain-Context Robustness Testing](#bookmark=kix.ste7niwbu1et). **Accurate:** Contributes contextual accuracy as a stated benchmark rather than general fluency: whether sector terms, symbols and priorities are read as the sector uses them, so that a plausible summary which misreads a critical alert fails a criterion (Implementation [1.4.2: Administrative & Support](#bookmark=kix.sdzrcas2bwiz)). It does not verify the artifacts the system generates once they exist. For that, see Practice [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul). **Fair:** Contributes comprehension across community and colloquial language variation as an explicit success criterion, so that uneven understanding across the populations an operator serves fails a stated requirement instead of passing unremarked. It does not evaluate unevenness in the decisions that follow or in the outcomes they produce. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accurate versus Explainable & Interpretable:** Where a domain requirement can be written as a checkable distinction, such as separating a forced outage from planned maintenance (Implementation [1.4.1: Maintenance & Operations](#bookmark=kix.61k3t63kmol8)), demonstrated understanding and measured accuracy move together. Where the requirement is tacit institutional knowledge (Implementation [1.4.4: Requirements for AI-Generated Code, Configurations, Procedures, and Other Logic](#bookmark=kix.qn0bhhgiwsng)), a system can score well on the part that was written down, while the evidence that it holds the rest is exactly what this Task declines to take from post-hoc statistical explanation. One resolution is to raise verification of the outputs themselves in proportion to consequence, which this Task provides for. Others include writing the tacit knowledge down before the requirement is set, or limiting the system's role to the part of the domain that can be stated. For where a stated part could be implemented symbolically, see Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s). **Related AI RMF subcategories include: MAP 2.2** (on defining AI system utility and oversight needs):This Task documents the sector terminology, operational ontologies and graphical conventions that a system must hold, so that its knowledge limits can be stated for one industry rather than in general; it does not define the oversight mode applied where those limits are reached. For that, see Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud). **MEASURE 2.9** (on measuring interpretability and explainability):This Task requires understanding to be verified in proportion to consequence, and treats post-hoc statistical explanations as insufficient evidence that the system understood the domain; it does not cover whether the confidence and rationale shown to an operator at run time reflect how the output was produced. For that, see Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s). **MEASURE 2.11** (on measuring fairness and bias):This Task makes comprehension of community and colloquial language variation an explicit success criterion, so that uneven understanding across communities fails a stated requirement instead of passing unremarked; it does not evaluate bias in the system's decisions or in the outcomes they produce.   | **1.4.1: Maintenance & Operations** For a predictive maintenance or digital twin system, require the AI to demonstrate mastery of technical standards (e.g., distinguishing between "forced outage" and "planned maintenance") to ensure correct prioritization. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.4.2: Administrative & Support** For report summarization or customer service systems, establish "Contextual Accuracy" benchmarks to verify that LLM-based tools correctly interpret industry-specific jargon without introducing "hallucinations" or misprioritizing critical alerts. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.4.3: Infrastructure Optimization** Define the specific operational states and physical constraints the system should "understand" (e.g., grid stability terminology in Energy) to ensure optimization recommendations are technically valid. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **1.4.4: Requirements for AI-Generated Code, Configurations, Procedures, and Other Logic** For AI systems that generate artifacts that affect execution, including by other systems (e.g., code and configurations) and by people (e.g., procedures, summaries of manuals) ensure that success criteria comprehensively capture both formal documented specifications and tacit institutional knowledge.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
| **2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations** Establish safeguards which maintain essential service delivery and functional safety when AI system performance deviates from expected levels, regardless of the cause.  Such safeguards should be considered at multiple points in the system to establish a layered approach and reduce the likelihood of cascading failures.  **Search Keywords:**  graceful degradation, fail-operational, quality of service (QoS), rate limiting, dynamic throttling, process-informed guardrails, watchdog controller, enforcement application, heterogeneous redundancy, defense in depth, digital twin testing, hardware-in-the-loop (HIL), distribution drift, concept drift, time-to-divergence, parameter cycling, multi-vendor interoperability, inductive bias, shortcut learning **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Resilient:** Contributes the layered safeguards that keep essential service delivery and functional safety in place while AI performance is away from expected levels, through fallback to reduced capability or to a known-safe mode of operation. It does not cover sustained operation without the AI system through a prolonged outage, which Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) covers. **Robust:** Contributes the expectation itself: the range of conditions and uses across which AI performance should hold, and the testing that shows where it stops holding. It does not establish the level of performance required in the first place, which Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) establishes. **Reliable:** Contributes repeated re-checking against a maintained reference, so that gradual departure from expected performance is found on a schedule rather than at the moment it reaches the service. It does not provide the moment-to-moment detection of sudden departures, which Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) provides. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Secure:** Redundant paths, independent monitors and fallback routes each add a further way for an instruction to reach the process. Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) covers the scoping of the attack and risk surface that grows as they are added. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Robust versus Resilient:** Where the AI system meets a departure it was designed to absorb, holding performance and degrading gracefully point the same way. Where safeguards are set to keep the AI-enabled function performing across a wider range of conditions, the boundary at which the fallback should engage becomes harder to observe, and the fallback, when it does engage, starts from further outside familiar territory. One resolution is to fix the point at which the fallback engages from the consequence of being wrong rather than from how far the AI system can be pushed. Others include treating the wider range as a documented allowance that prompts review rather than as normal operation. **Accurate versus Robust:** Where operation stays close to the conditions the AI system was tested in, closer agreement with the reference and steadiness across conditions move together. Where the expectation is set to hold across a wide range of conditions, an AI system tuned for its best average agreement may be the more fragile choice at the edges. One resolution is to state the required performance separately for normal and for stressed conditions, so that the two are procured, tested and accepted as distinct expectations. Others include accepting a narrower range of conditions of use and carrying the remainder on the fallback.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MEASURE 2.6** (on measuring safety):This Practice contributes the fail-safe behavior, derated modes and boundary testing that a demonstration of failing safely rests on; it does not set the residual risk tolerance against which acceptable safety is judged. For that, see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d). **MEASURE 2.7** (on measuring security and resiliency):This Practice contributes the resilience half of that evaluation, in redundancy, graceful degradation and layered safeguards that sustain service delivery when AI performance deviates; it does not catalog the adversarial exposure that MEASURE 2.7 pairs resilience with. For that, see Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93). **MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Practice contributes the automatic mechanisms, from rate limits through redundant paths to watchdog controllers, that disengage an AI system whose behavior is inconsistent with intended use; it does not cover the operator-initiated intervention that follows. For that, see Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf). **MANAGE 2.2** (on ensuring sustained value):This Practice contributes the quality-of-service floor that a deployed AI system continues to deliver while it degrades; it does not define the value that the system was adopted to deliver. For that, see Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k). **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.SP.800-82r3), from NIST. Section 4.1.1, Framing OT Risk, covers how an OT organization arrives at impact levels, and its Table 3 gives example definitions across outage duration, national infrastructure and services, cost as a share of revenue, legal exposure, public confidence and harm to people on site. Those categories are written as outcomes rather than as causes, so the same characterization reaches service loss that follows an AI performance deviation as well as service loss that follows an attack. **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-160v1r1, Engineering Trustworthy Secure Systems](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.SP.800-160v1r1), from NIST. Its Appendix E states the design principles for trustworthy secure design. Protective Failure (E.23) requires that the failure of one system element be contained, so that it does not cause unacceptable loss or trigger a further loss scenario, which is the cascading failure this Practice asks a layered approach to reduce; Loss Margins (E.18), Defense in Depth (E.9) and Protective Recovery (E.24) carry the rest of the layering. Section 3.5 reasons in terms of loss rather than graded impact, and its Table 2 names limited capacity, being diminished delivery of function, service or capability, as a loss limitation objective in its own right. **Standard** ANSI/ISA-62443-3-3-2013, Security for industrial automation and control systems, Part 3-3: System security requirements and security levels, from the International Society of Automation, a technical society that develops automation standards, approved as an American National Standard. Clause 11, Resource Availability, is the industrial control counterpart of the service delivery floor this Practice sets. **Guidance** [Adapting Zero Trust Principles to Operational Technology](https://www.cisa.gov/resources-tools/resources/adapting-zero-trust-principles-operational-technology), published 29 April 2026 by the Zero Trust Operational Technologies Security Working Group, a joint initiative of the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, the Department of War and the Department of Energy, with the Department of State, the Federal Bureau of Investigation and NIST contributing. Pages 12 and 13, on network and microsegmentation, hold that isolation prevents cascading failures across interconnected systems and that segmentation limits the blast radius of a cyber or operational incident, so the reasoning reaches a malfunctioning AI path and not only a compromised one. The guide’s Table 1 states what OT segmentation is for in contrast to IT segmentation: system availability and system safety rather than data confidentiality, with an extremely low tolerance for downtime because disruptions can have physical consequences.  | **2.1: Establish AI usage thresholds and rate control mechanisms** Define and implement technical constraints on the volume, frequency, and scope of AI-driven actions, communications, and resource usage, particularly where there are physical and safety limitations or constrained resources. This task reduces the potential for rapid, automated system instability and resource bottlenecks by helping ensure that AI interactions remain within predefined operational tolerances and do not overwhelm upstream or downstream legacy systems. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Resilient:** Contributes a ceiling on how fast a departure can propagate, so that an AI system behaving unexpectedly consumes a bounded amount of resource, control authority and operator attention before the wider system falls back to a safe state (Implementation [2.1.1: Implement baseline rate limiting](#bookmark=kix.5erbb4a4g9je)). It does not supply the independent mechanism that acts once that ceiling is reached, which Task [2.2: Establish requirements for redundancy in AI-Driven functions](#bookmark=kix.rbl0o5b5wwkj) supplies. **Safe:** Contributes limits placed where AI activity meets physical and resource constraints, holding the volume, frequency and scope of automated action within what the surrounding equipment and legacy systems were built to absorb. It does not define the operating boundary that an individual command should respect, which Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla) defines. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Transparent:** An AI system held at a limit continues to operate while its activity is being constrained, so the constraint is a fact about the deployment that should be recorded and notified rather than inferred. Implementation [2.1.1: Implement baseline rate limiting](#bookmark=kix.5erbb4a4g9je) directs notification of limit events, and Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers retention of the record. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Safe:** Where the AI system's demand is steady, a fixed ceiling holds automated activity inside what the surrounding equipment can absorb. Where a fast-moving abnormal condition calls for a higher volume of valid commands, such as a rapid pressure excursion or a cascading equipment fault, the same ceiling can suppress correct action at the moment it matters most. One resolution is Implementation [2.1.2: Develop process-informed guardrails](#bookmark=kix.twbuv0dvlcz4), which sets the permitted rate from the state of the process rather than as a single fixed value across all operating conditions. Others include raising the ceiling for a declared abnormal condition, or treating a limit reached during an excursion as a trigger for the fallback in Task [2.2: Establish requirements for redundancy in AI-Driven functions](#bookmark=kix.rbl0o5b5wwkj) rather than as a cap held in place. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 4.2** (on establishing risk controls for AI system components):This Task contributes rate limits, process-informed guardrails and throttles as the internal controls placed around the AI component itself; it does not cover the independent mechanism that acts once those controls are exceeded. For that, see Task [2.2: Establish requirements for redundancy in AI-Driven functions](#bookmark=kix.rbl0o5b5wwkj). **MEASURE 2.4** (on monitoring deployed AI systems):This Task contributes the automatic response to production monitoring, throttling the AI system when its behavior departs from expected patterns; it does not define the detection regime that recognizes the departure. For that, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk). | **2.1.1: Implement baseline rate limiting**  Rate limit the various resources and actions that an AI system can take, including consuming AI generation resources (often measured in tokens), making configuration change requests, taking control actions, and requesting attention from humans and other systems.  This reduces the likelihood that automated activity will saturate critical networks or cause instability in other systems, particularly legacy systems that may not be designed for the rapid changes in resource usage or control that can result from some AI systems. It may also highlight signs of adversarial action or interference.  Ensure that when rate limits are hit, the system's overall behavior falls back to a safe state and that operators are notified via non-disruptive alerts where appropriate (Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p) and Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y)). This may be particularly important where AI is used to improve efficiency in normal times, resulting in either more systems utilizing the same allocated resources or the same systems being allocated fewer resources. Such rate limits act as a first line of defense against overload, particularly in unusual or unforeseen situations where the efficiency gains may not hold.  To safeguard operator cognitive capacity and minimize alert fatigue, notification delivery metrics should follow exception batching, aggregation, and soft-throttling strategies, some of which are discussed in Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y). Rate limits should account for legitimate increases in command frequency during upset or abnormal operating conditions. An AI system responding correctly to a fast-moving abnormal condition, such as a rapid pressure excursion or a cascading equipment fault, may need to issue a higher volume of valid commands than during steady-state operation, and a rate limit tuned only to normal conditions could suppress legitimate corrective actions at the moment they matter most. Rate limit thresholds should therefore be informed by Implementation [2.1.2: Develop process-informed guardrails](#bookmark=kix.twbuv0dvlcz4), rather than applied as a single fixed ceiling across all operating conditions. |
|  |  | **2.1.2: Develop process-informed guardrails**  These dynamically determine the appropriate rate at which AI systems should be utilizing resources or taking actions. For example:  An AI-based optimization system may use more tokens during quiet periods.  Rapid changes to power grid load distribution should be avoided when the grid is running close to capacity.  These guardrails should be deterministic and based on the application's needs. Such guardrails give the AI system flexibility to use additional resources or take additional actions when the application requires it, such as during quieter periods, while maintaining tight control during rapid change or heightened activity.  |
|  |  | **2.1.3: Implement anomaly-driven detection and throttling**  This should be based on patterns of expected behavior of the AI system, particularly in situations where tight guardrails may be difficult to develop.  |
|  | **2.2: Establish requirements for redundancy in AI-Driven functions** Establish a risk-proportionate redundancy strategy to maintain continuous operations during AI degradation, distribution drift, or system failure.  Redundancy requirements should be commensurate with consequence severity. This may range from heterogeneous AI model and infrastructure diversity (for complex digital workflows where non-AI fallbacks are technically infeasible) up to deterministic, rule-based software algorithms or out-of-band non-AI physical interlocks (for high-consequence cyber-physical systems). This task directly preserves the system's Resilience by enforcing graceful degradation (operating in reduced capability or derated modes) or transitioning to a known fail-safe state or legacy/manual control, reducing the impact of a total loss of service due to AI component failure. This task complements Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk).   **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Resilient:** Contributes the graded fallback that keeps a function available while the AI system is not: secondary models on distinct architectures, training distributions and hosting where a non-AI path is infeasible, rising to deterministic algorithms or physical interlocks where consequence is high (Implementation [2.2.1: Maintain fully functional backup systems](#bookmark=kix.trfwcfjq6bu1)). It does not define the state that the control system enters once the AI system is disengaged, which Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p) defines. **Reliable:** Contributes an independent, non-AI check on whether the AI system is still performing as required, in continuous monitoring of output integrity, execution latency and setpoint stability (Implementation [2.2.2: Deploy “watchdog” controllers](#bookmark=kix.ygluakfzcdgd)). It does not produce the evidence that the fallback operates as intended when called on, which Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5) produces. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Secure:** Each redundant path, and each monitor able to force a transition, is a further route by which the process can be commanded or stopped. Implementation [2.2.2: Deploy “watchdog” controllers](#bookmark=kix.ygluakfzcdgd) hardens the watchdog itself through read-only telemetry access and isolation from the AI application stack, and Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93) covers scoping the exposure that the wider redundancy strategy adds. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Resilient:** Where the process can absorb a transition at any point, tripping to the fallback on a watchdog signal serves continuity and safety together. Where the trip would land during an active process transition or a critical control maneuver, the transition is itself the hazard, and a corrupted input or a deliberate actor can force it. One resolution is Implementation [2.2.2: Deploy “watchdog” controllers](#bookmark=kix.ygluakfzcdgd)'s state-aware interlocks and rate-of-change validation on the trigger, so that the trip is qualified by what the process is doing. Others include sequencing the fallback so that it reaches a deterministic state in steps rather than at once. **Resilient versus Resilient:** Where a non-AI path is infeasible, secondary models on distinct architectures, training distributions and hosting remove the single point of failure in the implementation. Where the shared exposure is to the operating environment instead, such as distribution drift that reaches every model built on similar data, that diversity does less, while a deterministic algorithm or a physical interlock holds under the same drift and carries less of the function. One resolution is Implementation [2.2.1: Maintain fully functional backup systems](#bookmark=kix.trfwcfjq6bu1)'s redundancy gradient, which fixes the degree of independence from the consequence tier rather than from what is convenient to build. Others include pairing the two, with the deterministic path bounding the diverse one.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Task contributes the independent, non-AI watchdog that disengages or supersedes an AI system whose output, latency or setpoint stability falls outside intended use; it does not define the state that the control system enters once the AI system is disengaged. For that, see Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p). **MEASURE 2.6** (on measuring safety):This Task contributes the redundancy gradient and watchdog controllers that make failing safely an implemented property rather than an assumed one; it does not produce the evidence that those mechanisms work. For that, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5). **GOVERN 6.2** (on establishing contingency plans for critical third parties):This Task contributes the runtime contingency for a failing third-party model, in secondary models built on distinct architectures, training distributions and hosting infrastructure; it does not cover the pre-acquisition visibility that identifies which third-party dependencies are high-risk. For that, see Practice [6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management](#bookmark=kix.gk6f3b3rnwyn). | **2.2.1: Maintain fully functional backup systems**  Establish a risk-proportionate redundancy gradient for AI-driven functions, matching the level of architectural isolation to system consequence tiers (Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d)).  For lower and moderate-consequence applications where non-AI fallbacks are technically infeasible, organizations should deploy heterogeneous AI redundancy, utilizing secondary models built on distinct architectures, training distributions, and hosting infrastructure to mitigate single-points-of-failure and shared model drift.  For high-consequence or cyber-physical functions, redundancy should escalate to include independent, deterministic algorithmic fallbacks or out-of-band non-AI physical safety interlocks (Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla)). Develop and communicate expected guarantees of performance during fail-over procedures so that downstream entities can prepare for potential impacts.  |
|  |  | **2.2.2: Deploy “watchdog” controllers**  Watchdog controllers, also referred to as “enforcement applications,” are based on independent, deterministic architectures that monitor AI output and automatically perform fail-over, consistent with Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p).  Deploy independent watchdog controllers to continuously monitor AI system health, execution latency, setpoint stability, and output integrity. To ensure watchdog mechanisms do not introduce unmanaged complexity, secondary attack surfaces, or weaponized failover vectors, organizations should enforce: Guaranteed Determinism (Non-AI Assurance): Watchdog logic should be strictly non-AI, lightweight, and deterministic. Example implementations may include dedicated hardware timers, separate Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) or microcontrollers, with minimal, formally verified software logic completely isolated from the primary AI application stack. Tamper & Exploit Resistance: Watchdog controllers should operate out-of-band on hardened, privileged infrastructure with read-only telemetry access, helping ensure that an upstream compromise (of the AI system or otherwise), prompt injection, or network intrusion cannot modify watchdog setpoints, disable execution checks, or corrupt trip logic. Note that the watchdog itself may be part of the attack surface as discussed in Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93). Mitigation of Destructive/Forced Failover: Watchdog trigger thresholds should still incorporate state-aware interlocks and rate-of-change validation to prevent corrupted inputs or malicious actors from forcing an unintended failover during sensitive operational windows (e.g., during active process transitions or critical control maneuvers). Triggers should initiate safe, deterministic fallback sequences (Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p)) rather than uncoordinated abrupt shutdowns. Ensure the watchdog itself is included within Implementation [3.4.3: Monitor validation control integrity](#bookmark=kix.h6w8xayde4wp). Notify operators of any watchdog-triggered fail-over per Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq), so that a failure the watchdog catches does not go unnoticed by operators.  **\> To the Reviewer:**  \> We understand that terms like “watchdog” controllers can vary from industry to industry. We’d love further suggestions as to other terms that might be used for such concepts, both here and in other places in this document, to make sure that we’re all on compatible pages even if we all use different terminology.  |
|  | **2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)** Perform rigorous testing and analysis specifically focused on the AI system’s behavior in anomalous or edge-case conditions and environments, extending the TEVV protocols established in Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) to govern specialized boundary and stress conditions. This task provides the verifiable evidence needed to confirm that fail-safe mechanisms and manual overrides operate as intended, supporting long-term reliability. It also supports rigorously identifying the attack and risk surface as discussed in Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93) and supports the evaluation of downstream risk as discussed in Task [3.5: Define and control risks to adjacent and related systems](#bookmark=kix.okyn4ifab2xw).  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Robust:** Contributes evidence of how the AI system behaves beyond the conditions it is expected to meet, by exercising it against edge cases, injected faults and stressed physical, network and human parameters in environments built for destructive testing (Implementation [2.3.3: Use Digital Twins to regularly test AI system behavior](#bookmark=kix.6utfavt3gwsk)). It does not set the criteria that the results are judged against, which Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) sets as the anchor for TEVV throughout this profile. **Reliable:** Contributes conformance over time as a measured quantity: a curated set of normal and known-fault inputs kept current, an initial high-frequency window that measures how fast this deployment actually diverges, and an evaluation cadence derived from that measured rate (Implementation [2.3.1: Baseline AI system performance](#bookmark=kix.379o6rmrakb9) and Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i)). It does not detect an acute departure between evaluations, which Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk) covers through continuous runtime monitoring. **Resilient:** Contributes the evidence that the fallback path works when called on, by injecting equipment trips, sensor failure modes and mistimed interventions rather than assuming the response to them (Implementation [2.3.3: Use Digital Twins to regularly test AI system behavior](#bookmark=kix.6utfavt3gwsk)). It does not specify the states themselves; Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p) does that. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Safe:** Boundary, adversarial and destructive testing places the AI system into the conditions it exists to avoid. Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj) restricts that testing to staging mirrors, hardware-in-the-loop rigs, simulators and digital twins where the production path cannot absorb the disruption. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Valid versus Safe:** Where the deployment has planned downtime or can absorb a temporary outage, evidence gathered on the live system is the evidence that applies to it, and Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj) treats that as preferable. Where the process cannot tolerate disruption, the evidence has to come from a twin or a rig whose behavior may differ from the installed system in the respects being tested. One resolution is the pairing described in Implementation [2.3.3: Use Digital Twins to regularly test AI system behavior](#bookmark=kix.6utfavt3gwsk), which combines twin-based evaluation with passive shadow mode or phased commissioning and compares live telemetry against twin predictions so that divergence stays measured. Others include limiting the claims drawn from twin results to the conditions the twin is known to reproduce. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MEASURE 2.3** (on ensuring realistic measurement):This Task contributes the high-fidelity digital twins and hardware-in-the-loop rigs that reproduce deployment conditions closely enough for destructive and edge-case testing; it does not decide when testing on the live production path is permissible. For that, see Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj). **MEASURE 2.1** (on documenting measurement approaches):This Task contributes the validated standard dataset of curated normal and known-fault inputs, kept current against drift, as the documented test set; it does not set the pass/fail criteria that the dataset is exercised against. For those, see Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b). **MEASURE 3.1** (on tracking known and emerging risks):This Task contributes the recurrent and trigger-based re-evaluation that tracks drift and emergent behavior from actual performance in deployed contexts; it does not set the drift limits at which a fail-safe response becomes necessary. For those, see Implementation [3.3.2: Establish Maximum Drift Thresholds](#bookmark=kix.8m48t6fuoxqu).  | **2.3.1: Baseline AI system performance**  Establish a “validated standard” dataset consisting of curated historical inputs into the system for both normal and known-fault conditions, and expected outputs, and expected levels of performance against this dataset. This dataset should be regularly reviewed and updated to account for drift in the environment, task, and/or expected behavior.  AI systems should be regularly tested against this dataset during scheduled intervals as defined in Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i) and deviations from acceptable thresholds for performance should be flagged for review and remediation as necessary.  |
|  |  | **2.3.2: Targeted failure mode mapping**  Map the highest risk AI-specific behaviors in response to both malicious attack (e.g., attacks listed in MITRE ATLAS and NIST AI 100-2e2025) as well as unintentional reasons (e.g., model drift). Conduct regular adversarial testing of AI systems to verify system behavior and proactively adjust or implement risk management controls where appropriate.  |
|  |  | **2.3.3: Use Digital Twins to regularly test AI system behavior**  Testing should cover anomalous conditions that are close to, or likely to result from, the system’s current operational state.  For AI systems deployed in cyber-physical environments (e.g., OT, SCADA, ICS, robotics, autonomous systems), testing should extend beyond offline data replays to high-fidelity digital twins and Hardware-in-the-Loop (HIL) simulation environments. Consider incorporating where relevant: Physical Dynamics & Non-Linearities: High-fidelity modeling of underlying physical processes, including thermodynamics, fluid dynamics, mechanical inertia, friction, wear-and-tear, substitution of nominally equivalent parts and components through repair and maintenance, and non-linear process behaviors across nominal and extreme operating regimes. Actuator Kinematics & Sensor Realism: Modeling of physical actuator response times, valve travel rates, motor saturation, sensor noise, calibration drift, signal quantization, and intermittent telemetry drops. Control Network Latency & Jitter: Replication of OT/ICS communication bus characteristics (e.g., Modbus, PROFINET, CAN bus latency), network jitter, packet drops, and processing delays between the AI agent, local PLCs, and physical assets. Internal Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) Dynamics: Simulation of operator behavioral factors, including realistic human response latencies during alarms, cognitive fatigue, manual override timing, mistimed interventions, and automation bias (both over-reliance and delayed manual takeover). External Human & Social Interactions: Modeling of external human inputs and behavioral variables, including rapid consumer demand surges, pedestrian/vehicular traffic patterns (for mobile or field-deployed assets), field technician maintenance actions, and unauthorized physical access events. Ambient & Exogenous Environmental Stressors: Simulation of external environmental conditions, including weather extremes (freezing, heatwaves, severe storms), ambient temperature/pressure fluctuations, electromagnetic interference (EMI), solar/radiation noise, and physical site degradation. Process Upset & Fault Injection Capabilities: Deterministic fault injection for sudden equipment trips, rapid environmental shifts, sensor failure modes, and simulated cyber-physical attack vectors (e.g., spoofed sensor telemetry or delayed command execution). These should be deterministic between tests to ensure that results are repeatable and can be traced appropriately.  Hardware In the Loop Interfacing (HIL): Direct integration with physical programmable logic controllers (PLCs), safety instrumented systems (SIS), or hardware proxies executing real-time control cycles to validate closed-loop stability before production deployment. While digital twins and HIL rigs provide safe environments for destructive and high-consequence stress testing, they cannot fully replicate all open-world operational variables. Testing strategies should balance twin-based evaluation with controlled, low-risk real-world testing (e.g., passive shadow mode or phased operational commissioning). Testing strategies are discussed in Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj). Continuous baseline comparisons between live sensor telemetry and digital twin predictions should be performed to identify model/twin divergence over time without exposing physical assets to unmanaged operational risk (Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i)). |
|  |  | **2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations** Establish periodic and trigger-based re-evaluation protocols to detect distribution drift, being the gradual or sudden deviation of real-world operational environments, seasonal population changes, physical sensor responses, equipment dynamics, or mission tasks from the conditions captured during model training and initial validation. This applies to both OT environments, such as optimization based on sensor data, as well as IT and office environments, such as summarizing medical reports where content and language distributions may vary depending on factors such as seasonal ailments, the arrival of new diseases and treatments, or changes in social media trends.  Because unmanaged drift can cause silent degradation of decision accuracy or violate safe operating boundaries, continuous monitoring and recurrent evaluation should be structured across two operational phases to establish empirical drift baselines: **Phase 1: Commissioning and Baselining (Burn-In Period)**Upon initial deployment or major re-training, the system should enter an initial high-frequency monitoring window (e.g., real-time telemetry checks, hourly/daily batch evaluations, scaled to consequence tier). This phase captures live field data to establish empirical baselines for operational drift, input distribution shifts, and real-world system decay under actual operating conditions. **Phase 2: Adaptive Recurrent Evaluation**Once empirical divergence behaviors are established during Phase 1, ongoing evaluation cadences should be set to a fraction (e.g., one-quarter) of the empirically measured Time to Divergence, commensurate with the consequence of undetected divergence. If operational drift accelerates or un-modeled environmental shifts occur, the evaluation interval should dynamically scale down to match the accelerated decay rate. **Event-Driven Triggers:**Independent of scheduled time intervals, full re-verification should be executed immediately upon: Upstream data schema shifts, sensor replacements, or physical equipment retrofits. Safety interlock trips, watchdog fallback events (Implementation [2.2.2: Deploy “watchdog” controllers](#bookmark=kix.ygluakfzcdgd)), or out-of-bounds operational excursions. Vendor model updates, upstream component patches, or security vulnerability disclosures (Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q)). Continuous telemetry otherwise indicates that actual drift velocity is accelerating faster than the estimated baseline. This is a scheduled, batch-level evaluation; see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk) for continuous, runtime anomaly detection and Implementation [3.3.2: Establish Maximum Drift Thresholds](#bookmark=kix.8m48t6fuoxqu) for the specific drift thresholds that trigger fail-safe responses. Where historical incident or drift data is too limited to support a confident quantitative estimate, organizations should use the best available approximation (e.g., expert judgment or analogous systems) and revise the cadence as real operational data accumulates, rather than treating the initial estimate as fixed.  To execute these evaluations without impacting availability or safety, organizations should follow Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj).  Systems should be tested against the "validated standard" datasets maintained under Implementation [2.3.1: Baseline AI system performance](#bookmark=kix.379o6rmrakb9) to quantify statistical drift, concept drift, and shifts in error distributions (such as false positive/negative rates).  If performance metrics degrade below the baselines established in Task [1.2: Create quantitative baselines for existing systems in both normal and challenging conditions](#bookmark=kix.yl9k2ljvy4q2) or intersect with the boundaries defined in Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla), the system should initiate formal change-management or mitigation workflows. These workflows may include model retraining, throttling via Task [2.1: Establish AI usage thresholds and rate control mechanisms](#bookmark=kix.t787t4oqukn9), or transitioning the process to the backup as specified in Implementation [2.2.1: Maintain fully functional backup systems](#bookmark=kix.trfwcfjq6bu1) while mitigations are implemented.  **References:**  **\>To the Reviewer:**  \>We are also evaluating the relevance of OCC Bulletin 2026-13 and ISO/IEC 42001:2023 for determining evaluation cadence; feedback and suggestions for other references welcome. |
|  | **2.4: Perform Linguistic and Domain-Context Robustness Testing** Integrate domain-specific linguistic and contextual validation into the system’s TEVV protocols. This Task helps ensure that the AI system, especially when utilizing pre-trained models, remains robust and reliable when encountering specialized industry terminology, operational jargon, or community-specific language. The goal is to prevent failure modes where linguistic ambiguity or a lack of domain awareness leads to incorrect system actions, safety-critical "hallucinations," or the misprioritization of critical infrastructure alerts. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Robust:** Contributes invariance across the language the AI system meets in service, testing whether performance holds on sector jargon, role-specific meanings and terms whose local sense differs from conventional use (Implementation [2.4.1: Benchmarking on application-specific terminology](#bookmark=kix.jqdx2zh1yjpi)). It does not identify which terminology and language varieties are in scope for a deployment, which Task [1.4: Establish the AI System’s Domain Knowledge and Linguistic Alignment Requirements](#bookmark=kix.ddne831a11g5) identifies. **Accurate:** Contributes a check aimed at the error that reads as correct, using subject matter experts to spot-check and red-team outputs that sound plausible and are technically wrong (Implementation [2.4.3: Technical Accuracy and "Plausible Hallucination" Audits](#bookmark=kix.osb3t4hofz96)). It does not verify an individual artifact before it is deployed, which Practice [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) covers. **Fair:** Contributes measurement of whether delivery holds across the service area, testing robustness against regional dialects and community-specific terminology for customer-facing and public-sector systems (Implementation [2.4.2: Localization and Community Validation](#bookmark=kix.kc7i0ggkdalb)). It does not determine what level of variation is acceptable, which remains a determination for the operator in its regulatory and operational context. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accurate versus Accurate:** Where the terminology set is broad and refreshed from current operational language, measured precision on it tracks understanding in service. Where it is small and fixed, an AI system can be tuned toward it and improve on the measure while getting worse at the task. One resolution is Implementation [2.4.3: Technical Accuracy and "Plausible Hallucination" Audits](#bookmark=kix.osb3t4hofz96)'s expert spot-check and red-team review, which tests against judgment the dataset does not contain. Others include refreshing the terminology set from operational text as it changes, or holding part of it back from those tuning the system. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MEASURE 2.11** (on measuring fairness and bias):This Task contributes robustness testing against regional dialects and community-specific terminology, so that information reaches the whole service area accurately; it does not identify which communities and language varieties are in scope. For that, see Task [1.4: Establish the AI System’s Domain Knowledge and Linguistic Alignment Requirements](#bookmark=kix.ddne831a11g5). **MEASURE 2.5** (on measuring validity and reliability):This Task contributes the linguistic half of generalizability, testing whether a model trained on general text stays reliable on sector jargon and role-specific meanings; it does not test generalization across physical components and hardware. For that, see Task [2.5: Verify and Ensure Policy Robustness Against Component, Hardware, and Data Schema Variance](#bookmark=kix.8csdyl8bfyt8). **MEASURE 4.1** (on gathering feedback on measurement approaches):This Task contributes subject matter expert review and community validation as the means by which test material is tied to the deployment context; it does not cover the physical and process context that MEASURE 4.1 also expects measurement approaches to reflect. For that, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5). | **2.4.1: Benchmarking on application-specific terminology**  Develop or utilize sector-specific datasets that include significant application-specific terminology. This includes ambiguous operational terms (e.g., distinguishing between a "soft trip" and a "hard shutdown"), colloquial terms used by different roles in the sector, and terms whose application- and role-specific meanings differ from conventional use. This dataset should be used to measure the model’s precision in interpreting application-specific text, such as in summarizing reports, interpreting natural language instructions, and performing internal and external-facing chatbot tasks. |
|  |  | **2.4.2: Localization and Community Validation**  For customer-facing or public-sector systems, test the AI’s robustness against regional dialects and community-specific terminology to ensure equitable and accurate delivery of information across the service area. |
|  |  | **2.4.3: Technical Accuracy and "Plausible Hallucination" Audits** Implement specific TEVV protocols to identify plausible-sounding but technically inaccurate outputs.  For AI systems providing or generating diagnostics, maintenance summaries, operational advice, procedures, code, or configurations, use subject matter experts to at least spot-check and/or assist in red-teaming the system's instructions. This testing should focus on edge cases where the AI might generate incorrect maintenance steps that appear valid on the surface but would damage physical assets or violate safety protocols if executed.  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST AI 600-1, Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.AI.600-1), from NIST. Section 2.2, Confabulation, names and defines the failure this Task tests for, including outputs that diverge from the prompt or contradict earlier statements in the same context, and treats it as a consequence of how generative models work rather than as an attack. **Guidance** [NIST AI 100-2e2025, Adversarial Machine Learning: A Taxonomy and Terminology of Attacks and Mitigations](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.AI.100-2e2025), from NIST. Section 3.4.2, Integrity Attacks, covers indirect prompt injection that drives a generative system to produce incorrect summaries of its sources or to return attacker-specified content, and records that the disruption is often subtle enough that an end user does not notice it. Its scope is inaccuracy an attacker induces, so an output that is plausible and wrong with no attacker present falls outside the taxonomy.   |
|  | **2.5: Verify and Ensure Policy Robustness Against Component, Hardware, and Data Schema Variance** Ensure that learning systems that optimize their behavior, such as reinforcement learning systems, do not learn to rely on undocumented, unspecified, or beyond-specification behavior of their target system.  Such dependence may cause unexpected suboptimal system behavior, failure, damage, or otherwise increase the attack and risk surface (see Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93)) if the AI system is subsequently deployed on, uses, depends on, or interacts with, other systems that have identical specifications but behave differently outside of their specified parameters.  For example, an AI system trained to optimize pressure levels might learn to rely on a training-system valve that can physically open wider than its specification requires, where the specification defines only a minimum flow rate. If this AI system is subsequently deployed to control a different system with the same specifications, but where the valve just meets specifications, the system may behave in an undesirable manner.  This task also supports Implementation [7.5.4: Audit Interoperability for Hardware or Data Substrate Substitution](#bookmark=kix.sg34y8w5ezvi) .  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Robust:** Contributes invariance across the specification range rather than across one installation, by cycling environmental, digital and physical parameters to their full allowed limits and testing functionally equivalent parts from different manufacturers at differing states of wear (Implementation [2.5.1: Train Across the Full Operating Range](#bookmark=kix.40sf6fywk6sm) and Implementation [2.5.2: Multi-Vendor Testing](#bookmark=kix.q2tobsyfcymj)). It does not trigger re-testing when a component or data feed is substituted in service, which Implementation [7.5.4: Audit Interoperability for Hardware or Data Substrate Substitution](#bookmark=kix.sg34y8w5ezvi) triggers. **Valid:** Contributes a check that an AI system proven in one deployment is the right system for another built to the same specification, by finding learned dependence on behavior that the specification permits but does not require. It does not confirm fit against the receiving deployment's own requirements, which Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) confirms. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Robust versus Accurate:** Where the assets an AI system will meet over its life genuinely span the specification range, a policy learned across that range performs better in service. Where one installation sits at a stable point within the range, cycling parameters across the whole of it spends learning capacity on configurations that will not be met, and the resulting policy may do less well on the one that is. One resolution is to scope the parameter cycling in Implementation [2.5.1: Train Across the Full Operating Range](#bookmark=kix.40sf6fywk6sm) to the range the AI system may actually be deployed across, including planned replacements and multi-vendor substitution. Others include keeping the narrower policy and treating any substitution as a re-qualification trigger under Implementation [7.5.4: Audit Interoperability for Hardware or Data Substrate Substitution](#bookmark=kix.sg34y8w5ezvi). **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MEASURE 2.5** (on measuring validity and reliability):This Task contributes the generalizability limit that matters most in cyber-physical deployment, where a learned policy depends on out-of-specification component behavior that was present during training; it does not test generalization to unfamiliar language and terminology. For that, see Task [2.4: Perform Linguistic and Domain-Context Robustness Testing](#bookmark=kix.ste7niwbu1et). **MAP 2.3** (on applying scientific methodologies):This Task contributes representativeness as a property of the hardware and parameter space rather than of the data alone, by cycling parameters across full specification limits and testing multi-vendor parts at varying wear; it does not cover the wider TEVV design that MAP 2.3 also names. For that, see Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b).  | **2.5.1: Train Across the Full Operating Range** AI systems trained within a narrow slice of their operating window risk failing when conditions legitimately vary across the broader system profile. To ensure resilience across both digital execution layers and, where applicable, cyber-physical operating ranges: Simulation & Synthetic Parameter Cycling: When training models via simulations or digital twins, developers should intentionally randomize and cycle environmental, digital, and physical process parameters across their full allowed specification limits (e.g., maximum/minimum rated latencies, tolerances, pressure and thermal thresholds, sensor noise, equipment wear states, and ambient variations). Physical & Hardware Representative Testing: When training or validating on physical hardware, testing should be conducted across a representative cross-section of functionally equivalent assets, incorporating varying levels of component wear, calibration drift, and environmental exposure. Symbolic Background Knowledge & Constraint Encoding: For systems utilizing symbolic background knowledge or formal domain rules, system operating envelopes and component specifications should be explicitly encoded so the AI learning process avoids relying on capabilities outside authorized design boundaries. This is intended to prevent the AI from constructing execution policies that fail when a system or component operates at a different, yet fully compliant, point within its formal specification sheet. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [ISO/IEC TR 5469:2024, Artificial intelligence: functional safety and AI systems](https://www.iso.org/standard/81283.html), a technical report from the International Organization for Standardization and the International Electrotechnical Commission. Covers risks associated with environmental complexity, data distribution against hazard analysis, and test coverage limitations. **Research** Taguchi, G. (1986). Introduction to Quality Engineering: Designing Quality into Products and Processes. Asian Productivity Organization, an intergovernmental body headquartered in Tokyo. Establishes the engineering principle of exposing a system to deliberate parameter variation during development. Its subject is manufactured products and the processes that make them, and the parameter cycling this Implementation asks of simulation and digital twin training applies that principle to a training regime. |
|  |  | **2.5.2: Multi-Vendor Testing**  For high-consequence systems, the AI system's validation should include testing in simulation environments, digital twins, and/or dedicated test-rigs. These should include equivalent parts from different manufacturers and at different levels of wear and tear that are representative of what may appear in the environments that the AI system would be deployed in over time. This is necessary to detect hidden, non-generalized model behavioral strategies (learned policies), such as a hidden reliance on capabilities that may be outside of the physical parts’ specifications.  For time-critical systems, testing should be performed on non-production environments as specified in Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj). |
