| **3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior** Establish well-defined, deterministic, actionable boundaries, policies, and oversight for high-risk actions that may cause disruption to safety-critical (cyber-)physical processes. This practice combines with the requirements defined in Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) and Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) to define the tradespace of the risks and benefits of using the AI system, and the costs associated with controls that may be needed.  It also defines the deterministic automated boundaries and immediate technical fail-safe states (See Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) for real-time human intervention and Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) for long-term manual continuity). **Search Keywords:**  agentic oversight, deterministic guardrails, safety wrappers, interlock, permissive, fail-safe state, fail loudly, blast radius containment, autonomy limits, operating envelope, prompt injection, training data poisoning, SSVC scoring, consequence-driven thresholds, automation complacency, manual control drills, shadow AI governance, operational enforceability, intercept points, zero trust **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes the boundary that holds whatever the AI system produces: limits and fallback states enforced outside the AI system itself, so that an action violating a defined constraint can be stopped before it reaches the process. It does not supply the human intervention that follows, which Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) supplies. **Accountable:** Contributes responsibility allocated before deployment, together with evidence that a decision to restrict or suspend an AI-enabled capability can be carried through into what the system actually does. It does not supply the record of what the system did, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) supplies. **Secure:** Contributes the scope of AI-specific exposure the organization is taking on, including AI capability that arrives outside procurement and is therefore absent from the risk profile. It does not supply the access controls that bound what an AI system may reach, which Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) supplies. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Safe:** The notifications, escalations and exception reports required here compete for the attention of the same operators, and an operator saturated with them is less able to act on the one that matters. Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) treats attention as a finite resource to be governed. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Valid:** Where the safe operating envelope is well characterized and stable, a boundary drawn around it costs little of the value the AI system was adopted for. Where the envelope shifts with the stage of the process, or is known only approximately, a boundary drawn conservatively also blocks the behavior that made the AI system worth adopting. One resolution is to derive the boundary from the process rather than from the AI system, and to revisit it as the process changes; others include narrowing the deployment to the range where the envelope is known, or accepting the reduced benefit as the price of the boundary.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 3.2** (on defining human-AI configuration):This Practice contributes the policies that differentiate what an AI system may decide for itself, what it may only recommend, and what a person should authorize before it acts; it does not define the oversight regimes that apply where an action happens faster than a person can respond. For those, see Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud). **MAP 4.2** (on establishing risk controls for AI system components):This Practice contributes the internal controls placed around an AI component, including deterministic guardrails, fail-safe states, compartmentalization and resource limits; it does not cover the controls a vendor should meet before a component is acquired. For those, see Task [6.1: Develop policies for AI supply chain requirements](#bookmark=kix.71o0na2rpzas). **MAP 5.1** (on identifying risk likelihood and impacts):This Practice contributes the identification of high-risk AI actions, the likelihood of each error, and its operational impact, reaching systems that are only adjacent to the AI system as well as those it acts on directly; it does not weigh those impacts against the benefits the system is meant to deliver. For the other side of that tradespace, see Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) and Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41). **GOVERN 2.1** (on establishing roles and responsibilities):This Practice contributes the accountable ownership, decision authority and escalation paths without which a governance decision about an AI capability cannot be enforced; it does not cover the roles exercised during an emergency intervention. For those, see Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf). **GOVERN 4.1** (on establishing a risk-aware culture):This Practice contributes practices that keep operators actively engaged with automated output, and that treat an unvetted AI tool as something to detect rather than tolerate; it does not deliver the training that sustains that mindset. For that, see Practice [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1).   **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST Special Publication 800-30 Revision 1, Guide for Conducting Risk Assessments](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-30r1.pdf), from NIST. Sets out the steps of a risk assessment, preparing, conducting, communicating and maintaining, at the organization, mission and system tiers. The method behind the risk determinations this Practice requires, written to give senior leaders what they need to choose a course of action. | **3.1: Identify risk tradeoffs** Document possible high-risk actions that the AI system may perform, including the generation of content or code, and their potential operational impacts. Maintain this documentation over time as the AI system, task, process conditions, and environment change.  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Accurate:** Contributes the error typology that gives an accuracy figure operational meaning: which kinds of error can occur at which decision point, from false positives and negatives through instruction misinterpretation to increased output latency. It does not measure how often each occurs, which Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) covers. **Safe:** Contributes the consequence side of the same map: how an error at each decision point reaches people, equipment or the environment, including the path where an engineer reviews AI-generated output before it becomes a deployed configuration. It does not define the boundary that keeps the AI system away from those consequences, which Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) defines. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accurate versus Safe:** Where one technical metric tracks the consequence closely, scoring on the metric and scoring on consequence rank the same risks first. Where the same error rate carries very different consequences at different decision points, a threshold set on the metric directs effort to the wrong place, which is why Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d) points thresholds toward consequence-driven schemes rather than vulnerability scores or model confidence. One resolution is to keep the metric as the trigger and the consequence class as the response; others include setting a separate threshold at each decision point, or treating the metric as evidence for likelihood alone. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 5.1** (on identifying risk likelihood and impacts):This Task identifies each decision point at which an AI error changes an operational outcome, and estimates the cost and likelihood of that error across normal, degraded and emergency conditions; it does not extend the analysis to systems affected only through shared adjacency or shared infrastructure. For those, see Task [3.5: Define and control risks to adjacent and related systems](#bookmark=kix.okyn4ifab2xw). **MAP 1.5** (on documenting risk tolerances):This Task sets consequence-driven threshold criteria that bound acceptable AI behavior, and ties each threshold to a defined organizational response; it does not identify who may accept a risk against those thresholds. For that, see Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz). **MAP 2.2** (on defining AI system utility and oversight needs):This Task documents how AI output reaches an operational decision, including the second-order path where an engineer reviews AI-generated configuration or code before it is deployed; it does not specify how an operator is told that the system has reached a limit. For that, see Task [3.3: Establish Failure Notification Requirements](#bookmark=kix.i2frfhwoohmg).  | **3.1.1: Identify AI system decision points and potential outcomes of AI system errors**  Determine decision points that may be affected by errors in AI-generated outputs (e.g. control action, predictions, recommendations, summaries, code, procedures, or system configurations) and how they can influence operations, including downstream effects.  In addition to unintentional errors, consider decision points where a capable adversary could deliberately manipulate AI-generated outputs to achieve a specific harmful outcome, distinct from the AI simply malfunctioning on its own. For cyber-physical and industrial systems, mapping should explicitly encompass decision points bridging enterprise IT and Operational Technology (OT) environments, establishing end-to-end visibility across the IT/OT boundary to detect telemetry spoofing, unauthorized command paths, or unverified configuration transfers. Error modes to evaluate include false positives/negatives, increased measurement bias, reduced precision, instruction misinterpretation, or increased output latency. Downstream operational effects to identify include:  An AI-based machine vision system for detecting the presence and extent of defects can affect the actions of both automated systems as well as humans, such as if the vision system overestimates or underestimates defects.  Conventional productivity tools, such as report summarizers, applied to summarizing technical condition reports, affect the decisions made by humans who rely on the summaries, particularly if they fail to recognize application-specific terminology (Implementation [2.4.1: Benchmarking on application-specific terminology](#bookmark=kix.jqdx2zh1yjpi)) and erroneously omit mentions of signs of imminent failure.  General-purpose AI assistants used to generate configuration files, automation scripts, or diagnostic guidance for OT/ICS systems can introduce the same type of error as an AI system directly connected to the control loop, even though the assistant itself has no OT connection. This second-order path, in which an engineer reviews assistant-generated output before it is deployed as a configuration or action, should be identified and assessed as a separate decision point, since the presence of human review does not by itself establish that the output was adequately verified. See Practice [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) for further discussion.  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST AI 200-1: AI Use Taxonomy: A Human-Centered Approach](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.200-1.pdf), from NIST. Its Table 1, Taxonomy of Human-AI Activities, page 4, lists the kinds of activity an AI system performs for a person, including content synthesis, detection, monitoring, prediction and decision making. A published counterpart to the list of output types above, and a fuller checklist when enumerating what a deployment actually does. **Guidance** [MITRE ATLAS, maintained by MITRE, a not-for-profit organization in the United States that operates federally funded research and development centers](https://atlas.mitre.org/). A matrix of tactics and techniques observed in attacks on AI-enabled systems, structured after the same organization's ATT\&CK matrix for enterprise systems. Supplies named techniques for the second half of the enumeration asked for here, the decision points at which an adversary shapes an output deliberately rather than the system erring on its own. **Commentary** [Dragos 2026 OT Cybersecurity Year in Review](https://www.dragos.com/ot-cybersecurity-year-in-review), from Dragos, a commercial industrial control system cybersecurity company in the United States, drawing on its own threat intelligence and field engagements. Estimates that fewer than 10 percent of OT networks worldwide have network visibility and monitoring in place, and reports poor segmentation between IT and OT networks as the most common architectural weakness in its 2025 service reports, present in 81 percent of them. Evidence for the condition under which the IT/OT boundary decision points named here go unobserved. |
|  |  | **3.1.2: Evaluate misclassification tradeoffs**  Assess or estimate the operational cost (financial and otherwise) and likelihoods of errors identified in Implementation [3.1.1: Identify AI system decision points and potential outcomes of AI system errors](#bookmark=kix.p5bk4pidwdza), across normal, degraded, and emergency operating conditions. Examples include production outages and reduced safety margins.  |
|  |  | **3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses**  Establish threshold criteria that bound acceptable AI behavior in terms of impact severity, likelihood, and recovery feasibility.  When establishing risk thresholds, avoid over-indexing on purely technical, point-in-time metrics, whether traditional vulnerability scoring systems (such as the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS)) or raw algorithmic performance benchmarks (such as model confidence scores, error rates, or statistical accuracy). Instead, utilize consequence-driven frameworks, drawing on the operational prioritization logic of models like Stakeholder-Specific Vulnerability Categorization (SSVC), to evaluate risks based on real-world impacts to public health, safety, and mission continuity. Associate these risk thresholds with a defined organizational response such as enforced action, system isolation, authority throttling, or operator escalation. Review these risk thresholds and control responses as the AI system, task, and operational environment change.  Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz) discusses how to determine who holds authority to accept a risk against these thresholds, and to restrict or suspend operation when they are exceeded. **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST Special Publication 800-30 Revision 1, Guide for Conducting Risk Assessments](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-30r1.pdf), from NIST. Sections 3.1 to 3.3 set out preparing for, conducting and communicating a risk assessment, with the conducting step broken into numbered tasks. Appendix G gives assessment scales for the likelihood of a threat event occurring and of its resulting in adverse impacts, Appendix H scales and a template for impact, and Appendix I scales for risk determination. These are the published scales behind the impact severity and likelihood limbs of a threshold set here, applied once per decision point. |
|  |  | **3.1.4: Artifact-driven impact analysis** Conduct an assessment of how an error in AI-generated content, code, procedures, or configurations could lead to cascading failures in physical infrastructure. |
|  | **3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs** Enclose AI systems within hard-coded, deterministic guardrails (or boundary logic) that do not depend on the AI system and detect if safety constraints are likely to be violated and take appropriate actions to maintain functional safety.  Such actions may include limiting the AI system’s output, engaging fallback controllers and plans, notifying human operators, and logging.  Independent, deterministic safety wrappers add a critical layer of defense alongside emergency software patching cycles during active exploits. By enforcing hard-coded constraints at the operational boundary, these wrappers can block, at machine speed, unsafe commands or invalid data transactions that violate defined constraints. This extends the zero trust principle of verifying at each boundary from access requests to the outputs of the AI system itself. Guardrail rigor should scale with how directly a system's output reaches a physical process, not with how the system is labeled or procured. A system that directly optimizes, controls, or influences physical equipment, such as an AI adjusting valve pressure or grid dispatch, should default to the full deterministic, independent guardrail treatment described above.  A general-purpose AI assistant used to summarize records, draft reports, or generate configuration files, automation code, or operational procedures may warrant lighter controls, such as content review, while its output stays purely informational. But because these assistants are often procured under standard IT or SaaS contracts, the path from assistant output to engineer review to deployed configuration can be missed by hazard review processes built around direct OT connections. Once that output is used to inform an operational decision or generate a deployed configuration or control artifact, full guardrail rigor should apply.  Guardrail design should also account for whether the underlying process is continuous or batch-based: continuous processes, such as power generation, can often define a single fixed safe operating envelope, while batch processes, such as many chemical operations, may require different operating boundaries at each stage of the batch cycle, making a static guardrail definition insufficient. Align these measures with Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) and in particular Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) to integrate appropriate, timely, and feasible human intervention, and with Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) to ensure visibility into the AI system’s attempted behavior, even when guardrails prevent actual harm.  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes enforcement that does not depend on the AI system being correct: deterministic limits applied at the process boundary to every command source, so an unsafe command is stopped whatever produced it. It bounds the hazards that were identified and written as constraints, and it stays a mitigation rather than a closure. For the hazards not yet identified, see Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93) and Implementation [3.8.3: Dynamic Risk Surface Coupling Analysis](#bookmark=id.soh3bu4jynwh). **Resilient:** Contributes the defined state the control system enters when a constraint is violated or the AI system becomes unavailable: holding last-known-safe setpoints, reverting to a conservative baseline, or de-escalating to a legacy deterministic controller, with the state-consistency rules that keep re-entry from producing an uncommanded action. It does not cover sustained operation in that state, which Task [11.2: Define manual and alternative operating procedures for long-term mission continuity](#bookmark=kix.27rg9c9cadm7) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Reliable:** The enforcement layer is a component in its own right, and a wrapper that has stopped enforcing looks much like one with nothing to stop. Implementation [3.4.3: Monitor validation control integrity](#bookmark=kix.h6w8xayde4wp) requires continuous monitoring of validation control states. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Robust:** Where the process holds one stable safe operating envelope, as many continuous processes do, a fixed deterministic boundary costs little of the AI system's ability to handle varied conditions. Where the envelope changes at each stage of a batch, or an experienced engineer is working through a situation the boundary never anticipated, a static constraint also blocks the response the situation calls for. One resolution is the explicit, authorized override under emergency safety protocols that Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla) provides; others include defining the constraint separately for each stage of the cycle, or deriving it from the current process state rather than fixing it at design time. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 4.2** (on establishing risk controls for AI system components):This Task specifies the control itself: a deterministic constraint enforced out of band at an identified intercept point, applied to every automated command source at the physical process boundary; it does not confirm that the control is still functioning once deployed. For that, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk). **MEASURE 2.6** (on measuring safety):This Task defines the fail-safe states the control system should enter when a constraint is violated or the AI system becomes unavailable, which is the behavior a safety evaluation is run against; it does not perform that evaluation. For that, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5). **MAP 3.5** (on establishing human oversight processes):This Task requires that where one AI system verifies another's output, the human reviewer holds authority to reject the artifact and capacity to examine it, because the presence of a reviewer is not by itself verification; it does not cover validation of the artifact against specification and institutional knowledge. For that, see Practice [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul).  | **3.2.1: Identify AI output intercept points** For AI decision points deemed to be significant (defined in Implementation [3.1.1: Identify AI system decision points and potential outcomes of AI system errors](#bookmark=kix.p5bk4pidwdza)), identify operational intercept points where monitoring and controls can be enforced across one or more locations in the system architecture.  These intercept points may indicate where a "guarded proxy" architecture is advisable (see Implementation [5.3.3: Separate privilege via encapsulated downstream agents and tools](#bookmark=id.p1isj88exw8y)): remove direct asset access from the AI agent or system and route execution requests through an encapsulated downstream tool equipped with independent deterministic constraints (Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla)).  For example, an AI flow-rate optimizer should not hold direct control-bus credentials; it should submit recommendations to a dedicated setpoint broker that independently verifies range, rate-of-change, and safety interlock rules before writing changes to physical hardware. In this scenario, intercept points exist at multiple operational layers, including the setpoint interface, the local controller, and existing emergency shutoff mechanisms. |
|  |  | **3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints** Define the boundary that the AI system should remain within for safe operation. These might be in- and out-of-range values, semantic and behavioral constraints, rate-of-change violations, and deterministic logical constraints that should be respected regardless of the AI system’s confidence level.  These deterministic constraints should be defined and enforced independent of the AI system's internal logic (e.g., via out-of-band safety wrappers, intermediate deterministic command validation proxies, or hardware interlocks at the physical process boundary). This out-of-band architecture helps ensure constraint enforcement remains active even during model failures, prompt injections, or autonomous agents exceeding their intended constraints. These constraints should reflect the most restrictive combination of the organization’s, equipment’s, application’s, and the AI system's safe operating limits (including the limits of the AI system’s training). Constraints should apply uniformly to all command sources at the physical process boundary, including AI recommendations, automated agents, remote operator sessions, and any other upstream system. Authentication and physical safety are separate concerns: confirming who or what sent a command (addressed in Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck)) does not guarantee that the command itself is safe to execute. An authenticated source can still issue a damaging command through error, misconfiguration, or compromise. Absent an explicit, authorized override, such safety wrappers should intercept physically unsafe commands regardless of their source or underlying cause.  Where such constraints need to be lifted, e.g., to facilitate an experienced human engineer remotely dealing with a novel situation, such constraints should be explicitly overridden in accordance with emergency safety protocols. The same principle of independently-enforced constraints applies beyond physical process control: for AI systems that generate or filter information rather than direct commands, the "constraint" may be one of required completeness or fidelity, independently enforced regardless of the AI system's own judgment about what is relevant. For example: An attacker gaining access to a plant human-machine interface (HMI) could issue setpoint commands indistinguishable from legitimate AI recommendations.  A valve controlled by an AI system might have constraints that the AI system may not have properly learned, such as not being opened and closed in quick succession, and not being opened at the same time as another valve.  A condition report summarization tool should be constrained to include information about every asset, even if the AI system judges a given condition not to be of concern. An AI system routing high-volume transactions or processing patient records should be bounded by deterministic validation rules that block anomalous account or resource allocations (e.g., assigning an atomic resource to two entities), prevent unauthorized data aggregation, or flag out-of-bounds risk parameters prior to downstream operational execution. An AI system executing automated financial trades or market transactions should be bounded by pre-trade risk controls, such as hard credit limits, maximum order value thresholds, and price collars (aligned with 17 CFR 240.15c3-5), that deterministically block out-of-bounds orders before market entry, regardless of the model's confidence score. An AI system's pressure setpoint recommendation should be restricted to a 10 to 30 PSI range before it reaches the physical system, by hard constraints to not exceed these limits regardless of its recommendation, with a safety layer that is not under AI control. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [Principles for the Secure Integration of Artificial Intelligence in Operational Technology](https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/joint-guidance-principles-for-the-secure-integration-of-artificial-intelligence-in-operational-technology-508c.pdf), December 2025, joint guidance from nine national cybersecurity agencies across seven countries, led by the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. Section 4.1 directs establishing safe operating bounds for OT devices and maintaining a known good state, or thresholds for safe behavior, against which departure can be recognized. Section 4.2 covers failsafe mechanisms that let an AI system fail without disrupting critical operations, including how to bypass or replace it. Section 2.4 lists challenges in AI-OT integration, among them helping ensure that failsafe mechanisms revert to traditional automation or to manual operation. **Standard** ISA/IEC 62443-3-3, SR 3.5 (Input Validation), SR 3.6 (Deterministic Output), and SR 3.4 (Software and Information Integrity), from the International Society of Automation and the International Electrotechnical Commission. The industrial control system requirements nearest to the input bounds, predictable output and integrity checks this Implementation places outside the AI system. **Guidance** [OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-genai-llm-top-10-2026/), version 1.0, 2026, from the OWASP GenAI Security Project, a volunteer community project of the Open Worldwide Application Security Project. LLM01:2026, Prompt Injection, covers the input boundary and asks that every model response be validated against a strict output schema in trusted application code before any downstream system acts on it, using structural validation rather than a second model call, and that credentials and state-change capability be held in application code with privileged calls routed through a deterministic policy engine that revalidates intent and arguments at execution time. LLM10:2026, Improper Output Handling, covers the output boundary, and LLM03:2026, Excessive Agency, covers the consequences of model output reaching privileged actions. **Regulation** [17 CFR 240.15c3-5, Risk management controls for brokers or dealers with market access](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/chapter-II/part-240/subject-group-ECFRc8401dcba174f73/section-240.15c3-5), a rule of the United States Securities and Exchange Commission codified in Title 17 of the Code of Federal Regulations. Paragraph (c)(1)(i) requires controls that reject orders exceeding pre-set credit or capital thresholds, and paragraph (c)(1)(ii) controls that reject orders exceeding price or size parameters. Paragraph (d) places those controls under the direct and exclusive control of the broker or dealer, which is the regulated form of the separation this Implementation asks for between the system that produces an action and the layer that permits it. |
|  |  | **3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior** Define deterministic states the control system should enter when AI output violates the constraints defined in Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla), when an AI action is refused under Implementation [5.2.2: Action-level, context-aware authorization policies](#bookmark=kix.68ohugbvqcsm), or when the AI system becomes unavailable or degraded.  These fail-safe states should incorporate clear protocols for tracking algorithmic state consistency during failover and re-commissioning cycles. Recovery logic should explicitly dictate whether an overridden AI agent resumes operations by synchronizing with its last active context window, resetting to a validated baseline template state, or gracefully de-escalating control to a legacy deterministic controller, preventing sudden uncommanded physical actions from an un-synchronized model state. For example: If an AI system commands a valve to be opened while another incompatible valve is also open, the system might fall back onto a less optimal, but known-safe non-AI controller to determine, for the current plant state, which valve has precedence.  If a device loses communication with its AI-based controller, it might default to a pre-defined safe state and broadcast an alert, rather than executing a possibly corrupted last AI-issued command. Note that a 'fail-safe state' does not necessarily mean simply de-energizing or shutting down a system, which can introduce secondary hazards. Fail-safe logic should define deterministic, application-appropriate states, such as holding last-known-safe setpoints, reverting to hard-coded conservative baselines, or reverting control to legacy non-AI controllers. Where an AI action is refused part-way through a sequence, the fail-safe state should account for the steps already executed, since stopping at the refused step can leave the process in a state nobody designed for. For example, if an AI agent changing over pumps is refused authorization to start the standby pump after it has already stopped the duty pump, the fallback might restart the duty pump or hand the changeover to a deterministic controller, rather than leaving both pumps stopped. This Implementation should be aligned with those of Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) so that immediate-term automated fail-safe and fallback policies are visible to, and align with, subsequent human intervention.  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Section 6.2.4.5, Response and Recovery Plans, pages 114 to 116, covers preparing and testing the plans a disrupted OT system is restored under. Appendix F.7.6, control CP-10, System Recovery and Reconstitution, page 251, addresses whether state variables should be returned to initial values or to the values held before the disruption, which is the choice this Implementation asks recovery logic to make explicit. Appendix F.7.17, control SC-24, Fail in Known State, page 288, covers selecting a failure state that keeps OT state variables consistent with the physical state they represent, and notes that a de-energized state can itself disturb the process. |
|  |  | **3.2.4: Define further logging and communication actions** Specify logging and communication actions that the organization should take when AI outputs violate constraints. Actions can include notifying necessary operators and systems, and enabling enhanced logging to provide further details.  For example, when a validation control rejects an AI-generated shutdown command on a generation unit, the plant control room, operators and any connected systems should be notified, and the incident logged along with associated information that may be necessary to update the AI system. Alarms tracking such failures should be throttled or rate-limited rather than flooding operator consoles and logs with repetitive rejection events. For example, multiple identical failures could be consolidated into a single notification. See Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) for further details.  |
|  |  | **3.2.5: Artifact verification** AI-generated content, such as code, configuration, or procedures, with a high potential for impact (see Implementation [3.1.4: Artifact-driven impact analysis](#bookmark=kix.ve7i6e7xkt06)) should be checked via independent means. This should ideally involve non-AI or deterministic techniques.  Where it is not possible to avoid the situation of AI systems checking AI outputs, significant care should be taken to ensure that any AI system used for verification doesn’t have correlated failure modes to the AI system used for generation (e.g., it doesn’t use the same model or training data), and that a human-in-the-loop oversight mode applies to the verification step, with the reviewer holding the authority to reject the artifact and sufficient oversight capacity to examine it. The presence of a reviewer is not by itself verification.  See Practice [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) for further discussion.  |
|  | **3.3: Establish Failure Notification Requirements** Define the operational conditions under which an AI system should overtly assert a state of exception ("fail loudly") versus when it may attempt autonomous error recovery, clearly distinguishing notification alerts from physical fail-safe states. This task helps ensure that for high-consequence applications, operators maintain sufficient situational awareness to make timely, informed intervention decisions and are protected against undetected operational drift caused by "quiet" AI failures. This is particularly important in multi-system and agentic workflows, where failures in one agent may be unintentionally masked by the resilience of other agents, silently reducing safety margins, degrading performance, or causing preventable damage.  The need to “fail loudly” should be considered carefully against the limited oversight capacity of humans (or AI systems) triaging the resulting alarms, to avoid “alarm flood” situations, where a large number of “high priority” alarms reduce the ability to address the underlying problem, especially when unusual failures happen as discussed in Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y).  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Transparent:** Contributes the requirement that a departure announces itself at the time: which conditions oblige the AI system to assert an exception, and the thresholds at which degradation becomes a reportable event rather than a later finding. It does not capture what the AI system did in the ordinary case, which Task [10.1: Define decision-level logging and metadata retention policies](#bookmark=kix.7y5rv6pa7wxa) covers. **Valid:** Contributes a quantitative limit on how far the operating environment, inputs, task and context may move from the conditions the AI system was established for before a mitigation is required, reviewed at a defined interval and on significant environmental change. It does not establish fit in the first place, which Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Safe:** Each condition that obliges the AI system to speak adds to what the same operators have to read, and this Task's own text notes that a large number of high-priority alarms reduces the ability to address the underlying problem. Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) governs notification volume as a question of capacity. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Resilient versus Transparent:** Where autonomous recovery restores an administrative function without touching a physical process or a safety margin, a retry that goes unannounced costs little. Where the same recovery masks a degradation that is consuming a safety margin, or one agent's resilience absorbs another agent's failure, the recovery removes the signal that anything moved. One resolution is the per-application assessment in Implementation [3.3.1: Context-Specific Failure Modes](#bookmark=kix.eu4o4y1aacbo), which sets the notification requirement from the consequence rather than from recovery being available; others include announcing the recovery at a lower priority, or counting it against a drift threshold so that repetition becomes visible. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  **MANAGE 4.3** (on incident response):This Task defines when an AI system should assert a failure overtly rather than recover quietly, and the pre-authorized pathway by which each class of output reaches the person who should act on it; it does not address what happens when notification volume exceeds what operators can triage. For that, see Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y). **MEASURE 2.5** (on measuring validity and reliability):This Task converts the documented limits of a model's generalizability into quantitative drift thresholds, each with a defined response when the operating environment moves beyond it; it does not establish those limits. For the testing that establishes them, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5). **GOVERN 4.3** (on enabling AI testing, incident identification, and information sharing):This Task makes an AI failure identifiable at the moment it occurs, by treating silent recovery as a choice to be justified for each application rather than as the default; it does not cover the analysis that follows once a failure has been identified. For that, see Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb). **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Appendix F.7.18 tailors the System and Information Integrity controls to OT, for cybersecurity failures. SI-17, Fail-Safe Procedures, is added to all three baselines so that an organization holds a written record of which failures draw which response, and its OT discussion holds that the same failure event may warrant different responses at different impact levels, and that a fail-safe state should account for impacts on human safety, physical systems and the environment. SI-13, Predictable Failure Prevention, and SI-6, Security and Privacy Function Verification, each record that restarting or shutting down on the identification of an anomaly may not be acceptable in OT for operational reasons, which is what makes quiet recovery a decision rather than a default.  **Standard** ANSI/ISA-62443-3-3, from the American National Standards Institute and the International Society of Automation. Sets system-level security requirements and security levels for industrial automation and control systems. **Standard** ISA-18.2, Management of Alarm Systems for the Process Industries, from the International Society of Automation. Covers exception reporting and alarm prioritization, which is the established practice a requirement to fail loudly has to sit inside if the resulting alarms are to stay actionable.  **\>To the Reviewer:**  \>This concept is one that is familiar to the OT community and, for that matter, the electronic and mechanical engineering disciplines. For example, in many cases the purpose of an electrical or mechanical fuse is to cause a piece of equipment to “fail loudly” even if it could have kept going for a little bit longer (but then may have caused more damage when it finally failed).  \>What would be examples of sector-specific guidance/regulations relating to the concept of “failing loudly” that would be applicable to AI (both within OT/ICS and otherwise)?  | **3.3.1: Context-Specific Failure Modes** Conduct an engineering assessment for each application to determine its failure notification requirements.  For example, silent resilience (e.g., automated retries or soft failovers) may be appropriate for non-critical administrative tasks where internal algorithmic recovery does not alter physical processes or mask safety margins.  Conversely, for safety-critical IT/OT/ICS applications, a 'fail loudly' protocol should be enforced with explicit human notifications, even if the system continues to operate, perhaps in a degraded capacity.  These notification states should be programmatically linked to the independent safety wrappers in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) to ensure that an unacknowledged 'loud' failure automatically forces a transition to a deterministic fallback state (per Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p)). To prevent alarm floods and alert fatigue, utilize containment techniques such as time-delay hysteresis, dynamic deadbands (control and sensor ranges where minor fluctuations are ignored and assumed to be noise), and state-based alarm aggregation to compress rapid automated iterations into a single operational event change. See Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) for further details.  |
|  |  | **3.3.2: Establish Maximum Drift Thresholds** Establish quantitative monitoring and thresholds against which the AI system is continuously or periodically evaluated for how far the real-world operating environment, data inputs, task, and system context can shift from the historic conditions on which the system was trained before mitigations are put in place, defining the specific thresholds that determine when the periodic evaluations in Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i) or the continuous monitoring in Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk) should trigger a fail-safe response.  Determine if exceeding a maximum drift threshold should result in notifications, forced fail-over, or other restrictions on the operation of the system.  Minimum responses should include operator notification. For safety-critical applications, responses should include automatic restriction of AI authority or forced failover to a deterministic backup system.  Drift thresholds should account for seasonal, operational, and environmental variation relevant to the CI sector, in addition to the physical degradation and changes over time of underlying OT hardware.  For example, an AI model trained on summer demand patterns in a water distribution system should have explicit thresholds that detect and flag degraded performance during winter conditions before that degradation can cause operators to miss a safety-relevant alert. Similarly, a healthcare diagnostic AI should flag clinical drift when shifted between different patient demographics, and a financial liquidity model should trigger boundaries during sudden, non-historical macroeconomic shocks. Drift thresholds should be reviewed and recalibrated at a defined interval or whenever a significant change in the operational environment occurs. This implementation mitigates hazard risk associated with silent AI performance degradation. An AI model trained on historical operational data can progressively lose accuracy as the operational environment shifts, without generating any visible failure or alert. Operators could continue to rely on the AI's outputs without realizing its reliability has degraded below acceptable thresholds.  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [Principles for the Secure Integration of Artificial Intelligence in Operational Technology](https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/joint-guidance-principles-for-the-secure-integration-of-artificial-intelligence-in-operational-technology-508c.pdf), December 2025, joint guidance from nine national cybersecurity agencies across seven countries, led by the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. Principle 4, Embed Oversight and Failsafe Practices Into AI and AI-Enabled OT Systems, pages 18 to 20\. Section 4.1 directs establishing safe operating bounds for OT devices that detect AI drift and model changes affecting safety and performance, and maintaining a known good state or thresholds for safe behavior. Section 4.2 covers the failsafe mechanisms a breached threshold can hand control to. |
|  |  | **3.3.3: Actionable Operator Decision & Escalation Pathways** Establish clear, pre-authorized decision pathways that dictate how personnel respond to AI outputs across normal, degraded, and emergency operating conditions.  Organizations should operationalize these pathways into simplified quick-reference guides, HSI prompts, and standard operating procedures. Examples to consider include:  **Trust & Execute:** Routine, high-confidence recommendations within validated baseline operating envelopes (as identified in Task [1.1: Identify current requirements](#bookmark=kix.uqvdk8m0gq1)) executed without further review and notified for visibility.  **Spot-Check & Investigate:** Recommendations with moderate confidence scores or novel input parameters (e.g., an unusual maintenance condition, borderline prior-authorization claim, or atypical network routing request), requiring a brief manual sanity check before execution.   **Escalate to Oversight:** Persistent, non-critical model anomalies or recurring "soft" failures that do not threaten immediate safety or service continuity but indicate algorithmic drift or data schema shifts (as discussed in Implementation [3.3.2: Establish Maximum Drift Thresholds](#bookmark=kix.8m48t6fuoxqu)).  **Immediate Emergency Override:** Any AI output that intersects with deterministic safety/policy wrappers (as discussed in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)), violates hard physical or financial limits, or occurs during an unannounced multi-system outage. |
|  | **3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior** Define policies and procedures for detecting acute, undesired, unexpected, and unusual AI system behavior, as continuous, real-time monitoring that complements the scheduled batch evaluations in Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i) and operates against the thresholds defined in Implementation [3.3.2: Establish Maximum Drift Thresholds](#bookmark=kix.8m48t6fuoxqu).  This includes violations and near violations of constraints defined in Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla) as well as other metrics such as unusual changes in performance or output, even if they remain within safe constraints. Detection of regular anomalous behavior should also feed into the estimation of drift velocity in Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i).  This includes distinguishing, where feasible, between anomalies likely caused by incidental drift or error and those consistent with deliberate interference. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Reliable:** Contributes the continuous check that the AI system still performs as it did when it was accepted, covering near-violations and changes in output that stay inside the safe constraints, so degradation arising between scheduled evaluations is caught. It does not set the limits those observations are judged against, which Implementation [3.3.2: Establish Maximum Drift Thresholds](#bookmark=kix.8m48t6fuoxqu) sets. **Secure:** Contributes the runtime separation of an anomaly consistent with drift or error from one consistent with deliberate interference, together with confirmation that the enforcement layer is still functioning. It does not monitor access boundaries or privilege escalation by AI entities, which Task [5.4: Implement continuous security monitoring for AI entity actions](#bookmark=kix.wzb03yleoteg) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Privacy-Enhanced:** Observing output patterns means retaining outputs, and for an AI system working on records or documents those outputs carry whatever the source carried. Implementation [10.1.3: Forensic Integrity and Privacy-Preserving Log Partitioning](#bookmark=id.vziiyw6bsa9k) sets the masking and partitioning arrangements for telemetry that holds sensitive material. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Secure versus Reliable:** Where the cause of an anomaly is clear, the response follows from it. Where one observation could be drift or could be interference, the reliability response of recalibrating or widening the baseline and the security response of preserving state and investigating point in opposite directions, and handling manipulation as drift adapts the AI system toward the manipulated distribution. One resolution is to hold the baseline and escalate under Implementation [3.4.4: Define runtime anomaly escalation procedures](#bookmark=kix.ro8m94lv3otg) until the cause is established; others include preserving the evidence while continuing to operate under tightened constraints. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MEASURE 2.4** (on monitoring deployed AI systems):This Task defines the observable behaviors, operational baselines and deviation ranges by which a deployed AI system is watched continuously, including whether its validation controls are still functioning; it does not cover the scheduled evaluation that runs alongside that monitoring. For that, see Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i). **MEASURE 3.1** (on tracking known and emerging risks):This Task tracks emergent behavior against an operational baseline, and distinguishes, where feasible, anomalies caused by drift or error from those consistent with deliberate interference; it does not track risks that leave no observable trace in the system's own behavior. **MANAGE 2.3** (on responding to emerging risks):This Task defines the escalation procedure that a sustained or recurring anomaly should trigger where it matches no previously characterized failure mode; it does not cover the intervention and recovery that follow. For those, see Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf).  | **3.4.1: Define observability requirements** Identify observable AI system behaviors and metrics, including output patterns, decision frequencies, and validation control states. |
|  |  | **3.4.2: Establish operational baselines and deviation thresholds** Define what normal AI system behavior looks like under authorized operating conditions, and establish the deviation ranges to determine abnormal behavior. |
|  |  | **3.4.3: Monitor validation control integrity** Establish continuous monitoring of validation control states (defined in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)) to validate that mechanisms are actively and correctly functioning. |
|  |  | **3.4.4: Define runtime anomaly escalation procedures** Extend communications actions defined under Implementation [3.2.4: Define further logging and communication actions](#bookmark=kix.ewd7mi5bakey) to cover sustained or recurring behavioral anomalies of AI systems. |
|  | **3.5: Define and control risks to adjacent and related systems**  Define and control risks to other systems that may be affected by a failure in this system, including:  Systems that are intentionally interdependent with this system, where errors travel along functional, data, or operational dependencies (“cascading failures”). Systems that may be unrelated except for shared adjacency in a physical, logical, network, resource, supply chain, or other sense (“splash damage” or “blast radius”).   **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes containment of consequence beyond the AI system's own function: execution confined to isolated segments, and limits on shared resources such as compute, bandwidth, power and exclusive sensor access, so a failure reaches a bounded set of assets. It does not bound what the AI system is permitted to reach in the first place, which Task [5.3: Implement principles of least agency and least privilege for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.qgwy2q3nxzcn) covers. **Resilient:** Contributes the common-cause analysis that redundancy rests on: whether systems and safeguards that are functionally independent share a facility, a power or cooling supply, a network path, a hypervisor or a municipal substrate. It does not establish the redundancy itself, which Task [2.2: Establish requirements for redundancy in AI-Driven functions](#bookmark=kix.rbl0o5b5wwkj) covers. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Reliable:** Where an AI system's demand for a shared resource is steady, a hard limit protects the systems alongside it while the AI-enabled function continues to perform as required. Where demand rises during the same upset that raises demand elsewhere, the limit removes capacity from the function at the point it is most needed. One resolution is the graded arrangement in Implementation [3.5.4: Define and implement resource limiting](#bookmark=kix.7eknkkudt237), where a soft limit and a notification precede the hard limit; others include reserving capacity for the highest-consequence function, or defining the state the function enters when it is throttled.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  **MAP 5.1** (on identifying risk likelihood and impacts):This Task identifies impacts on systems that share physical space, network paths, logical substrates or scarce resources with the AI system, and on systems downstream of it; it does not cover impacts at the AI system's own decision points. For those, see Task [3.1: Identify risk tradeoffs](#bookmark=kix.gj0yiaenfuib). **MAP 3.3** (on documenting application scope of the AI system):This Task bounds the scope an AI system can reach, by confining its execution to isolated segments and capping the shared resources it may consume; it does not define the intended scope from which those bounds should be derived. For that, see Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k). **GOVERN 4.2** (on documenting impact):This Task requires that impacts estimated beyond the facility perimeter be recorded as bounded engineering assumptions where telemetry is unavailable, so that the limits of the assessment stay visible to a later reader; it does not cover communicating those impacts to the operators of the affected systems.  | **3.5.1: Map direct functional and algorithmic dependencies** Trace and document downstream logical, algorithmic, and operational paths of AI outputs to identify how errors, whether in control outputs, direct executable code, or human-mediated summaries, could trigger a chain reaction through connected assets.  These dependencies often extend beyond traditional IT/OT/ICS boundaries; for example, an AI agent defending an IT network might erroneously disable authentication traffic, physically locking personnel out of a facility.  To fully capture these risks, organizations should conduct a high-value asset (HVA) assessment that extends outward to map functional dependencies on upstream suppliers and downstream customers, helping ensure automated errors do not propagate along the extended supply chain. |
|  |  | **3.5.2: Identify indirect shared infrastructure and collateral damage exposure** Audit the operational environment to identify functionally independent systems that are vulnerable to collateral damage from an AI failure. Evaluation criteria should include cross-cutting environmental and infrastructure commonalities, such as: Physical Proximity: Independent physical assets co-located in the same facility or enclosure, or that share the same power, cooling, or other physical resources. Network Topography: Systems sharing the same network switches, backbones, or uplink paths. Logical Substrates: Unrelated applications sharing the same hypervisor, underlying cloud tenant, base operating system image, or database instance. This mapping should also consider whether a single event or action could affect multiple identified safeguards at once, rather than assuming redundant controls fail independently. This asset assessment should, where possible, reach outward past the facility's immediate perimeter to evaluate shared municipal substrates, such as the regional electrical grid, water networks, or telecommunication backbones, where a localized AI failure or resource-saturation event could cause regional collateral damage.  Where direct technical visibility or telemetry past the facility's perimeter is unavailable, organizations should utilize qualified engineering approximations and best-available sector intelligence to estimate these external impacts. These estimations should be explicitly documented as bounded technical assumptions to ensure transparency regarding data limitations, preventing the assessment from stalling due to incomplete external information. The task of mapping internal dependencies is common to Implementation [7.6.1: Internal Dependency Mapping (Orphan System Prevention)](#bookmark=kix.xxh12qj5nq5v). Maintaining this map can reduce the effort needed when this system is later decommissioned. This mapping is also the basis for identifying what depends on an AI system at retirement, under Implementation [13.3.3: Determine and record what happens to everything that depended on the system](#bookmark=id.gknfd9voaeez). |
|  |  | **3.5.3: Impact compartmentalization (sandboxing)** Restrict AI agent execution to isolated micro-segments of the network or other infrastructure. For example, an AI optimizing valve pressure should not be able to affect core safety instrumented systems (SIS).  |
|  |  | **3.5.4: Define and implement resource limiting** Identify limited resources that the AI agent or system shares with other systems (AI or otherwise), such as compute, tokens, exclusive hardware/sensor access, power, network bandwidth, and cooling. Define and implement appropriate soft and hard limits on the AI system’s use of these resources, along with appropriate notifications for when these limits are approached or exceeded.  |
|  | **3.6: Mitigate automation complacency and skills degradation** Establish continuous readiness and training protocols that keep critical human capabilities exercised. Reliance on an AI system is often the intended result of adopting one, be it to make new aspects of the task possible or perform existing tasks with more efficiency. Where it takes over work people did before, it reduces the occasions on which those capabilities are used (see Bainbridge 1983).  Practice to sustain these capabilities has to be arranged rather than left to routine operation. This task requires organizations to identify critical human capabilities that should be preserved, helping ensure that operators retain the technical expertise, cognitive situational awareness, and manual or legacy override proficiency necessary to manage operations during prolonged AI outages or catastrophic failures. Note that this includes full manual overrides (where system velocity makes this possible, see Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud)), the ability to monitor and operate deterministic fallback or legacy systems (Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)), and decision-making processes necessary to perform appropriately risk-managed unplanned shutdowns (Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f)).  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Resilient:** Contributes periodic exercise of the fallback path under real load: drills in which deterministic controllers, analog backups and safety interlocks take over the operation, which is what separates a documented fallback from a demonstrated one. It does not cover sustained operation once the fallback is carrying the process, which Task [11.2: Define manual and alternative operating procedures for long-term mission continuity](#bookmark=kix.27rg9c9cadm7) covers. **Bias Managed:** Contributes a bound on one human-cognitive error source, automation bias, by placing active verification at high-consequence boundary thresholds so that agreement with an AI output is an act rather than a default. It does not address the notification rate that produces desensitization, which Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Reliable:** Simulating a failure of the AI system is itself an operational action, and a drill placed in a window judged low-risk can still meet a real disturbance. Implementation [3.6.2: Scheduled "Manual Control" Drills](#bookmark=kix.qmn0t8peb3km) directs drills that cannot be run safely on the live system to non-production environments, which Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj) categorizes. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Safe:** Where verification steps are reserved for high-consequence boundary thresholds, operator engagement is spent where the consequence is greatest. Where they are applied widely, the same steps become routine, and a check performed routinely returns agreement without examination while still consuming capacity needed elsewhere. One resolution is the restriction to high-consequence thresholds in Implementation [3.6.3: Cognitive Engagement Monitoring](#bookmark=kix.gxi0ft2m0kuf), deployed with the calm technology and mixed-initiative choices in Implementation [4.4.4: Applying consolidation and low-cognitive-load design techniques](#bookmark=id.923fjnayomc5); others include varying when the check falls so that it is not anticipated, or moving the exercise into scheduled drills. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 3.4** (on defining operator proficiency):This Task identifies the manual and interpretive skills that day-to-day AI reliance erodes, and requires periodic audited verification that operators can still run the infrastructure without AI assistance; it does not deliver the instruction that builds those skills. For that, see Task [9.2: Deliver specialized training on human-AI interaction and automation bias](#bookmark=kix.wdmhy3mmlldl). **GOVERN 4.1** (on establishing a risk-aware culture):This Task designs the operator interface against passive monitoring, placing active verification steps at high-consequence boundaries so that trust in an AI output stays deliberate; it does not address the alert fatigue that too many such checkpoints would cause. For that, see Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y). **References:**  **Research** [Bainbridge, L., *Ironies of Automation*, *Automatica* volume 19 number 6, 1983, pages 775 to 779](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/0005109883900468). *Automatica* is the journal of the International Federation of Automatic Control, an international body whose members are national control engineering organizations. A seminal paper on the human factors of automated process control. Its subject is the process-control computers of 1983, and the mechanisms it identifies are relevant to AI systems. Section 1.1.1 sets out why manual skills decay when unused, so that "a formerly experienced operator who has been monitoring an automated process may now be an inexperienced one", and section 2.3 discusses what maintaining them requires, including hands-on control periods and simulator practice. | **3.6.1: Baseline Human Competency Retention**  Identify and document the core operational skills (e.g., manual loop tuning, raw data interpretation, manual dispatch) that risk being eroded by day-to-day AI reliance.  Incorporate periodic, audited training or, commensurate with available operational resources, simulator-based testing to verify that human operators can safely run the infrastructure without AI assistance. |
|  |  | **3.6.2: Scheduled "Manual Control" Drills** Where appropriate, establish a routine cadence of scheduled operational drills analogous to facility "live-load generator exercising" or "blackout drills" in OT, paper-based charting and manual triage cycles in healthcare, or offline transaction reconciliation procedures in finance. During designated low-risk or off-peak windows, simulate a failure of the AI system. The failure could include a loss of availability or a combination of other failures as identified in Task [3.1: Identify risk tradeoffs](#bookmark=kix.gj0yiaenfuib). These drills should be designed to achieve two primary outcomes: Technical Validation: Verify that fallback deterministic control systems, analog backups, and safety interlocks successfully assume the operational load without data loss or safety breaches. Human Readiness: Force human operators to actively manage operations via legacy or manual methods, preserving the critical muscle memory, tactical domain skills, and situational awareness required to safely navigate a real-world, unannounced AI outage. For applications where this is not possible within safety or regulatory constraints, they should be performed within non-production systems, see Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj) for further details.  |
|  |  | **3.6.3: Cognitive Engagement Monitoring**  Design the human-system or human-machine interface (HSI or HMI) to prevent passive monitoring. Consider active human verification step-throughs or "sanity checks" for truly high-consequence AI recommendations to counter the psychological tendency of operators to automatically trust machine outputs (automation bias). To manage the critical tradespace between active human validation and cognitive exhaustion, these checkpoints should be reserved strictly for high-consequence operational boundary thresholds. They should be deployed flexibly, utilizing the mixed-initiative and calm technology design choices referenced in Implementation [4.4.4: Applying consolidation and low-cognitive-load design techniques](#bookmark=id.923fjnayomc5) to prevent alert fatigue from driving superficial operator compliance. |
|  | **3.7: Govern and mitigate risks from unauthorized, transient, and "Shadow AI" systems** Establish organizational policies, procurement gates, and contractor onboarding workflows to detect, restrict, and manage risks associated with unvetted or unauthorized AI systems, localized open-source foundational models, and agentic tools brought into the operational environment by internal personnel or third-party contractors. In combination with Practice [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m), this task helps ensure that any AI capability utilized within the facility's physical or logical boundary is explicitly accounted for in the organization's risk profile and configuration management baselines. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Secure:** Contributes visibility of AI capability that entered the environment outside procurement: contractor attestation, detection of traffic to public AI endpoints, and scanning for unapproved applications and local models, so an AI system operating outside the credential directory becomes known. It does not deny those entities access, which Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) covers. **Privacy-Enhanced:** Contributes the point at which an undeclared external AI service becomes visible, where condition reports, operational records or facility information may otherwise leave through an endpoint nobody cataloged. It does not govern the dataflows of services the organization has authorized, which Task [6.3: Identify and control dataflows across the AI supply chain](#bookmark=kix.t3co6t9bp59v) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Privacy-Enhanced:** Detection works by observing the traffic and endpoints that personnel and contractors use, so it also produces a record of how individuals work. Governing that record belongs with the organization's existing workforce monitoring and privacy practice. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Secure versus Secure:** Where sanctioned tooling covers the work people need to do, restricting unvetted AI removes exposure at a modest cost. Where it leaves the need unmet, the same restriction moves the use to personal devices and accounts, which detection and logging reach less well than the corporate network did. One resolution is to pair each restriction with a sanctioned route that meets the underlying need; others include a time-bounded permit for a declared tool under the attestation in Implementation [3.7.1: AI Attestation](#bookmark=kix.g3tyngheh6j3), or accepting the restriction and resourcing the gap it leaves. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 1.6** (on building the AI inventory):This Task brings AI that entered the environment without procurement, including contractor tooling and locally run open-source models, into the same inventory as sanctioned systems; it does not cover registration of components the organization has deliberately acquired. For those, see Task [6.2: Align AI and existing CI asset lifecycle management](#bookmark=kix.j8kjkb5179e3). **GOVERN 6.1** (on establishing third-party risk management processes):This Task treats contractor attestation and onboarding as the point at which third-party AI use becomes declarable at all; it does not set the trustworthiness and visibility requirements that a vetted vendor should meet. For those, see Task [6.1: Develop policies for AI supply chain requirements](#bookmark=kix.71o0na2rpzas). **MANAGE 3.1** (on monitoring and responding to third-party risks):This Task detects use of external commercial AI services by logging traffic to known API endpoints and scanning for unapproved models and containers; it does not monitor the actions of AI entities that hold authorized credentials. For those, see Task [5.4: Implement continuous security monitoring for AI entity actions](#bookmark=kix.wzb03yleoteg).   **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Control CM-8, System Component Inventory, with enhancement (3), Automated Unauthorized Component Detection, is the established control for finding components that entered an environment without authorization, and the OT tailoring selects it at the moderate and high baselines. The tailoring adds no OT-specific discussion to this control, so what it gives here is the control and its baseline placement rather than commentary on applying it in an OT setting. **Guidance** [AI Shared Responsibility Framework, version 1.0](https://www.coalitionforsecureai.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/CoSAI-Shared-Responsibility-Framework.pdf), Workstream 2: Preparing Defenders for a Changing Cybersecurity Landscape, from the Coalition for Secure AI, an OASIS Open Project. Page 4 names the condition this Task exists for: unsanctioned AI usage thriving in the gray areas between IT security, business units and external vendors, where, left unassigned, no single group monitors for rogue usage or owns the risk of sensitive data exposure. Page 15 gives a detection list covering discovery of ungoverned AI tool usage, network monitoring for AI service API calls and web usage, departmental AI spending and subscription tracking, and risk assessment of what is found, alongside criteria for when tool usage should escalate into a governed enterprise system. Its responsibility matrix places accountability for shadow AI detection on the operating organization rather than on any provider. | **3.7.1: AI Attestation**  Consider requiring third-party contractors to sign a formal attestation declaring any AI-backed analytical tools, Large Language Models (LLMs), or automated code/configuration generation software utilized during their contract period. These should be cataloged in the organization’s AI system inventory and subject to appropriate risk management controls as described in Implementation [7.1.1: Create and maintain a “Master AI Asset List”](#bookmark=kix.pxpy1qc9bvxd) (regarding inventory) and Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) and Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) and Practice [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m) (regarding controls). **\>To the Reviewer:**  \>This implementation is about visibility, with the aim of enabling oversight of otherwise shadow IT systems through the other controls in this document (e.g., TEVV, identity and access control, etc.). On the one hand, we want to keep this concise, on the other hand perhaps a little more direction might be useful to direct the reader to why this attestation is useful. How would you strike this balance?  |
|  |  | **3.7.2: AI Use Detection** Incorporate the detection and logging of traffic to known public commercial AI API endpoints. Include scanning for unapproved AI applications, including containerized applications and software models. Monitor for unusual high-volume traffic that may indicate an unauthorized AI system ingesting data.  |
|  | **3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface** Catalog both the adversarial exploitation vectors (AI Attack Surface) and the non-adversarial, systemic vulnerability vectors (AI Risk Surface) introduced by AI deployment across all lifecycle stages. Technical managers should evaluate how non-deterministic AI logic expands operational risk beyond traditional cybersecurity boundaries to encompass all TACIP Trustworthiness Characteristics described in [Table 2](#bookmark=id.j664aovubcbh).  To enable the evaluation of the reachable risk surface of third-party AI components, vendors should provide the disclosures, described in Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q), to formally declare which upstream model limitations are operationally contained before deployment. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Secure:** Contributes the catalog of AI-specific adversarial exposure: the interfaces at which a crafted input can reach inference, from direct and indirect prompt injection to training data poisoning, model extraction and tool invocation. It does not place controls at the interfaces it finds.
