For those, see Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) and Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck). **Safe:** Contributes the half of the picture where harm arises with no attacker present: feedback loops or distribution shifts driving controllers out of limits, plausible hallucinations in generated logic, and opaque pathways that slow a response. It does not weigh the consequence of each exposure, which Task [3.1: Identify risk tradeoffs](#bookmark=kix.gj0yiaenfuib) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Secure:** The catalog states where an AI system can be reached and which hazards a deployment has left reachable, which is useful to an adversary who obtains it. Handling it belongs with the organization's existing information classification and handling practice. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Safe:** Where a hazard has been shown unreachable in this deployment and the control that makes it so is stable, treating it as contained lets attention go to the deviations that can occur. Where autonomy is widened, one agent is chained to another, or the operating environment moves, the same hazard can become reachable while the record still shows it contained. One resolution is the periodic coupling analysis in Implementation [3.8.3: Dynamic Risk Surface Coupling Analysis](#bookmark=id.soh3bu4jynwh), which treats a widened risk surface as a trigger to re-evaluate the guardrails a containment claim rested on; others include time-bounding a containment claim, or re-confirming it whenever guardrail scope changes. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 4.1** (on understanding AI component-level risks):This Task catalogs the adversarial vectors that can reach AI inference logic, including direct and indirect prompt injection, data poisoning, model extraction and malicious tool invocation; it does not obtain the vendor statements that show which upstream model limitations remain reachable in deployment. For those, see Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q). **MEASURE 2.7** (on measuring security and resiliency):This Task supplies the attack and risk surface that a security and resilience evaluation is scoped against, which is the identification MEASURE 2.7 refers back to the MAP function for; it does not carry out that evaluation. For that, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5). **GOVERN 1.2** (on defining trustworthy AI policies):This Task turns each trustworthiness characteristic into a category of operational exposure to be mapped, so that safety, explainability, privacy, fairness and accuracy are examined as vulnerability surfaces rather than as aspirations; it does not define the characteristic set or the terms used for it.   **References:**  \>Suggestions welcome | **3.8.1: AI Attack Surface Mapping (Adversarial Exposure)** Identify and catalog technical AI-specific attack vectors, such as direct and indirect prompt injection, training data poisoning, model extraction, context window manipulation, and malicious tool/API invocation. Document all interfaces where adversarial inputs can reach AI inference logic.  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST AI 100-2e2025, Adversarial Machine Learning: A Taxonomy and Terminology of Attacks and Mitigations](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.AI.100-2e2025), from NIST. Section 2.3 covers poisoning attacks on predictive systems and section 2.4.4 model extraction. Sections 3.3 and 3.4 cover direct prompting attacks and indirect prompt injection against generative systems. Section 3.5 covers agents that select and call tools, where a prompt injection becomes an action taken in the environment. Published counterparts for most of the vectors this Implementation asks to be cataloged, each with the mitigations reported for it. **Guidance** [MITRE ATLAS, maintained by MITRE, a not-for-profit organization in the United States that operates federally funded research and development centers](https://atlas.mitre.org/). A matrix of tactics and techniques observed in attacks on AI-enabled systems, structured after the same organization's ATT\&CK matrix for enterprise systems. Organized by the stage of an attack rather than by the kind of attack, which is the axis that matches documenting the interfaces at which an adversarial input reaches inference. |
|  |  | **3.8.2: System-Wide Risk Surface Mapping (Non-Adversarial Trustworthiness Exposure)** Map system vulnerabilities across all TACIP Trustworthiness Characteristics where non-deterministic AI behavior can cause physical, operational, or administrative harm without an active attacker. For example:  Safety & Resilience: Unintended feedback loops or distribution shifts driving physical controllers out of safe limits. Explainability & Forensic Auditability: Opaque decision pathways that impede real-time operator situational awareness or post-event root-cause analysis. Privacy & Data Protection: Systemic ingestion or leakage of sensitive telemetry, facility blueprints, or personally identifiable information (PII) into retrieval repositories or prompts. Fairness & Bias: Algorithmic distortion causing unequal resource dispatch, asymmetric hazard detection across different equipment ages/vendors, or biased administrative reviews. Accuracy & Reliability: Plausible hallucinations in report summaries, diagnostic feeds, or AI-generated execution logic. |
|  |  | **3.8.3: Dynamic Risk Surface Coupling Analysis** Conduct periodic evaluations of how changes in the operating environment, data distribution drift, increased agentic autonomy, or multi-agent chaining expand or contract the system’s risk surface over time. Ensure that risk surface expansion automatically triggers re-evaluation of independent guardrails (Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)) and of the oversight thresholds established under Implementation [4.5.1: Define engineering time-constant thresholds](#bookmark=id.mum8tq4afxyp), since a widened risk surface can move a path across a threshold that was previously satisfied. |
|  | **3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability** Determine whether the governance and oversight mechanisms applicable to an AI-enabled capability are executable for the specific capability and operating context. Make this determination before deployment, and again whenever a material change occurs. For the conditions that trigger reassessment, see Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz). This assessment is capability- and context-specific rather than a general organizational maturity assessment. An organization may have established AI policies and governance structures while still lacking the accountability, authority, escalation paths, operational capability, or enforceability necessary to govern a particular AI-enabled capability. This is distinct from Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud), which concerns whether a person can intervene in a single machine-speed action as it happens. This Task concerns whether a governance decision, once made, can be enforced at all, be it before deployment, throughout operation, or after material change. The conditions under which a capability is retired, and the authority to decide, are identified under Implementation [13.1.1: Identify retirement triggers, including those outside the organization's control](#bookmark=id.4abk974ttodd) and recorded, with the deciding authority, under Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz). **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Accountable:** Contributes the demonstration that a governance decision reaches the system's operating state: that restriction, suspension, rollback or reduction of delegated authority takes effect, within a window set against consequence, confirmable without relying on the capability's own report, and holding until an authorized decision changes it. It does not cover intervention in a single machine-speed action as it happens. Task [4.1: Establish “break-glass” intervention procedures](#bookmark=kix.arzfz46ud7mj) covers procedures while Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud) covers the feasibility of intervention. **Valid:** Contributes a readiness determination made for this capability in this operating context, re-opened when the capability, use case, delegated authority or environment materially changes, so the determination does not outlive the conditions it was made under. It does not determine whether the AI system suits the deployment, which Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Reliable:** Demonstrating that a suspension or a rollback takes effect means exercising it, and exercising it on a live deployment interrupts the function it governs. Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj) categorizes the environments in which such exercises belong, and names the applications for which an authorized outage window is the safer place. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accountable versus Resilient:** Where the restricted capability is not the one keeping a service running, authority that holds until an authorized decision reverses it is the protection intended. Where it is, a restriction that cannot be lifted quickly extends the loss of that service. One resolution is to settle the restoration decision and its authority at the same time as the restriction, so reversal is an authorized path rather than an exception; others include scoping the restriction to the function at issue rather than the whole capability, which the range of decisions in Implementation [3.9.5: Demonstrate Operational Enforceability](#bookmark=id.u6re9dsbehqs) anticipates, or carrying the service on the continuity arrangements in Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) while the restriction holds. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  **GOVERN 2.1** (on establishing roles and responsibilities):This Task identifies an accountable owner for the AI-enabled capability, and tests that escalation paths terminate with a role empowered to act rather than only to receive or record; it does not cover the operator-level pathways for responding to individual AI outputs. For those, see Implementation [3.3.3: Actionable Operator Decision & Escalation Pathways](#bookmark=id.wfndrt86zm06). **GOVERN 1.3** (on establishing risk management processes):This Task scales the depth of governance to the authority delegated to a capability and to the consequence of its actions, and re-opens the readiness determination on material change; it does not set the thresholds that place a capability in a consequence class. For those, see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d). **MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Task requires evidence, before deployment, that a decision to restrict, suspend, revert or reduce a capability's delegated authority can be made effective, verifiable and durable in the running system; it does not cover the orderly removal of an AI component at end of life. For that, see Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych). **GOVERN 1.5** (on establishing monitoring and review processes):This Task defines the material changes that should re-open a readiness determination, which gives the review an event trigger rather than only a calendar; it does not set a frequency for periodic review.  | **3.9.1: Establish Accountable Ownership** Identify an accountable owner for the AI-enabled capability and associated governance decisions. Document responsibility for governance decisions across the AI lifecycle, including responsibility for determining whether the capability may be deployed, continue operating, or operate under modified conditions. Where responsibilities span multiple organizational functions, identify the role accountable for resolving conflicting recommendations or decisions.  |
|  |  | **3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers** Document who has authority to accept risk, restrict deployment or operation, suspend use, require remediation, roll back the capability, or reduce its delegated authority. Define the scope and limits of that authority in relation to the potential consequences associated with the capability and its operating context. The depth of governance should scale with both the authority granted to the AI-enabled capability and the potential consequence of its actions.  Establish conditions that trigger reassessment of organizational readiness following material changes to the AI-enabled capability, use case, delegated authority, operating environment, dependencies, or potential consequences. Where a material change means the organization can no longer confirm that previously established governance or enforcement paths remain effective, re-open the readiness determination rather than assuming that the prior determination remains valid. Two other parts of the profile define re-evaluation triggers: Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i), and Implementation [3.8.3: Dynamic Risk Surface Coupling Analysis](#bookmark=id.soh3bu4jynwh). Where either trigger activates for a capability whose governance rests on a prior readiness determination, treat it as a material change for the purposes of this Implementation. The authority tiers referred to here should be consistent with those established in Implementation [4.1.2: Establish tiered intervention procedures, training, and authorization](#bookmark=kix.63u7h4w0bn26). |
|  |  | **3.9.3: Establish Escalation to Empowered Authority** Define escalation paths that move AI risk information to a role with sufficient authority to make the required governance decision. Identify escalation points for circumstances in which the risk, consequence, scope, or required response exceeds the decision authority of the person or function initially receiving or identifying the issue. Test or otherwise validate that escalation paths terminate with a role empowered to act rather than solely with a role responsible for receiving or documenting the issue. Where the escalation concerns an in-progress operational anomaly rather than a governance decision, Implementation [4.1.2: Establish tiered intervention procedures, training, and authorization](#bookmark=kix.63u7h4w0bn26) applies. This Implementation covers escalation of the governance decision itself. |
|  |  | **3.9.4: Assess Operational Capability** Assess whether existing organizational functions collectively provide sufficient capability to execute the governance requirements applicable to the AI-enabled capability. Consider whether accountability, decision authority, escalation, lifecycle responsibilities, monitoring, and control execution operate together sufficiently for the intended use and operating context. Where these conditions are met, existing governance structures may be extended to cover the AI-enabled capability. Where authority, accountability, lifecycle coverage, or operational capability is fragmented or absent, establish additional governance mechanisms as necessary. Where the assessment turns on whether a governance decision could actually be carried out, use the five properties in Implementation [3.9.5: Demonstrate Operational Enforceability](#bookmark=id.u6re9dsbehqs) as the test, rather than a general judgment of sufficiency. Where the assessment turns on whether personnel can actually perform the roles assigned to them, apply the role-specific training and competency requirements in Implementation [9.2.1: Include role-specific training on “AI failure modes”](#bookmark=kix.2u307dyu52tc) and the simulation training in Implementation [9.2.2: Implement realistic incident simulation training](#bookmark=kix.slhmi67qv3as). Organizational capability includes the people who must exercise it. |
|  |  | **3.9.5: Demonstrate Operational Enforceability** Before deployment and following material changes that could affect established enforcement paths, demonstrate that a governance decision to restrict, suspend, roll back, or reduce the delegated authority of an AI-enabled capability can be translated into an actual change in the system's operational state, not only recorded as policy or intent, and that the change is: (a) Effective: the decision produces the intended change in what the system is permitted to do or continues to do. (b) Timely relative to consequence: the change takes effect within a window that the organization defines and documents in advance, proportionate to the consequence class of the capability and the operational risk it presents, determined using the threshold criteria in Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d). (c) Verifiable: the organization can confirm the change actually took effect, rather than relying exclusively on the capability's self-attestation that it complied. (d) Durable: once enforced, the restricted or reduced authority holds until a new authorized decision changes it; the capability cannot silently resume, re-expand, or reacquire the withdrawn authority. (e) Sufficient in scope: enforceability covers the range of decisions the governance structure may need to make (restriction, suspension, rollback, and reduction of delegated authority), not a single all-or-nothing stop. The evidence needed to confirm (c) and (d) should come from the operating records required under Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) and, where the decision is an incident response, the incident records under Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb), rather than from a separate attestation process. |
| **4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness** Establish and maintain documented procedures that allow operators to intervene in, suspend, or override AI system actions that are unreliable, unexpected, or potentially harmful to critical operations.(See Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) for sustained manual operations). **Search Keywords:**  break-glass override, emergency intervention, emergency stop (e-stop), kill switch, manual takeover, disengagement, out-of-band control, situational awareness, alarm management, alarm flood mitigation, alert fatigue, cognitive overload, calm technology, visual confidence bands, human-on-the-loop, human-out-of-the-loop, human response time, state-at-handoff display, explanation fidelity, rationale display **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes the human intervention path: stopping, overriding or falling back when the system's own constraints have not prevented a hazard. It does not supply those constraints, which Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) supplies. **Resilient:** Contributes the human-mediated fallback: suspending, overriding or reverting the AI-enabled function so operation can continue while the AI system is not trusted. It does not supply sustained manual operation, which Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) covers. **Explainable & Interpretable:** Contributes the situation awareness an intervening person needs at the moment of acting: what the AI system is doing, and what response that calls for. It does not supply the underlying telemetry, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Secure:** Creates privileged routes into the control system. Every intervention tier, out-of-band channel and emergency mode defined here bypasses the normal control path, and Implementation [4.1.2: Establish tiered intervention procedures, training, and authorization](#bookmark=kix.63u7h4w0bn26) places tiered authorization on the personnel routes. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Resilient:** Where the process has a defined safe state that it can be stopped into, a deterministic trip and graceful degradation point the same way. Where stopping is itself hazardous, such as a vehicle or industrial process, they separate: Task [4.1: Establish “break-glass” intervention procedures](#bookmark=kix.arzfz46ud7mj) notes that disabling the AI system can leave the overall system unsafe, so the tiered ladder in Implementation [4.1.2: Establish tiered intervention procedures, training, and authorization](#bookmark=kix.63u7h4w0bn26) exists to let an operator choose a point between a full isolation that halts the process and a partial suspension that keeps it running.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 3.2** (on defining human-AI configuration):This Practice contributes the oversight modes, intervention tiers and authorization boundaries that such policies have to differentiate; it does not establish whether the organization can enforce an oversight decision once it is made. For that, see Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x). **MAP 3.5** (on establishing human oversight processes):This Practice defines the human oversight processes used in an emergency and assesses them through exercise; it does not develop the role-specific competencies that those processes assume. For those, see Practice [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1). **MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Practice supplies the mechanisms and the assigned authority by which an AI system is overridden, suspended or disengaged when its behavior is inconsistent with intended use; it does not cover how essential functions are sustained afterwards. For that, see Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f). **MANAGE 4.1** (on post-deployment risk management):This Practice contributes the override and immediate-recovery limbs of a post-deployment monitoring plan, together with the independent channels that carry them; it does not cover the change management and decommissioning limbs that MANAGE 4.1 also names. **GOVERN 4.3** (on enabling AI testing, incident identification, and information sharing):This Practice contributes the exercise regime for intervention procedures and the AI-independent channels through which an incident becomes visible in the first place; it does not cover sharing incident information outside the organization. For that, see Task [8.4: Define external reporting and regulatory disclosure protocols following AI incidents](#bookmark=kix.jtgfqqqxhyf5).   **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-61r3, Incident Response Recommendations and Considerations for Cybersecurity Risk Management](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.SP.800-61r3), from NIST. Section 2.1 sets out the incident response life cycle within which an override and the recovery after it sit. Section 2.3 lists what the incident response policy should settle in advance, including which roles hold the authority to confiscate, disconnect or shut down technology assets, which is the authorization question this Practice asks about AI systems. **Standard** [IEC 61508, Functional safety of electrical/electronic/programmable electronic safety-related systems](https://webstore.iec.ch/en/publication/22273), from the International Electrotechnical Commission, an international standards body for electrical, electronic and related technologies. Defines functional safety for electrical, electronic and programmable electronic safety-related systems, meaning systems whose safety depends on their operating correctly. **Standard** [ISO 26262-1:2018 Road vehicles \- Functional safety](https://www.iso.org/standard/68383.html), from the International Organization for Standardization. The road vehicle sector's functional safety series.  | **4.1: Establish “break-glass” intervention procedures** Define procedures by which authorized, trained personnel can safely override AI-driven actions in a timely manner during an emergency situation, similar to how a “break-glass” button provides the ability to override access control in the event of a fire or other emergency.  Note that in many applications, simply disabling the AI system, for example with a kill switch that cuts it off without a defined handover, can leave the overall system in an unsafe state. This task should establish overall procedures for personnel to obtain appropriate situational awareness (Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq)), and decide on the appropriate action to take. These should align with the fallback behaviors in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn).  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes the executable override path: intercept points that are reliably implemented (Implementation [4.1.1: Ensure that AI systems can be reliably overridden](#bookmark=kix.sp3nfl81yh8d)), and a ladder of tiers so that intervention need not be all or nothing. It does not supply the fallback states entered on override, which Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) defines. **Accountable:** Contributes authorization allocated in advance: which tier a role may invoke, where a second authorizer is required, and who decides when the cause of an anomaly is not yet clear. It does not supply the ability to enforce that decision, which Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Reliable:** Exercising the override path is itself an operational action, and rehearsal on a production system can trip or suspend the function it protects. Implementation [4.1.3: Regularly practice and evaluate intervention processes](#bookmark=kix.h13vn9nyfxoi) directs that testing to the environments categorized in Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Secure:** Where intervening personnel are pre-credentialed and the tier has been rehearsed, authorization and speed move together. Where the intervening party is an external responder arriving unannounced, each authorization step lengthens the emergency that the break-glass path exists to shorten. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Task establishes how authorized personnel disengage or override an AI system at the moment its behavior becomes unsafe, and the tiers of authority for doing so; it does not supply the situational picture on which that decision rests. For that, see Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq). **MAP 3.5** (on establishing human oversight processes):This Task defines the intervention procedure itself and assesses it through tabletop exercise under adversarial scenarios; it does not determine which operational contexts admit real-time human oversight at all. For that, see Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud). **MAP 3.4** (on defining operator proficiency):This Task sets tiered training, authorization and access control for the personnel permitted to intervene, including external emergency responders; it does not cover the manual operating skills that erode through routine reliance on the AI. For those, see Task [3.6: Mitigate automation complacency and skills degradation](#bookmark=kix.d601y3q6ud1n). **MANAGE 2.3** (on responding to emerging risks):This Task pre-establishes decision authority for anomalies whose underlying cause is not yet clear, so that a responder is not delayed during a machine-speed event; it does not cover the investigation that afterwards determines what the risk was. For that, see Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb).   | **4.1.1: Ensure that AI systems can be reliably overridden** Ensure that technical processes for intervening in the AI system’s operation are reliably implemented at appropriate points. This Implementation is likely to align with Implementation [3.2.1: Identify AI output intercept points](#bookmark=kix.ahnz12t5d7al). **References:** \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-190, Application Container Security Guide](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-190.pdf), from NIST. Section 2.3.3 describes the orchestrator as the component that deploys images into containers and manages the running containers, with hosts running and stopping them as the orchestrator directs. Section 4.3 sets the countermeasures that govern it: a least privilege access model, tightly controlled cluster-wide administrative accounts, and node trust. For a containerized AI deployment the orchestrator is one of the appropriate points at which an intervention is implemented, and Section 4.3 is what decides who can exercise it. |
|  |  | **4.1.2: Establish tiered intervention procedures, training, and authorization** Implement or utilize existing tiered training, authorization, and access control for personnel intervening in AI system operation. This may include requiring multiple personnel to be available to authorize particularly high impact interventions.  Examples of tiers could include placing the system into “emergency mode,” overriding individual actions, partial suspension of operation, and full system isolation.  Ensure that sufficient appropriately trained and authorized personnel are available commensurate with the system's designated autonomy level and operational criticality. This is especially important where real-time human intervention is explicitly required as an active risk mitigation control.  Consider an “emergency mode” protocol for external emergency response personnel who may need to safely isolate or override the AI system in an emergency situation. This may be important for reasons that include:  Organizational personnel may be unable to safely approach or communicate with the AI system during an emergency.  AI systems may behave in ways that responding personnel may not expect and may not have time or resources to address.  Emergencies and other incidents may generate inputs into the AI system that it does not expect and for which it may not have been tested, resulting in greater probability of erroneous and inappropriate outputs.  For example, during a fire or chemical emergency, or in a widespread disaster situation, organizational personnel may be unable to safely approach or communicate with a remote facility. Responders may need out-of-band emergency interfaces, like secured external emergency stop buttons (E-stops) (subject to applicable functional-safety certification requirements, such as IEC 60204-1, independent of any AI-specific considerations), to place AI-enabled systems into a known safe state. For high-consequence systems, consider whether decision-making authority should be pre-established for scenarios where the underlying cause of an anomaly isn't yet clear, so that responders aren't delayed during a machine-speed event, adversarial or otherwise.  Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x) covers the prior question of whether the organization can enforce such a decision once it is made; this Implementation covers who is authorized to make it in the moment. |
|  |  | **4.1.3: Regularly practice and evaluate intervention processes** Conduct tabletop exercises to test intervention procedures from Implementation [4.1.1: Ensure that AI systems can be reliably overridden](#bookmark=kix.sp3nfl81yh8d) and Implementation [4.1.2: Establish tiered intervention procedures, training, and authorization](#bookmark=kix.63u7h4w0bn26) under adversarial and unexpected scenarios. To prevent unintended operational triggers during these simulations, testing should be performed on systems in accordance with Implementation [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj).  Use established TEVV protocols from Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) and as extended in Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5) to guide intervention procedure testing.  Where feasible, at least one exercise per cycle should involve a correlated scenario in which multiple safeguards are degraded together, rather than testing single-point failures only. **References:** \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-53r5, Security and Privacy Controls for Information Systems and Organizations](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.SP.800-53r5), from NIST. Control IR-3, Incident Response Testing, requires the effectiveness of the incident response capability to be tested at a defined frequency, and its discussion names checklists, walk-through and tabletop exercises, and parallel or full-interrupt simulations as the forms that testing takes. Enhancement IR-3(2) coordinates that testing with contingency, continuity and critical infrastructure plans, which is where an exercise of a correlated scenario such as the one in this Implementation would be coordinated.  |
|  | **4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness** Implement role-appropriate mechanisms for providing personnel with rapid, actionable situational awareness as to the state of the AI system, its task, recent decisions, environment, and anomalies.  The goal is to reduce the likelihood of humans making mistakes when overriding the AI system, particularly in chaotic, emergency, unusual, time-sensitive, and high pressure situations.  This may take the form of displays, interfaces to existing information management tools, printouts, or a combination that aims to reduce the cognitive latency required for an operator to transition from passive monitoring to active intervention. Risk management should account for the operational latency required to comprehend a machine-speed cascade from a cold start. Prioritize system learnability and transparency in interface design to ensure personnel can rapidly form accurate mental models of AI behavior during both routine operations and emergency transitions. Care should be taken to ensure that the mechanisms do not cause cognitive overload or confusion, particularly for those who may be unfamiliar with the system, such as external responders and maintenance personnel. See Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) for additional considerations.  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Explainable & Interpretable:** Contributes the operator-facing layer: what an output means now and what response it calls for, from uncertainty indications to triage cues. It does not determine whether the confidence and rationale it presents reflect how the output was produced. For that, see Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s). **Safe:** Contributes the comprehension that makes an intervention correct rather than merely fast, including for external responders meeting a specific deployment for the first time. It does not supply the means of intervening, which Task [4.1: Establish “break-glass” intervention procedures](#bookmark=kix.arzfz46ud7mj) provides. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Explainable & Interpretable versus Safe:** Where the audience works with the AI system daily, more rationale can improve the decision. Where the audience is a responder under time pressure, it consumes the capacity to act, which is why Implementation [4.2.1: Develop situational awareness processes and procedures for personnel who may need to intervene in the AI system’s operation](#bookmark=kix.xry0usuocd41) gives responders only enough to choose an emergency mode. Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) handles the volume side of the same limit. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 2.2** (on defining AI system utility and oversight needs):This Task puts the AI system's knowledge limits in front of the operator at the moment of decision, through calibrated confidence bands, out-of-distribution warnings and standardized triage indicators; it does not cover the volume at which that information arrives. For that, see Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y). **MEASURE 1.2** (on continuously improving measurement):This Task submits the situational awareness mechanisms themselves to regular evaluation, checking both that they reflect the system's true state and that their intended audience understands them; it does not evaluate the AI system's own performance against its baselines. For that, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5).  | **4.2.1: Develop situational awareness processes and procedures for personnel who may need to intervene in the AI system’s operation** Divide personnel into groups based on the decisions that they will be expected to make in intervening in the AI system (including assessing the need to intervene), and their expected background knowledge.  Include personnel who regularly work with the system as well as those who may need to interact with the system at short notice, during an incident, and in an emergency.  For example: Engineers who work with the system day-to-day may require a summary of deviations from normal operation to anticipate and avoid potential incidents. These summaries prevent important information from being buried within “normal” status reports that they may become accustomed to skimming over.  External maintenance engineers, who may be familiar with the equipment family but not the specific installation, may benefit from a combination of printed material and dynamic displays that highlight how this AI system’s operational learning has caused its behavior to differ from a generic or freshly deployed system, allowing them to avoid taking actions that would ordinarily be safe but may pose a risk for this specific deployment.  Emergency responders who may be non-technical and need to make decisions under time pressure may only need just enough information to decide which of a pre-set number of emergency modes to put the system in, such as reverting to a “safe” controller or shutting down the system completely.  |
|  |  | **4.2.2: Regularly evaluate situational awareness mechanisms** Test situational awareness mechanisms on a regular basis, to both ensure that they appropriately reflect the underlying state of the AI system, and that their intended audience appropriately understands them. This should be combined with Implementation [4.1.3: Regularly practice and evaluate intervention processes](#bookmark=kix.h13vn9nyfxoi)**.**  |
|  |  | **4.2.3: Human-Centered Interfaces for AI Uncertainty and Actionable Triggers** Design Human-System or Human-Machine Interfaces (HSIs or HMIs), such as operational dashboards, clinical decision-support tools, fraud triage queues, network management displays, or field interfaces, to communicate AI system state and confidence bounds while avoiding cognitive overload. Examples of features that should be considered include:  Calibrated indications of uncertainty: Present recommendations alongside visual confidence bands, data freshness indicators, and out-of-distribution warnings (e.g., flagging unhistorical conditions in finance, novel patient demographics in healthcare, or uncharacteristic network traffic in telecommunications) so personnel can quickly judge how far to rely on the output.  Actionable Triage Indicators: Provide standardized visual cues that explicitly signal the required human response: Proceed (high confidence, within baseline bounds), Investigate (moderate confidence, novel inputs/telemetry), Escalate (low confidence, cross-system anomaly), or Override Immediately (boundary or policy constraint violation). A Proceed cue indicates that the output falls within the conditions the AI system was validated for, not that it is correct.  Displayed confidence metrics and data freshness warnings should be derived from the AI system's decision making process (Implementation [4.6.2: Derive confidence and rationale for learned components from their decision processes](#bookmark=id.kdty3el67ume)). Where the interface also displays a rationale for a recommendation, see Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s). For TEVV protocols for model calibration and performance baselining, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5). **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **4.3: Establish independent (“out-of-band”) communication, monitoring, alerting, and control capabilities** Ensure that internal and external personnel who may need to intervene in the AI system’s operation have access to the necessary information independent of the AI system’s functionality.  Akin to the analog gauges present on aircraft that otherwise have fully digital cockpits, these mechanisms allow for situational awareness, control, and risk management even when a fault renders AI-mediated channels untrustworthy or unavailable.  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Resilient:** Contributes the oversight path that survives the failure: monitoring, alerting and control that stay available when the AI system degrades them, down to physically bypassing the automation. It does not keep the function running, which Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) and Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) address. **Transparent:** Contributes availability of the information rather than its content: Implementation [4.3.1: Inventory channels for situational awareness and control that may be affected by the AI system](#bookmark=kix.k2wn6x19m05k) traces which awareness channels the AI system can degrade, including through shared infrastructure. It does not determine what is captured in the first place, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Resilient versus Secure:** Where the independent channel carries only telemetry, the exposure it adds is small. Where it carries control, as with a satellite or cellular link or a trained manual bypass, each fallback level is a further route into the process, and Implementation [4.1.2: Establish tiered intervention procedures, training, and authorization](#bookmark=kix.63u7h4w0bn26) places tiered authorization on who may use it. One resolution is to hold a control-carrying channel to the same access controls as the primary path; others include limiting it to telemetry and accepting that intervention then takes a slower route. \>TBD \- Securing the independent channels themselves, as distinct from authorizing the people who use them. Suggestions welcome.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MEASURE 2.4** (on monitoring deployed AI systems):This Task requires that the path by which a system is monitored not depend on the system being monitored, so that a fault cannot conceal itself; it does not define what is monitored or what counts as anomalous. For that, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk). **MANAGE 4.3** (on incident response):This Task keeps the notification path to responders available when AI-mediated channels have become unavailable or untrustworthy, down to satellite, cellular and physical bypass; it does not define what is disclosed outside the organization. For that, see Task [8.4: Define external reporting and regulatory disclosure protocols following AI incidents](#bookmark=kix.jtgfqqqxhyf5). **MEASURE 3.1** (on tracking known and emerging risks):This Task traces the information flows that oversight depends on and identifies secondary couplings, such as a malfunctioning AI system saturating a channel shared with an otherwise independent camera feed; it does not track the risks arising from the AI system's own outputs. For those, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk).  | **4.3.1: Inventory channels for situational awareness and control that may be affected by the AI system** Trace the critical information flows that feed the situational awareness mechanisms identified in Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq) and identify locations where associated AI systems may cause accuracy or availability issues.  Be aware of potential secondary and tertiary effects. For example, a malfunctioning AI system may generate enough traffic to overload a wireless communications channel, affecting an otherwise independent camera feed that shares the same channel. Similarly, a malfunctioning AI system responsible for intrusion detection may cause the inability to authenticate to an emergency intervention dashboard.  |
|  |  | **4.3.2: Implement AI-independent channels** Based on Implementation [4.3.1: Inventory channels for situational awareness and control that may be affected by the AI system](#bookmark=kix.k2wn6x19m05k), establish independent channels separate from monitored AI systems so that notification delivery is not disrupted by the system failures. Depending on the system, having multiple levels of fallback, even down to having personnel available and trained to physically bypass the automated system entirely, along with completely independent channels such as satellite or cell connections, may be necessary.  **References:** \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Control SC-47, Alternate Communications Paths, asks an organization to determine which systems need an alternate path so that a loss of communication does not lead to an unacceptable loss of view or control, or to a safety event, and gives continuity of command and control during an incident as the reason. Control CP-8, Telecommunications Services, carries the provider side, with enhancements for single points of failure and for separation of primary and alternate providers, which is what keeps a nominally independent second channel from sharing a carrier with the first.  |
|  | **4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation** Treat human attention as a finite resource that AI systems should use efficiently. AI-driven notification volume can exceed an operator's ability to triage, reducing effectiveness rather than improving it. This may be by overwhelming a domain with no prior experience managing this risk, breaking the assumptions behind a domain's existing alarm flood mitigations, or generating a correlated surge across many systems at once.  Automatically filtering AI telemetry using vendor hazard reachability attestations, once independently verified or corroborated (Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q)), can prevent alert floods by suppressing operational warnings for model anomalies that have already been rendered unreachable by downstream deterministic guardrails (Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)), allowing operators to focus strictly on actionable operational deviations. This task complements Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq) by addressing cases where notification volume, not just notification content or clarity, is what threatens an operator's ability to respond effectively. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes the capacity side of intervention: keeping notification volume within what the available people can triage, including when one external event makes many AI systems request attention at once. It does not improve any single notification, which Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq) covers. **Bias Managed:** Contributes a bound on one human-cognitive error source, desensitization produced by notification rate, by treating that rate as a designed quantity rather than a byproduct. It does not address over-trust in an output that does arrive, which Task [3.6: Mitigate automation complacency and skills degradation](#bookmark=kix.d601y3q6ud1n) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Transparent:** Filtering, suppression and consolidation decide what reaches a person, not what is recorded. Whether a suppressed notification remains retrievable afterwards is governed by the logging requirements in Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Reliable:** Where notifications repeat one underlying condition, consolidation loses nothing and both improve. Where the urgent signal is a single isolated one, Implementation [4.4.4: Applying consolidation and low-cognitive-load design techniques](#bookmark=id.923fjnayomc5) notes that aggressive batching buys operator capacity by delaying the alert that needed to arrive immediately.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MANAGE 2.1** (on allocating resources):This Task treats operator attention as a resource that has to be sized for correlated worst-case surges across every system drawing on the same oversight pool, rather than for average per-system load; it does not consider the viable non-AI alternative systems that MANAGE 2.1 also names. **MEASURE 1.2** (on continuously improving measurement):This Task reassesses whether an established alarm-management practice remains effective once AI introduces non-deterministic failure modes; it does not evaluate whether a given display is understood by the audience that reads it. For that, see Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq).  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** Health and Safety Executive (2000) [Better alarm handling](https://www.hse.gov.uk/pubns/chis6.pdf), Chemicals Sheet No 6, from Great Britain's workplace health and safety regulator. States target alarm rates for process plant: no more than one every ten minutes in normal operation, and no more than ten in the first ten minutes after a major upset. **Guidance** [EEMUA Publication 191, "Alarm Systems: A Guide to Design, Management and Procurement"](https://www.eemua.org/products/publications/print/eemua-publication-191), from the Engineering Equipment and Materials Users' Association, a UK association of industrial plant owner-operators. The underlying baseline for alarm metrics in process industries, and the source of the targets above. **Incident Report** [Health and Safety Executive (1997) The Explosion and Fires at the Texaco Refinery, Milford Haven, 24 July 1994](https://www.jesip.org.uk/wp-content/uploads/2022/03/Texaco-Refinery-Explosion.pdf). In the final 10.7 minutes two operators had to recognize, acknowledge and act on at least 275 alarms, arriving at one every two to three seconds, and the report records operators cancelling alarms for their nuisance value without recognizing what they meant. **Guidance** [The Joint Commission (2013) Sentinel Event Alert 50: Medical device alarm safety in hospitals](https://www.jointcommission.org/en-us/knowledge-library/newsletters/sentinel-event-alert/issue-50), from the US non-profit that accredits healthcare organizations, issued on the basis of aggregated reported events. Documents the same failure in a sector with entirely different equipment. **Research** [McFarlane, D.C. (1999), "Coordinating Interruptions in Human-Computer Interaction"](https://www.interruptions.net/literature/McFarlane-Interact99-Coordinating.pdf). The first empirical comparison of the four ways a system can time an interruption, immediate, negotiated, mediated and scheduled, which is the evidence behind Implementation [4.4.4: Applying consolidation and low-cognitive-load design techniques](#bookmark=id.923fjnayomc5)'s discussion of batching rather than deliver immediately. **Research** [Simon, H. (1971), "Designing Organizations for an Information-Rich World"](https://gwern.net/doc/design/1971-simon.pdf). The origin of the framing this Task opens with, that a wealth of information creates a poverty of attention. **\>To the Reviewer:**  \>This is still rather a nascent field and, while there are academic publications specific to the topic of AI-induced cognitive overload and similar issues, we’re having some trouble finding good references that would speak to an end-user audience. Would pointing to more academic references where there is a lack of industry publications be useful here or would it be more likely to cause confusion?  | **4.4.1: Recognizing novel AI-driven alert floods in unfamiliar domains** Domains with no prior experience managing alarm floods may not recognize the pattern when AI introduces it, or know that established mitigation techniques exist. For example, a fraud-detection system entering a confused state can flood a review queue with false positives; a summarization tool can generate excessive content when it should stay silent; a hospital's AI-assisted clinical decision support can generate alert volumes comparable to those long documented in traditional medical device alarm fatigue, but without the institutional awareness built up around that older problem. Measures to reduce this risk range from training personnel to recognize alert-flood patterns as they emerge in the tool they're using and developing procedures for manual prioritization, to automated triage and consolidation of output (e.g., rate-limiting generated content, grouping duplicate flags). |
|  |  | **4.4.2: Adapting legacy alarm management for AI's non-deterministic failure modes** Domains with mature, trusted alarm-management practices may find those practices don't transfer cleanly to some AI systems. Traditional mitigations often assume alarms correspond to discrete, mechanical fault states (e.g., a stuck valve or tripped breaker).  Examples of ways in which AI-generated alarms can differ from those in more traditional systems include:  An AI system may report the same underlying error in slightly different ways.  Adaptive AI systems may respond inconsistently to an underlying error, resulting in hidden or inconsistent alarms. Inconsistent AI alerts may result in only downstream systems alerting while the system that is the root cause stays silent.  Agentic systems can build dependencies dynamically and in ways that may not be documented, so a failure may cause unexpected and seemingly unrelated systems to send notifications of failure.  Machine learned detection algorithms and thresholds may be undocumented and applied inconsistently, making it difficult to determine what any given alert means.  The dependency mapping in Implementation [3.5.1: Map direct functional and algorithmic dependencies](#bookmark=kix.gfi873l406c2) and Implementation [3.5.2: Identify indirect shared infrastructure and collateral damage exposure](#bookmark=kix.hw9y3h9onk8j) can help make these dependency chains more visible in advance, even though it does not resolve the underlying detection problem. Some partial, non-CI-specific and non-AI-specific approaches exist in the broader IT/software observability field, such as similarity-based alert clustering and distributed tracing, but these generally assume mature instrumentation that many current AI deployments, and especially OT environments, may not yet have in place. This is particularly challenging in agentic systems, which can make dependency decisions dynamically and without documenting them. **\>To the Reviewer:**  \>Mitigating this is still an open research topic, particularly in agentic systems. Are there other partial mitigations, especially for the multi-agent feedback-loop case, that we're missing?  |
|  |  | **4.4.3: Managing correlated multi-system attention surges** A single external event can trigger a simultaneous surge of attention requests across many AI-controlled systems at once, overwhelming a limited pool of human operators even if each individual system's alert volume is otherwise manageable. Reduced staffing is frequently among the expected benefits of adopting an AI system. Where it is, the oversight capacity the AI system depends on is drawn from the same pool that the expected benefit reduces, and sizing the two separately can produce a shortfall that neither decision would have chosen. For example, a widespread power failure or loss of traffic signals can cause an entire autonomous vehicle fleet to simultaneously request remote operator attention, exceeding the capacity of the remote operations center. Measures to address this risk include: Identifying which AI-driven systems draw on the same oversight capacity, and estimating what external events could cause them to request attention simultaneously.  Designing and resourcing oversight capacity and escalation procedures explicitly for correlated worst-case surges, not just average or independent per-system load. Accounting for oversight capacity as a cost of the AI system rather than as a resource already available, so that it enters the tradespace of benefits and control costs that Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k), Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41), and Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) define. Developing automated alert triaging mechanisms so that limited operator attention is not spent sorting through large quantities of alerts or addressing low priority issues.  Re-checking oversight capacity when staffing levels, shift patterns, training, or authorizations change, rather than only when the AI systems drawing on it change. |
|  |  | **4.4.4: Applying consolidation and low-cognitive-load design techniques** Once alarm flood risks are identified, apply techniques that reduce the rate and cognitive cost of notifications without hiding genuinely urgent, isolated signals.  Examples of measures to consider include:  Implementing basic consolidation, such as grouping repeated identical alerts into a single notification. Applying user-interface design choices that reduce distraction (often referred to as calm technology or mixed-initiative interfaces) such as exception batching, trend dashboards, dynamic deadbands, time-delay hysteresis, matched to the specific application, oversight mode, and the range of personnel familiarity likely to encounter these notifications, from regular operators to occasional external responders. Note that there is a trade-off between many of these measures. For example, aggressive batching reduces fatigue but risks delaying a genuinely urgent, isolated signal. |
|  | **4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation** Identify operational contexts where real-time, manual human-in-the-loop (HITL) oversight is physically or technically infeasible due to tight system time constants (such as stability anomalies operating in milliseconds, real-time industrial control loops, or machine-speed cyber defense events).  Oversight of any mode degrades into a purely nominal or superficial check where the unfolding event outruns what the operator can be relied upon to do about it, particularly in situations where fatigue or stress are factors; the oversight thresholds established under Implementation [4.5.1: Define engineering time-constant thresholds](#bookmark=id.mum8tq4afxyp) are what make this testable rather than assumed.  For these regimes, formally define the boundaries where the human-on-the-loop oversight mode (action proceeds, with intervention capability retained) or the human-out-of-the-loop oversight mode (autonomous execution with retrospective post-hoc review) should substitute for the human-in-the-loop mode, and specify the deterministic automated protections required to support them.  Operating in a less direct oversight mode does not remove human decision-making; it moves it earlier and later. Even where real-time intervention is infeasible, a specific role decides whether the system may operate in that mode, on what conditions, and when to suspend or roll it back (Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz)). This task separates the paths where human intervention can be relied upon from those where it cannot, so that automated boundaries carry the role where a person cannot. **Search Keywords:**  human-on-the-loop, human-out-of-the-loop, human-in-the-loop, supervisory control, closed-loop control, system time constant, control loop rate, human reaction time, operator response time, response time budget, automated fail-safe, infeasible oversight, autonomy regime **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes the test that decides whether human intervention counts as a safety control: whether what the operator would have to do is something a person can be relied upon to do in the time available, and documenting the paths where it cannot. It does not supply the replacement protection, which Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) defines. **Accountable:** Contributes the human decision that remains when real-time intervention is infeasible: a named role decides whether a regime may be entered, on what conditions, and when to suspend it. It does not define that authority, which Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz) does. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Transparent:** Out-of-the-loop operation moves the whole review to afterwards. Implementation [4.5.2: Establish deterministic boundary constraints for automated regimes](#bookmark=id.ss5nifoozp7w) requires a log when a deterministic boundary blocks a command, but autonomous actions that stay within bounds depend on telemetry captured under Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accountable versus Safe:** Where the operator can be relied upon to act, keeping a person in the loop contributes both accountability and safety. Where they cannot, the two separate; human oversight may identify a responsible party without addressing safety. One resolution is to move the human decision to a point where there is sufficient time and information to make it well, such as deciding in advance whether the system may run in this mode and on what conditions. Others include changing the process so that intervention becomes feasible, or not operating in that mode. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 3.2** (on defining human-AI configuration):This Task differentiates the human-in-the-loop, human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop oversight modes and fixes the boundaries between them; it does not define the intervention procedure that applies once a person is in the loop. For that, see Task [4.1: Establish “break-glass” intervention procedures](#bookmark=kix.arzfz46ud7mj). **MAP 1.1** (on defining operating context):This Task adds the process time constants to the documented operating context, so that the feasibility of real-time human oversight is established rather than assumed; it does not cover the intended purposes and user expectations that MAP 1.1 also names. **MEASURE 2.6** (on measuring safety):This Task supplies the human reaction and execution times that a response-time safety metric has to be set against, and requires deterministic boundaries able to execute a fallback state with no human present; it does not produce evidence that those boundaries hold. For that, see Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5).  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [Principles for the Secure Integration of Artificial Intelligence in Operational Technology](https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/joint-guidance-principles-for-the-secure-integration-of-artificial-intelligence-in-operational-technology-508c.pdf), December 2025, joint guidance from nine national cybersecurity agencies across seven countries, led by the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. Section 4.2, Embed Safety and Failsafe Mechanisms, asks that AI systems be able to fail gracefully without disrupting critical operations, and that the new failure states an AI system introduces, including how to bypass or replace it, be written into existing functional safety and incident response procedures. Those are the deterministic protections this Task requires where real-time human intervention is infeasible. **Standard** ISA-84 / IEC 61511, Functional Safety: Safety Instrumented Systems for the Process Industry Sector (defining automated protective layers independent of human operator loops).  | **4.5.1: Define engineering time-constant thresholds** Audit automated processes to identify paths where the window between an AI anomaly and an irreversible consequence is shorter than what human intervention can be relied upon to achieve. For these paths, document that real-time human intervention is not a primary risk mitigation control. What can be relied upon depends on the time available and on what the operator has to do with it. Where the time is measured in minutes, an operator may be able to detect, diagnose, and act. Where it is measured in seconds, a memorized response to an unambiguous trigger is the most dependable option. For example, FAA guidance on standard operating procedures (see FAA AC 120-71B) advises avoiding such responses wherever possible and limiting unavoidable ones to fewer than three items containing no conditional decision steps. Judging whether an AI-selected action is appropriate is likely to be a conditional decision that does not meet that bound. Time is not the only limit. An operator can rarely be expected to verify in real time that a complex AI system is following its rules, and can judge only whether its actions, perhaps with an accompanying explanation, appear acceptable (see Bainbridge 1983). Where the system was adopted because it handles cases human judgment handles poorly, that judgment is what the oversight arrangement relies on. This even applies to an attentive operator, and is compounded where automatic control masks a developing problem by compensating for it up until the point where human intervention becomes necessary. See Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) for automated controls, as well as Implementation [4.2.3: Human-Centered Interfaces for AI Uncertainty and Actionable Triggers](#bookmark=id.64phfsnphbx3), Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s), Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y), and Implementation [11.2.3: Human Factors and Cognitive Transition Protocols for Control Handoffs](#bookmark=id.scvcymfio39v) for further details.  **References:** \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Research** [Lisanne Bainbridge, "Ironies of Automation", Automatica volume 19 number 6, 1983, pages 775 to 779](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/0005109883900468). Automatica is the journal of the International Federation of Automatic Control, an international body whose members are national control engineering organizations. A seminal paper on the human factors of automated process control. Its subject is the process-control computers of 1983, and the mechanisms it identifies are relevant to AI systems. Section 1.1.3 sets out why an operator cannot verify in real time that an automated system is following its rules. **Guidance** [FAA AC 120-71B, *Standard Operating Procedures and Pilot Monitoring Duties for Flight Deck Crewmembers*](https://www.faa.gov/regulations_policies/advisory_circulars/index.cfm/go/document.information/documentID/1030486), 10 January 2017, from the United States Federal Aviation Administration, the civil aviation authority of the United States. The AC states that it is not mandatory and does not constitute a regulation. Paragraph 4.2.5 gives the conditions under which a response may be relied on from memory: avoided wherever possible, clearly identified, emphasized in training, fewer than three items, and containing no conditional decision steps.  |
|  |  | **4.5.2: Establish deterministic boundary constraints for automated regimes**  Identified human-out-of-the-loop operational regimes should include independent, non-AI deterministic safety wrappers detailed in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn). Ensure that these physical process boundaries have total authority to block unsafe autonomous commands, execute fallback states per Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p), and log the event prior to subsequent human administrative review. |
|  | **4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output** Establish requirements that any confidence indication or rationale shown to personnel is derived from the process by which the AI system produced its output, and that rationale displays are designed and tested for whether they help personnel decide. This applies to routine decision support as well as to intervention. A rationale can be persuasive without being accurate as an explanation: an explanation constructed after the fact can look reasonable without corresponding to how the output was produced. Rationale displays have also been found to increase acceptance of incorrect recommendations as well as correct ones (Implementation [4.6.1: Design rationale displays that give personnel something to check](#bookmark=id.sazux36izue4)). How accurately a rationale can reflect the AI system’s process depends on how the AI system reasons. For learned components, confidence and rationale can be derived from calibrated probabilities and decision-level telemetry (Implementation [4.6.2: Derive confidence and rationale for learned components from their decision processes](#bookmark=id.kdty3el67ume)). Where the AI system, or part of it, reasons symbolically or is a self-interpretable model, the rationale can be a trace of the steps it actually applied (Implementation [4.6.3: Display execution traces from symbolic, hybrid, and self-interpretable components](#bookmark=id.ddgwjbohk09a)). In either case, a rationale shows at most how the AI system reached its output, not that the output is correct. This task complements Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq) and Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y), which address what information reaches personnel and in what volume, by addressing whether the confidence and rationale in that information reflect how the output was produced.   **Search Keywords:** explanation fidelity, faithful explanation, explanation accuracy, introspective explanation, post-hoc explanation, SHAP, LIME, rationale display, decision support, confidence calibration, reasoning trace, rule trace, interpretable model, self-interpretable model, decision tree, hybrid AI, neuro-symbolic AI, over-reliance, automation bias **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:**  **Explainable & Interpretable:** Contributes explanation accuracy (see NIST IR 8312): the confidence and rationale that personnel see are derived from the process that produced the output, or are treated as presentation where they are not. It does not decide how that information is laid out or when it reaches personnel. For that, see Task [4.2: Ensure that personnel are able to gain and maintain situational awareness](#bookmark=kix.r74ert803tyq).  **Safe:** Contributes a check against rationale displays that make incorrect recommendations more convincing: rationale is designed to be checked, and can be tested for whether personnel still reject incorrect recommendations. It does not supply the means of intervening. For that, see Task [4.1: Establish “break-glass” intervention procedures](#bookmark=kix.arzfz46ud7mj). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:**  **Accurate versus Explainable & Interpretable:** Where the knowledge a decision depends on can be stated as rules, constraints, or a self-interpretable model, the two need not separate: a symbolic or hybrid design can make the rationale an operator sees the reasoning the AI system actually used (Implementation [4.6.3: Display execution traces from symbolic, hybrid, and self-interpretable components](#bookmark=id.ddgwjbohk09a)). Where that knowledge cannot readily be stated, a learned model may perform better, and its rationale can be checked only indirectly (Implementation [4.6.2: Derive confidence and rationale for learned components from their decision processes](#bookmark=id.kdty3el67ume)). Whether interpretable models must in general give up accuracy is disputed (see NIST IR 8312), and the answer depends on how much of the domain can be stated, the same condition behind the tension in Task [1.4: Establish the AI System’s Domain Knowledge and Linguistic Alignment Requirements](#bookmark=kix.ddne831a11g5). One resolution, where the choice of approach is still open and operators must be able to check the reasoning at the moment of decision, is to weigh a symbolic or hybrid design for the part of the decision that can be stated. Others include relying more heavily on verifying the outputs of a learned model, or limiting its role to recommendations that personnel can check against independent evidence. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  **MEASURE 2.9** (on measuring interpretability and explainability):This Task requires that confidence and rationale shown to personnel derive from the process that produced the output, and rules out post-hoc attribution tools as the primary source; it does not cover capturing the decision-level telemetry that such a display depends on. For that, see Implementation [10.1.2: Immutable Runtime Telemetry and Artifact Tracking](#bookmark=id.xxgb2fikrm8l). **References:**  **Guidance** [NIST IR 8312, Four Principles of Explainable Artificial Intelligence](https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.IR.8312). Section 2.3 distinguishes explanation accuracy (whether an explanation reflects how the system reached its output) from decision accuracy, and Section 2.4 describes knowledge limits (a system identifying cases it was not designed for or cannot answer reliably). Section 6.1 describes self-interpretable models, which are themselves the explanation, and records that whether they trade accuracy for interpretability is disputed. It covers models such as decision trees, decision lists, and regression, not knowledge-based or hybrid systems. | **4.6.1: Design rationale displays that give personnel something to check** Where rationale is displayed, offer concise, "one-click" or hoverable rationale summaries highlighting the primary inputs, telemetry feeds, patient factors, transaction parameters, or business rules that drove the AI recommendation. Where a summary points to evidence that personnel can check for themselves, such as a sensor reading they can confirm against a second instrument, a transaction detail they can look up, or a rule they can compare with the governing procedure, it can help them judge a recommendation under time pressure without parsing raw logs. Rationale displays can help or hinder decisions, depending on the situation. A rationale can make a recommendation more convincing without making it easier to tell when it is wrong, and rationale displays have been found to increase acceptance of incorrect recommendations as well as correct ones (see Bansal 2021). Interface designers should therefore aim for rationale that gives personnel something to check rather than something to believe. They should also consider the time available to act: for responders who need only enough information to choose an emergency mode, see Implementation [4.2.1: Develop situational awareness processes and procedures for personnel who may need to intervene in the AI system’s operation](#bookmark=kix.xry0usuocd41). Where rationale is displayed to support decisions in the moment, consider testing under Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) whether it improves decisions for the personnel who will use it, including whether they still reject incorrect recommendations. For training personnel on the effect of rationale on reliance, see Task [9.2: Deliver specialized training on human-AI interaction and automation bias](#bookmark=kix.wdmhy3mmlldl). **References:**  **Research** [Bansal, G. et al., "Does the Whole Exceed its Parts?
