The Effect of AI Explanations on Complementary Team Performance", Proceedings of the 2021 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems](https://doi.org/10.1145/3411764.3445717). A study of crowdworkers deciding with the help of AI recommendations on text classification and reasoning tasks. Explanations were either generated post-hoc (using LIME) or written by an expert, and neither reflected the model's actual reasoning. They tended to raise accuracy when the AI was correct and lower it when it was wrong, with no net gain over showing a calibrated confidence score. The authors note the results may not generalize to expert users in high-stakes domains.  |
|  |  | **4.6.2: Derive confidence and rationale for learned components from their decision processes** To avoid misinforming personnel with uncalibrated confidence scores or plausible-sounding explanations that do not reflect reality, displayed confidence metrics, data freshness warnings, and rationale summaries should be derived from the underlying AI system's decision making processes. For learned components, examples include calibrated model probabilities and tamper-evident, decision-level runtime telemetry captured at inference time. Natural-language summaries generated by a language model should be treated as user-facing presentation layers rather than as evidence of model decision logic. Interface designers and system leads should not rely on post-hoc, perturbation-based explanation tools (such as SHAP or LIME) as primary sources for rationale displays, as these generate post-hoc hypotheses that need not correspond to the AI system's actual decision logic or internal state. For further discussion on capturing decision-level telemetry, see:  Implementation [8.1.2: Identify AI systems that may not satisfy root cause analysis requirements](#bookmark=kix.o0fgigw7uop7) (Forensic interpretability gaps and limitations of post-hoc attributions).  Implementation [10.1.2: Immutable Runtime Telemetry and Artifact Tracking](#bookmark=id.xxgb2fikrm8l) (Capturing decision-level runtime telemetry rather than relying on post-hoc explanations).  Implementation [12.1.2: Provenance and Traceability](#bookmark=id.cv8paq86ig7) (Linking generated logic strictly to hard inference-context metadata for audit paths). **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **4.6.3: Display execution traces from symbolic, hybrid, and self-interpretable components** Where the AI system, or part of it, reasons symbolically, for example by applying encoded rules, constraints, or a knowledge base, or is a self-interpretable model such as a decision tree, the displayed rationale can be a trace of the steps it actually applied rather than a hypothesis constructed after the fact (see Sheh 2021). In a hybrid system, such a trace is an accurate explanation only of the symbolic part: facts supplied by learned components, such as a classification of a vibration signature as a bearing fault, enter the trace as premises and should be displayed with their own uncertainty (Implementation [4.6.2: Derive confidence and rationale for learned components from their decision processes](#bookmark=id.kdty3el67ume)). Such a trace shows how the AI system reached its output, not that its rules are correct. Where a trace is too long to use under time pressure, it can be condensed by a deterministic approximation with known error, and the display should indicate that it has been condensed. For when such a design might be weighed against a learned model, see Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s).  **References:**  **Research** [Sheh, R., "Explainable Artificial Intelligence Requirements for Safe, Intelligent Robots", Proceedings of the 2021 IEEE International Conference on Intelligence and Safety for Robotics (ISR), pages 382 to 387](https://doi.org/10.1109/ISR50024.2021.9419498). A position paper on specifying explainability requirements for safety-critical robots. It distinguishes introspective explanations, drawn from the process that made the decision, from post-hoc explanations, and argues that local post-hoc methods such as LIME and SHAP hold only near the case being explained, within bounds that are hard to determine. It identifies decision trees, inductive logic programming, and models built on domain knowledge as able to give introspective explanations, argues that the trade-off against accuracy should be made deliberately, and notes that each stage of a system, from sensing to decision making, can have different explainability requirements. It does not address how explanations should be presented to operators.  |
| **5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools** Establish IdAM controls for AI agents and tools appropriate to their roles, auditability, risk, and oversight, at points in the AI system lifecycle from deployment through to decommissioning.  Establishing these boundaries reduces the risk of malicious or unauthorized actions reaching risk-sensitive processes, and limits the impacts across the operational environment. It reduces both the attack surface (by eliminating unauthorized access points and lateral pathways) and the overall risk surface (by bounding an agent's autonomous authority, operational scope, and “blast radius”) as defined in Task [3.5: Define and control risks to adjacent and related systems](#bookmark=kix.okyn4ifab2xw) and Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93).  This applies zero trust principles to AI entities: an authenticated AI agent is not thereby trusted with every action it can request, because an agent can act outside its intended scope without being compromised.  Controls modelled after human user permissions are a starting point but access should be further restricted based on actual need of individual AI agents and tools.  Access controls rely on effective asset visibility. Unauthorized "Shadow AI", including open-source models deployed without authorization, operates outside credential directories, bypassing identity controls and defeating least-privilege protections.  While the preventative site-access gates and procurement policies for these transient assets reside within the governance structures of Task [3.7: Govern and mitigate risks from unauthorized, transient, and "Shadow AI" systems](#bookmark=id.mxt6b4p4v993), the IdAM framework should actively interface with the network monitoring telemetry defined in Task [5.4: Implement continuous security monitoring for AI entity actions](#bookmark=kix.wzb03yleoteg). It should automatically detect, flag, and deny directory access to unauthorized AI entities attempting cross-segment authentication, whether a physical rogue device connected directly to an OT network port, or a localized open-source foundational model executing within an unvetted cloud container, virtualized shadow-IT tenant, or transient API instance. **\>To the Reviewer:**  \>We welcome suggestions for other ways to bring cybersecurity concepts into this document without duplicating cybersecurity-specific guidance.   **Search Keywords:**  non-human identity (NHI), service account, workload identity, least agency, least privilege, excessive agency, action-level authorization, task-bounded authority, cryptographically verifiable identity, machine-to-machine authentication, proxy agent, encapsulated tools, secrets management, credential rotation, just-in-time access, API key isolation, SOC behavioral monitoring, zero trust **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Secure:** Contributes identity and access boundaries for the AI agents and tools themselves, so that each one is recognized, held to the access its role needs, and cut off when it acts outside that role. It does not keep unvetted AI systems out of the operating environment, which Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) governs. **Safe:** Contributes a limit on how far a malfunctioning or compromised AI agent can reach into risk-sensitive processes, so consequence is bounded whether the cause is an attack or an error. It does not provide the means for people to intervene once an action already permitted is going wrong, which Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) establishes. **Accountable:** Contributes attribution: identities distinct enough that an action can be traced to the agent or tool that took it, allocated before deployment rather than reconstructed after an event. It does not capture the decision-level record of how the AI system arrived at that action, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Secure versus Reliable:** Where an AI agent's role is settled and its needs are known, confining it to that role costs little in operation. Where an operational need appears outside what was anticipated, or the decision to grant access cannot be reached in time, the same confinement stops legitimate work. One resolution is to settle in advance what the AI system should do when its authority cannot be confirmed, so the outcome is a designed behavior rather than an arbitrary one. Others include granting authority for a bounded period and renewing it as needed, or reducing what the operation depends on the AI agent to do.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 3.2** (on defining human-AI configuration):This Practice contributes the machine side of that configuration: which actions an AI entity may take on its own, and which need human approval; it does not define the oversight modes in which people supervise. For those, see Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud). **MAP 4.2** (on establishing risk controls for AI system components):This Practice treats identity, authorization and privilege limits as the internal controls applied to each AI component; it does not cover the deterministic constraints enforced at the physical process boundary. For those, see Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn). **GOVERN 1.6** (on building the AI inventory):This Practice extends the inventory to the credentialed AI entity, so that an entity missing from the directory is detected and denied at authentication; it does not cover the procurement gates that keep unvetted AI out. For those, see Task [3.7: Govern and mitigate risks from unauthorized, transient, and "Shadow AI" systems](#bookmark=id.mxt6b4p4v993). **MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Practice makes credential revocation the mechanism by which one misbehaving AI entity is disengaged without disturbing the rest of the architecture; it does not cover retiring an AI asset at end of life. For that, see Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych).   **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Section 6.1.1, Asset Management, sets out the inventory that access control depends on: unique identifiers, hardware and software inventory, and the vendor and ownership information attached to each asset. Section 6.3.2.1, Network Monitoring, covers the telemetry side, including baselining device-to-device communication and identifying misconfigurations and malfunctions of networked devices, so it reaches non-adversarial hazards as well as attacks. **Working Document** [Accelerating the Adoption of Software and AI Agent Identity and Authorization](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/other/2026/02/05/accelerating-the-adoption-of-software-and-ai-agent/ipd), a concept paper from the National Cybersecurity Center of Excellence at NIST. Sets out the problem this Practice addresses and the state of work on it, rather than controls to apply. **Guidance** [Agentic Identity and Access Management](https://www.coalitionforsecureai.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/agentic-identity-and-access-control.pdf), Workstream 4: Secure Design Patterns for Agentic Systems, version 1.0, 20 March 2026, from the Coalition for Secure AI, an OASIS Open Project. Addresses this Practice's subject directly. Section 3.1 holds that enterprises must treat agents as first-class identities, distinct from both human users and traditional service accounts, each with a unique persistent identity that survives its lifecycle and static and dynamic claims attached to it. Section 3.4 covers authorization and delegation, including the case of an agent acting on behalf of a user whose identity and intent are carried into the workflow, and separating an agent's own baseline rights from the rights it exercises on that user's behalf.   | **5.1: Establish identities and authentication for AI entities** Inventory AI entities and define authentication requirements that uniquely identify each AI entity, including those that are ephemeral, and ensure those identities are distinct across system boundaries, operational roles, and multi-agent deployment. In this task, an AI entity refers to an invocation of an AI agent or tool that performs atomic tasks, at a suitable level of granularity, and whose behavior should be meaningfully traced and disambiguated from the behavior of other invocations of the same or similar AI agents and tools. If the AI entity is compromised or otherwise found to be behaving in an undesired manner, the level of granularity should be sufficient to identify and revoke access while minimizing the effect on other AI entities.  The end of an identity's lifecycle, where it is withdrawn permanently rather than revoked in response to a problem, is in Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych). The appropriate level of granularity will vary depending on variables that include:  Available resources (finance, time, expertise).  Expected threat level.  Expected impact.  Other mitigating controls.  Scale.  Vendor co-operation.  Application complexity.  Organizations should implement this task flexibly, to allow for changes in granularity as these variables change.  Where cryptographic authentication is not possible, such as with legacy or compute-constrained OT devices, alternative controls may be necessary and enforced at an alternative point in the system.  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Accountable:** Contributes attribution at the granularity at which behavior needs to be disambiguated: a distinct identity for each AI entity, including ephemeral invocations, so an action traces to one invocation rather than to a shared service account. It does not record what that entity then did, which Task [5.4: Implement continuous security monitoring for AI entity actions](#bookmark=kix.wzb03yleoteg) logs at the access layer and Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) at the decision layer. **Secure:** Contributes mutual, cryptographically verifiable authentication between an AI entity and the system it calls, so a spoofed component is kept from presenting itself as a trusted agent, and a compromised one can be cut off by revoking its credentials. One entity's credentials can be revoked without withdrawing those the rest of the deployment depends on. It does not determine what an authenticated entity may reach, which Task [5.2: Determine and implement specific access requirements](#bookmark=kix.d2o6bmrmrgug) determines. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accountable versus Reliable:** Where AI entities are software invocations on capable hosts, splitting identity finely improves attribution at modest cost. Where the entity runs on legacy or compute-constrained equipment, per-invocation cryptographic credentials may exceed what the device can carry, and issuing them can degrade the function the device performs. One resolution this Task names is to enforce the identity at an alternative point in the system and accept coarser attribution at the device. Others include revisiting the granularity as resources, scale or expected threat change, which this Task asks organizations to plan for. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 1.6** (on building the AI inventory):This Task inventories AI entities at the granularity of a single agent or tool invocation, which is the unit at which an action can be attributed; it does not cover the system-level register of deployed AI. For that, see Task [7.1: Maintain an Internal Registry of AI Deployments](#bookmark=kix.svuun1hdnz08). **MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Task sets the identity granularity that lets one compromised entity be revoked while the others keep running; it does not bound how long an authority lasts when nothing has gone wrong. For that, see Task [5.3: Implement principles of least agency and least privilege for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.qgwy2q3nxzcn).  | **5.1.1: Audit for and replace shared credentials, service accounts, secrets, passwords, and API keys** Technically enforce distinct, cryptographically verifiable non-human identities (NHIs \- credentials designed to be issued to software/AI agents rather than people) for every autonomous sub-agent, micro-service hook, or transient tool invocation, particularly in AI systems built from multiple independent parts that each act and access resources on their own, including agentic AI systems. Where technically feasible, avoid the reuse of a single, shared enterprise API key or master service account across multiple independent agent nodes. For compute-constrained or legacy assets where unique cryptographic identities cannot be natively generated, enforce compensating controls at an alternative point in the system. Relying on shared accounts or group credentials heavily degrades situational awareness and complicates post-incident forensics because it obscures the true root cause of an action. In a complex or chained multi-agent system, being able to trace actions back to a unique, non-shared programmatic identity is essential for granular attribution and containment. Appropriately implementing granular NHIs helps ensure that if an individual model node experiences behavioral drift, runtime errors, or prompt compromise, its specific access tokens, API keys, session credentials, or similar can be isolated and revoked by the Security Operations Center (SOC) without invalidating the wider multi-tiered asset application architecture. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Section 6.2.1, Identity Management and Access Control, covers credential issuance, revocation and update for users, devices and services in an OT environment, and the compensating countermeasures to select where equipment cannot support authentication. Within it, section 6.2.1.4.5 states that shared credentials limit the ability to positively identify the person, process or device that accessed a protected resource, which is the attribution loss this Implementation acts on. |
|  |  | **5.1.2: Establish unique identities for AI service accounts and agents** Assign each AI service account or agent a unique, non-shared identifier at deployment, distinct from any human user account it may act on behalf of. For example, a grid-optimization agent and a chatbot summarizer running on the same server should never share a single service account, since doing so makes it impossible to attribute an anomalous action to a specific system during a review. Similarly, an AI assistant working on behalf of a person should be assigned a separate identity that allows its behavior to be logged and permissions managed separately from the person.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **5.1.3: Implement cryptographically verifiable identities and automated mutual authentication** Require AI entities to authenticate using cryptographic credentials (such as certificates or signed tokens) rather than static passwords, and require the receiving system to verify the AI entity's identity in return before exchanging data or commands. This helps prevent a spoofed component from posing as a trusted AI agent (this is a gap that default or reused credentials on exposed devices can open).\>TBD \- mini case study on these incidents **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **5.2: Determine and implement specific access requirements** Define specific systems, data sources, and tools each AI entity is authorized to access, and ensure those permissions are technically enforced and auditable against the scope of deployment and operational context. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Secure:** Contributes the enforced boundary between an authenticated AI entity and the assets it may reach, recorded in a map kept current as roles and deployments change, so the reach of a compromised entity is a known quantity. It does not detect an entity misusing access it legitimately holds, which Task [5.4: Implement continuous security monitoring for AI entity actions](#bookmark=kix.wzb03yleoteg) monitors. **Safe:** Contributes authorization of the individual action at the moment of execution, against the requesting entity's task scope, parameter limits and operational state, so an authenticated session carries no blanket permission for the consequential commands that follow. It does not define the state the process enters when an action is refused, which Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p) specifies. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Safe versus Safe:** Where the refused action is one the process can proceed without, blocking it is the safe outcome and the trade does not arise. Where the entity is part-way through a sequence, refusal can leave the process in a state that is itself hazardous, and Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p) notes that a fail-safe state is not necessarily simply stopping. One resolution is to design what refusal means for each consequential action, so a blocked command lands in a defined state rather than in whatever state the process was in. Others include placing the authorization check before a sequence begins rather than at each step within it, or scoping the grant to the whole sequence where the steps cannot be safely separated. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MAP 4.2** (on establishing risk controls for AI system components):This Task records which systems, data sources and tools each AI entity may reach, and requires that record to be technically enforced and auditable; it does not pare those permissions back to the minimum. For that, see Task [5.3: Implement principles of least agency and least privilege for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.qgwy2q3nxzcn). **GOVERN 3.2** (on defining human-AI configuration):This Task puts the human approval point inside the runtime authorization decision, so that a consequential action is checked individually rather than granted by an authenticated session; it does not cover authorizing the people who modify AI systems. For that, see Task [7.4: Define Authorization and Access Controls for AI Modifications](#bookmark=kix.ui59uhrv51qo).   | **5.2.1: Map and document AI agent access to operational and data assets** Create and maintain a record of the systems, data sources, and physical assets each AI agent can reach, updated whenever an agent's role or deployment changes. This map is the prerequisite for enforcing least privilege in Task [5.3: Implement principles of least agency and least privilege for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.qgwy2q3nxzcn) and for scoping what a compromised agent could actually affect. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **5.2.2: Action-level, context-aware authorization policies** Evaluate and authorize individual AI actions (such as invoking tools, modifying setpoints, or executing financial transactions) at runtime before execution.  Authorization checks should evaluate the requesting AI agent's identity, current task scope, parameter limits, operational state, and any required human approvals. An authenticated session or prior authorization should not grant blanket permission for subsequent commands.  If an action is unauthorized or authority cannot be verified, the system should block the request or transition to a pre-defined safe state (see Implementation  [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p)). This helps ensure that an authenticated AI agent with general system access is explicitly authorized for each specific consequential command.  This Implementation decides whether a specific proposed action is permitted at the moment of execution. That is a different question from whether the requesting entity is permitted to interact with the system at all, which is established elsewhere in Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck), and in Task [7.4: Define Authorization and Access Controls for AI Modifications](#bookmark=kix.ui59uhrv51qo) for people modifying AI systems. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-207, Zero Trust Architecture](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/207/final), from NIST. Section 2 defines zero trust around least privilege per-request access decisions taken in a network treated as compromised. The tenets in Section 2.1 include that authentication and authorization to one resource will not automatically grant access to a different resource, which is the principle that this Implementation extends to individual actions within an authenticated session. **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-162, Guide to Attribute Based Access Control (ABAC) Definition and Considerations](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/162/upd2/final), from NIST. Section 2.2 gives the working definition: an access decision computed by matching subject attributes, object attributes and environment conditions against a policy. Environment conditions are the dynamic factors independent of subject and object, which is the mechanism by which operational state enters a decision taken at the moment of the request. |
|  |  | **5.2.3: Determine the appropriate level of agent, asset, and permission granularity** Define how finely access should be split, for example, whether a single agent identity should cover: Control of an entire pipeline segment versus individual valves.  Edit rights to an entire workspace versus individual documents.  Single-signon access to post emergency updates via all social media accounts versus individual accounts.  This should be based on the consequence tier of the asset and the resources available to manage that granularity. Legacy or compute-constrained OT devices may require coarser groupings with compensating controls, while high-consequence assets may warrant the finest practical split. **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **5.3: Implement principles of least agency and least privilege for AI entities** Least agency requires that the system's capability to initiate independent actions or invoke external operational tools is minimized to only what is strictly necessary for its defined function. This is distinct from least privilege which restricts what it is allowed to do should access be attempted.  This helps ensure that systems are restricted from being granted permanent access to control systems, configuration interfaces, or operational tools beyond the active operational need, and that they avoid accessing these unnecessarily even when access exists. Minimize access permissions defined under Task [5.2: Determine and implement specific access requirements](#bookmark=kix.d2o6bmrmrgug) to only what is strictly necessary for the AI entity’s defined function.  Controls based on those applied to human users are a suitable point to build from, with additional controls put in place to account for AI agents and systems that may:  Have limited needs and whose access should be further compartmentalized.  Be influenced by multiple people beyond those notionally responsible.  Interact with other AI agents and systems, resulting in potential emergent behavior.  Operate at speeds beyond those expected of humans.  Be unable to provide meaningful explanations for their actions.  Where authority is withdrawn permanently rather than bounded, Implementation [13.2.2: Revoke authority delegated to other entities, and record what could not be confirmed](#bookmark=id.brqjlsfeexjz) suggests that the withdrawal reaches every level of delegation and that anything which cannot be confirmed revoked is recorded. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Safe:** Contributes a structural limit on what an AI system can set in motion: the capability to invoke operational tools is held to the defined function, and high-consequence actions are reached through a deterministic intermediary holding the execution credentials. It does not supply the validation logic and parameter collars that intermediary applies, which Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) supplies. **Secure:** Contributes the removal of standing authority: permissions bounded to a task, mode or time window, with expiration and rapid revocation, so a compromised entity holds less at any moment than permanent access would give it. It does not establish the identity whose authority is being bounded, which Task [5.1: Establish identities and authentication for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.46vcje448pa0) establishes. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Accountable:** Where a complex AI system reaches an asset through an encapsulated downstream agent, the credential presented at the asset belongs to that intermediary rather than to the system that asked for the action. Tracing the action back to the requesting entity then depends on that entity's own identity under Task [5.1: Establish identities and authentication for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.46vcje448pa0) appearing in the record, and on the access event logging in Task [5.4: Implement continuous security monitoring for AI entity actions](#bookmark=kix.wzb03yleoteg). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Resilient versus Secure:** Where the authority service is reachable, task-bounded and revocable authority and continued operation move together. Where a site is partitioned, keeping an entity working means letting it act on authority that nobody present can withdraw. One resolution, which Implementation [5.3.2: Task-Bounded and Revocable Agent Authority](#bookmark=kix.u9imrlz0bbhe) takes, is to revert to pre-engineered local fallback behavior rather than maintain unverified standing authority. Others include siting an authority service where the partition leaves it reachable, or narrowing in advance what an entity may do while partitioned. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems):This Task makes authority expire by default, bounding it to a task, mode or time window so that standing privilege lapses without anyone intervening; it does not establish the identities that make selective revocation possible. For that, see Task [5.1: Establish identities and authentication for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.46vcje448pa0). **MANAGE 2.1** (on allocating resources):This Task substitutes a deterministic, single-purpose proxy tool for the probabilistic agent at the point where a high-consequence action is executed, which is a non-AI alternative applied narrowly; it does not consider replacing the AI function as a whole.  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), Version 2026, published December 2025 by the Agentic Security Initiative of the OWASP Gen AI Security Project, a volunteer community project of the Open Worldwide Application Security Project rather than a standards body or a public authority. Entry ASI02, Tool Misuse and Exploitation, carries this Task's first principle under its own name: the leading mitigation is titled Least Agency and Least Privilege for Tools, and asks for per-tool profiles setting scopes, maximum rate and egress allowlists, with read-only database queries and summarizers holding no send or delete rights as worked examples. Entry ASI03, Identity and Privilege Abuse, carries the second: un-scoped privilege inheritance down a delegation chain, and authorization that was valid when a workflow began but not when it executed, answered by task-scoped and time-bound permissions with automated revocation. The document holds the two apart as this Task does, with ASI02 covering an agent that misapplies a tool inside the privileges it already holds and ASI03 covering an agent that acquires privileges it should not have.  | **5.3.1: Determine the practical minimum necessary permissions for a specific task and apply them to the AI system** Starting from the access map in Implementation [5.2.1: Map and document AI agent access to operational and data assets](#bookmark=kix.4sabjoovf2rt), pare each AI agent's permissions down to only what its current task requires, removing standing access to systems it does not actively need. For example, an AI system that only reads sensor telemetry should never retain write access to control setpoints, even if it was granted that access during initial testing. \>TBD: Add tension with possible unusual needs **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **5.3.2: Task-Bounded and Revocable Agent Authority** Bound an AI agent's authority to a specific task, operational mode, or time window rather than granting permanent standing privileges.  When an AI system delegates execution to downstream tools or sub-agents, authority should be explicitly scoped per task. If a downstream agent or tool possesses specialized privileges as described in Implementation [5.3.3: Separate privilege via encapsulated downstream agents and tools](#bookmark=id.p1isj88exw8y), strict, deterministic validation and hard-coded operational boundaries should be enforced to prevent execution beyond its intended design. Systems should support rapid authority revocation and automatic expiration. In partitioned or remote environments where real-time authority checks may fail, systems should revert to pre-engineered, local fallback behaviors (Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p)) rather than maintaining unverified standing authority. |
|  |  | **5.3.3: Separate privilege via encapsulated downstream agents and tools** Avoid granting complex, probabilistic, adaptive AI agents and systems, and those with exposure to untrusted inputs, direct access to high-consequence actions on sensitive assets (e.g., write access to PLCs, databases, or emergency controls).  Instead, such AI agents or systems should issue structured requests to specialized downstream “proxy” tools or agents that hold the necessary execution credentials and maintain their own independent validation logic, parameter collars, state checks, and safety constraints described in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn).  This structural separation helps to prevent an AI model hallucination or prompt compromise from resulting in out-of-bounds physical or digital actions, as execution authority is mediated by a deterministic, single-purpose boundary. For shared assets, it also avoids relying on the guardrails of each upstream AI agent or system.  |
|  | **5.4: Implement continuous security monitoring for AI entity actions** Continuously monitor AI entity actions against defined access boundaries to detect out of scope actions, unauthorized access attempts, and privilege escalations. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Secure:** Contributes detection of the entity that authenticates correctly and then acts beyond its boundary, through monitoring against defined access boundaries and against an established pattern of access. It does not set the boundaries a deviation is measured against, which Task [5.2: Determine and implement specific access requirements](#bookmark=kix.d2o6bmrmrgug) and Task [5.3: Implement principles of least agency and least privilege for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.qgwy2q3nxzcn) set. **Transparent:** Contributes a record of AI entity authentication and access events, including the attempts that failed, together with a defined cadence at which a person examines it. It does not capture the inputs, prompts and model context needed to reconstruct why an action was taken, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Safe:** Detection at machine speed produces notifications, and an entity population that is partly ephemeral produces deviations from an established pattern as a matter of course. Where those notifications reach the same people who watch operations, Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) treats notification volume as a designed quantity rather than a byproduct. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MEASURE 2.4** (on monitoring deployed AI systems):This Task monitors AI entity actions in production against defined access boundaries, and alerts in real time where a later log review would arrive too late; it does not monitor the quality of what the AI produces. For that, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk). **MEASURE 2.7** (on measuring security and resiliency):This Task supplies continuous runtime evidence of security posture, in the form of authentication, access and privilege-escalation events; it does not evaluate the resilience that MEASURE 2.7 also names. For that, see Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41).  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [MITRE ATLAS](https://atlas.mitre.org/), from MITRE, a United States not-for-profit corporation that operates federally funded research and development centers. A public catalog of adversary tactics and techniques observed against AI systems, used here to categorize runtime telemetry anomalies and to guide post-incident triage. | **5.4.1: Establish logging and periodic review of AI agent authentication and access events** Log every authentication attempt and access request made by an AI entity, including failures, and assign a defined cadence (such as weekly or after any privilege change) for a person to review this log for irregular patterns. This helps to narrow the gap that opens where access is technically logged but never actually reviewed until after significant damage has occurred.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **5.4.2: Deploy real-time behavioral anomaly detection for AI agent activity within the Security Operations Center (SOC)**  Extend the periodic review in Implementation [5.4.1: Establish logging and periodic review of AI agent authentication and access events](#bookmark=kix.lit92ffqmn7h) with automated, real-time alerting when an AI agent's access pattern deviates from its established baseline, such as an unusual login time, an unfamiliar source location, or a spike in request volume. This matters most in machine-speed environments where a human reviewing logs after the fact may be too late to prevent harm.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
| **6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management** Apply pre-acquisition and procurement-phase governance over external vendor dependencies and third-party assets. Managed primarily by Procurement, Legal, and Third-Party Risk Management teams, this practice extends traditional supply chain oversight to account for AI’s unique external concerns. These include:  Data provenance  Data drift Model integrity Supervision of retrieval augmentation (systems that retrieve data to help generate their outputs)  Supervision of agentic systems The latter two have their own autonomy over the data they consider, and the flow of privileged information among numerous AI agents and tools.  This practice addresses the governance of external vendor dependencies and third-party assets. It outlines supply chain mechanisms, specifically machine-readable AI Bills of Materials (AIBOMs) and the disclosures of Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q), needed to verify whether upstream third-party model hazards actively expand the enterprise's reachable risk surface (Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93)) or have been rendered operationally unreachable by other controls.  For post-acquisition operator controls over internal data and localized assets, see Practice [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m). *Note on Supply Chain Transparency:* While Software Bills of Materials (SBOMs) pair with VEX by referencing centralized vulnerability databases (CVEs), AI Bills of Materials (AIBOMs) catalog probabilistic model components where no universal vulnerability database exists. This profile explicitly separates traditional software VEX (for underlying ML code libraries) from Contextual Hazard Reachability (which evaluates whether probabilistic model failure modes can reach physical control paths). **Search Keywords:**  AI bill of materials (AIBOM), software bill of materials (SBOM), supply chain risk management (SCRM), third-party risk management (TPRM), fourth-party risk, vendor due diligence, procurement requirements, contractual flow-down, model card, model provenance attestation, contextual hazard reachability, VEX for AI, silent update risk, resilient-by-design, data egress monitoring **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Transparent:** Contributes the pre-acquisition disclosure set for components the operator cannot instrument from the inside: AIBOMs, model cards, provenance attestations and hazard reachability statements, obtained by contract rather than by logging. It does not cover assets under direct organizational control, which Practice [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m) covers. **Accountable:** Contributes allocation of responsibility to the vendor in advance, through procurement terms and service-level agreements, rather than after an incident. It does not make a vendor's attestations true: the operator still has to corroborate each before relying on it. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Secure:** The disclosures this Practice requires list what a system is made of, how it fails, and what stands in the way of each hazard. Protecting those documents is a matter for the organization's existing information classification and handling practice.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 6.1** (on establishing third-party risk management processes):This Practice contributes the AI-specific disclosures, attestations and inventory that such processes need to require; it does not address the intellectual property and rights infringement that GOVERN 6.1 also names. **GOVERN 6.2** (on establishing contingency plans for critical third parties):This Practice provides the pre-acquisition visibility that makes a third-party failure detectable and an exit possible; it does not define the contingency response itself. For incidents see Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb), Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f). **GOVERN 1.6** (on building the AI inventory):This Practice extends the asset inventory to externally sourced models, tools and services; it does not cover AI assets under direct organizational control. For those, see Practice [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m).   **References:** \>None yet \- suggestions welcome | **6.1: Develop policies for AI supply chain requirements** Establish organizational policies that, for each application of AI, define minimum trustworthiness and visibility requirements for AI-related vendors and assets.  These policies should provide a scalable framework where the rigor of due diligence, such as requirements for AIBOMs, model cards, data provenance attestations, and adversarial robustness testing, is based on the system’s criticality and the organization's risk tolerance.  By formalizing these expectations in procurement and service-level agreements, the organization helps ensure a consistent baseline for trust while remaining adaptable to varying resource levels and application threat profiles. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Valid:** Contributes procurement tests of fit for this deployment: attested representation of similar environments in the training data (Implementation [6.1.2: Require AIBOM for mission-critical applications](#bookmark=kix.86017r9tu0hx)) and demonstrated proficiency in sector terminology and standards (Implementation [6.1.3: Domain-Specific Terminology and Contextual Attestation](#bookmark=kix.qbvml0fn317r)). It does not confirm fit in operation, which Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b) and Implementation [2.3.1: Baseline AI system performance](#bookmark=kix.379o6rmrakb9) do. **Robust:** Contributes upstream characterization of behavior beyond expected conditions, in failure envelopes, boundary constraints and vendor-supplied test vectors (Implementation [6.1.4: Manufacturer Accountability and AI Resilient-by-Design](#bookmark=id.pebgeyv0y8fw)), placing the initial profiling burden on the manufacturer. It does not set the robustness expectations those envelopes are judged against, which Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) sets. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Reliable:** Due-diligence rigor is set once, from criticality assessed at acquisition, while Implementation [6.1.1: Add AI disclosure to existing procurement policies](#bookmark=kix.untd4qjdgbrs) notes that AI features arrive during otherwise routine updates, so a component can drift away from the profile it was scoped against. Task [6.2: Align AI and existing CI asset lifecycle management](#bookmark=kix.j8kjkb5179e3) carries the re-evaluation trigger. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 6.1** (on establishing third-party risk management processes):This Task sets the minimum trustworthiness and visibility that a third-party AI policy should require, tiered by criticality; it does not cover enforcement once a vendor is engaged. For that, see Task [6.2: Align AI and existing CI asset lifecycle management](#bookmark=kix.j8kjkb5179e3). **MAP 4.1** (on understanding AI component-level risks):This Task makes AIBOMs, model cards and provenance attestations the mechanism by which third-party component risk is mapped; it does not perform that mapping for a given system. For the mapping itself, see Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93). **MAP 1.5** (on documenting risk tolerances):This Task consumes documented risk tolerances to set proportionate due-diligence tiers; it does not determine those tolerances. For those, see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d).   | **6.1.1: Add AI disclosure to existing procurement policies**  Leverage existing legal/contractual workflows to provide baseline visibility into hidden AI dependencies in standard IT/OT/ICS software, particularly when AI features are added during otherwise routine updates.  Procurement policies should explicitly require AI vendors to disclose:  The nature and frequency of model updates, including updates that do not require operator action to take effect (especially ‘silent’ updates and cloud-side changes). The provenance and version of training data used in each model release. Any changes to safety-relevant model behavior introduced in each update. Where feasible, establish contractual rights to suspend automatic updates pending internal review for any AI system that influences safety-critical CI operations. Organizations should carefully balance this suspension mechanism against the competing risk of delaying critical security patches or performance fixes, helping ensure that any update deferral is strictly time-bound, risk-assessed, and accompanied by enhanced temporary monitoring. Where such contractual obligations are unfeasible, organizations should substitute equivalent technical compensating controls to contain update risk. These alternative controls may include: Isolated Perimeter Staging: Route all incoming software updates or vendor endpoint connections through isolated staging networks, hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) test rigs, or digital twins to execute initial behavioral verification before promoting the update to the active production path. Network Egress and API Gates: Deploy strict network filtering or proxy blocks that restrict the AI application's ability to pull silent, cloud-side model changes or unvetted external dependencies at runtime until the connection is manually authorized by an administrator. Temporary Safety Wrapper Tightening: Temporarily apply stricter, pre-authorized threshold margins on independent safety wrappers (defined in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)) during the initial post-update deployment window. This step helps ensure that unexpected or erratic behavior introduced by the silent update can be blocked at the process boundary before it can cause operational harm. This implementation mitigates hazard risk of a compromised AI vendor update pipeline, where a trusted vendor's software update mechanism is breached, delivering malicious updates to AI systems across multiple CI operators simultaneously, as occurred with conventional software in the compromise described in CISA advisory AA20-352A.  In this scenario, the direct CI analog is an AI model that receives a silent update changing its detection behavior, classification thresholds, or safety logic without the operator's knowledge or review.  **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Secure versus Secure:** Where an update carries a feature or model change, suspending it pending review guards against a compromised vendor update pipeline at negligible security cost. Where the same channel carries a critical security patch, the hold extends exposure, so any deferral should be time-bound, risk-assessed and monitored. **Secure versus Reliable:** Where a vendor ships fixes separately from behavioral changes, a review hold costs little. Where one release carries both, holding it to review a silent model change keeps a known performance defect in service; isolated staging and tightened safety wrappers instead let the update land under containment. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-161 Revision 1, Cybersecurity Supply Chain Risk Management Practices for Systems and Organizations](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/161/r1/upd1/final), from NIST. Control SR-8, Notification Agreements, is the established form of what this Implementation asks of AI vendors: requiring suppliers to establish notification agreements with entities in the supply chain that have a role in a critical service or product. The base control covers notification of supply chain compromises, assessment results, or other organization-defined information, which an organization can define to include model changes. Control SR-4, Provenance, asks that provenance be documented for systems, components and associated data throughout the development lifecycle, and that software bills of materials be produced and digitally signed, which is the counterpart to the training data provenance and per-release version disclosures required here. **Incident Report** [Advanced Persistent Threat Compromise of Government Agencies, Critical Infrastructure, and Private Sector Organizations](https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa20-352a), advisory AA20-352A from the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, the federal agency responsible for civilian cybersecurity. A malicious binary was inserted into the vendor's software lifecycle and then signed by that vendor’s legitimate code signing certificate, reaching government agencies, critical infrastructure entities and private sector organizations through the normal update path. This is the hazard this Implementation transposes to a silent model update. |
|  |  | **6.1.2: Require AIBOM for mission-critical applications** AIBOM should be at an appropriate level of detail, from vendors for the current and each subsequent model release so that their AI system’s supply chain is documented. Ideally this should be machine readable.  It should include model(s) in use, primary training data sources, data retrieved at runtime, and software used at training and run time including any significant third-party dependencies.  Purposes for such an AIBOM include:  Monitoring for application and/or environment drifting outside the support of the training data and background information.  Detecting the presence of input data and information that becomes or is later found to be inaccurate or invalid.  Tracing errors, vulnerabilities, and threats. The concept of AIBOM is still in its infancy and as of writing, formal specifications are still being developed (see CISA 2026 SBOM Minimum Elements). Operators should consider their application’s risk profile in deciding on the level of detail that they should request of their vendors. For example, a vendor providing an AI system that relies on environmental sensor data as part of its mission-critical decision making should, at a minimum, be obliged to attest that the AI system’s training data includes an appropriate representation from similar environments.  If a vendor is unable to provide an appropriate AIBOM for a high-consequence application, the operator faces a hidden residual risk profile. Rather than treating this as an all-or-nothing requirement, organizations should request the most complete documentation available, then apply compensating controls proportional to the gaps, such as enhanced runtime monitoring, guardrails and watchdogs as described in Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk) and Implementation [2.2.2: Deploy “watchdog” controllers](#bookmark=kix.ygluakfzcdgd), and more frequent recurrent risk and TEVV evaluations as described in Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i).  Any gaps in meaningful supply chain documentation that cannot be compensated for should themselves be treated as a documented risk, escalated and factored into the organization's go/no-go deployment decision. **\>To the Reviewer:** We've framed this above as a gradient of compensating controls rather than a hard requirement, since we recognize AIBOM standards are still emerging and few vendors can fully meet this today. Does this framing strike the right balance for your procurement process, or would a different structure (e.g., minimum mandatory disclosures vs. best-effort extras) work better for your sector?   **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [2026 Minimum Elements for a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM)](https://www.cisa.gov/resources-tools/resources/2026-minimum-elements-software-bill-materials-sbom), published 29 July 2026 by the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. Sets out what a software bill of materials has to carry and is deliberately format-neutral, asking organizations to accept any widely used, interoperable and machine-processable format, and naming SPDX (ISO/IEC 5962:2021) and CycloneDX (ECMA-424) as the two in wide use. The section titled SBOM for AI Software Systems, at page 15, is the part that bears on this Implementation: it holds that because AI is software the minimum elements already illuminate many of its components, that there may be additional useful supply chain data for AI, for example what practitioners discuss as model cards and data cards, and that the document does not itself introduce elements for AI systems. **Standard** NERC CIP-013-2, Supply Chain Risk Management for High and Medium Impact BES Cyber Systems, from the North American Electric Reliability Corporation, the body that develops reliability standards for the North American bulk power system and enforces them on registered entities. The requirements at R1 and R2 apply to supply chain risk management for the high and medium impact BES Cyber Systems the title names. Energy sector only. **Standard** [System Package Data Exchange (SPDX)](https://spdx.dev/), a project of the Linux Foundation, published as ISO/IEC 5962:2021. A bill of materials format for software components. Its coverage of AI-specific components is limited in the ratified version, and AI content is being developed for a later release of the specification. **Standard** [CycloneDX](https://cyclonedx.org/), a flagship project of the Open Worldwide Application Security Project, published as ECMA-424, CycloneDX Bill of Materials Specification, December 2025\. A bill of materials format covering software, hardware and machine-learning components, with a machine learning bill of materials form already defined, and AI content under continued development. **Guidance** [Establish Risks and Controls for the AI Supply Chain, version 1.0](https://www.coalitionforsecureai.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/risks-and-controls-for-the-ai-supply-chain-v1.pdf), 12 June 2025, from Workstream 1, Software Supply Chain Security for AI Systems, of the Coalition for Secure AI, an OASIS Open Project whose other workstreams address adjacent parts of this profile's subject. Its Supply Chain Documentation Requirements section, at page 10, enumerates the runtime half of the inventory this Implementation asks for: retrieval-augmented generation infrastructure including embedding models and retrieval algorithms, vector database providers and their isolation mechanisms, caching systems with isolation and encryption, the tools and external systems reachable during inference, and dependency trees with their integrity verification methods. |
|  |  | **6.1.3: Domain-Specific Terminology and Contextual Attestation**  Establish procurement policies that require vendors to provide formal attestations or evidence, such as via Model Cards or AIBOM, demonstrating that pre-trained AI systems are proficient in the industry terminology, technical standards, and community-specific linguistic nuances of the target critical infrastructure sector. This helps ensure that systems, particularly those used for report summarization, predictive maintenance, or customer interaction, accurately interpret the specialized "language of the mission" before they are introduced into the operational environment. |
|  |  | **6.1.4: Manufacturer Accountability and AI Resilient-by-Design**  Specify AI-specific "Resilient-by-Design" principles, that build upon conventional "Secure-by-Design" guidance during procurement. Vendors should be required to deliver pre-tested model boundary constraints and standardized behavioral validation datasets (test vectors) tailored to the operational context.  Because probabilistic machine learning models cannot be made entirely deterministic or immune to unexpected inputs, policies should mandate that manufacturers explicitly document how their systems behave under stress, such as failure envelopes for contextual drift, adversarial prompting, or data schema variance. Vendors should provide clear instructions on how the model safely degrades or transitions to backup logic when these boundaries are exceeded, shifting the burden of initial behavioral profiling upstream to the manufacturer. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  **Guidance** [Guidelines for Secure AI System Development](https://www.ncsc.gov.uk/sites/default/files/documents/Guidelines-for-secure-AI-system-development.pdf), 2023, published by the United Kingdom National Cyber Security Centre and the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency with twenty-one further national agencies. Organizes secure-by-design practice across four stages of the AI system lifecycle. Section 1, Secure design, covers integrating the most secure settings into a system by default and applying restrictions to the actions an AI component may trigger, including non-AI fail-safes. Section 2, Secure development, asks that documentation cover intended scope and limitations, guardrails, cryptographic hashes or signatures, and potential failure modes. Section 3, Secure deployment, covers computing and sharing hashes or signatures of model files and datasets so that consuming systems can validate them, and being clear to users about known limitations and potential failure modes on release. **Guidance** [ISO/IEC TR 5469:2024, Artificial intelligence: functional safety and AI systems](https://www.iso.org/standard/81283.html), a Technical Report published jointly by ISO and IEC. Addresses the use of AI within functions that carry a safety role, which is the question an operator faces when a vendor's probabilistic component sits alongside deterministic protection. |
|  |  | **6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures** AI vendors and system integrators should supply machine-readable hazard reachability attestations, adapting Vulnerability Exploitability eXchange (VEX) workflows, alongside AIBOM disclosures.  Organizations should recognize a fundamental distinction between software-level vulnerabilities and model-level behavioral risks:   Deterministic Software Dependencies (Standard VEX): For traditional software libraries, machine learning frameworks (e.g., PyTorch, CUDA, vLLM), and execution drivers listed in the AIBOM, vendors should supply standard Vulnerability Exploitability eXchange (VEX) statements (e.g., CSAF or CycloneDX) that cross-reference known CVE databases to declare whether software flaws are reachable in the compiled product. Probabilistic Model Behaviors (Contextual Hazard Reachability): Because no centralized, indexed vulnerability database (equivalent to CVE/NVD) exists for the stochastic failure modes of AI models (e.g., hallucination propensities, context window corruption, or distribution drift sensitivity), traditional VEX database lookups cannot be directly applied to AI model weights. Instead, vendors should adapt VEX workflow principles by providing a Contextual Hazard Reachability Attestation. This attestation documents whether a known model limitation identified during TEVV (Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) and Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41)) or mapped during risk-surface scoping (Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93)) is rendered operationally unreachable within the specific deployment architecture. Vendors declaring a model hazard as "Not Affected" should supply the machine-readable justification, such as independent safety wrappers (Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)), execution sandboxing (Implementation [3.5.3: Impact compartmentalization (sandboxing)](#bookmark=kix.ak8chxmlireg)), or strict least-agency tool restrictions (Task [5.3: Implement principles of least agency and least privilege for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.qgwy2q3nxzcn)). The existing VEX justification "Inline mitigations already exist" (see CISA 2023\) is limited to protections built into the product that the user cannot configure or disable, so controls that the operator deploys around the AI system would need to be stated as a separate justification. These measures are intended to break the reachability path between the AI flaw and physical or mission consequences. Contextual Hazard Reachability Attestations used to trigger auto-suppression under Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) should require either independent verification by the operator (e.g., reproducible test cases run against the actual deployment architecture) or corroboration against the operator's own TEVV evidence (Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b), Implementation [2.3.1: Baseline AI system performance](#bookmark=kix.379o6rmrakb9)) before being trusted to silence an alert.  Vendor self-attestation alone should not be treated as sufficient to suppress a warning. In traditional software, where the shared vulnerability database described above does exist, the tools that generate VEX data still show substantial disagreement about which vulnerabilities are present (see Churakova et al. 2026). Absent that database, a contextual attestation is harder to corroborate rather than easier.  **References:** The application of VEX workflows and concepts to AI risk reachability is still a topic of active work. The first two references describe the state of VEX practice in traditional software, which is the basis for the caution above. The remainder are cybersecurity-specific VEX resources that may be useful as a guide for how to incorporate these principles more broadly. **Research** Churakova, Y., Ekstedt, M., and Schmid, L., *Vexed by VEX Tools: Consistency Evaluation of Container Vulnerability Scanners*, in *Foundations and Practice of Security: 18th International Symposium, FPS 2025, Revised Selected Papers, Part I*, Lecture Notes in Computer Science volume 16402, Springer, 2026, pages 139 to 156\. Open access preprint at [arXiv:2503.14388](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14388). Research from KTH Royal Institute of Technology. Compares seven VEX-generation tools across 48 container images and finds a highest pairwise similarity of 69%, with most five-tool combinations agreeing on nothing at all and the best reaching 3.4%. It measures consistency between the tools that generate VEX data rather than the accuracy of any supplier's claim, so it bears on how much weight a single unchecked attestation can carry. **Commentary** [Signal in the Noise: An Industry-Wide Perspective on the State of VEX](https://openssf.org/blog/2026/01/08/signal-in-the-noise-an-industry-wide-perspective-on-the-state-of-vex/), from the Open Source Security Foundation, a cross-industry body hosted by the Linux Foundation, drawing on contributions from Amazon, Aquasec, Chainguard, Cisco, Debian, Ericsson, Freexian, Google, Microsoft, Red Hat and OpenSUSE. Reports that *"the reality today is that VEX feels more like a promise than a practice"*, and that for many enterprise consumers trust is *"currently based on where the file is hosted rather than cryptographic proof"*. **Guidance** CISA (2023) [Minimum Requirements for Vulnerability Exploitability eXchange (VEX)](https://www.cisa.gov/resources-tools/resources/minimum-requirements-vulnerability-exploitability-exchange-vex), from the United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, the federal agency responsible for civilian cybersecurity. Specifies the minimum elements a VEX document must carry, independent of format, including the justifications a supplier gives for a "Not Affected" status. That is the model Contextual Hazard Reachability Attestations adapt. **Guidance** CISA (2022) [Vulnerability Exploitability eXchange (VEX) \- Use Cases](https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/VEX_Use_Cases_April2022.pdf), from the same agency. Sets out scenarios with proposed implementations, showing how the minimum data elements are assembled in practice. **Commentary** Friedman, A. (2026) [Practical Guidance for Managing VEX Workflows](https://fossa.com/blog/allan-friedman-practical-guidance-managing-vex-workflows/), published by FOSSA, a commercial supplier of software composition analysis tooling that sells VEX workflow automation. Friedman led the SBOM and VEX communities at NTIA and then CISA and now advises independently, so this is one of VEX's principal advocates describing what running it actually requires, and observing that adoption is still catching up to SBOM.  |
|  | **6.2: Align AI and existing CI asset lifecycle management** Externally sourced AI components should be integrated into existing asset lifecycle management processes, helping ensure that authorized updates are formally managed via change control, while runtime telemetry is utilized to catch and isolate unannounced behavioral shifts resulting from silent third-party model modifications. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Reliable:** Contributes the record that keeps qualification evidence attached to a known configuration: model version, training data provenance and inference endpoint routed through change control, with re-evaluation on change (Implementation [6.2.2: Track vendor updates and third-party entity changes through change management process](#bookmark=kix.kiwwb3qmwzvw)). It does not cover drift within an unchanged model, which Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i) and Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk) discuss. **Accountable:** Contributes tracking of the counterparty itself, including acquisition, sub-processor change and end of support (Implementation [6.2.2: Track vendor updates and third-party entity changes through change management process](#bookmark=kix.kiwwb3qmwzvw)), so the party carrying the procurement obligations stays identified. It does not create those obligations, which Task [6.1: Develop policies for AI supply chain requirements](#bookmark=kix.71o0na2rpzas) creates. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Reliable versus any characteristic the release improves, including itself:** Where a vendor release is a feature or model change, reviewing it before deployment may add little risk. Where it carries a fix, such as to safety, security, accuracy or reliability, holding it defers that benefit, so Implementation [6.2.2: Track vendor updates and third-party entity changes through change management process](#bookmark=kix.kiwwb3qmwzvw) permits ungated deployment where delay is judged an unreasonable risk, recorded as a known limitation.   **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  GOVERN 1.6** (on building the AI inventory):This Task registers externally sourced models, tools and services in the existing asset management system at acquisition; inventory alone does not detect change, so this Task pairs registration with change control and telemetry. **MANAGE 3.2** (on monitoring pre-trained model risks):This Task routes vendor model updates through change control and watches for unannounced version shifts; it does not cover behavioral drift within an unchanged model. For that, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk). **MAP 4.2** (on establishing risk controls for AI system components):This Task treats change management and runtime telemetry as the internal controls applied to third-party components; it does not cover controls at the physical process boundary. For those, see Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn).   | **6.2.1: Register externally sourced AI components in asset management systems** Add every externally sourced AI model, tool, or service to the organization's existing asset management system at the point of acquisition, capturing vendor, version, and purpose so it is visible alongside traditional IT and OT assets rather than tracked separately or not at all.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome  |
|  |  | **6.2.2: Track vendor updates and third-party entity changes through change management process** Where possible, route vendor-issued updates and third-party configuration changes through the organization's standard change management process, helping ensure updates are reviewed and approved before deployment rather than applied silently. Where the deployment model does not permit an update to be gated, such as for cloud-hosted and vendor-managed models or where associated delays are deemed to incur an unreasonable risk, document that as a known limitation. Combine required disclosures, as discussed in Implementation [6.1.1: Add AI disclosure to existing procurement policies](#bookmark=kix.untd4qjdgbrs), with runtime monitoring and telemetry, as discussed in Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk), to detect unannounced behavioral shifts after the fact, rather than treating the component as static.  Changes to track include model version, training data provenance, inference endpoint, and change to the third-party entity itself, such as acquisition, sub-processor change, or end of support. Changes should trigger re-evaluation under Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i).  In particular, newly released or substantially revised models and other system components, AI or otherwise, warrant elevated monitoring and testing for a defined period after adoption. Treat these as a distinct category with a documented exit criterion for returning them to routine monitoring. Implementation [7.4.1: Define and implement requirements for approvals and logging of updates and changes to AI systems](#bookmark=kix.ro5z34rjokwl) sets the equivalent requirement for changes to internally sourced components.   **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **6.2.3: Manage end-of-life transitions for externally sourced AI components** Define a formal offboarding process for externally sourced AI components reaching end-of-life or vendor discontinuation, including secure decommissioning and confirmation that no dependent systems are left relying on an unsupported component. Vendor discontinuation is one of the external triggers for retirement recorded under Implementation [13.1.1: Identify retirement triggers, including those outside the organization's control](#bookmark=id.4abk974ttodd), and the withdrawal of the component's authority follows Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych).   **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **6.3: Identify and control dataflows across the AI supply chain** Define and maintain visibility over the dataflows between AI systems and external vendors, platforms, and services they connect to.  Ensure that data exchanged with these external dependencies, including telemetry, inference requests (a trained AI model producing an output for a given input), model updates, and any operational or sensitive information transmitted outside the organization is authorized, monitored, and bound by data handling policies. **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Privacy-Enhanced:** Contributes containment of what leaves continuously rather than as a one-off transfer: inference requests and operational telemetry, cataloged in Implementation [6.3.1: Catalog data egress points and third-party processing destinations](#bookmark=kix.iorw2lg5xnt) and routed through restricted enclaves in Implementation [6.3.2: Implement egress monitoring and isolated, access-restricted enclaves for AI training and telemetry](#bookmark=kix.x50lia9bh36t). It does not address privacy risk arising from the AI system's own outputs, which Implementation [7.5.3: Integrate Privacy Impact Assessments (PIA) into Update Cycles](#bookmark=kix.ry08l2wx613h) addresses in part, through privacy impact assessment at each significant update. **Secure:** Contributes exfiltration detection at the AI egress path, watching cataloged destinations for unexpected volume or destination change (Implementation [6.3.2: Implement egress monitoring and isolated, access-restricted enclaves for AI training and telemetry](#bookmark=kix.x50lia9bh36t)), which is how loss of a model, training data or telemetry through a vendor endpoint becomes visible. It does not authenticate the requesting AI entities, which Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) does. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Privacy-Enhanced versus Reliable:** Reliability may depend on 3rd party remote monitoring, such as by a vendor with specialized knowledge. Restricting telemetry may impair system reliability as the vendor may be unable to respond to degradation appropriately.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:  MANAGE 3.1** (on monitoring and responding to third-party risks):This Task monitors what leaves the organization to reach third-party AI services and applies egress controls; it does not monitor the third party's own behavior or performance. For that, see Task [6.2: Align AI and existing CI asset lifecycle management](#bookmark=kix.j8kjkb5179e3). **MEASURE 2.10** (on measuring privacy):This Task supplies the data-exposure inventory that a privacy-risk examination depends on; it does not perform that examination or assess privacy risk arising from the system's own outputs.  | **6.3.1: Catalog data egress points and third-party processing destinations** Identify every point where organizational data leaves the environment to reach a third-party AI vendor or service, and document what data is sent, why, and where it is processed, so the organization has a complete picture of its external data exposure.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **6.3.2: Implement egress monitoring and isolated, access-restricted enclaves for AI training and telemetry** Monitor the egress points cataloged in Implementation [6.3.1: Catalog data egress points and third-party processing destinations](#bookmark=kix.iorw2lg5xnt) for unexpected volume or destination changes, and route sensitive data used for AI training or telemetry through isolated, access-restricted environments rather than general-purpose network paths.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
| **7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance** Establish post-acquisition operational oversight for AI assets under direct organizational control.
