Managed primarily by Internal IT, Engineering, and Data Governance teams, this practice focuses on the internal mechanics of deployed systems, including local fine-tuning, retrieval-augmented data repositories, version control for safety wrappers, local data provenance and flow, and localized AI model and system configurations.  This practice also provides the visibility needed to mitigate 'Shadow AI' risks and helps ensure that internal modifications to automated logic remain traceable and mission-aligned. In addition, it provides actionable measures to implement Task [3.7: Govern and mitigate risks from unauthorized, transient, and "Shadow AI" systems](#bookmark=id.mxt6b4p4v993) and informs the need to update the AI attack and risk surface as discussed in Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93).  For pre-acquisition vendor vetting and procurement controls, see Practice [6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management](#bookmark=kix.gk6f3b3rnwyn).  **Search Keywords:**  data provenance, data lineage, master AI asset list, AI asset inventory, model registry, fine-tuning, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), vector store, prompt versioning, configuration management, policy-as-code, policy lock, online learning governance, champion-challenger testing, catastrophic forgetting, privacy impact assessment (PIA), substrate substitution, model archiving, data poisoning prevention  **Trustworthiness Characteristics:** \>None yet \- suggestions welcome  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome | **7.1: Maintain an Internal Registry of AI Deployments** Catalog internal instances, versions, and specific use-cases of AI systems deployed across different departments, and the authorization associated with each (including legacy AI systems that predate formal authorization).  This task helps ensure that internal stakeholders have a clear inventory of "where" AI is operating within the organization, enabling consistent oversight and maintenance even when models are embedded in departmental workflows. | **7.1.1: Create and maintain a “Master AI Asset List”**  Document the names, versions, vendors, hosts (cloud vs on-premises), responsible contacts, dependencies (such as required authorizations, data, compute, and other resources), operational purpose, and risk management controls for all AI systems and models.  This includes AI systems used for logistical or administrative purposes, such as chatbots, meeting transcribers, and document summarization tools.  Document model lineage and component hierarchy, tracking parent-child relationships between base foundation models, fine-tuned adapters (e.g., LoRAs), vector index embeddings, and quantized runtime distributions. |
|  |  | **7.1.2: Maintain a data inventory** Document organizational data sources that are, or may be, used in AI systems.  Use may include training, fine-tuning (further training an existing AI model on an organization's own data to adapt it), retrieval augmentation, or agentic use. Data sources may be static knowledge bases, historic, and/or real-time feeds.  The inventory should identify the data’s:  Source and provenance, including its origin and chain of custody. See Task [7.2: Validate data provenance for model inputs](#bookmark=kix.1ax7q7mnm5yj).  Role within the AI system, such as for training or retrieval augmentation.  Data type and classification, including format and sensitivity level.  Metadata and versioning, including verification of integrity such as hashes.  Licensing, terms, and other restrictions on usage.  Lineage and transformations performed, including cleaning, masking, and synthetic data generation.  Quality and integrity metrics and checks performed.  Ownership and accountability, including contacts for incident response.  Security and access controls, including identity and access management, logging, and encryption requirements.  Hosting and storage location, including redundancy and backups.   |
|  |  | **7.1.3: Integrate automated internal software and AI bill of material management**  Develop policies for regular scanning of internal networks for AI systems and behavior. This integrates with information obtained in Practice [6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management](#bookmark=kix.gk6f3b3rnwyn).  |
|  | **7.2: Validate data provenance for model inputs** Document the source, cleaning process, permitted use policies, and authorization of internal datasets (e.g., maintenance logs, sensor telemetry) used to fine-tune or prompt AI models.  This task improves visibility into the risk of internal "data silos" that introduce biased or unvetted information into AI systems, whether for training or in retrieval-augmented or agentic systems.  This visibility can also highlight opportunities to leverage data across departments while maintaining appropriate protections for that data.  The outputs of this task should be documented as set out in Implementation [7.1.2: Maintain a data inventory](#bookmark=kix.l547y7d01iim).  **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  NIST AI 100-2e2025 (contextualizing data supply-chain integrity controls against malicious training data poisoning or telemetry manipulation). | **7.2.1: Implement data quality monitoring**  Appropriately knowledgeable and resourced personnel should certify that organizational data to be used in an AI system, such as to train a model or to enable retrieval-augmented or database functionality, is appropriate for the task.  |
|  |  | **7.2.2: Verify data provenance**  Tools such as cryptographic hashing and data lineage tools should be used to maintain an immutable audit trail for tracing critical data from source to final AI system output.  |
|  |  | **7.2.3: Assess the conditions under which source data is created** Extend provenance validation upstream of ingestion to the point of capture. Data quality certification performed under Implementation [7.2.1: Implement data quality monitoring](#bookmark=kix.x0svr7f9v58s) can only assess data that successfully arrived; it cannot reveal records that were degraded, overwritten, or never transferred during collection. Document the field conditions, tooling, and manual steps involved in creating source data, and identify where those conditions systematically introduce loss or inconsistency. Potential failure modes include multi-stage transfer chains in which field personnel must move data from local device storage to intermediate media and then to a central or cloud repository, where an incomplete stage silently drops records; inadvertent overwriting of stored data during subsequent collection cycles; and loss or inconsistency introduced by migration between maintenance management systems (for example, CMMS platforms) or by limitations within them. Where a manual step cannot be removed, consider monitoring how completely it is performed, for example the proportion of expected records that reach the central repository. Where the capture environment is intermittently connected or resource-constrained, coordinate with Implementation [10.1.4: Asynchronous Telemetry and Inference Logging for Constrained Edges](#bookmark=id.vdwbfkx1mlpw). Findings should be recorded against the relevant entries in the data inventory maintained under Implementation [7.1.2: Maintain a data inventory](#bookmark=kix.l547y7d01iim), and should inform training delivered under Implementation [9.1.3: Include data-originating roles in competency mapping](#bookmark=id.73ub9anha1k5).  **References:**  **Research** [Kang, J. et al., "A data quality management framework for equipment failure risk estimation: Application to the oil and gas industry", Engineering Applications of Artificial Intelligence 136 (2024) 108834](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.engappai.2024.108834). A framework for deciding whether field data is of sufficient quality for an equipment failure risk estimation model, with a case study of electronic boards in downhole drilling tools. Section 3.4.2 briefly identifies root causes of poor field data, including manual transfer steps that field personnel skip, inadvertent overwriting, and loss or inconsistency from maintenance management system migrations, and suggests training and data collection KPIs as remedies.  |
|  | **7.3: Manage Version Control and Logging for Internal AI Safety Mechanisms** Establish rigorous configuration management and logging for the internal "wrappers," system prompts, and safety filters built around both internal and external AI systems.  This task helps ensure that the mechanisms used to constrain AI behavior, including those that relate to other practices, are documented, traceable, reversible, auditable, and protected from unauthorized internal modification. | **7.3.1: Maintain a registry of AI systems and changes**  This registry should uniquely identify the specific versions of the AI systems and models, versions of prompt templates, and the specific "if/then" safety logic used for output filtering. Document every change to these safety parameters in a centralized change log that identifies the person who authorized the change and the technical justification. |
|  |  | **7.3.2: Automate Policy-as-Code**  Translate safety and operational policies into automated, machine-readable logic rules embedded directly within the deployment workflow (often referred to as Policy-as-Code, utilizing tools like Open Policy Agent). Inference requests that possess mission impact should be logged with their full context, including version identifiers of safety filters and any triggered rules (e.g., "Blocked: Safety Boundary Violation"). All changes to underlying safety logic should require cryptographic signatures, appropriate approval oversight, and automated regression testing (informed by Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41)) to ensure updates do not inadvertently degrade system availability. |
|  |  | **7.3.3: Implement Logical Policy Locks for Adaptive and Online-Learning Models** For AI systems utilizing online learning (including reinforcement learning), continuous local fine-tuning, dynamic prompt optimization, real-time retraining, or other forms of adaptation at runtime, implement an automated "Policy Lock" that programmatically freezes all changes to the system during operational windows.  This may include model weights (the model’s internal, learned parameters) as well as other online adaptation configuration parameters such as hyper-parameters (pre-defined configuration settings that control how the model learns or operates), and boundary logic (the programmatic code defining the safe operating limits the system should stay within, as established in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)).  Shifting a deployed model from a static runtime state into a tuning cycle should require a physical confirmation step or an out-of-band administrative governance mechanism. This prevents streaming production data or unvetted operational telemetry from silently altering the model's learned behavioral thresholds outside of formal configuration change control channels. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  NIST SP 800-82r3 (Section 6.2.4.2 on Configuration Change Control) |
|  | **7.4: Define Authorization and Access Controls for AI Modifications** Establish strict identity-based access controls for individuals authorized to modify internal AI models, systems, AI safety mechanisms, datasets, or configurations.  This task implements and extends the principle of least privilege to ensure that only qualified personnel can alter the behavior of critical functions, reducing the risk of accidental or malicious internal interference. The controls in this Task determine who may act on the system. Whether a specific proposed action is permitted at the moment of execution is a separate question, addressed in Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) and Implementation [5.2.2: Action-level, context-aware authorization policies](#bookmark=kix.68ohugbvqcsm). | **7.4.1: Define and implement requirements for approvals and logging of updates and changes to AI systems** Approvals and logging are particularly important for changes to models, knowledge bases, and guardrails, especially for applications operating in a reduced oversight mode, or where automation bias is a known risk and thus where changes in behavior may not be immediately noticed.  Record for each change what was altered, who approved it, and the evidence the approval rested on, so that a later potential investigation can reconstruct why the change was judged safe. Where a change alters model behavior rather than configuration alone, treat it as a trigger for re-evaluation under Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i), the same treatment a disclosed vendor update receives for an externally sourced component (Implementation [6.2.2: Track vendor updates and third-party entity changes through change management process](#bookmark=kix.kiwwb3qmwzvw)). An internal change reaching production unreviewed is the internally sourced equivalent of a silent vendor update, and would otherwise be caught, if at all, only by Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk). |
|  |  | **7.4.2: Implement regular “integrity checks” for systems that interface with external AI services**  These should verify that external AI systems and services have not been unexpectedly altered. This may incorporate TEVV protocols such as those from Task [2.3: Establish protocols for and regularly perform systematic and achievable Testing, Evaluation, Validation, and Verification (TEVV)](#bookmark=kix.af6uispxw0j5).  |
|  | **7.5: Conduct Impact Assessments and Risk Management for AI System and Model Updates**  Establish procedures for and perform structured risk and performance assessment before deploying any internal updates to AI systems, such as model prompts, configuration changes, data inputs, or safety wrappers.  Where increases in risk are identified, if the deployment proceeds then mitigating controls should be identified and implemented.  This task verifies that "minor" internal adjustments do not unintentionally degrade overall system resilience or violate established functional safety benchmarks and requirements (see Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41)), be it through unintentional side effects of new behaviors or “catastrophic forgetting” (AI losing previously learned correct behavior after update or retraining) of previous good behavior. This task also applies where a change in operating context alters rules that are encoded in the AI system, such as in its prompts, retrieval corpus, or guardrails, even though nothing in the AI system itself has changed (see Implementation [1.1.3: Establish triggers for re-validating requirements on change of operating context](#bookmark=id.5udtrmqlq9tq)). | **7.5.1: Perform pre-update validation and re-calibration**  Validation and re-calibration should be performed before deploying any updates to models, system prompts, databases, and other components of AI systems that affect performance and safety. This should be informed by Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) and Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41).  Changes to performance should be documented and, where necessary, mitigating controls should be put in place prior to deployment.  For example, a new model for an AI system that summarizes and prioritizes equipment condition reports for maintenance scheduling might have improved accuracy overall. Pre-update validation might reveal an unacceptably higher false negative rate for specific types of equipment that could not be addressed by re-calibrating thresholds. Mitigating controls can then be put in place, such as having the AI system fall back onto the previous model for reports from these types of equipment.  |
|  |  | **7.5.2: Perform automated “champion-challenger” and drift assessment**  This should be done by concurrently running existing production AI systems (“champion”) alongside the prospective updated AI system (“challenger”) in a “shadow mode” to determine the differences in behavior.  The “challenger” system should demonstrate appropriate improvements in performance, as measured in Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) and Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41), utilizing evaluation metrics that align with Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b), and mitigating controls established, prior to switch-over to the new system.  This process also supports Practice [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) in training those interacting with the system, should this be required by any change in the behavior of the new system.  |
|  |  | **7.5.3: Integrate Privacy Impact Assessments (PIA) into Update Cycles**  Conduct a PIA for all significant model or system updates to identify risks related to data minimization, re-identification, and inference. Ensure that updates do not bypass existing privacy controls. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  [NIST Privacy Framework](https://www.nist.gov/privacy-framework) [NIST SP 800-53 Rev 5](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-53r5.pdf): Control RA-8, Privacy Impact Assessments, which applies where the AI system processes personally identifiable information; see also the Personally Identifiable Information Processing and Transparency (PT) family. |
|  |  | **7.5.4: Audit Interoperability for Hardware or Data Substrate Substitution** Establish a change-management trigger within the internal supply chain for when physical operational components or software data feeds (e.g., valves, pumps, switches, or sensors, or API schemas, electronic health record modules, or transaction journals) are replaced with equivalents from a different vendor or manufacturer.  Even if the replacement part complies perfectly with existing engineering specifications, the internal supply chain process should mandate a regression test of any governing AI systems to ensure the vendor swap does not invalidate undocumented physical dependencies embedded within the AI system's learned model as discussed in Task [2.5: Verify and Ensure Policy Robustness Against Component, Hardware, and Data Schema Variance](#bookmark=kix.8csdyl8bfyt8). If mandating full regression testing for every replacement-in-kind substitution is not feasible given the volume of routine component swaps in normal maintenance, organizations can consider the following guidance for action: If the substituted component has no plausible link to the AI system, such as an unrelated office HVAC sensor, standard engineering sign-off is sufficient. If the component sits within the AI system's operating envelope but outside any safety-critical path, such as a sensor on a non-critical secondary line, flag it for engineering review under Task [2.5: Verify and Ensure Policy Robustness Against Component, Hardware, and Data Schema Variance](#bookmark=kix.8csdyl8bfyt8) rather than a full regression test If the component sits within a safety-critical control path, such as a valve actuator on a shutdown system or a sensor feeding a chemical batch temperature loop, require full regression testing before deployment. |
|  | **7.6: Establish processes for internal AI component deprecation and decommissioning** Establish secure, structured protocols for the sunsetting, deprecation, and decommissioning of internally developed AI agents, systems, tools, models, training datasets, and associated software dependencies.  This task helps ensure that when internal AI assets reach end-of-life, they are safely removed from production environments without breaking legacy system dependencies or leaving behind unmonitored attack surfaces. This Task covers the artifacts: the models, datasets, pipelines and tools themselves. The withdrawal of an operating AI system's authority, the preservation of what it knew, and the decisions recorded for everything that depended on it are in Practice [13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority](#bookmark=id.ieoypls7vre2). | **7.6.1: Internal Dependency Mapping (Orphan System Prevention)**  Before decommissioning an internal AI agent, system, tool, model or API, organizations should conduct a comprehensive audit of the internal supply chain to identify all downstream IT, OT, or ICS systems that rely on its outputs. Establish strict "deprecation timelines" and notifications to ensure dependent systems are safely refactored or reverted to deterministic fallbacks before the model is turned off. The process of mapping dependencies is common to Implementation [3.5.2: Identify indirect shared infrastructure and collateral damage exposure](#bookmark=kix.hw9y3h9onk8j). If that mapping has already been performed and kept current, much of the groundwork for decommissioning will already be in place. Implementation [13.3.3: Determine and record what happens to everything that depended on the system](#bookmark=id.gknfd9voaeez) requires that a decision is recorded for each dependent identified here, including where that decision is to make no provision. |
|  |  | **7.6.2: Secure Archiving and Destruction of Model and Other AI Artifacts**  Define destruction (cryptographically secure as appropriate) or secure archiving standards for retired models, training pipelines, and datasets. This prevents "zombie models" from being accidentally used, resurrected or exfiltrated.  This may be particularly important for models that were found to behave inappropriately, do not comply with regulations, are found to contain illegal or otherwise undesirable content, or found to be susceptible to reverse engineering and leakage of confidential or proprietary information.  |
|  |  | **7.6.3: Legacy Logic Vulnerability Management**  For AI systems that cannot be immediately decommissioned due to legacy constraints (e.g., dependent systems that cannot be updated), establish an "Extended Isolation Protocol." This requires appropriate controls, such as redeployment onto isolated systems, additional monitoring, or stricter fail-over conditions, to contain risks associated with continuing to use an otherwise deprecated system.  |
| **8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures** Develop and implement protocols for responding to and investigating AI-related failures, addressing the unique challenges posed by probabilistic and non-deterministic system behaviors in operational environments.  This practice provides the forensic clarity necessary to support regulatory oversight, safety investigations, and situational awareness during ongoing incident response, helping ensure that AI-driven incidents do not compromise organizational accountability or the reliable operation of CI. Cybersecurity-focused playbooks for incident response should be expanded to cover the broader AI Risk Surface discussed in Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93). Responders need to determine whether an anomaly was caused by an active exploit, or by non-adversarial risks across the trustworthiness characteristics, such as unannounced model drift, context window poisoning, dynamic multi-agent feedback loops, or plausible hallucinations in generated logic. This practice focuses on governance and resourcing to satisfy transparency and regulatory requirements. It dovetails with Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41), which focuses on technical and functional requirements, and Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f), which focuses on maintaining operations.  **Search Keywords:**  AI incident response, incident investigation, root-cause analysis (RCA), safety investigation, just culture, near-miss reporting, non-punitive disclosure, evidence preservation, chain of custody, multi-agent cascade reconstruction, forensic interpretability gap, post-hoc attribution limitations, attack vs. risk surface triage, AI incident response team (AIRT), regulatory notification, de-identified incident sharing, lessons learned, after-action review (AAR)  **Trustworthiness Characteristics:** \>None yet \- suggestions welcome  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome | **8.1: Identify scenarios requiring deterministic root cause analysis and incident reconstruction** Define the specific applications, operational thresholds, and safety-critical triggers that mandate a comprehensive, deterministic reconstruction of an AI-driven failure, for both post-event investigation as well as to provide situational awareness for immediate-term incident response.  Incident response criteria should explicitly account for the full AI Risk Surface (Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93)), helping ensure triage procedures can differentiate between adversarial cyber exploits and non-adversarial failures. This task helps ensure that applications requiring forensic root cause analysis are performed by AI systems that support such investigations.  It also helps ensure that forensic resources are prioritized for high-impact events and that the organization meets the evidentiary standards required for regulatory and safety oversight. | **8.1.1: Evaluate AI applications**  Evaluate AI applications to identify the extent of root cause analysis required to satisfy risk management and regulatory requirements. This evaluation should explicitly screen for process paths where deterministic forensic reconstruction is a safety or operational requirement. Where a proposed AI system cannot satisfy these specific analytical demands, organizations should either substitute systems inherently capable of true root cause analysis, demonstrably close the gap via validated compensating controls, or implement deterministic boundaries to ensure execution hands over to manual or legacy systems capable of the required analysis. |
|  |  | **8.1.2: Identify AI systems that may not satisfy root cause analysis requirements**  Identify specific AI system classes that carry inherent forensic gaps that cannot be fully closed by techniques like runtime telemetry. These include large language models with non-deterministic sampling, agentic systems with ephemeral context, and remote-hosted inference services without preserved telemetry. In addition, some architectures and systems rely on inherent operational randomness that may not be able to be meaningfully replayed.  Post-hoc explainability tools such as those based on SHAP and LIME should not be treated as forensic proof of how or why an incident occurred. Such techniques test a system with altered inputs and infer, after the fact, one hypothesis for what likely drove the system’s output, without consideration for the system’s actual decision making process.  Where such systems are identified, technical managers should cross-reference with Task [8.2: Identify additional controls that may be required in scenarios where deterministic root cause analysis is not possible](#bookmark=kix.9o7cvndsjc9t) to implement appropriate safety margins or alternative validation frameworks. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  [Swidey, E. (2026). "Utilities must ask AI vendors these questions to meet critical infrastructure protection standards" Utility Dive.](https://www.utilitydive.com/news/why-post-hoc-ai-auditing-is-already-obsolete-for-nerc-cip/812008/) (Argues that vendor "explainability" features are post-hoc rationalisations rather than audit trails, and that auditors treat reconstructed explanations the way they treat backdated logs. Written for NERC CIP-015-1; transfers wherever forensic reconstruction is a regulatory obligation.) [Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) and International Partners (2025). "Principles for the Secure Integration of Artificial Intelligence in Operational Technology." CISA.](https://media.defense.gov/2025/Dec/03/2003834257/-1/-1/0/JOINT_GUIDANCE_PRINCIPLES_FOR_THE_SECURE_INTEGRATION_OF_AI_IN_OT.PDF) (Emphasises the need for a true “why” and “how” decisions are made.) [Haha, M. A. B., Bohli, A., Haddour, N., & Bouallegue, R. (2026). "A Systematic Review of Industrial IoT Anomaly Detection and the Forensic Interpretability Gap." MDPI Electronics.](https://www.mdpi.com/2079-9292/15/11/2240) (Examining wastewater, manufacturing, and energy use cases, this review provides a global template for industrial operators by distinguishing model-centric post-hoc attributions from system-centric forensics, showing why standard correlational explainers mistake downstream symptoms for root causes.) [Slack, D., Hilgard, S., Jia, E., Singh, S., & Lakkaraju, H. (2020). "Fooling LIME and SHAP: Adversarial Attacks on Post hoc Explanation Methods." AAAI/ACM Conference on AI, Ethics, and Society (AIES).](https://arxiv.org/abs/1911.02508) (Addressing cybersecurity threat hunters and auditors across critical networks, this study proves that perturbation-based explainers can be fooled by adversarial "scaffolding" wrappers that detect synthetic inputs and fake clean explanations during active system tampering.) [causaLens (2023). "Explainable AI (XAI) Doesn't Explain Enough." causaLens.](https://causalai.causalens.com/resources/blog/xai-doesnt-explain/) (Details why correlational explainers fail to support high-risk operational decisions because they highlight superficial statistical associations rather than the causal dynamics of the system.) |
|  |  | **8.1.3: Update existing incident response policies and playbooks**  Include documenting the state of AI systems at the time of the incident, such as information from Task [7.1: Maintain an Internal Registry of AI Deployments](#bookmark=kix.svuun1hdnz08).  |
|  |  | **8.1.4: Conduct incident reconstruction drills**  Such drills should be conducted within digital twins or high fidelity simulators to ensure that the resulting root cause analysis process and outputs are both accurate and accurately understood by responding personnel.  Regular drills also detect shifts in the capabilities of underlying systems to enable root cause analysis, particularly where AI functionality is provided by external parties.  This is particularly important in applications where such analysis is required in real-time to inform ongoing, time-sensitive incident response activities.  |
|  |  | **8.1.5: Multi-agent cascade reconstruction capabilities** For deployments involving multiple cooperating or nested AI agents, establish logging and telemetry requirements that explicitly span agent boundaries. Telemetry should capture inter-agent communications, dynamic delegation chains, and context window handoffs. This tracking is necessary to trace how errors propagate through an ecosystem, helping ensure forensic investigators can accurately distinguish an originating system anomaly from downstream algorithmic amplification. **References:** \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications (Version 2026\) (particularly ASI06 (Memory and Context Poisoning) to govern context integrity across chained agent handoffs). MITRE ATLAS (utilizing the framework's multi-stage attack paths to model how localized agent failures or exploits cascade across adjacent automated systems).  |
|  |  | **8.1.6: Incident Triage Across the Dual Attack/Risk Surface** Incorporate explicit triage pathways in incident playbooks to systematically isolate whether an AI anomaly originated on the Attack Surface (e.g., unauthorized access, API key abuse, or malicious prompt injection) or within the non-adversarial Risk Surface (e.g., sensor schema variance, distribution drift, or multi-agent cascade failure). This helps ensure forensic resources are directed toward the true root cause rather than misclassifying systemic algorithmic errors as external cyber intrusions, or vice versa. |
|  | **8.2: Identify additional controls that may be required in scenarios where deterministic root cause analysis is not possible** Establish formal risk-based protocols and alternative verification standards for scenarios where the non-deterministic nature of AI prevents a precise root-cause identification. This provides a governance framework for making informed, defensible decisions regarding initial system deployment and re-commissioning.  Such systems should not be deployed in applications where forensic root cause analysis is required by regulation or safety obligation unless compensating controls demonstrably close the gap; otherwise, organizations should substitute systems inherently capable of satisfying root cause analysis requirements. | **8.2.1: Identify applications and situations for which root cause analysis requirements may be relaxed**  Relaxing these requirements should only be done where appropriate compensating risk management controls are in place. For example, a load-balancing optimization application might typically require forensic root-cause analysis for all actions. It may be acceptable to revise this so that it does not require forensic root-cause analysis for actions that differ by no more than a margin the organization defines and documents from a verified-safe (but sub-optimal) legacy system.  |
|  |  | **8.2.2: Identify opaque systems and appropriate safety margins** within which they will operate to acceptably minimize the likelihood of failures for which root-cause analysis cannot be performed. These margins may be relative to other systems, as per Implementation [8.2.1: Identify applications and situations for which root cause analysis requirements may be relaxed](#bookmark=kix.ufng1i3b3l37).  Beyond these safety margins, establish appropriate procedures for hand-off to deterministic or legacy systems, or for manual oversight.  |
|  | **8.3: Designate roles for AI incident governance** Formulate a cross-functional AI Incident Response Team (AIRT) that includes representatives from legal, technical, risk management, and communications.  This task helps ensure that the high-level management of an incident is supported by a "digital chain of command" capable of making informed decisions regarding liability, public safety, and mission continuity. | **8.3.1: Identify internal incident management points of contact** for every AI system that can affect the organization’s mission, and ensure that they are resourced and well-practiced to foresee, prepare for, and manage incidents relating to their AI system(s). This includes both internally hosted AI systems and purchased-as-a-service systems.  Before an incident occurs, they maintain documentation of their system’s mission-criticality, organizational impacts, fail-over options, and dependencies. During an incident, they lead evidence-based prioritization efforts to restore the most mission-critical functions first, while external resources are being brought online.  |
|  |  | **8.3.2: Form a permanent AI incident governance team** that includes appropriate organizational and leadership, such as the CISO, technical, and legal staff with expertise in AI, and operations management to oversee the recovery and recommissioning of AI systems following an incident.  |
|  | **8.4: Define external reporting and regulatory disclosure protocols following AI incidents** Establish formal procedures and relationships with external organizations for notifying sector-specific regulators and the public following an AI-related anomaly.  This task helps ensure that the organization meets its legal and transparency obligations, helping to maintain community trust and contribute to broader sector-wide threat intelligence. | **8.4.1: Integrate AI-specific triggers into existing incident disclosure and reporting protocols** Add specific triggers to the organization's existing incident reporting protocols that call out AI-driven causes, such as model drift or automated decision error, helping ensure these events are captured as completely as a traditional cybersecurity incident would be, addressing the kind of incomplete disclosure seen in past autonomous vehicle reporting failures.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **8.4.2: Establish automated notification and detailed impact disclosure for sector-specific authorities** Where regulatory timelines require rapid notification, automate the initial alert to relevant sector authorities based on the triggers defined in Implementation [8.4.1: Integrate AI-specific triggers into existing incident disclosure and reporting protocols](#bookmark=kix.p2ee9iwvinlb), followed by a more detailed impact disclosure prepared by the incident response team.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **8.5: Pre-arrange specialized resources for investigating and recovering from AI incidents** Ensure that the organization has at least pre-identified, and ideally pre-arranged, access to the specialized personnel and budgetary resources needed for complex investigations, such as third-party AI forensic auditors or specialized legal counsel.  The market for third-party AI forensic auditors is currently nascent. Interim approaches include developing internal capability through targeted training, establishing sector-level mutual aid via ISACs and Sector Coordinating Councils, and forming partnerships with academic centers conducting AI security and forensics research. This task addresses potential internal skill gaps and helps ensure that the investigation of and recovery from non-deterministic AI failures is adequately funded and supported. | **8.5.1: Identify third-party AI forensic and legal experts for contingency access** Identify and pre-qualify external AI forensic specialists and legal counsel experienced in AI-related incidents before one occurs, since this market is still nascent and locating qualified help during an active incident wastes critical response time.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **8.5.2: Establish formal retainer-style relationships with specialized AI incident responders and auditors** Where budget allows, convert the contacts identified in Implementation [8.5.1: Identify third-party AI forensic and legal experts for contingency access](#bookmark=kix.i4o2wwx4vp6x) into formal retainer agreements, guaranteeing priority access to specialized responders rather than competing for their availability during a live incident.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **8.6: Establish non-punitive reporting for AI-related near-misses** Establish channels through which personnel can report AI-attributable near-misses, degradation, and anomalous behavior without bearing personal or organizational cost for the disclosure.  Where the recipient of a report needs to be independent of the function responsible for the deployment, that channel may sit outside the organization; see Implementation [8.6.2: Protect reporter identity through an independent reporting route](#bookmark=id.jp1wadxdrg3q). **References:** The [Shared AI Findings Exchange (SAFE)](https://github.com/OpenSecureAIAlliance/RFCs) Working Group was published by the Open Secure AI Alliance and is a proposed non-regulatory framework for sharing AI incidents and near-misses across organizations, with specific notification timelines. | **8.6.1: Separate benefit-claiming from risk-reporting**  Identify, for each AI deployment, which organizational function claims its operational or efficiency benefit, and ensure that function is not the sole route through which risks and degradation associated with that deployment are escalated.  |
|  |  | **8.6.2: Protect reporter identity through an independent reporting route**  Provide a reporting route that does not require the reporter to disclose their identity to the function responsible for the deployment. Where internal anonymity is impractical, or where the deployment function has influence over the reporter's role, work or standing, route reporting to a recipient outside the organization, consistent with the sector-level pooling approach already described in Task [8.5: Pre-arrange specialized resources for investigating and recovering from AI incidents](#bookmark=kix.plt52mp5jcwu). |
|  |  | **8.6.3: Ensure supplier terms do not penalize degradation disclosure** Review procurement terms and service-level agreements to confirm they do not create financial or contractual disincentives for vendors to disclose model degradation, behavioral change, or known limitations. This complements the disclosure requirements in Implementation [6.1.1: Add AI disclosure to existing procurement policies](#bookmark=kix.untd4qjdgbrs) and the hazard reachability attestations in Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q), both of which currently rely on vendor self-report.  |
|  |  | **8.6.4: Share de-identified incident records for collective defense** Where reports captured under Implementation [8.6.2: Protect reporter identity through an independent reporting route](#bookmark=id.jp1wadxdrg3q) reveal failure modes with cross-organizational relevance, contribute a de-identified version to a trusted sharing community so peer operators can learn from a failure mode before it recurs elsewhere. This differs in purpose from the mandatory regulatory disclosure in Task [8.4: Define external reporting and regulatory disclosure protocols following AI incidents](#bookmark=kix.jtgfqqqxhyf5): sharing here is voluntary, structural knowledge transfer intended to raise collective resilience, and it draws on the reporting channel established in Implementation [8.6.2: Protect reporter identity through an independent reporting route](#bookmark=id.jp1wadxdrg3q) without replacing it. Specify a minimum useful shared record (the AI system's role and consequence class, the failure mode, contributing factors, how it was detected, and which control did or did not hold) and a de-identification standard that strips the reporting organization's identity, proprietary model internals, and customer or safety-sensitive data while preserving the pattern that makes the report useful to peers. Because this record derives from information captured under Implementation [8.6.2: Protect reporter identity through an independent reporting route](#bookmark=id.jp1wadxdrg3q), its de-identification standard should be at least as protective of the original reporter as the originating channel that produced it. Route sharing through a trusted intermediary rather than new bilateral arrangements, such as a sector ISAC or Sector Coordinating Council where one exists, or a cross-sector AI incident clearinghouse where none exists. Define how the organization reciprocally ingests peer reports back into its own MAP and MEASURE activity, for example, updating guardrails or drift thresholds in response to a failure mode others have shared.  Note: Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) governs what the system records, which governs whether a person who observes something the system did not record has a viable route to say so. It is the input stage for this Implementation. Cross-organizational sharing depends on incidents having been reported within organizations first. |
| **9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training**  Establish a systematic program for AI risk management training tailored to specific operational roles and decision-making responsibilities.  This practice helps ensure that stakeholders across the organization possess the calibrated knowledge necessary to identify AI risks and maintain mission continuity. It also assists in preventing the safety and security gaps that arise from over- or under-trust of automated systems.  **Search Keywords:**  automation bias, automation complacency, calibrated trust, mental model calibration, AI literacy, human factors, role-based competency matrix, incident simulation training, simulator training, skill fade, skills degradation, manual proficiency, manual control retention, over-trust mitigation, under-trust mitigation, workforce readiness, reporting disincentive awareness  **Trustworthiness Characteristics:** \>None yet \- suggestions welcome  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome | **9.1: Perform role-based AI risk competency mapping**  Identify specific AI-related knowledge and skills required for different organizational roles, from executive level to front-line operations.  This task helps ensure that training is targeted to the specific risks encountered in each role and provides those responsible for mission continuity with relevant, actionable information and practice.  | **9.1.1: Identify required AI literacy and risk awareness levels for existing operational roles** For each operational role that interacts with, supervises, or can override an AI system, define the specific AI literacy and risk awareness that role requires, distinguishing between what a front-line operator needs to know and what an executive decision-maker needs to know.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **9.1.2: Develop a comprehensive AI risk competency matrix and personalized learning paths** Turn the role requirements from Implementation [9.1.1: Identify required AI literacy and risk awareness levels for existing operational roles](#bookmark=kix.sn60hsvhpov0) into a structured competency matrix mapping each role to specific training modules, and build individualized learning paths so personnel are trained on what their specific role requires rather than a one-size-fits-all curriculum.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **9.1.3: Include data-originating roles in competency mapping** Include personnel who create or record source data: field engineers, maintenance technicians, and operators completing work orders or condition reports in the role-based mapping performed under Task [9.1: Perform role-based AI risk competency mapping](#bookmark=kix.si6987svrqjp), even where those roles do not directly use or supervise an AI system. Training should cover which specific fields are consumed by downstream AI systems and analytics, what makes an individual entry usable or unusable for that purpose (for example, free text where a coded value is expected, blank fields silently treated as a default, or entries reconstructed from memory after the fact), the operational impact of a degraded record on the teams and systems that depend on it, and the correct handling of any manual transfer or synchronization step required to preserve the data. Where data capture depends on such a step, it should be treated as part of the task rather than as an administrative afterthought. |
|  | **9.2: Deliver specialized training on human-AI interaction and automation bias** Provide operational personnel with the skills to recognize and mitigate “automation bias”, the tendency to over-trust automated outputs, and other psychological factors that lead to miscalibrated over or under trust.  This task reduces the risk of human error during AI-driven anomalies and helps ensure that manual overrides remain an effective and timely safety-net for critical infrastructure functions. Training programs should explicitly cultivate accurate mental models of AI behavior across all operational roles, from grid dispatchers and plant operators to clinicians, fraud analysts, and network engineers.  Personnel should be trained not only on how the AI functions during ideal conditions, but on its known operational limitations, edge-case failure modes, and boundary behaviors. Hands-on simulation drills (Implementation [9.2.2: Implement realistic incident simulation training](#bookmark=kix.slhmi67qv3as)) should periodically expose personnel to ambiguous, out-of-distribution, or low-confidence AI outputs, building tactical muscle memory for evaluating uncertainty, performing spot-check investigations, and executing timely overrides under realistic time pressure. Training should also address the effect of explanation and rationale features on reliance, so that personnel do not treat a plausible rationale as evidence that a recommendation is correct (see Implementation [4.6.1: Design rationale displays that give personnel something to check](#bookmark=id.sazux36izue4)). | **9.2.1: Include role-specific training on “AI failure modes”**  This training should be incorporated into standard regular (e.g., annual) technical or cybersecurity training. It should include identifying applications and situations in which personnel in that role may use, interact with, or supervise AI systems; the potential mission impact of AI system failures; situations in which AI systems should be overridden; the procedure for safely doing so; how to obtain the necessary situational awareness; and communication, reporting, and documentation requirements.  Training should also address how operating conditions that have implications for human cognitive fatigue (such as extended shift lengths, remote or isolated duty stations, and non-native-language communication) can reduce an individual's capacity to catch an AI-generated error. This includes both OT and IT applications, such as those from a general-purpose assistant summarizing or drafting operationally relevant material. Role-specific guidance should be provided for compensating for these conditions. This implementation mitigates hazard risk of an operator accepting an incorrect AI recommendation without independent verification, because repeated correct outputs have conditioned them to treat AI recommendations as authoritative. This is an emerging human factors failure mode specific to AI-driven automation and assistants, and as such is not yet well addressed by traditional security frameworks and guidance. It applies wherever personnel supervise, interact with, or can override AI systems in CI operational environments.  Because established frameworks offer limited pre-built guidance, organizations should proactively extend existing TEVV protocols (Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b)) to probe for this specific failure mode, use qualified engineering and operational judgment to anticipate how automation bias might manifest in their particular application before an incident forces the question, and incorporate the results into ongoing training. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  CISA, Principles for the Secure Integration of Artificial Intelligence in Operational Technology, Sections 1.3, 4.1 and 2.4.  NERC, Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning in Real-Time System Operations, Chapter 3\. |
|  |  | **9.2.2: Implement realistic incident simulation training**  Personnel who play a significant AI risk management role, such as those supervising or expected to take over from AI systems, should undertake such training. This may include the use of digital twins and realistic simulation environments, both to generate test scenarios and to determine the kinds of failures that are realistic.  Particular care should be taken in situations where the existence or nature of an AI system failure or error may be hidden or non-obvious, particularly due to other errors in related AI systems.  For example, hallucinations in an AI-based summarization or dashboard system may result in early reports of failure from upstream systems being downplayed, omitted, or presented in a confusing or misleading manner, particularly where those reports are very unusual.  |
|  |  | **9.2.3: Recognizing disincentives to reporting AI degradation** Include, in role-specific training for personnel who supervise, rely on, or can override AI systems, explicit coverage of conditions under which an individual may be discouraged from reporting observed AI degradation, near-misses, or anomalous outputs. Implementation [9.2.1: Include role-specific training on “AI failure modes”](#bookmark=kix.2u307dyu52tc) addresses automation bias, in which personnel trust an AI output more than it warrants. This implementation addresses a related but distinct condition: personnel who correctly identify an AI failure but face organizational disincentives to report it. Examples include cases where the deployment is attributed as an efficiency gain to the reporting individual's own business unit; where reporting is likely to trigger scrutiny of a colleague's or supervisor's work; or where individual or team performance metrics are tied to the AI system's continued adoption, availability, or utilization rate. Training should ensure that personnel can recognize when they are in such a position, know which reporting channel is available to them and what protections attach to it, and understand that a near-miss involving no realized harm remains reportable. This is particularly relevant for degradation that accumulates gradually, where no single observation appears individually significant enough to justify the cost of reporting it. Training alone does not resolve this condition. Where an organization's reporting structure makes disclosure individually costly, personnel who have been trained to report will still under-report. This implementation should therefore be paired with the structural measures in Task [8.6: Establish non-punitive reporting for AI-related near-misses](#bookmark=id.jjat17xw4ys6), and organizations should treat persistent under-reporting as evidence of a *structural* rather than a *training* deficiency.  |
|  | **9.3: Establish audit-ready systems for verifying workforce readiness**   Create formal mechanisms to assess and document the effectiveness of AI risk training through structured assessments, tabletop simulations, and periodic drills.  This task provides the verifiable evidence of "Workforce Readiness" required for regulatory compliance and helps ensure that personnel maintain the calibrated judgment necessary for high-stakes decision-making across the AI lifecycle. | **9.3.1: Maintain logs of completed AI training and basic proficiency assessment scores** Keep a centralized, auditable record of who has completed required AI risk training and their assessment scores, so the organization can demonstrate workforce readiness to regulators and identify gaps in coverage before an incident exposes them.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **9.3.2: Implement periodic "readiness drills" and validated skills certifications for critical roles** For personnel in roles carrying the highest AI oversight responsibility, require periodic hands-on readiness drills and formal certification renewal, rather than relying on one-time training completion to demonstrate ongoing competence.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
| **10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities** Establish a framework for the systematic capture and preservation of operational telemetry across the AI lifecycle to ensure decision-making logic remains transparent and verifiable.  This practice establishes the evidentiary foundation required to audit actions across the entire AI Risk Surface (Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93)). While traditional logging captures network perimeter events to monitor the Attack Surface, AI-aware logging captures decision-level context, including prompts, model parameters, context window states, and guardrail interactions, necessary to reconstruct non-deterministic operational failures. This practice supports the development of audit-ready systems that support forensic investigations and regulatory compliance, providing the foundational data required to maintain organizational accountability for automated actions in critical infrastructure. **Search Keywords:**  decision-level logging, audit trail, data historian, runtime telemetry, edge telemetry, observability, inference receipt, immutable audit log, tamper-evident storage, prompt history tracking, log partitioning, metadata retention, model drift auditing, post-hoc explanation gap, chain of custody, evidentiary record  **Trustworthiness Characteristics:** \>None yet \- suggestions welcome  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  [OMB M-26-14 Ensuring Effective and Efficient Agency Logging and Network Visibility to Defend Against Evolving Cyber Threats](https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/M-26-14-Ensuring-Effective-and-Efficient-Agency-Logging-and-Network-Visibility-to-Defend-Against-Evolving-Cyber-Threats.pdf) | **10.1: Define decision-level logging and metadata retention policies**  Specify the AI systems and applications for which information, including system inputs, outputs, system prompts, and version-specific metadata, will be captured to ensure automated actions are deterministically reconstructable or statistically reproducible, and overall performance is continuously monitored.  Logging policies should extend beyond traditional network event logs to capture telemetry across the entire AI Risk Surface (Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93)), helping ensure forensic investigators can evaluate safety violations, explainability gaps, and accuracy degradation across the asset lifecycle. This task establishes the essential evidentiary foundation for transparency, helping ensure the organization possesses the necessary runtime context to perform the forensic root-cause analysis required by Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb), support the success metrics of Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k), and verify the bounds described in Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41).  | **10.1.1: Identify AI systems that may take or directly influence mission-critical actions** Ensure that they are appropriately inventoried (Implementation [7.1.1: Create and maintain a “Master AI Asset List”](#bookmark=kix.pxpy1qc9bvxd)) and that logs of inputs, outputs, and other data to reconstruct their decisions are stored in accordance with data retention and regulatory requirements.  Where this information is already logged (e.g., by other OT/ICS systems) ensure that appropriate metadata is also logged and retention policies synchronized to ensure that this information can be merged after-the-fact.  |
|  |  | **10.1.2: Immutable Runtime Telemetry and Artifact Tracking** Capture immutable runtime telemetry at an appropriate level of abstraction.  For generated execution artifacts (such as code, Programmable Logic Controller (PLC) logic, configurations, or automated procedures), logs should uniquely link the output to the specific model version and prompt context.  For AI systems providing operational decision support or diagnostics from structured data, runtime telemetry should capture an 'inference receipt', logging the specific data elements selected, active threshold criteria, and versioned configuration parameters that generated the output, alongside any execution artifacts. Where the AI system applies encoded rules or a self-interpretable model, the receipt can also record the rules or branches that were applied, which provides the trace displayed under Implementation [4.6.3: Display execution traces from symbolic, hybrid, and self-interpretable components](#bookmark=id.ddgwjbohk09a). This provides the technical evidence necessary to validate static governance records during audits or post-incident reviews. Statistical hypotheses generated after the fact by post-hoc evaluation tools (such as LIME or SHAP) should not be relied on as a substitute for true decision-level logs. See Implementation [8.1.2: Identify AI systems that may not satisfy root cause analysis requirements](#bookmark=kix.o0fgigw7uop7) for further details.  |
|  |  | **10.1.3: Forensic Integrity and Privacy-Preserving Log Partitioning** Incorporate automated data-masking, encryption, and sanitization boundaries to ensure log collection does not inadvertently retain protected privacy data within open, day-to-day operational repositories. Where a structural conflict exists, organizations should explicitly define which data fields are essential to root-cause reconstruction (Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb)) and restrict masking only to non-essential elements to preserve actionable incident utility. For high-consequence applications processing sensitive person-centric data, such as healthcare clinical support decisions or financial transaction journals, the "immutable" tracking guidance of Task [10.2: Establish data integrity and tamper-evidence controls for AI telemetry and logging](#bookmark=kix.q1kzifuph2jw) should be achieved by routing the raw, unredacted text and prompt history (the exact model inputs and outputs) directly into highly secured, tamper-evident storage environments that are logically separated from routine operational visibility. This helps ensure strict compliance with data privacy boundaries without compromising the availability or validity of the audit trail. |
|  |  | **10.1.4: Asynchronous Telemetry and Inference Logging for Constrained Edges** For bandwidth-constrained, remote, or legacy edge environments where continuous, high-volume raw transfer is unfeasible, satisfy the immutable tracking mandate via edge-side log compression or secure local storage paired with delayed, off-peak batch telemetry transfers to the central log management infrastructure, or high-level metadata reviews. |
|  | **10.2: Establish data integrity and tamper-evidence controls for AI telemetry and logging**   Implement appropriate access controls and security protocols to ensure the immutability and reliability of captured AI system logs.  This task protects the "digital chain of custody," helping prevent unauthorized modification of operational records and helping ensure that audit data remains a trustworthy source of truth for regulatory oversight and liability management. | **10.2.1: Ensure that logs are stored in an immutable manner**  If existing logging facilities are being used, ensure that they can handle the potential additional bandwidth and storage capacity requirements of AI systems without interfering with other logging activities.  For example, it is necessary to ensure that AI logging bandwidth does not “flood out” logging from other devices, such as edge devices, and that the additional storage requirements do not result in logs being deleted earlier than is required.  See Implementation [10.1.4: Asynchronous Telemetry and Inference Logging for Constrained Edges](#bookmark=id.vdwbfkx1mlpw) for bandwidth-constrained, remote, or legacy edge environments where this is not feasible. |
|  | **10.3: Implement systematic audit review and performance trend analysis**  Define formal procedures for the periodic review of AI system logs to identify model drift, emerging biases, or performance anomalies.  This task transforms raw telemetry into actionable oversight, allowing technical management to detect degrading model reliability or "silent" failures before they escalate into mission-critical incidents or cause quality of service issues. | **10.3.1: Conduct periodic manual reviews of AI logs for performance issues or drift** Assign a defined cadence for qualified personnel to manually review AI system logs captured under Task [10.1: Define decision-level logging and metadata retention policies](#bookmark=kix.7y5rv6pa7wxa), looking specifically for signs of performance degradation or drift that automated monitoring may not yet be tuned to catch.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **10.3.2: Implement automated trend analysis and dashboarding for continuous audit oversight** Build automated dashboards that surface performance trends from the logs over time, reducing reliance on manual review alone and giving technical managers continuous visibility into slow-developing issues between formal audit cycles.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
| **11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning**  Establish a strategic framework for mission continuity to manage the immediate term alternative operating modes, and the longer-term recovery and restoration of essential functions following a significant AI failure.  This practice highlights the need to pre-allocate recovery resources and deliberately prioritize service restoration to ensure organizational resilience. This practice focuses on the “Plan B” when an incident occurs. It dovetails with Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf), which focuses on technical aspects that support this practice, and Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb), which focuses on governance obligations.  **Search Keywords:**  mission continuity, business continuity planning (BCP), disaster recovery, recovery time objective (RTO), manual fallback playbook, fail-to-manual, manual mode, degraded mode, cold start mitigation, isolated standby infrastructure, restoration priority, tabletop exercise, return to service, shadow mode re-validation, graceful degradation, state-at-handoff display  **Trustworthiness Characteristics:** \>None yet \- suggestions welcome  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome | **11.1: Identify and prioritize AI-dependent essential functions**   Catalog all infrastructure services that rely on AI components and rank them based on their impact on mission continuity and public safety.  This task helps ensure that "Restoration Priorities" are established as a matter of policy, directing limited recovery resources toward the most critical life-safety and service-delivery functions first. It also prioritizes the allocation of resources to maintain non-AI backup systems for critical functions.  | **11.1.1: Rank operational processes based on the impact of AI failure or unavailability** Score each AI-dependent process by the severity of consequence if the AI component became unavailable, from life-safety and service-continuity impacts down to minor administrative inconvenience, and use this ranking to direct recovery resources during an actual outage. This gives the organization a documented basis for deciding, in the moment, which system gets restored first.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **11.1.2: Conduct comprehensive AI dependency mapping across all sector-critical functions** Extend the ranking in Implementation [11.1.1: Rank operational processes based on the impact of AI failure or unavailability](#bookmark=kix.kfb3h7oaftat) into a full map of which sector-critical functions rely on which AI components, directly or indirectly, so that a single AI failure's downstream reach is understood before an incident forces the question.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **11.2: Define manual and alternative operating procedures for long-term mission continuity**  Develop and document comprehensive "Manual Mode" instructions for running essential functions for extended periods without AI support. These playbooks should define orchestrated fallback pathways tailored to the system's operational timeline: For critical systems requiring 24/7 availability, they should dictate the process for a safe transition to a legacy deterministic systems without interrupting availability. For critical systems that do not need to be realtime (such as financial fraud queues, non-emergency triage systems, or administrative compliance tools), they should outline the protocols to safely pull the AI infrastructure offline while minimizing other risks, such as security and data corruption, and route accumulated tasks into structured manual workflows.  This task complements those of Practice [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) by providing the operational playbook for sustained, non-automated service delivery during a prolonged outage. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  NIST SP 800-82r3 (Section 6.2.4.5 on Response and Recovery Plans, featuring “graceful degradation” and the need to safely accumulate backlogs) | **11.2.1: Document basic manual fallback procedures for AI-dependent control points** Write down the specific manual steps operators would take to keep a process running if its AI component became unavailable, at a level of detail someone unfamiliar with day-to-day operation of that specific system could still follow under pressure.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **11.2.2: Develop a validated "Safe Operating Mode" that runs independently of AI infrastructure** Beyond documenting manual steps, build and test a defined operating mode that does not depend on the AI system at all, confirming it can sustain essential function, even at reduced capacity, for as long as a realistic outage might last.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **11.2.3: Human Factors and Cognitive Transition Protocols for Control Handoffs** Establish standardized interface and procedural protocols for transitioning operational authority from automated, agentic, or advisory AI systems to manual human control during emergencies, system outages, or recovery scenarios.  To prevent "cold start" cognitive shock, where personnel taking over from an AI system lack immediate context regarding current system state, such as accumulated transaction backlogs, clinical patient trends, or recent automated decisions, consider the following in handover protocols: **Generate a State-at-Handoff Summary Display:** Automatically render a concise "State-at-Handoff" screen summarizing recent AI actions, active system trends, accumulated task queues, unacknowledged alerts, and open physical or policy constraints.  **Enforce Bounded Handoff Pacing:** Where execution time constants permit (Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud)), provide a structured step-through sequence that requires personnel to review and explicitly acknowledge current operational states (e.g., active setpoints, pending financial approvals, or clinical triage statuses) before manual control fully engages. **Support Incremental De-escalation:** Allow personnel to assume manual control over specific sub-systems, transaction queues, or decision branches while leaving adjacent automated safety or policy interlocks active, avoiding unnecessary "all-or-nothing" operational disruptions where feasible. |
|  |  | **11.2.4: Unplanned Shutdown and Controlled Isolation Protocols** Establish documented, practiced protocols for executing controlled, rapid shutdowns of AI-dependent operational processes when automated or manual safety boundaries are intersected but where there are insufficient resources or time to continue operations on fallback legacy or manual systems. This prevents secondary process hazards (such as thermal or pressure excursions) during abrupt operational halts. |
|  | **11.3: Pre-allocate operational resources for alternative service delivery**  Ensure the availability and maintenance of physical tools, trained standby personnel, and budgetary reserves required to perform fail-over to non-AI backup systems, which may be less efficient and require additional resources and oversight.  While Task [8.5: Pre-arrange specialized resources for investigating and recovering from AI incidents](#bookmark=kix.plt52mp5jcwu) secures investigation experts, this task secures the operational resources needed to keep the water flowing or the power on while the AI is offline. | **11.3.1: Identify standby personnel and physical tools required for manual operations** Name specific trained personnel and the physical tools or spare equipment needed to execute the manual fallback procedures from Task [11.2: Define manual and alternative operating procedures for long-term mission continuity](#bookmark=kix.27rg9c9cadm7), and confirm their availability on a defined schedule rather than assuming they will be reachable when an incident occurs.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **11.3.2: Maintain Isolated Standby Infrastructure and Allocated Reserves** Set aside dedicated backup infrastructure and budget reserves for fail-over to non-AI systems, and periodically confirm this infrastructure still functions and remains isolated from the primary AI system's failure modes.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **11.4: Establish system integrity re-validation and re-commissioning protocols**  Define the technical benchmarks and safety tests a system should pass before it is allowed to transition from manual mode back to automated operations. Re-validation testing pathways should explicitly reference and execute the TEVV protocols established in Task [1.3: Implement evaluation and test plans, including TEVV, across the AI lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.ervwi65lzw5b).  This helps ensure that "turning the AI back on" does not introduce new cascading risks and is supported by verifiable, auditable evidence of system health. | **11.4.1: Define a checklist for validating system safety before re-engaging AI control** Create a specific, itemized checklist to be completed and signed off before returning a process from manual mode back to AI control, confirming the root cause of the original outage has been addressed and the system behaves as expected against known baselines.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **11.4.2: Implement "Shadow Mode" validation and essential safety-interlock checks for AI re-commissioning** Before fully returning control to the AI system, run it in shadow mode alongside the manual process to compare outputs and confirm safety interlocks respond correctly, catching problems before the AI system resumes live authority.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  | **11.5: Execute periodic mission continuity simulations and stress tests**  Conduct regular "fail-to-manual" exercises to verify that alternative operating procedures are effective and that personnel are capable of maintaining the mission under stress.  This provides the audit-ready evidence of organizational preparedness required for regulatory oversight and identifies gaps in the continuity framework. These exercises should be performed both during development, to ensure that resilience is “baked in,” and when in operation, to ensure that resilience is maintained despite changes in the operating environment, task, and condition of the system.  | **11.5.1: Conduct "fail-to-manual" tabletop exercises for key personnel** Run at least annual (or another frequency aligned with organizational failover testing policies) tabletop exercises walking key personnel through a simulated AI outage, testing whether the procedures in Task [11.2: Define manual and alternative operating procedures for long-term mission continuity](#bookmark=kix.27rg9c9cadm7) and the standby resources in Task [11.3: Pre-allocate operational resources for alternative service delivery](#bookmark=kix.qlsktei7cttt) actually work as documented, and updating the plans based on what the exercise reveals.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **11.5.2: Execute high-fidelity simulation drills involving total AI infrastructure loss and recovery**  Beyond tabletop exercises, periodically run a full simulation of total AI infrastructure loss using digital twins or dedicated test environments. Include at least one scenario where the underlying cause is not immediately apparent, to confirm personnel can maintain continuity while the nature of the failure, accidental or deliberate, is still being determined.  **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
| **12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle**  Establish rigorous validation and verification protocols for AI-generated artifacts, including code, configurations, and procedures, that direct or influence the execution of critical infrastructure operations.  This practice helps ensure that machine-authored code, configurations, and procedures are scrutinized against formal specifications and tacit institutional knowledge, preventing the deployment of 'plausible but inaccurate' logic in high-consequence IT, OT, and ICS environments. **Search Keywords:**  machine-authored code, AI-generated configurations, infrastructure as code (IaC), direct-execution logic, human-mediated logic, independent verification and validation (IV\&V), four-eyes, peer review, subject matter expert (SME) review, static code analysis, sandbox verification, acceptance testing, plausible hallucination verification, tacit knowledge validation, artifact provenance, latent defect, known-good rollback  **Trustworthiness Characteristics:** \>None yet \- suggestions welcome **Related AI RMF subcategories include:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome **References:**  \>None yet \- suggestions welcome | **12.1: Establish Governance and Accountability for AI-Generated Execution Artifacts** Define the policy framework and responsibility structures for the creation, classification, and authorization of AI-generated logic. This task helps ensure that every machine-authored artifact, from code to procedural summaries, is assigned a consequence based risk tier (see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d)) and a clear human owner responsible for its final approval and operational deployment. The general requirement to identify an accountable owner, and to establish that the organization can act on that accountability, is in Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x). | **12.1.1: Artifact Risk Classification**  Categorize AI-generated logic and outputs (artifacts) based primarily on consequence severity (see Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d)).
