Proximity to execution and influence on human decisions should be used to determine the validation procedures. To ensure appropriate validation velocity, organizations should explicitly distinguish between 'Direct-Execution Logic' (which directly commands physical or digital assets like Programmable Logic Controller (PLC) code or system configurations, requiring automated validation gates) and 'Human-Mediated Logic' (which influences human choices like procedural summaries or diagnostic reports, relying on structured expert review workflows). Artifacts such as PLC code, Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) configurations or automated control parameters may carry the highest execution risk as they have close proximity to execution.  For example: AI-generated valve control logic is likely to be classified as a High Risk, Direct Execution artifact.  Artifacts such as procedure summaries, condition reports or operational recommendations carry risk in proportion to the consequence of human actions they may influence.  AI-generated summary of a maintenance procedure should be classified as High Risk Human Mediated artifacts due to safety consequences if incorrect. A draft internal email summary of this same procedure that is subject to ongoing human brainstorming and refinement might be classified as Low Risk.  |
|  |  | **12.1.2: Provenance and Traceability**  (Referencing Task [10.1: Define decision-level logging and metadata retention policies](#bookmark=kix.7y5rv6pa7wxa)) Establish metadata requirements that uniquely link any generated code or procedure to the specific model version, prompt, and "inference context" used to create it. Validation leads should rely strictly on this hard configuration data for audit paths, rather than relying on post-hoc explanation tools which can mistake downstream symptoms for the root cause of an artifact error. See Implementation [8.1.2: Identify AI systems that may not satisfy root cause analysis requirements](#bookmark=kix.o0fgigw7uop7) for further details.  |
|  |  | **12.1.3: Authorization of Machine-Authored Logic**  Define which roles (e.g., Lead Engineer, Site Supervisor) are authorized to "promote" AI-generated logic from a draft state to a production-ready operational artifact. These roles should sit within the decision authority documented under Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz), rather than being defined only for this artifact class. |
|  | **12.2: Implement Multi-Layered Validation of Machine-Authored Logic**  Deploy a hybrid verification strategy that subjects AI-generated outputs to both deterministic automated testing and expert human scrutiny. By checking artifacts against formal specifications and tacit domain knowledge, this task helps ensure that machine-generated instructions remain within safe operational boundaries and are free from 'plausible hallucinations'. **References:**  \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome  NIST AI 600-1 (guiding validation frameworks to address safety, accuracy, and hidden tech-debt risks inherent to generative AI-authored logic and code). | **12.2.1: Deterministic and Rule-Based Guardrails**  (Referencing Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)) Verify that all AI-generated executable artifacts (like network configurations or PLC logic) pass through non-AI, deterministic verifiers, such as static code analyzers or schema validators, to ensure they meet hard-coded safety constraints.  Appropriate meta-safeguards should be in place if these non-AI, deterministic verifiers are, themselves, written with the assistance of AI.  |
|  |  | **12.2.2: Subject Matter Expert (SME) Review of Procedures**  For non-executable but safety-critical artifacts (like procedural summaries), conduct a "red-team" review by SMEs to ensure no "plausible hallucinations" have introduced safety risks or omitted critical warnings (as discussed in Implementation [2.4.3: Technical Accuracy and "Plausible Hallucination" Audits](#bookmark=kix.osb3t4hofz96)). |
|  |  | **12.2.3: Validation against Tacit and Formal Requirements**  (Referencing Implementation [1.4.4: Requirements for AI-Generated Code, Configurations, Procedures, and Other Logic](#bookmark=kix.qn0bhhgiwsng)) Ensure the validation process explicitly checks the generated logic against both codified technical standards and the "unwritten" operational norms of the specific facility. |
|  | **12.3: Manage Operational Continuity and Resilience for Generated Artifacts** Establish safeguards to protect system availability and safety throughout the lifecycle of AI-generated logic. This includes utilizing isolated testing environments, maintaining strict version control for machine-authored artifacts, and maintaining human-verified 'known-good' configurations for rapid rollback and recovery. | **12.3.1: Isolated Execution Testing (Sandboxing)**  Where feasible, test AI-generated configurations or code in a digital twin or isolated simulation environment before deployment to production systems. |
|  |  | **12.3.2: Version Control and Rollback Procedures**  Maintain a strict version history of all AI-generated operational logic. Ensure that a "known-good" human-verified configuration or procedure is always available as a fallback in the event of an AI-generated logic failure. |
|  |  | **12.3.3: Monitoring for latent failures**  Establish monitoring for systems running on AI-generated configurations to detect "drift" or latent errors that may not be apparent during the initial validation phase. |
| **13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority** Plan for the permanent removal of an AI capability from operation, including the withdrawal of its authority, the preservation of what it knew, and the transfer or deliberate abandonment of what depended on it. Retiring an AI system is not an extreme form of updating one. An update transfers trust from one version to the next and seeks continuity; retirement severs authority, removes a capability that other things may have come to rely on, and destroys operational knowledge that may exist nowhere else.  Retirement may also be imposed rather than chosen, by a withdrawn vendor, a deprecated model, a failed dependency, or a regulatory decision, and it may have to be carried out on a system that can no longer be run.  The preparation this Practice requires therefore belongs in the stages where the system still works, rather than at the point of retirement. (See Task [7.6: Establish processes for internal AI component deprecation and decommissioning](#bookmark=id.5ka8tq8kurdg) for the deprecation, archiving and destruction of internally developed AI artifacts.) Where an AI system is returned to service after retirement, it is admitted as a new deployment subject to the requirements of Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k), rather than resuming its former authority and identity.  **Search Keywords:**  decommissioning, retirement, end of life, EOL, sunset, offboarding, disposal, media sanitization, certificate of sanitization, data destruction, teardown, revocation, orphaned credentials, zombie agent, non-human identity offboarding, knowledge retention, successor system  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Secure:** Contributes the removal of standing authority at the end of a system's life: credentials, triggers, registered interfaces and delegated authority withdrawn and confirmed withdrawn, so that a system recorded as retired cannot still act. It does not establish the identities and revocation mechanisms being exercised, which Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) establishes. **Accountable:** Contributes a named decision and a record of what was removed, so that retirement is an authorized act with an owner rather than an administrative consequence of something else. It does not establish who holds that authority in the first place, which Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x) establishes. **Transparent:** Contributes a record of what the system had become and what its removal takes away, captured while the system can still be interrogated. It does not cover the routine operational logging that record draws on, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Reliable:** Removing a capability that other processes and people have adapted to degrades their performance whether or not the retirement itself is clean, and the effect is largest where the adaptation was informal and undocumented. Implementation [13.3.3: Determine and record what happens to everything that depended on the system](#bookmark=id.gknfd9voaeez) requires the dependents to be identified and a decision recorded for each; Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) covers continuing to operate without the capability. **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Secure versus Transparent:** Where the retired system held no sensitive data and performed no protective function, purging everything is the simpler and safer course. Where the same learned material is both the thing that must be purged for data protection and the thing needed to interpret past decisions or to brief a successor, the two requirements act directly against each other on one artifact. One resolution is to separate the artifact from what is derived from it, retaining thresholds, patterns and exception cases in a form that does not carry the underlying data; others include a documented adjudication before teardown, or retention under legal hold with access controls narrower than the system had. **Safe versus Secure:** Where every delegation can be confirmed revoked, completing the retirement and withdrawing all authority are the same act. Where a delegation cannot be confirmed, often because the failed dependency that forced the retirement is also the means of confirming it, refusing to complete leaves the retirement indefinitely open while treating it as complete leaves authority possibly live. One resolution is to complete on a recorded exception naming what could not be confirmed and what compensating control covers it, so that the residual authority is tracked rather than either ignored or blocking.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:** **GOVERN 1.7** (on decommissioning and phasing out AI systems): This Practice contributes the procedures that subcategory asks for, covering the withdrawal of authority, the preservation of what the system knew, and a decision on what happens to everything that depended on it; it does not cover the decision to retire a system on performance grounds. For that, see Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i) for detecting the degradation and Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz) for who decides. **MANAGE 4.1** (on post-deployment monitoring plans including decommissioning): This Practice contributes the decommissioning element of a post-deployment plan, and requires that its preparation is treated as a continuing duty rather than a closing activity; it does not cover the monitoring that would detect the conditions leading to retirement. For that, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk). **MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems): This Practice contributes the retirement path for a system that is being withdrawn permanently, including where the trigger is external; it does not cover disengaging a system that is behaving inconsistently with intended use while remaining in service. For that, see Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41).  **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-88r2, Guidelines for Media Sanitization](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/88/r2/final), Revision 2, September 2025, from NIST. Section 4.3.1 states the principle this Practice is built on, that the need and methods for sanitization should be identified and developed before the disposal phase is reached rather than at it. Section 3.1 gives the Clear, Purge and Destroy method taxonomy, and its Appendix C provides a Certificate of Sanitization form, which is the established documented form of a confirmed and evidenced teardown. **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/82/r3/final), from NIST. Task M-7, System Disposal, places a disposal strategy in the OT risk management lifecycle. Its OT-specific consideration bears directly on retirement timing: planned obsolescence in IT components may not extend to OT components, which are often required to be sustained beyond the availability of the IT components they depend on. **Standard** [ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288:2023, Systems and software engineering: system life cycle processes](https://www.iso.org/standard/81702.html), published jointly by ISO, IEC and IEEE. Clause 6.4.14, the disposal process, is the systems-engineering counterpart of this Practice and is stated more strongly than the AI-specific sources: its first listed outcome is that disposal constraints are provided as inputs to requirements, architecture, design and implementation, making retirement a design input rather than a closing activity. It also holds that where a system is still useful, the disposal process manages its transition to a new life cycle rather than a continuation of the existing one, and that records of disposal actions and analysis are available. **Guidance** [Agentic Identity and Access Management](https://www.coalitionforsecureai.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/agentic-identity-and-access-control.pdf), Workstream 4: Secure Design Patterns for Agentic Systems, version 1.0, 20 March 2026, from the Coalition for Secure AI, an OASIS Open Project. Section 3.5 treats decommissioning as the closing phase of an agent's identity lifecycle rather than as a separate activity, and sets out what it requires. Its logging appendix bears on the tension above: the per-action log it specifies records the policy version in force at the time, so a decision record remains interpretable only while the policy it was taken under is also retained. | **13.1: Prepare for retirement while the system is still running** Establish, during design and operation, the records and capabilities that a future retirement will require. This includes knowing what would trigger retirement, maintaining a current account of what the system actually does rather than what it was specified to do, and retaining the ability to extract the operational knowledge it holds. These are continuing obligations rather than a closing task, because retirement can arrive without notice and can arrive after the system has stopped working, at which point none of them can be performed. **Search Keywords:**  exit planning, design for decommissioning, retirement triggers, behavioral drift documentation, as-built documentation, knowledge extraction, data portability, vendor lock-in, vendor insolvency, model deprecation  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Accountable:** Contributes the conditions under which retirement becomes required and the authority that decides, recorded before either is needed, so that an imposed retirement is executed against a prior decision rather than improvised. It does not establish the decision authority itself, which Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz) establishes. **Transparent:** Contributes a maintained account of what the system does in operation, reconciled against what it was specified to do, so that the difference is known at the point the system is removed. It does not cover detecting the drift that produces the difference, which Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i) and Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk) cover. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Privacy-Enhanced:** Retaining an extractable account of what a system learned keeps material alive beyond the system that produced it, and that material may carry the properties of the data it was derived from. Practice [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m) governs the provenance and handling of that data, and any retention should be justified against a specific need. \> To the Reviewer: \> We are actively looking for examples of record retention guidelines and regulations that may require special handling for AI systems.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:** **GOVERN 1.7** (on decommissioning and phasing out AI systems): This Task contributes the part of those procedures that cannot be performed at retirement time, establishing triggers, behavioral records and knowledge extraction as duties held while the system runs; it does not cover the withdrawal of authority itself. For that, see Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych). **MANAGE 4.1** (on post-deployment monitoring plans including decommissioning): This Task contributes the decommissioning preparation that a post-deployment plan should carry, including triggers originating outside the organization; it does not cover the monitoring activity itself. For that, see Task [3.4: Monitor for anomalous AI behavior](#bookmark=kix.oljdtmg4yzhk).  | **13.1.1: Identify retirement triggers, including those outside the organization's control** Record the conditions that would initiate retirement of each AI capability, and who holds the authority to decide, under Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz). Internal triggers include a sponsor decision, a policy change, or an incident. External triggers are the ones most easily overlooked: deprecation of a foundation model, withdrawal of a third-party interface, insolvency of a vendor, or a regulatory prohibition. An externally imposed trigger compresses the timeline and may remove the option of an orderly shutdown. **References:**   \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **13.1.2: Maintain a current account of system behavior, not only its specification** Maintain documentation of how the AI system behaves in operation, reconciled periodically against the behavior it was specified and accepted for. Behavior may diverge over time through adaptation, data drift, or changes in upstream systems, and the divergence is not always recorded. At retirement, the difference between the two determines what is actually being removed, and it cannot be reconstructed after the system stops running. **References:**   \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **13.1.3: Retain the ability to extract operational knowledge before it is needed** Establish and periodically exercise a means of recovering the operational knowledge an AI system has accumulated, in a form usable without the system itself. This may include thresholds, learned patterns, exception cases, and the conditions under which the system departs from its normal behavior. For systems whose behavior is derived rather than specified, this capability has to be built in and maintained; it cannot be added at the point of retirement. **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-88r2, Guidelines for Media Sanitization](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/88/r2/final), from NIST. Section 4.3.1 makes the same argument for sanitization that this Implementation makes for knowledge: the need and the method should be established before the disposal phase, because the choice of what is used in the system determines what will be possible at the end of its life. |
|  |  | **13.1.4: Establish what retirement the product itself supports** Determine, for each acquired AI product or AI-enabled device, what its supplier provides for retiring it: whether configuration and operational data can be removed, whether ownership can be transferred without carrying that data with it, and whether the product can be rendered inoperable by logical or physical means. For embedded and field-deployed AI, this is a property of the product rather than a choice available to the operator, and it is settled at acquisition whether or not anyone asked. Where the supplier provides no such capability, record this as a constraint on retirement rather than discovering it at the point of retirement. Disclosure requirements for procurement are in Implementation [6.1.1: Add AI disclosure to existing procurement policies](#bookmark=kix.untd4qjdgbrs), and the offboarding process for a component that reaches end of life is in Implementation [6.2.3: Manage end-of-life transitions for externally sourced AI components](#bookmark=kix.21o7ua4uecm4). **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST IR 8259r1, Foundational Cybersecurity Activities for IoT Product Manufacturers](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8259/r1/final), from NIST. Section 4.3.5, Product Retirement Options, sets out what a manufacturer should communicate about retiring a product, and its questions are the ones an acquirer should be asking in return: whether ownership can be transferred without the user and configuration data going with it, and whether the device can be rendered inoperable, by logical means such as an application or by physical means on the device itself. It is written to manufacturers rather than to operators, which is what makes it useful here as the other side of the same conversation. |
|  | **13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn** Remove every means by which a retired AI system can still act, and verify the removal rather than assuming it. This covers credentials and tokens, scheduled and event-driven triggers, authority delegated to other entities, and the compute resources that may still hold any of these. An AI system that retains an active trigger and a valid credential continues to act after it has been recorded as retired. **Search Keywords:**  credential revocation, token purge, integration teardown, webhook deregistration, scheduled task removal, delegated authority, cascading revocation, orphaned container, residual access, teardown receipt  **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Secure:** Contributes the exhaustive and evidenced withdrawal of everything by which a retired system could act, extended through delegated authority and through the resources that may hold credentials independently of the identity being revoked. It does not establish the identities and revocation mechanisms being exercised, which Task [5.1: Establish identities and authentication for AI entities](#bookmark=kix.46vcje448pa0) establishes. **Accountable:** Contributes a record of what was revoked, what was confirmed, and what could not be confirmed, so that residual authority after a retirement is a tracked item rather than an unknown. It does not cover the logging infrastructure that record relies on, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers. **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Resilient:** Withdrawing authority exhaustively and at once removes the option of a partial or staged retirement, so a dependency discovered afterwards cannot be served by briefly restoring access. Identifying dependents under Implementation [13.3.3: Determine and record what happens to everything that depended on the system](#bookmark=id.gknfd9voaeez) before this point is what makes the irreversibility acceptable.  **Related AI RMF subcategories include:** **MANAGE 2.4** (on decommissioning or superseding AI systems): This Task contributes the mechanism by which a decommissioned system is actually prevented from acting, including authority it delegated to other entities; it does not cover the decision to decommission or the conditions that prompt it. For that, see Implementation [13.1.1: Identify retirement triggers, including those outside the organization's control](#bookmark=id.4abk974ttodd). **GOVERN 1.7** (on decommissioning and phasing out AI systems): This Task contributes the part of those procedures that helps ensure phasing out does not itself increase risk, by treating unconfirmed revocation as a recorded exception rather than as either success or an indefinite block; it does not cover what is preserved before teardown. For that, see Task [13.3: Preserve what is needed and hand over what depended on the system](#bookmark=id.7u2ur7qhofep). | **13.2.1: Revoke credentials and disable triggers, and confirm the result** Revoke the identities, credentials, tokens and keys held by the AI system, and disable the mechanisms that can invoke it, including scheduled tasks, event subscriptions, callbacks and registered interfaces. Confirm each revocation, not only the request, and retain the confirmation. Identity revocation is covered as a capability in Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck); what this Implementation adds is that retirement requires it to be exhaustive and evidenced. **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-88r2, Guidelines for Media Sanitization](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/88/r2/final), from NIST. Its Appendix C provides a sample Certificate of Sanitization, a record naming what was acted on, by what method, by whom, and how the result was verified. It is the established documented form of the confirmed and retained result this Implementation asks for, and transfers directly to credentials and triggers. **Guidance** [Agentic Identity and Access Management](https://www.coalitionforsecureai.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/agentic-identity-and-access-control.pdf), Workstream 4: Secure Design Patterns for Agentic Systems, from the Coalition for Secure AI. The Decommissioning subsection of Section 3.5 states the actions this Implementation requires: revoke the identity and invalidate tokens, securely erase private keys, purge or appropriately handle sensitive data consistent with data-protection obligations, and revoke downstream delegated rights. The paper’s Appendix E supplies the corresponding record, an agent registry holding lifecycle state and an agent.decommissioned lifecycle event, which is where the confirmation is retained rather than in the teardown procedure itself. |
|  |  | **13.2.2: Revoke authority delegated to other entities, and record what could not be confirmed** Where the AI system could delegate authority or create subordinate agents, trace and revoke that authority through every level it reaches. Where a delegation cannot be confirmed revoked, this should be recorded and escalated rather than treated as either success or an indefinite block on completing the retirement. Under an externally imposed trigger the means of confirming revocation may itself have failed, which is precisely when an unresolvable check will occur. **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [Agentic Identity and Access Management](https://www.coalitionforsecureai.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/agentic-identity-and-access-control.pdf), Workstream 4: Secure Design Patterns for Agentic Systems, from the Coalition for Secure AI. Section 3.5 requires that decommissioning revoke downstream delegated rights, and the paper’s Appendix D describes the mechanism that makes this achievable at retirement: short-lived, tightly scoped delegation tokens, a centralized revocation service supporting near real-time invalidation, and runtime components subscribing to revocation events so that they stop accepting revoked tokens rather than waiting for expiry. The paper’s Appendix E names delegation.revoked as a logged event, which is what allows a revocation to be shown rather than assumed. |
|  |  | **13.2.3: Reclaim resources, not only permissions** Identify and reclaim the compute and storage resources associated with the AI system, including containers, sandboxes, scratch storage and cached data. These are distinct from the permissions revoked above and are often missed because the retirement record is organized around authorizations. An orphaned execution environment may hold a mounted credential that survives the revocation of everything else. What happens to the model, training pipeline and datasets themselves is addressed in Implementation [7.6.2: Secure Archiving and Destruction of Model and Other AI Artifacts](#bookmark=kix.xx5v4ox8cnac). **References:**   \>Candidates \- suggestions welcome **Guidance** [NIST SP 800-88r2, Guidelines for Media Sanitization](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/88/r2/final), from NIST. Section 3.1 sets out the Clear, Purge and Destroy methods and the basis for choosing between them, which is the decision to be made for each storage resource associated with a retired AI system rather than one choice applied to all of them. |
|  | **13.3: Preserve what is needed and hand over what depended on the system** Before an AI system is torn down, capture the evidence that will not survive it, and address what was relying on it. This includes volatile state that exists only while the system is running, the operational function the system was performing for others, and any protective function it was performing that would otherwise disappear without a corresponding record.  **Search Keywords:**  forensic preservation, volatile state, working memory, control handover, successor system, downstream dependents, deprecation notice, control regression, data migration, records retention schedule, legal hold **Main Trustworthiness Contributions:** **Transparent:** Contributes the capture of state that exists only while the system runs, before the teardown that destroys it, so that an incident-driven retirement does not also destroy the evidence of what prompted it. It does not cover the durable logging that survives teardown on its own, which Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl) covers. **Secure:** Contributes the treatment of a retired detection or filtering capability as a change to controls as well as an asset disposal, so that a protective function is handed over rather than silently discontinued. It draws on, but does not itself maintain, the account of system behavior kept under Implementation [13.1.2: Maintain a current account of system behavior, not only its specification](#bookmark=id.c2yjrmdtswl). **Trustworthiness Effects to Consider:** **Privacy-Enhanced:** Preserving volatile state and learned material before teardown retains content that the retirement was in part intended to remove, and volatile state may contain material never intended to persist at all. Practice [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m) governs how that material is then handled, and the tension is set out at Practice [13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority](#bookmark=id.ieoypls7vre2). **Trustworthiness Tensions to Balance:** **Accountable versus Safe:** Where the retirement is planned and the capability is not protective, preserving evidence and handing over the function can both be done at an unhurried pace. Where the retirement follows an incident, the same actions compete: capturing volatile state takes time during which a system suspected of causing harm is still running. One resolution is to separate isolation from teardown, cutting the system's authority and reach first under Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych) while leaving it running long enough to capture state; others include pre-authorizing the capture so it does not require a decision in the moment. **Related AI RMF subcategories include:** **GOVERN 4.2** (on documenting and communicating risks and impacts): This Task contributes the identification of parties that depended on a retired AI system and a recorded decision for each, including where that decision is to make no provision; it does not cover the technical mapping of internal systems consuming its outputs, or the deprecation timeline given to them. For that, see Implementation [7.6.1: Internal Dependency Mapping (Orphan System Prevention)](#bookmark=kix.xxh12qj5nq5v). **GOVERN 1.7** (on decommissioning and phasing out AI systems): This Task contributes the requirement that phasing out does not decrease trustworthiness by removing a protective function without handover, and that evidence is captured before teardown makes it unrecoverable; it does not cover the withdrawal of authority. For that, see Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych). | **13.3.1: Capture volatile state before teardown** Where retirement follows an incident or a suspected failure, capture working memory, in-flight state and intermediate reasoning artifacts before the execution environment is destroyed. Durable logs and stored records are addressed under Practice [10: Implement multi-tiered AI system logging and audit capabilities](#bookmark=kix.i8atdoe4ikbl); this Implementation concerns the state that exists only while the system is running and that teardown destroys permanently. This step should precede teardown and resource reclamation, because those actions can make the state unrecoverable. **References:**   \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **13.3.2: Treat the retirement of a protective AI capability as a change to controls** Where an AI system performs a detection, monitoring, screening or filtering function, its retirement removes a control and should be processed as a control change as well as an asset disposal. Establish what the system was detecting, including behavior it had learned to detect that is not documented elsewhere, and what will perform that function afterward. A replacement that begins without this information starts without the accumulated basis of the one it replaces, and the resulting gap may not be visible from either system's records. **References:**   \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
|  |  | **13.3.3: Determine and record what happens to everything that depended on the system** Identify the processes, systems and people that relied on the AI system's output, and record for each whether it will be migrated to an alternative, formally notified and wound down, or left without provision. Abandonment may be a legitimate decision, but it should be a recorded one. Human operators and downstream automation may have adapted to the retired system's particular behavior, so the effect of removal is not limited to the function it formally provided. Where the dependents are internal systems consuming the AI system's outputs, the mapping and the deprecation timeline are in Implementation [7.6.1: Internal Dependency Mapping (Orphan System Prevention)](#bookmark=kix.xxh12qj5nq5v); what this Implementation adds is that a decision is recorded for each dependent, including where that decision is to make no provision. **References:**   \>None yet \- suggestions welcome |
| **14, 15, etc.: (TBD) \>To the Reviewer:**  \>If you've identified a risk area that doesn't fit naturally as a Task or Implementation under Practices 1–13, we'd welcome your suggestion for a new Practice here,  including a short description of the governance-level risk it addresses and why it needs its own Practice rather than sitting under an existing one.  |  |  |

# 

# Glossary and Terminology Crosswalk 

\>(TBD \- suggestions and contributions welcome, particularly for terms that have different meanings between sectors that might not be obvious)

* **Human oversight:** An umbrella term for arrangements by which people remain responsible for an AI system's behavior in operation. It denotes a category, not a single control, and comprises four distinct objects defined below: oversight mode, oversight capacity, oversight threshold and oversight authority.  
  *Candidate references: ISO/IEC DIS 42105, Guidance for Human Oversight of AI Systems. ISO/IEC TS 8200:2024, Controllability of Automated Systems.*

  * **Oversight mode:** The regime governing how a person participates in an AI system's decisions: human-in-the-loop (approval required before action), human-on-the-loop (action proceeds, with intervention capability retained), or human-out-of-the-loop (autonomous execution with retrospective review). Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud) defines the boundaries between them.  
    *Candidate references: DHS/CISA Principles for the Secure Integration of AI in Operational Technology, Section 4.2*

  * **Oversight capacity:** The finite human attention available to exercise oversight, measured across all AI systems drawing on the same pool of people rather than per system. Capacity is exhaustible, is consumed concurrently by every system an operator supervises, and can be saturated by a correlated event even where each system's individual demand is manageable.   
    *Candidate references: EEMUA Publication 191*

  * **Oversight threshold:** The point beyond which a given oversight mode ceases to be effective: where the interval between an anomaly and an irreversible consequence is shorter than what human intervention can be relied upon to achieve. Implementation [4.5.1: Define engineering time-constant thresholds](#bookmark=id.mum8tq4afxyp) defines how these are established.  
    *Candidate references: ISA-84 / IEC 61511*

  * **Oversight authority:** The named, non-delegable authority to set the oversight mode, to step it up or down, and to revoke the operating envelope. Distinct from the ability to intervene in a particular action: authority persists in regimes where real-time intervention is infeasible. 

* **Industrial control system (ICS):** A control system that monitors and manipulates physical processes, such as supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) systems, distributed control systems (DCS) and programmable logic controllers (PLCs), made up of control components that act together to achieve an industrial objective. ICS are a subset of operational technology (OT), which also includes systems such as building automation and physical access control. In emergency management and public safety, ICS more commonly means the Incident Command System, the standardized incident management structure within the National Incident Management System (NIMS). This profile does not use ICS in that sense.   
  *Candidate references: NIST SP 800-82r3, Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security, Appendix B (Glossary), entries for "industrial control system" and "operational technology".*

* **Zero trust:** A cybersecurity approach in which no implicit trust is granted to users, devices or services based solely on their network location or ownership, and access to each resource is authenticated and authorized separately. This profile applies the underlying principle, that trust is verified at each boundary rather than assumed, to AI risk more generally. The extension is in what is not assumed: in cybersecurity, chiefly that a requester is who it claims to be and is permitted to act; here, also that a legitimate, uncompromised AI system is behaving as intended. An AI system's outputs, actions and inputs therefore warrant verification where they cross into other systems, physical processes and human decisions, whether a departure from expected behavior arises from compromise, malfunction or a change in operating conditions. For example, an AI agent holding valid credentials is still authorized action by action (Implementation [5.2.2: Action-level, context-aware authorization policies](#bookmark=kix.68ohugbvqcsm)), and guardrails independent of the AI system check its outputs before they reach a physical process (Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn)).  
  *Candidate references: NIST SP 800-207, Zero Trust Architecture; Adapting Zero Trust Principles to Operational Technology (CISA, DoW, DOE, 2026\)*

# Appendix A: Trustworthiness Characteristics

\> To the Reviewer: 

\> This appendix will give the source definition of each characteristic, explain the grouping and where it departs from the AI RMF's own groupings, and set out the tensions between characteristics. We would welcome comment on the grouping in Table 2 in the meantime.

# Appendix B: Historic Incident-to-Practice Mapping 

This appendix presents case studies of several historic AI-related or AI-relevant incidents and maps them to examples of one or more of the 13 TACIP Practices. This includes incidents that may not have inherently involved AI at the time, in which case the mapping was performed for a hypothetical, similar AI-enabled variant. The goal is to provide concrete examples of how each Practice may have contributed to limiting the likelihood and severity of harm, be it in terms of lives, dollars, downtime, or public trust. Note that this mapping is not exhaustive, rather it is intended to demonstrate how practices are relevant to real-world incidents. 

**\> To the Reviewer:**   
\> This mapping is preliminary. We specifically seek feedback on whether these real and hypothetical scenarios are useful, whether the level of abstraction and detail is appropriate, and if this successfully grounds the practices in an operationally relevant manner.

| Incident | Practices | Commentary |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| **i1: Medical prediction model (predictive AI clinical tool)** A proprietary machine-learning medical condition prediction tool, in widespread use across US hospitals, was found by independent external validation to miss roughly two-thirds of positive cases while generating substantial false-positive alert fatigue. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Healthcare and Public Health [JAMA Internal Medicine](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/2781307) | **Practice** [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) | AI clinical decision-support model is deployed for risk prediction without verified, real-world performance acceptance criteria, causing clinicians and hospitals to rely on vendor-reported accuracy that does not hold under operational conditions. |
|  | **Practice** [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) | AI prediction model performs inconsistently across hospitals and care-timing contexts because robustness, calibration, and site-specific quality-of-service expectations were not validated before scaled clinical use. |
|  | **Practice** [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) | AI-generated alerts had poor positive predictive value, creating alert fatigue and reducing appropriate trust in the model’s outputs during high-consequence clinical decision-making. |
| **i2: Health insurer prediction algorithm** A Senate investigation found that as a large health insurer automated post-acute-care claims review (including with their AI system), its denial rate for that care more than doubled (10.9% to 22.7%, 2020-2022), and that the insurer explored using AI to target denials least likely to be appealed. A separate federal lawsuit alleges that the tool displaced physicians' clinical judgment. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Healthcare and Public Health [NPJ Digital Medicine](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12979811/) | **Practice** [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) | An AI-enabled claims-review process is optimized around denial likelihood or cost-control objectives rather than validated patient-care, medical-necessity, and fairness requirements, resulting in automated decisions misaligned with the system’s intended healthcare purpose. |
|  | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | A high-consequence AI-assisted claims-review workflow issues or supports post-acute-care denials without defined risk thresholds, independent review gates, or oversight controls before the decision affects patient access to care. |
|  | **Practice** [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) | AI-generated post-acute-care recommendation is allowed to displace treating-physician judgment, leaving no reliable emergency override or recovery path when the automated output conflicts with clinical judgment. |
|  | **Practice** [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb) | AI-assisted claims-review decisions produce systematic patterns of inappropriate care denials, but no procedures exist to investigate AI decision behavior, identify affected patients, assess harm at scale, and coordinate timely remediation before significant patient-care impacts occur. |
|  | **Practice** [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) | Business and clinical users are insufficiently trained to recognize when AI-enabled claims-review outputs reflect an objective other than clinical appropriateness, such as cost or the likelihood that a decision will be challenged. |
| **i3: Robotaxi pedestrian-dragging incident** An autonomous taxi's own perception and planning system executed a post-collision pullover maneuver while a pedestrian was trapped underneath, dragging the pedestrian about 20 feet. The autonomous taxi company then incompletely reported the crash to federal regulators, leading to the suspension of the company’s California operating permits, a voluntary nationwide halt of its driverless fleet, and a \$1.5 million NHTSA fine. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Transportation Systems [National Highway Traffic Safety Administration](https://www.nhtsa.gov/press-releases/consent-order-cruise-crash-reporting) | **Practice** [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) | Autonomous-vehicle AI system is deployed without targeted validation for rare but severe post-collision failure modes, including scenarios where a pedestrian or object remains beneath the vehicle after impact. |
|  | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | Autonomous-vehicle AI planning system continues executing a movement maneuver after detecting a collision, without a deterministic fail-safe state that prevents additional motion when a person or object may be trapped underneath. |
|  | **Practice** [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb) | Organization fails to perform and disclose a complete AI-aware incident analysis after an autonomous-vehicle harm event, omitting critical AI behavior needed by regulators to understand the full hazard sequence. |
|  | **Practice** [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) | Autonomous system executes AI-generated direct-control logic for vehicle movement without deterministic verification that the planned maneuver is safe in the current hazardous state. |
| **i4: Facial recognition wrongful arrest** City police relied on a facial-recognition match, later shown to be only the ninth-best candidate, as the effective basis for arresting an unrelated person, who spent about 30 hours in custody before being told the computer had gotten it wrong. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Emergency Services [American Civil Liberties Union](https://www.aclu.org/cases/williams-v-city-of-detroit-face-recognition-false-arrest) | **Practice** [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) | Facial-recognition system is used to support a custodial arrest without validated accuracy, evidentiary, and consequence-calibrated acceptance requirements for law-enforcement use. |
|  | **Practice** [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) | Facial-recognition system’s false-match performance under degraded real-world image conditions is not tested or validated before its output is relied on in a high-consequence criminal investigation. |
|  | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | Probabilistic facial-recognition match is treated as sufficient basis for a suspect identification without mandatory corroborating evidence, defined confidence thresholds, or multi-party oversight before arrest. |
|  | **Practice** [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) | Investigators over-trust an AI-generated facial-recognition match and treat the system output as determinative rather than as an uncertain lead requiring corroboration and human review. |
| **i5: Airline chatbot hallucination** An airline’s generative AI customer-service chatbot fabricated a bereavement-fare policy detail that contradicted the airline's actual policy; a Canadian tribunal held the airline liable for the chatbot's misinformation in a first-of-its-kind ruling. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Transportation Systems [Civil Resolution Tribunal of British Columbia](https://canlii.ca/t/k2spq) | **Practice** [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) | No validated contextual-accuracy benchmark existed for the chatbot's handling of the airline's own published policies before it was deployed publicly. |
|  | **Practice** [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) | AI chatbot fabricated a detail of the airline's bereavement-fare policy (that it could be applied for retroactively) that directly contradicted the airline's actual published policy. |
|  | **Practice** [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) | AI chatbot produces policy guidance that is not validated against authoritative business rules, causing users to rely on an AI-generated outcome that conflicts with the organization’s actual published policy. |
| **i6: National childcare benefits scandal** A self-learning machine-learning model used by a national tax authority to flag childcare-benefit fraud used nationality as a risk factor, wrongly flagging tens of thousands of families (mostly non-citizen or dual-national) whose benefits were terminated and full repayment demanded, ultimately triggering the government's resignation in 2021\. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Government Services and Facilities [Amnesty International](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/eur35/4686/2021/en/) | **Practice** [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) | A self-learning ML risk-classification model was fielded by the Dutch tax authority to flag childcare-benefit applications for fraud, using nationality as one of its risk factors, with no pre-deployment fairness or human-rights impact assessment. |
|  | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | Once an application was flagged by the model, benefits were automatically discontinued and full repayment demanded, in many cases with minimal further human assessment before the harm to the family occurred. |
|  | **Practice** [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb) | AI-driven fraud-risk classifications generate widespread erroneous benefit-enforcement actions, but the organization lacks procedures to detect systemic model-driven harm, reconstruct decision pathways, identify impacted populations, and coordinate corrective action before large-scale public harm occurs. |
|  | **Practice** [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) | Officials over-trust AI-generated fraud-risk flags and treat them as determinative findings, causing families to be penalized without sufficient independent review, fact-checking, or calibrated understanding of model uncertainty. |
| **i7: Autonomous-vehicle pedestrian fatality** An autonomous test vehicle detected a pedestrian \~6 seconds out but misclassified the detection and had its emergency braking suppressed by design; the over-trusting, distracted safety operator did not intervene, and the pedestrian was killed \- the first pedestrian fatality involving a self-driving car. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Transportation Systems [National Transportation Safety Board](https://www.ntsb.gov/investigations/accidentreports/reports/har1903.pdf) | **Practice** [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) | An AI driving system is tested on public roads without an established safety case, acceptance benchmarks, or defined handling of its known functional limitations. |
|  | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | The AI classifies the pedestrian inconsistently and its emergency-braking response is suppressed by design, with no bounded safe fallback under uncertainty. |
|  | **Practice** [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) | The AI does not alert the human to take over when it cannot handle the situation. |
|  | **Practice** [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) | The safety operator over-trusts the AI and disengages from monitoring (automation complacency). |

Incidents i8 onward re-examine historical cybersecurity events as hypothetical case studies, to illustrate how TACIP practices apply if equivalent disruptions were driven by or otherwise involved AI risk.

| Incident | Practices | Commentary |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| **i8: Energy sector pipeline ransomware attack *(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Ransomware on a pipeline operator’s IT/billing systems (via a legacy VPN account without MFA) led the operator to precautionarily shut the pipeline, disrupting fuel supply across the US East Coast. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Energy [U.S. Government Accountability Office](https://www.gao.gov/assets/gao-21-105263.pdf) | **Practice** [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) | AI-mediated pipeline scheduling or operational-support function depends on compromised IT systems without validated resilience expectations, causing loss of confidence in operational integrity and triggering precautionary disruption of fuel-delivery operations. |
|  | **Practice** [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) | AI system has access to the VPN account or direct access to IT/OT operations. One agent's credential is phished, stolen, or left over-scoped, causing disruption in the pipeline. |
|  | **Practice** [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) | AI-supported pipeline operation is taken offline after a compromise, and the absence of AI-aware continuity and trusted-state recovery planning prolongs disruption to mission-critical fuel distribution. |
| **i9: IT management platform compromise  *(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Attackers added malicious code to certain versions of an IT management platform, which was downloaded by thousands of private and government organizations; of those, an estimated 100–250 were breached with active follow-on access. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Information Technology [Cybersecurity & Infrastructure Security Agency](https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/directives/ed-21-01-mitigate-solarwinds-orion-code-compromise-closed)   | **Practice** [6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management](#bookmark=kix.gk6f3b3rnwyn) | A trusted vendor ships a poisoned model, tampered weights, or an AI-generated build component through its signed update channel. |
|  | **Practice** [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m) | The tainted model/component is now deployed across many internal systems that fine-tune on or call it. |
|  | **Practice** [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) | AI generates build code/configuration inside the pipeline and that machine-authored logic carries a flaw or backdoor. |
| **i10: Email and collaboration server on-prem exploitation*(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Zero-day flaws in on-premises email and collaboration servers were mass-exploited to drop web shells that gave attackers persistent access to government and enterprise networks. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Government Services and Facilities [U.S. Government Accountability Office](https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-22-104746) | **Practice** [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) | Unauthorized or shadow AI agent executes with excessive privilege in an unvetted environment and persists after remediation, enabling continued attacker access despite patching or cleanup efforts. |
|  | **Practice** [7: Manage internal AI supply chain and data provenance](#bookmark=kix.umm6oc9t2c0m) | A locally hosted AI model or serving component with unverified provenance is exploited and used to maintain persistent unauthorized logic inside the enterprise environment. |
| **i11: Remote management tool supply-chain ransomware *(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Attackers exploited zero-days in a remote-management tool to push ransomware downward through managed service providers to their many downstream customers. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Information Technology [Cybersecurity & Infrastructure Security Agency](https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/news/kaseya-ransomware-attack-guidance-affected-msps-and-their-customers) | **Practice** [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) | AI-enabled management agent retains broad standing privilege across multiple customer environments, allowing a single compromise to propagate malicious actions or ransomware at downstream scale. |
|  | **Practice** [6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management](#bookmark=kix.gk6f3b3rnwyn) | Trusted AI orchestration or remote-management platform in the external supply chain is compromised, enabling attackers to distribute malicious activity across dependent tenants, service providers, or downstream customers. |
| **i12: Health system ransomware *(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Ransomware entered a national healthcare IT system via a malicious email, spread over weeks, and forced a shutdown of IT-driven clinical systems across the country’s public health service. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Healthcare and Public Health [Health Service Executive](https://about.hse.ie/publications/conti-cyber-attack-on-the-hse-independent-post-incident-review/)  | **Practice** [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) | The compromise cascades to a total lockout of all clinical systems. |
|  | **Practice** [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) | A compromised identity/agent escalates and moves laterally across \~70,000 devices. |
|  | **Practice** [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb) | A compromise of AI-enabled healthcare systems propagates across clinical operations, but responders lack procedures to identify affected AI services, assess impacts to patient-care functions, coordinate recovery priorities, and investigate how the disruption spread through interconnected systems. |
|  | **Practice** [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) | All IT-driven clinical systems (including any AI) are down, affecting healthcare services. |
| **i13: Healthcare payments system ransomware*(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Ransomware hit a large healthcare payments provider (entering via a portal without MFA), knocking out claims and payment processing for much of the national healthcare system. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Healthcare and Public Health [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/731766/000073176624000045/unh-20240221.htm) | **Practice** [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) | A highly concentrated AI-mediated claims and payment clearinghouse lacks validated availability and resilience expectations, causing widespread healthcare transaction disruption when the service becomes unavailable. |
|  | **Practice** [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) | An AI/agent access path to the clearinghouse lacks MFA/least-privilege and is compromised. |
|  | **Practice** [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb) | Compromise of a highly concentrated AI-mediated healthcare transaction platform disrupts dependent organizations, but the organization lacks procedures to assess ecosystem-wide impacts, coordinate incident response, prioritize restoration efforts, and communicate operational consequences to affected stakeholders. |
|  | **Practice** [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) | An AI-mediated claims and payment-processing service lacks effective continuity alternatives, causing prolonged transaction outages and financial disruption for dependent healthcare providers. |
| **i14: Water treatment incident *(hypothetical AI scenario)*** An operator at a city water plant saw and reversed a remote change that spiked sodium-hydroxide dosing; the event's status as a deliberate attack was later disputed. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Water and Wastewater Systems [Cybersecurity & Infrastructure Security Agency](https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa21-042a) | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | An AI agent can write chemical-dosing setpoints; it (or an attacker acting through it) drives sodium hydroxide to a dangerous level. |
|  | **Practice** [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) | The AI's setpoint action must be caught and reversed in real time. |
|  | **Practice** [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) | AI-managed or agent-generated credentials for OT interfaces are weak, default, or insufficiently governed, allowing exposed water-system devices to be accessed and manipulated by unauthorized actors. |
| **i15: Active exploitation of water utility control systems*(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Attackers believed to be nation-state actors took over internet-exposed programmable logic controllers (PLCs) at water utilities by using the devices' default password. **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Water and Wastewater Systems [Cybersecurity & Infrastructure Security Agency](https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-335a) | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | An agent with authority to generate or modify control logic installs logic that does not perform the required function, with no boundary outside the device positioned to detect that it has stopped doing so. |
|  | **Practice** [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation awareness](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf) | The interface operators would use to override the system is itself disabled, and the display they would read is replaced, so intervention depends on a path independent of the affected device. |
|  | **Practice** [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) | Internet-exposed AI/OT devices or agents run on default or weak credentials. |
| **i16: National power grid attack*(hypothetical AI scenario)*** Attackers believed to be nation-state actors phished into three power distributors and remotely opened breakers, cutting power to \~225,000 people (restored via manual operation). **Critical Infrastructure Sector:** Energy [Cybersecurity & Infrastructure Security Agency](https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/ics-alerts/ir-alert-h-16-056-01) | **Practice** [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) | An agent with breaker-control authority (or an attacker through it) opens breakers. |
