# Appendix D: Crosswalks

\> **To the Reviewer:**

\> While TACIP prioritizes operational workflows and entry points for critical infrastructure acquirers, owners, and operators, we recognize that systems engineering teams, software developers, and compliance auditors often organize risk management activities within formal lifecycle models.

\> This appendix is under active development to provide explicit crosswalks mapping TACIP’s 13 Practices to established systems engineering standards (ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288, NIST SP 800-160) and AI management frameworks (ISO/IEC 42001, NIST AI RMF). We welcome feedback on which specific lifecycle phases, decision gates, or sub-processes within these standards offer the greatest utility for your organization's compliance and engineering workflows, and additional general resources that we should prioritize for crosswalking.

\> We also welcome suggestions for sector-specific crosswalks that we can pass on to sector-specific organizations for their consideration. 

# 

# Appendix E: Additional Information for the Reviewer 

\>This appendix supports the review of this discussion draft and will not appear in the published profile. 

## Document Design Criteria: 

The guidelines in this section are internal design suggestions for document authors that guide them when writing the practices, tasks, and implementations, not operational controls for critical infrastructure. They are not compulsory although deviating from these should be done with care. Note that as this document is still very much a draft and work in progress, with text at different levels of refinement, it’s likely that some parts are inadvertently out of compliance with these criteria. Feel free to note this if you see cases where this is particularly important. 

### Overall: 

1. **“Do no harm.”** This criterion serves as an internal writing constraint to protect operators from the dangers of blind compliance, misinformed blanket regulations, or rigid management mandates. Every practice, task, and implementation should be drafted so that qualified professional and engineering judgment is baked into the requirement itself. To achieve this, avoid broad or generalized statements, instead bounding commands with conditional triggers and engineering qualifiers (e.g., "where technically feasible" or "commensurate with process safety thresholds"). Furthermore, text should leverage deliberate semantic scoping so that if a control is truly inapplicable or physically unsafe for a specific environment, an operator can explicitly satisfy the mandate "to the letter" by formally declaring that specific operational path out of scope, although boundaries should be constructed tightly to prevent loopholes.

2. **Modular and Entry-Point Agnostic.** Every practice, task, and implementation is designed under the assumption that the reader has reviewed the foundational frontmatter, but not necessarily any other specific row in the framework. While rows work synergistically, an individual reading a single item in isolation should find sufficient context to understand its immediate risk-management utility. If a hard dependency or critical relationship exists with another practice, the row should explicitly route the reader to that section to ensure a seamless "random-access" reading experience. 

3. **Refer to other standards/guidance/resources as much as possible.** Use as few words as necessary to help the reader to understand if this relates to them and to help them scope and interpret those references, especially if it’s a long reference and only part of it is relevant. 

4. **Understandable to the target audience.** Target audiences are described more below. 

5. **Actionable and useful.** At each level of abstraction, there should be a clear line between the practice/task/implementation and something that can actually be done and will improve risk management, particularly given the resource restrictions and legacy infrastructure and policies often present in CI. 

6. **Achievable and provides a gradient.** While our goal is to cover the ideal and industry best practice alongside what a 'minimum viable' looks like for resource-constrained environments, we are still evaluating how to best standardize these definitions across different critical infrastructure sectors . This criteria is currently treated as an exploratory objective to determine if tiered implementations prevent compliance fatigue, or if they inject unnecessary complexity into cross-sector governance. 

7. **Risk-proportional.** Practices, tasks, and implementations should help the reader understand the significance of a risk (or lack thereof) to help them allocate limited time, attention, and other resources. 

8. **Acknowledge the tradespace.** Risk management involves managing all relevant risks. Reducing risk in one area might increase risk in others and there may already be existing legacy trade-offs that organizations are used to (or may have mandated) that might be difficult to adjust. We should give stakeholders space to make this trade-off, even if they are obliged to follow a given practice/task/implementation. 

9. **General to all forms of AI that could be in scope.** The terminology we use should be relatable to the various audiences but also leave open room for novel approaches that don’t fit into existing (or commonly understood) approaches. For example, whatever we say should make sense for a neural network approach, as well as a traditional logic-based approach or a neuro-symbolic approach. Actually, I expect some of what we would say would be implicitly comparing/contrasting these. (If we find something that is narrow but really impactful, we need to make that scope clear.)

10. **Verifiable, measurable, and audit-ready.** Outcomes of these practices, tasks, and implementations should be naturally measurable, and compatible with the “Measure” function of the AI RMF, to allow leadership to track risk mitigation progress and enable regulatory oversight and forensic investigation. 

### Practices: 

1. **Management/Governance audience.** This might be the only column that a non-technical manager might read and decide to tell the folks under them to do. 

2. **Clear connection to the organization's goals.** It should be clear to a management audience what the incentive is to pay attention to this practice. This should reference the business/organizational goals and governance, rather than purely technical goals. 

3. **Industry/Sector keywords.** This should tie AI risk management concepts to industry and sector keywords so a non-AI, non-technical person who is just scanning keywords is likely to have an accurate intuition as to the type of risk and mitigation we’re talking about, and the point in the process lifecycle at which it applies. 

4. **Defensively non-technical.** Where possible, language should either be general enough, or appropriately explained, so that interpretation is accurate regardless of if this document is being read in a technical, sector-specific, or colloquial context. 

5. **Clear Ownership with Explicit Routing.** Each practice should allow the reader to identify which internal team or department is best suited to lead the objective. Rather than treating the Practice as an isolated silo, its standalone tasks and implementations serve as navigational signposts, guiding the lead department to the cross-functional collaborators they need to coordinate with next. 

### Tasks: 

1. **Technical Management audience.** Note that this could be hands-on-keyboard technical, but it could also be technical policy. 

2. **Discrete, assignable steps.** 

3. **Each should be easily identifiable with achieving some aspect of the practice.** 

4. **May be executed independently.** 

### Implementations: 

1. **Should point to specific ways of implementing a task.** Avoid actually going into technical/sequencing details beyond what is necessary to help the reader contextualize what we’re pointing at. 

2. **Should provide necessary, scoped context.** Avoid just pointing at a document and saying “Read this …” especially if the document is very long, involved, and/or from an area that the reader is unlikely to be familiar with. There should be just enough words to tell the reader which aspect of that document they need to pay attention to. 

3. **Ideally should include a minimal implementation.** This should give the reader a good idea of what some minimal steps are to reduce their risk, at minimal expense (and, by implication, when they shouldn’t bother if they don’t even have the resources to do that \- that they might be better off spending their resources on something else that has a greater impact). 

4. **Ideally should include an “ideal” implementation.** This should give the reader a good idea of what a “reasonable” attempt looks like, beyond which it might be worth looking at other measures should be taken to achieve a balanced risk management strategy. These paired structures are experimental; rows may leverage general illustrative examples if a strict resource split proves artificial or unhelpful for a given task. 

5. **Should concretely acknowledge the tradespace.** At least some hints as to specific things that might be traded off, particularly when it comes to mismatched lifecycles (20-30 years for typical CI assets, vs possibly \<1 year for many AI systems) and the need to scale across distributed infrastructure with limited resources. 

6. **Should try to be broadly relatable.** With 16 critical infrastructure sectors, it is likely impossible to come up with singular implementations that are directly relatable to all of them but language should be structured, where possible, to allow folks in sectors that are adjacent to still understand how the crux of the implementation might relate to them. This might require a fleshed out “primary” implementation and some minimal “secondary” ones that relate to the same idea but cover another group of sectors. 

## Ideas and References Already in Progress: 

We've identified a number of promising ideas, structural changes, and candidate references that we're actively working through internally, but haven't yet incorporated into the document itself. We're sharing this list so that if you were about to suggest one of these, you'll know it's already on our radar, and so you can help us prioritize, refine, or strengthen it.

For each item below, we'd especially value:

* **Prioritization:** If you could only see three of these addressed in the next revision, which would they be?

* **Better or additional references:** Especially ones with a brief note on which part of the reference applies and where.

* **Placement:** Where in the document (which Practice/Task/Implementation) a given idea or reference would be most useful as an illustrative example.

* **Sector-specific angles:** Whether an idea needs to be adapted, split, or caveated differently for your sector.

This list is not exhaustive, and its presence here doesn't guarantee inclusion in the next draft. It reflects our current thinking, not a commitment. If something below turns out to be off-base or lower priority than we think, please tell us that too. 

### Cross-Cutting Themes

These don't sit neatly under a single Practice, with each touching several. We've noted the Practices most relevant to each so you can jump to related content if useful.

**Enrichment of Search Keywords and references.** The comprehensive enrichment of "Search Keywords" and the operational contextualization of references are ongoing. In this draft, keyword fields remain sparse or blank, and references primarily serve as pointers to what we are considering and where we think they fit in. These will be expanded with additional context in later iterations and we welcome your feedback and suggestions on what would be most useful.

**Autonomy isn't one-dimensional.** We want to avoid framing AI autonomy as a single linear "level," since real systems vary across multiple independent dimensions. *Relates to: Practices 3, 4\. Feedback welcome on: whether a multi-dimensional framing would be clearer for your sector, and what those dimensions should be.*

**Theoretical vs. actual capability.** Distinguishing what a system is formally capable of from what it reliably does in practice. *Relates to: Practices 1, 2, 3\. Feedback welcome on: where this distinction most needs to be made explicit.*

**Cost as a risk-management input.** Considering a "cost of controls" framing so that overinvestment in one control doesn't crowd out resources for higher-value ones elsewhere. *Relates to: Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k), but potentially applicable wherever a gradient of implementations is offered. Feedback welcome on: whether this deserves standalone treatment or should be folded into the existing gradient/"minimum viable" language.*

**“Living off the land.”** Noting that intended, legitimate functionality (not just malicious code) can still be used to cause harm, both from adversarial action (human or AI), and from legitimate, known, but misbehaving, internal or external AI systems. *Relates to: Practices 2, 3, 5\. Feedback welcome on: whether this needs its own treatment or is better folded into existing guardrail/access-control language.*

**Predictive/monitoring use cases** (e.g., environmental or hazard forecasting) as possible additions to the Scope section's example list. *Feedback welcome on: sector-appropriate variants beyond a single illustrative case.*

**Document format changes.** This document has outgrown its original tabular format as presented in the PDF version. As we are also ensuring that the same information is also available in the Markdown, we are investigating other ways of presenting views into this same information that can be automatically (and deterministically) generated that may be easier (for both people and AI assistants) to digest, be it in print or interactively. We welcome suggestions. 

### Practice-Specific Items

#### Practice [1: Establish requirements for success](#bookmark=kix.c8cs921upv7k) (Establish requirements for success)

**Understanding what the system is actually optimizing for.** Ensuring requirements-setting explicitly accounts for possible gaps between a stated objective and what a system optimizes toward in practice.

#### Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41) (Robustness, resilience, and QoS)

**Specification gaming.** Addressing cases where a system optimizes for a stated metric in ways that violate the intent behind it.

**Financial/resource risk from compute cost volatility.** Both unpredictable usage spikes and vendor pricing changes over time as an operational risk category.

#### Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p) (Risks, policies, and oversight for automated/agentic behavior)

**Agentic interconnection and interdependence.** Framing point on how agentic systems' dynamic, multi-system interactions differ structurally from more static traditional software.

**Untested tool/dependency pulls.** Risk of an agent adopting an updated tool or dependency that hasn't itself been through TEVV.

#### Practice [6: Integrate visibility of the external supply chain of AI into vendor and 3rd party risk management](#bookmark=kix.gk6f3b3rnwyn) / 7 (Supply chain and provenance)

**AI/use-case inventory.** Considering a structured example-use-case index or appendix, separate from the main practice table.

**Reference candidates:** NIST SP 800-53 AI control overlays (COSAIS, as opposed to CoSAI); additional context around the emerging AIBOM/SBOM standards cluster (SPDX AI & Datasets, OWASP AIBOM, CycloneDX ML-BOM) as candidate machine-readable formats for Implementation [6.1.2: Require AIBOM for mission-critical applications](#bookmark=kix.86017r9tu0hx); MITRE System of Trust as a possible supply-chain reference alongside NIST SP 800-161.

**Reference candidate:** NCCoE's Software and AI Agent Identity and Authorization project, which is directly relevant to Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck)'s identity requirements; worth checking for any newly published output.

#### Practice [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb) (Incident analysis and response)

**Near-miss reporting.** Expand on capturing near-misses, not just realized incidents.

**Aligning incident definitions.** Checking whether the profile's working definition of an "incident" should explicitly cross-reference existing definitions used elsewhere (e.g., JCDC-style playbooks).

#### Practice [9: Provide calibrated, needs-based AI risk management training](#bookmark=kix.ujtyohnpy9k1) (Training)

**Explicit stakeholder-engagement task.** Adding task/implementation guidance ensuring the right people are involved in developing AI policies and procedures and receive adequate training, not solely technical controls.

**Maintaining skills for human oversight.** Adding guidance here (and in other practices as relevant) to better stress that human oversight and human-as-backup requires not just existing, but possibly new, training as taking over from an AI system (particularly in an emergency) can require additional skills compared to just performing the task. 

#### Practice [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) (Validating AI-generated artifacts)

**AI-assisted coding tech debt.** Risk that AI-generated code or documentation drifts from, or misrepresents, actual system behavior over time.

### Sector-Specific References to be Included

We have compiled an initial baseline of established, sector-specific cybersecurity, process safety, and operational standards brought to our attention by the community. Moving forward, our internal roadmap aims to do more than simply index these documents; we intend to explicitly translate them into an AI risk management context.

We actively seek targeted input from domain experts on how to best frame this translation, which specific clauses or sub-sections offer the most transferable utility to AI deployment, where these references can be most effectively mapped into our profile, supporting text to properly contextualize them, and additional/alternative references that we should consider. 

**Oil and Natural Gas:** API Standard 1164 (Pipeline Control Systems Cybersecurity), API RP 780 (Security Risk Assessment), API RP 754 (Process Safety Performance Indicators), IOGP Report 556 (Cybersecurity Capability and Maturity), TSA Security Directive Pipeline-2021-02, and ONG-ISAC threat advisories.

**Electric / Bulk Power:** NERC CIP-002 through CIP-014 (with emphasis on CIP-013-2 for supply chain controls), IEEE Standard 1547-2018 (Interconnection and Interoperability of Distributed Energy Resources), IEC 61850 (Power Utility Automation Networks), EPRI AI research program outputs, and DOE CESER AI guidance.

**Water and Wastewater Systems:** AWWA G430 (Security Practices for Operation and Management), AWWA M19 (Emergency Planning), the EPA Cybersecurity for the Water Sector toolkit, America's Water Infrastructure Act (AWIA) of 2018 Section 2013 risk assessment requirements, and WaterISAC 15 Cybersecurity Fundamentals.

**Healthcare and Public Health:** HHS 405(d) Health Industry Cybersecurity Practices, FDA Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development, the HIPAA Security Rule (45 CFR Part 164, Subpart C as applied to AI processing of protected health info), and HHS HC3 sector advisories.

**Emergency Services / Public Safety:** NFPA 1500 (Fire Department Occupational Safety, Health, and Wellness), NFPA 1600 (Continuity, Emergency, and Crisis Management), and NIST Public Safety Communications Research (PSCR) AI for First Responders program outputs, and placeholders for upcoming IAFC AI working group outputs.

**Cross-Sector Industrial & Process Safety Foundations:** ISA-84 / IEC 61511 (Functional Safety: Safety Instrumented Systems for the Process Industry), ANSI/ISA-95 (Enterprise-Control System Integration), IEC 61508 (Functional Safety of Electronic Safety-Related Systems), and ISO 31000 (Risk Management Guidelines), and ISA-18.2 (Alarm Management Systems).

### Standards Bodies, Consortia, and Efforts We're Tracking

Here's a broader (non-exhaustive) list of groups, standards, and initiatives already on our radar, including some we now cite in the table and are continuing to track as their work develops. Please feel free to suggest additions or additional contextualizations, particularly with a note on which specific part is most relevant to this profile.

**AI supply chain / AIBOM:** SPDX AI & Datasets Team; OWASP AIBOM Project and GenAI AI-SBOM Initiative; CycloneDX ML-BOM guidance; OpenChain AI SBOM compliance guide; MITRE System of Trust; the G7 shared vision paper on SBOM for AI; FDA's AIBOM effort.

**Agentic system security:** CoSAI Workstreams 1, 3, and 4 (supply chain; AI risk governance; secure design for agentic systems), including their persona/risk-map work and Project Codeguard; OWASP GenAI Agentic Security Initiative; AIUC-1 agent standards; GUAC (software security metadata aggregation).

**Cross-industry consortia and working groups:** OpenSSF's AI/ML Working Group and GEMARA project; the Coalition for Secure AI (CoSAI) more broadly; FINOS Common Cloud Controls; OSCAL-COMPASS; the Alliance for Critical Infrastructure (ACI) launched Feb 26, 2026 as a cross-sector resilience and crisis-coordination nonprofit

**Sector-specific:** Cyber Risk Institute's Financial Services AI RMF profile and associated guidebook/control matrix; FS-ISAC; FSSCC's AI lexicon; Oil and Natural Gas Sector Coordinating Council (ONG-SCC); DOE's Supply Chain Cybersecurity Principles and ICS cybersecurity testing guidance.

**Standards bodies:** ISO/IEC 22989 (AI concepts/terminology) and ISO/IEC TS 5723 (trustworthiness vocabulary); ISO/IEC TR 5469 (functional safety and AI systems); IEEE P7001 (transparency of autonomous systems) and IEEE 2841 (deep learning evaluation framework); the ISA/IEC 62443 series and IEC 62351 (grid automation/control protocol security).

**Risk frameworks and taxonomies:** FAIR Institute's cyber risk scenario taxonomy and risk ontology; UC Berkeley CLTC's work on AI risk thresholds and a standalone AI trustworthiness taxonomy.

**International:** OECD's Framework for the Classification of AI Systems; relevant EU AI Act provisions (Articles 25 and 53); national AI governance directives and automated-decision-making frameworks in other jurisdictions where CI supply chains cross borders.

**Related NIST/NCCoE efforts already coordinated with this work:** SP 800-53 AI control overlays (COSAIS) and the associated predictive-AI outline; NCCoE's Software and AI Agent Identity and Authorization project; the forthcoming Cyber AI Profile (IR 8596).

*As with all items above, we're especially interested in: (1) whether these are worth including at all, (2) where they'd best fit if so, and (3) any stronger or more specific references you can point us to.*

# 

# Conceptual Changelog 

This section summarizes key conceptual changes between draft versions, deliberately omitting minor edits. It is not an exhaustive diff, but a targeted summary to help reviewers identify substantive updates relevant to their feedback.

## Sep 28, 2026(a)

This is a minor update to the Sep 13 draft. It adds one Task, six Implementations and a section on using the profile, and otherwise clarifies and corrects existing text. No Practice, Task or Implementation has been removed.

**Three new Implementations address how operating context and source data can change without any change to the AI system.** Implementation [1.1.3: Establish triggers for re-validating requirements…](#bookmark=id.nynas4d7wk96) covers assets whose applicable rules change when they relocate or their contract status changes, such as a mobile asset entering a different jurisdiction, and Task [7.5: Conduct Impact Assessments and Risk Management for AI System and Model Updates](#bookmark=kix.up940xmlpl4i) now also applies where such a change alters rules encoded in the AI system, such as in its prompts, retrieval corpus, or guardrails. Implementation [7.2.3: Assess the conditions under which source data is created](#bookmark=id.alyth0u9vept) extends provenance upstream of ingestion to the point of capture, where records can be lost in manual transfer chains, overwritten, or degraded by migration between maintenance management systems. Implementation [9.1.3: Include data-originating roles in competency mapping](#bookmark=id.73ub9anha1k5) brings field engineers, maintenance technicians and others who create source data into role-based training, even where they never use an AI system directly.

**A new Section 1.4, Using this Profile, suggests where different readers might start.** It gathers the guidance on non-linear reading and on starting from Appendix B that was previously spread through the Introduction, adds starting points for technical staff, for those focused on a trustworthiness characteristic, for those mapping to other frameworks and for organizations with limited resources, and discusses using the document with an AI assistant, including the organizational context an assistant needs and the ways it can misrepresent the document.

**The profile now states how it relates to existing assurance regimes and to zero trust.** The Scope now says that existing assurance regimes, including authorization and continuous monitoring, can remain the foundation, and that this profile builds on them with attention to what should trigger reassessment and to monitoring what an AI system does, not only how it is configured. It also describes the profile as applying the zero trust principle, that trust is verified at each boundary rather than assumed, to AI risk more generally, including to a legitimate, uncompromised AI system that is not behaving as intended. This is carried into Practice [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and agentic behavior](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p), Task [3.2: Implement independent guardrails (“safety wrappers”) for AI outputs](#bookmark=kix.4os1ox8rgrqn) and Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck), and zero trust is added to the Glossary.

**Language incorrectly implying guarantees removed.** Throughout the table, statements that an item "ensures" an outcome now say that it "helps ensure" it, and similar claims elsewhere, such as guardrails that structurally block unsafe commands even from a fully compromised AI system, have been restated as what the item can do.

**Terms used in their general sense do not carry sector-specific meanings.** The introduction to the table now states that where a sector, regulation or standard gives a term such as validation, verification, incident or safety function a precise meaning, this profile does not invoke that meaning, and following the profile does not by itself establish conformance with requirements stated in those terms. The Glossary adds industrial control system, noting that ICS means something different in emergency management. Table 1 now cites National Security Memorandum 22 as the source of the 16 sectors.

**Explanation features are no longer treated as evidence that a recommendation is correct.** A new Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s) gathers the requirements on confidence and rationale shown to personnel, previously in Implementation [4.2.3: Human-Centered Interfaces for AI Uncertainty and Actionable Triggers](#bookmark=id.64phfsnphbx3), and adds guidance for symbolic, hybrid and self-interpretable AI systems, whose rationale can be a trace of the steps actually applied. Implementation [4.6.1: Design rationale displays that give personnel something to check](#bookmark=id.sazux36izue4) notes research finding that showing explanations alongside recommendations can increase acceptance whether or not the recommendation is correct, and suggests testing whether a rationale display improves decisions for the personnel who will use it, including whether they still reject incorrect recommendations. Task [9.2: Deliver specialized training on human-AI interaction and automation bias](#bookmark=kix.wdmhy3mmlldl) adds training on this effect. Task [4.6: Ensure that confidence and rationale shown to personnel reflect how the AI system reached its output](#bookmark=id.l3bjk6gfhz8s) also carries a new tension between the Accurate and Explainable & Interpretable characteristics, and Implementation [10.1.2: Immutable Runtime Telemetry and Artifact Tracking](#bookmark=id.xxgb2fikrm8l) notes that the inference receipt can record the rules applied, which provides the displayed trace. Implementation [4.2.3: Human-Centered Interfaces for AI Uncertainty and Actionable Triggers](#bookmark=id.64phfsnphbx3), previously titled Human-Centered Interfaces for AI Uncertainty, Rationale, and Actionable Triggers, now addresses uncertainty and triage cues, and its Trust/Execute cue is renamed Proceed, which indicates that an output falls within the conditions the AI system was validated for rather than that it is correct. 

**A refused action part-way through a sequence needs its own fail-safe state.** Implementation [3.2.3: Specify fail-safe states and fallback behavior](#bookmark=kix.fbdjwm7150p) now covers the case where an AI action is refused under Implementation [5.2.2: Action-level, context-aware authorization policies](#bookmark=kix.68ohugbvqcsm) after earlier steps have already executed, with a worked example of a pump changeover.

**Vendor hazard reachability attestations should be corroborated before they suppress alarms.** Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q) now says vendor self-attestation should not be treated as sufficient to suppress a warning, and Task [4.4: Attention Governance and Alert/Alarm Flood Mitigation](#bookmark=kix.l9ljdahqd6y) applies this to alarm suppression. Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q) also notes that the existing VEX justification for inline mitigations covers only protections built into the product, so controls that an operator deploys around an AI system would need to be stated as a separate justification.

**Retiring a protective AI capability is a change to controls as well as an asset disposal.** Implementation [13.3.2: Treat the retirement of a protective AI capability as a change to controls](#bookmark=id.7lcrj6u5dyyf) and Task [13.3: Preserve what is needed and hand over what depended on the system](#bookmark=id.7u2ur7qhofep) previously framed these as alternatives.

**Some statements now cite their source in the text.** Where a sentence rests on a specific study or document, the source is now named in the form "(see Author Year)" or “(see ID Year)”, for example in Task [3.6: Mitigate automation complacency and skills degradation](#bookmark=kix.d601y3q6ud1n), Implementation [4.5.1: Define engineering time-constant thresholds](#bookmark=id.mum8tq4afxyp), Implementation [4.6.1: Design rationale displays that give personnel something to check](#bookmark=id.sazux36izue4), Implementation [6.1.2: Require AIBOM for mission-critical applications](#bookmark=kix.86017r9tu0hx) and Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q).

**Corrections.** The example in Implementation [2.1.2: Develop process-informed guardrails](#bookmark=kix.twbuv0dvlcz4) had the operating conditions reversed; guardrails now allow more headroom during quieter periods and tighter control during rapid change or heightened activity. Several pointers to where an adjacent concern is handled now point to a more specific item, most notably from Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x) and Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) to Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych) rather than Task [7.6: Establish processes for internal AI component deprecation and decommissioning](#bookmark=id.5ka8tq8kurdg), and from Practice [13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority](#bookmark=id.ieoypls7vre2) to Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i) and Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz) rather than Practice [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41).

**Other clarifications.** Table 2 relates the Reliable, Robust and Resilient characteristics to the terms of a typical service-level agreement, and revises the Bias Managed and Fair entries. Implementation [2.2.2: Deploy “watchdog” controllers](#bookmark=kix.ygluakfzcdgd) notes that a watchdog may itself be part of the attack surface. The References field description notes that citation descriptions are meant to help locate successors to revised or superseded documents. Search Keywords were added in Practices [2: Define AI Robustness, Resilience, and Quality of Service expectations](#bookmark=kix.za6xza2uty41), [3: Define risks, policies, and oversight for automated AI and…](#bookmark=kix.2ojl7hy6lr0p), [4: Define procedures for emergency avoidance override, recovery, and situation…](#bookmark=kix.6enzvnk23tsf), [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents…](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) and [8: Incorporate AI-aware incident analysis and response procedures](#bookmark=kix.n5h96sew2gzb), and reviewer notes were added on the crosswalks, the glossary, and record retention for retired systems.

## 

## Sep 13, 2026

This entry is organized by what changed rather than by where, because most of what follows was answered in more than one place. Feedback on the August draft was often combined with related input and addressed somewhere other than where it was raised, sometimes at a different level of the table, and sometimes through several smaller changes that only add up when read together. If you are looking for whether something you raised was acted on, you may find it more quickly by scanning the themes below than by returning to the cell you commented on.

We have indicated where changes apply substantially to only part of the document. No Practice, Task or Implementation has been removed; everything present in the August draft is still there.

**A thirteenth Practice covers retiring AI systems.** Practice [13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority](#bookmark=id.ieoypls7vre2) treats the permanent removal of an AI capability as its own problem rather than as an extreme form of updating one: an update transfers trust from one version to the next, while retirement withdraws authority, removes a capability other things have come to rely on, and destroys operational knowledge that may exist nowhere else. Task [13.1: Prepare for retirement while the system is still running](#bookmark=id.dmgpdkrwold1) covers what has to be prepared while the system still runs, because retirement can be imposed by a withdrawn vendor or a deprecated model and can arrive after the system has stopped working. Task [13.2: Withdraw authority completely and confirm that it is withdrawn](#bookmark=id.yrqploy3uych) covers withdrawing authority completely and confirming it. Task [13.3: Preserve what is needed and hand over what depended on the system](#bookmark=id.7u2ur7qhofep) covers preserving evidence that teardown would destroy and recording what happens to everything that depended on the system. The existing treatment of internal AI artifacts, in Task [7.6: Establish processes for internal AI component deprecation and decommissioning](#bookmark=id.5ka8tq8kurdg), is unchanged; Practice [13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority](#bookmark=id.ieoypls7vre2) addresses the operating capability and its authority rather than the models, datasets and pipelines themselves.

**Placeholder Implementations are now written, most heavily in Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck) and Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f).** Thirty-two Implementations that carried a single line of intent in the August draft now carry actual guidance. Nine of them are in Practice [5: Implement identity and access management (IdAM) for AI agents, systems, and tools](#bookmark=kix.1dfxkyb5ysck), covering AI agent identity, access mapping, action-level authorization, permission scope, and logging and anomaly detection for agent activity. Nine more are in Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f), covering process ranking, dependency mapping, manual fallback, safe operating mode, standby personnel and infrastructure, re-engagement validation, and fail-to-manual exercises. If you passed over either Practice in August because there was little to review, they are worth a second look.

**Organizational readiness is now something to assess and record before adopting an AI capability.** New Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x), with Implementation [3.9.1: Establish Accountable Ownership](#bookmark=id.w3n0hyju2d4t), Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz), Implementation [3.9.3: Establish Escalation to Empowered Authority](#bookmark=id.9759jl1m3g58), Implementation [3.9.4: Assess Operational Capability](#bookmark=id.yrvsqv9lawpo), and Implementation [3.9.5: Demonstrate Operational Enforceability](#bookmark=id.u6re9dsbehqs) covering accountable ownership, decision authority and reassessment triggers, escalation to an empowered authority, operational capability, and demonstrated enforceability. Related material sits in Task [3.6: Mitigate automation complacency and skills degradation](#bookmark=kix.d601y3q6ud1n) on maintaining human capability and in Implementation [9.2.1: Include role-specific training on “AI failure modes”](#bookmark=kix.2u307dyu52tc) and Implementation [9.2.2: Implement realistic incident simulation training](#bookmark=kix.slhmi67qv3as) on role-specific training.

**Near-misses can now be reported without the reporter bearing the cost.** New Task [8.6: Establish non-punitive reporting for AI-related near-misses](#bookmark=id.jjat17xw4ys6), with Implementation [8.6.1: Separate benefit-claiming from risk-reporting](#bookmark=id.yqpv6d8y5jkm) on separating benefit-claiming from risk-reporting, Implementation [8.6.2: Protect reporter identity through an independent reporting route](#bookmark=id.jp1wadxdrg3q) on protecting reporter identity through an independent route, Implementation [8.6.3: Ensure supplier terms do not penalize degradation disclosure](#bookmark=id.aac5ure6pkzc) on supplier terms that penalize disclosure, and Implementation [8.6.4: Share de-identified incident records for collective defense](#bookmark=id.f1wih8dn4veu) on sharing de-identified records. Implementation [9.2.3: Recognizing disincentives to reporting AI degradation](#bookmark=id.nkod4zi9q2i8) addresses recognizing the disincentives themselves.

**External AI components are now tracked from procurement through end of life.** Implementation [6.1.1: Add AI disclosure to existing procurement policies](#bookmark=kix.untd4qjdgbrs) on AI disclosure in procurement policy and Implementation [6.1.2: Require AIBOM for mission-critical applications](#bookmark=kix.86017r9tu0hx) on requiring an AI Bill of Materials, and Implementation [6.2.1: Register externally sourced AI components in asset management systems](#bookmark=kix.h4lh8o2v4js0), Implementation [6.2.2: Track vendor updates and third-party entity changes through change management process](#bookmark=kix.kiwwb3qmwzvw), and Implementation [6.2.3: Manage end-of-life transitions for externally sourced AI components](#bookmark=kix.21o7ua4uecm4) on registering externally sourced components, tracking vendor updates and third-party changes through change management, and managing end-of-life transitions.

**An AI entity's authority can be bounded, delegated and withdrawn.** Implementation [5.3.2: Task-Bounded and Revocable Agent Authority](#bookmark=kix.u9imrlz0bbhe) became Task-Bounded and Revocable Agent Authority, Implementation [5.2.2: Action-level, context-aware authorization policies](#bookmark=kix.68ohugbvqcsm) became Action-level, context-aware authorization policies, and new Implementation [5.3.3: Separate privilege via encapsulated downstream agents and tools](#bookmark=id.p1isj88exw8y) covers separating privilege through encapsulated downstream agents and tools.

**Constraints on AI behavior are now required to be enforced independently of the AI system.** Implementation [3.2.2: Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints](#bookmark=kix.lq647hpnawla) became "Define and independently enforce deterministic operational constraints", and Practice [12: Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle](#bookmark=kix.9rkfrz8cn8ul) became "Independently verify AI-generated operational logic and artifacts across their lifecycle". Both titles changed to carry that point rather than imply it.

**In Practices 1 to 6, each Practice and Task now states what it does not cover and where the adjacent concern is handled.** This is the combined effect of three rebuilt fields: the AI RMF mappings now say what an item contributes and what it does not; the trustworthiness entries state the limits of each contribution; and reference descriptions name the part of a source that applies and where it stops. Practices 7 to 12 have not yet had this work and carry markers instead; Practice [13: Establish procedures for retiring AI systems and withdrawing their authority](#bookmark=id.ieoypls7vre2) was written on the new scheme from the start.

**The AI RMF mapping field was replaced rather than renamed.** "Primary AI RMF Mappings" is now "Related AI RMF subcategories", and the change is one of meaning: listing a subcategory indicates that an item can contribute toward that outcome, not that following it satisfies the subcategory. If you commented on the previous field, the basis of that comment has changed.

**References now say which part of a source applies and why it is cited here.** Each entry opens with a label for the kind of document, and the description names the clause, section or control that bears on the item. This has been done for Practices 1 to 6 and 13; Practices 7 to 12 retain their earlier reference lists.

**Cross-references now carry the title of what they point to.** Every internal reference in the table is a link showing the name of the target Practice, Task or Implementation, rather than an index number alone.

**Human oversight has a defined vocabulary.** The Glossary and Terminology Crosswalk now defines human oversight together with oversight mode, oversight capacity, oversight threshold and oversight authority, and these terms are used consistently through the table.

**An appendix is reserved for crosswalks to external frameworks.** Appendix D is now Crosswalks, and is a placeholder under development. The reviewer material that was Appendix D in the August draft is now Appendix E. If you have notes referring to "Appendix D", check which one you meant. 

**The profile states that it covers high-consequence applications beyond critical infrastructure.** This was implicit in the August draft and is now stated in the title and carried through the Introduction and Scope.

**Search Keywords updated across the document.** These have been chosen to both assist readers (and their AI assistants) in locating relevant Practices and Tasks in this document, as well as to provide candidate keywords for readers to use when searching other resources. 

### Cells whose text was substantially rewritten

Thirty Practices, Tasks and Implementations that carried substantive text in the August draft have been materially rewritten, over and above the thirty-two placeholders now written out and the thirty-two cells that are new. If you commented on any of these, the text you commented on has changed.

| Location | What changed |
| :---- | :---- |
| **1** | Requirements now cover the end-to-end system, not the AI component alone; states that deciding against AI is a valid outcome |
| **1.1.2** | Expanded from a requirements matrix into named categories: task and mission scope, dependencies, and the operational system around the AI |
| **1.3.1** | Test suites now scale to consequence tier, with nominal and boundary envelope testing named |
| **1.3.4** | Evidence now pairs governance artifacts with technical artifacts |
| **1.4** | Scoped to systems that interact in natural language, and extended to ontologies, graphical conventions and multimodal output |
| **2.2** | Recast from "integrate non-AI backups" to a risk-proportionate redundancy strategy matched to consequence severity |
| **2.2.1** | Same recasting at implementation level, with a redundancy gradient tied to risk thresholds |
| **2.2.2** | Watchdogs must not themselves introduce unmanaged complexity or new attack surface |
| **2.3.3** | Substantially expanded: digital twins and hardware-in-the-loop, beyond offline data replay |
| **2.3.4** | Re-evaluation is now periodic **and** trigger-based, with distribution drift defined |
| **2.5** | Adds the worked example of a learned policy depending on out-of-specification component behavior |
| **2.5.1** | Training across the full operating range, with simulation and synthetic parameter cycling |
| **3** | Now states its relationship to Practices 1, 2 and 4, and separates deterministic boundaries from human intervention |
| **3.2.1** | Intercept points may be enforced at more than one location; introduces the guarded proxy architecture |
| **3.2.5** | A reviewer must hold authority to reject and capacity to examine; presence of a reviewer is not verification |
| **3.6** | Reframed: reliance on AI is the intended result of adopting it, and practice has to be arranged rather than assumed |
| **3.8.3** | Couples the risk surface to the time-constant thresholds, since a widened surface can cross a previously satisfied threshold |
| **4** | Now points to Practice [11: Maintain AI-aware mission continuity and disaster recovery planning](#bookmark=kix.oink7y7pfa1f) for sustained manual operation |
| **4.4.3** | Rewritten: reduced staffing is named as an expected benefit, and oversight capacity is drawn from the pool that benefit reduces |
| **4.5** | Oversight of any mode fails where the event outruns what the operator can be relied on to do; thresholds make this testable |
| **4.5.1** | Rebuilt around what intervention can be relied upon at minutes versus seconds, rather than a single reaction-time figure |
| **6.1.5** | Extended to contextual hazard reachability attestations and their use in alarm suppression |
| **7.4** | Separates who may act on the system from whether a specific action is permitted at execution time |
| **7.4.1** | Approvals and logging framed around reduced oversight modes and automation bias; records the evidence an approval rested on |
| **10.1.2** | Adds the inference receipt: the data elements, thresholds and configuration that produced an output |
| **11.5.2** | Simulation drills now require at least one full loss of AI infrastructure |
| **12.1** | Accountable ownership now routes to Task [3.9: Assess and document organizational readiness for each AI-enabled capability](#bookmark=id.fr37s87lrv5x) rather than being defined only here |
| **12.1.3** | Authorization roles sit within the decision authority documented at Implementation [3.9.2: Define Decision Authority and Reassessment Triggers](#bookmark=id.juld683w9rdz) |
| **3.2.5, 4.1.1, 3.8.1** | Changed only by relocating a citation into the References field or adding a cross-reference; the guidance itself is unchanged |

Note: Versions “Sep 13, 2026 (a)” and “Sep 13, 2026 (b)” only differ by line/breaks, table spacing, and this note. 

## Aug 3, 2026

### 1\. Document Scope & Formatting Updates

* **Markdown Availability & Parsing Guidance (*Document Structure*)**: Updated from HTML to Markdown for AI assistant pre-processing. Added explicit warnings that pre-processors may truncate table rows or appendices unless directed to parse the document in its entirety.

* **General-Purpose AI Assistants (*Section 2: Scope*)**: Expanded scope to explicitly cover general-purpose AI tools (e.g., code, configuration, or procedure generators; summarizers). Added the concept of "second-order risk," noting that engineer review of AI-generated output does not eliminate operational risk when deployed to OT/ICS environments.

### 2\. Brand New Tasks & Major Structural Additions

* **Task [3.8: Scope and map the expanded AI Attack Surface and Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.sq4rjxi0nx93)**: Added a dedicated task explicitly bifurcating exposure into adversarial threats (**AI Attack Surface**) and systemic/operational non-adversarial failure modes across all 7 TACIP Trustworthiness Characteristics (**AI Risk Surface**).

* **Task [4.5: Define operational regimes for human-on-the-loop and human-out-of-the-loop operation](#bookmark=id.74l42rpltud)**: Added controls for processes with tight time constants where real-time human intervention should not serve as a primary risk mitigation control. 

* **Implementation [3.3.3: Actionable Operator Decision & Escalation Pathways](#bookmark=id.wfndrt86zm06)**: Added four operational response buckets (*Trust & Execute*, *Spot-Check & Investigate*, *Escalate to Oversight*, *Immediate Emergency Override*).

* **Implementation [4.2.3: Human-Centered Interfaces for AI Uncertainty and Actionable Triggers](#bookmark=id.64phfsnphbx3)**: Suggests requirements for HSI/HMI displays (visual confidence bands, rationale summaries). Explicitly recommends against post-hoc attribution tools (e.g., SHAP, LIME) for primary operator rationale displays.

* **Implementation [6.1.5: Operational AI Hazard Reachability Disclosures](#bookmark=id.pr7bqdwtcb1q)**: Adapts VEX principles to AI by separating standard software CVE VEX from *Contextual Hazard Reachability* for probabilistic model behaviors.

* **Implementation [8.1.5: Multi-agent cascade reconstruction capabilities](#bookmark=id.7lfs6assprtz) & [8.1.6: Incident Triage Across the Dual Attack/Risk Surface](#bookmark=id.k4b1b3pizi4w)**: Added guidance to differentiate cyber intrusions from non-adversarial algorithmic failures.

* **Implementation [10.1.3: Forensic Integrity and Privacy-Preserving Log Partitioning](#bookmark=id.vziiyw6bsa9k)**: Added guidance on log partitioning to route raw unredacted text/prompts directly into appropriately isolated, secure, tamper-evident storage.

* **Implementation [11.2.3: Human Factors and Cognitive Transition Protocols for Control Handoffs](#bookmark=id.scvcymfio39v) & [11.2.4: Unplanned Shutdown and Controlled Isolation Protocols](#bookmark=id.b149vgyv8fe5)**: Added *State-at-Handoff* displays and controlled isolation steps to reduce operator "cold start" cognitive shock during forced manual handoffs.

### 3\. Key Conceptual Revisions to Existing Controls

* **TEVV Production Guidance (*Implementation [1.3.1: Define TEVV scope and requirements per AI system](#bookmark=kix.54aziklwcuxz) & [1.3.2: Categorization of Testing Environments](#bookmark=kix.cmzu78ucduaj)*)**: Advises that high-risk testing, fuzzing, or red-teaming should be performed on isolated staging, HIL test rigs, digital twins, or during scheduled downtime windows, rather than on live systems.

* **Process-Informed Rate Limiting (*Implementation [2.1.1: Implement baseline rate limiting](#bookmark=kix.5erbb4a4g9je)*)**: Clarified that rate limits should be dynamic and process-informed (Implementation [2.1.2: Develop process-informed guardrails](#bookmark=kix.twbuv0dvlcz4)) rather than fixed ceilings, so that valid high-volume commands are less likely to be suppressed during abnormal process upsets.

* **Time-to-Divergence Cadence (*Implementation [2.3.4: Recurrent Risk Evaluations](#bookmark=kix.vvvo5d4fb2i)*)**: Introduced quantitative *Time-to-Divergence* baselining during staging to dictate model risk evaluation schedules as a fraction of estimated drift velocity.

* **Consequence-Driven Risk Thresholds (*Implementation [3.1.3: Define acceptable risk thresholds and associated control responses](#bookmark=kix.xm0g6xvrum0d)*)**: Warns against over-indexing on CVSS or raw confidence scores, directing organizations to consequence-driven frameworks like SSVC.

* **Non-Human Identity (NHI) Enforcement (*Implementation [5.1.1: Audit for and replace shared credentials, service accounts, secrets, passwords, and API keys](#bookmark=kix.qvztrp15ksqx)*)**: Specifies distinct cryptographic identities per sub-agent/tool invocation and warns against sharing enterprise API keys or master service accounts across agent nodes.

* **Compensating Controls for Silent Updates (*Implementation [6.1.1: Add AI disclosure to existing procurement policies](#bookmark=kix.untd4qjdgbrs) & [6.1.2: Require AIBOM for mission-critical applications](#bookmark=kix.86017r9tu0hx)*)**: Added alternative controls (perimeter staging, egress gates, safety wrapper tightening) when suspending automated vendor updates is unfeasible, and reframed AIBOM compliance as a flexible gradient.

* **Logical Policy Locks (*Implementation [7.3.3: Implement Logical Policy Locks for Adaptive and Online-Learning Models](#bookmark=id.c0vkbw7ex3vd)*)**: Specifies automated "Policy Locks" to programmatically freeze model weights, hyperparameters, and boundary logic for adaptive/online-learning models during operational windows.

* **Substrate Substitution Triage (*Implementation [7.5.4: Audit Interoperability for Hardware or Data Substrate Substitution](#bookmark=kix.sg34y8w5ezvi)*)**: Expanded component replacement change-management logic into a 3-tier triage approach (unrelated vs. non-critical vs. safety-critical paths requiring full regression).

* **Forensic Interpretability Gap (*Implementation [8.1.2: Identify AI systems that may not satisfy root cause analysis requirements](#bookmark=kix.o0fgigw7uop7)*)**: Added new citations and warnings emphasizing that post-hoc explanations (SHAP/LIME) are ungrounded "reconstructed narratives" and should not satisfy forensic requirements.

### 4\. New Appendices

* **Appendix B (*Historic Incident-to-Practice Mapping*)**: Added a complete table mapping 32 real-world incidents (i1–i32) across CI sectors directly to the 12 Practices to justify governance controls.

* **Appendix A & C**: Designated explicit placeholders for *Trustworthiness Characteristics* (App A) and *Use Cases & Operational Scenarios* (App C).
